 \exists x,y\ge0\ \bigl(x^2=2y^2+1\ \wedge\ x>t\ \wedge\ y>0\bigr),
\end{equation}
existential with a quantifier-free ordered-ring matrix. Over $\Oz_{\ge0}$,
\[
 \Phi(0),\qquad
 \forall t\ge0\ (\Phi(t)\Rightarrow\Phi(t+1)),
 \qquad \neg\Phi(\omega),
\]
and $\Phi(t)$ holds exactly for the ordinary nonnegative integers $t$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The pair $(3,2)$ witnesses $\Phi(0)$. Given witnesses $x,y$ for $\Phi(t)$,
set $x'=3x+4y$, $y'=2x+3y$. The identity of \cref{odg:lem:pellsequence}
gives $(x')^2=2(y')^2+1$; positivity and discreteness give $x,y\ge1$, so
$x'>x+1>t+1$ and $y'>0$. This proves the successor step for every omnific
$t$. Pell rigidity makes every witness ordinary, so $x$ cannot exceed an
infinite $t$ such as $\omega$. Conversely an ordinary nonnegative $t$ has a
larger positive ordinary Pell witness by \cref{odg:lem:pellsequence}. By
\cref{odg:prop:units} these are the only two kinds of nonnegative omnific
integers.
\end{proof}

By Shepherdson's theorem, an integer part of a real closed ordered field
satisfies open induction on its nonnegative cone, that is, induction for
quantifier-free ordered-ring formulas with parameters
\cite{Shepherdson,Glivicky}. In a divisible set-sized Hahn workspace $H$, the ring
$\Oz_H$ is an integer part of the real closed field of real Hahn series with
exponents in $H$: the floor formula in \cref{odg:thm:floor} only truncates
support and changes the constant, so stays in this workspace.

Here is the passage to the full $\Oz$ in detail. Fix a quantifier-free
formula $\theta(t,\mathbf a)$ whose base case and successor implication hold
in $\Oz_{\ge0}$, and suppose it fails at some $b\ge0$. Choose $H$ to contain
the supports of the finite parameter tuple $\mathbf a$ and of $b$.
Quantifier-free formulas agree between an ordered subring and its ambient
ring: polynomial values, equality, order and Boolean operations are
preserved. Thus the base case and successor implication hold in
$(\Oz_H)_{\ge0}$, whereas $\theta(b,\mathbf a)$ still fails there,
contradicting Shepherdson's theorem. This proves each instance of the schema
without treating the proper class $\Oz$ as a set-sized model \src{05}.
There is no conflict with
\cref{odg:thm:induction}: $\Phi$ is existential, not quantifier free. The
sentence ``every nonnegative $t$ has a larger positive Pell solution'' is
true over $\Z$ and false over $\Oz$; thus $\Oz$ is not an elementary extension
of $\Z$ as an ordered ring.

\begin{remark}[An induction failure in the pure ring language \mergetag]\label{odg:rem:ringinduction}
Induction on the nonnegative cone also fails for an existential formula
of the pure ring language, without order in its matrix:
the existential formula $\Std(t)$ of \cref{odg:thm:standarddef} satisfies
$\Std(0)$ and $\forall t\,(\Std(t)\Rightarrow\Std(t+1))$ over $\Oz$ but not
$\Std(\omega)$, because it defines $\Z$, which contains $0$ and is closed
under $t\mapsto t+1$, and $\omega\notin\Z$. The induction variable is still
restricted to the nonnegative cone; the displayed successor assertion is
stronger, since it holds for every $t\in\Oz$.

One can also keep every existential witness inside that cone. In the
language $\{0,1,+,\cdot\}$ of the nonnegative semiring, use
\[
 \exists x,y,z_1,z_2,z_3,z_4\quad
 x^2=2y^2+1\ \land\ x=t^2+\sum_{j=1}^4 z_j^2.
\]
For each ordinary $t\ge0$, the Pell sequence and the four-square theorem
supply nonnegative ordinary witnesses. Conversely, any witnesses in the
nonnegative cone are also witnesses in $\Oz$, so force $t\in\Z$.
This order-free existential formula therefore defines precisely $\N$
inside $\Oz_{\ge0}$, and has the same base case, successor step and failure
at $\omega$. No subtraction or order predicate is needed in its matrix.

Separately, over the whole ring, the formula
$\Std(t)$ also shows that $\Oz$ is not an elementary extension of $\Z$ in the
ring language: the sentence $\forall t\,\Std(t)$ holds in $\Z$ and fails in
$\Oz$.
\end{remark}

\section{Diophantine arithmetic in omnific rings}\label{odg:def:sec:arithmetic}

This section and the next two are built from sources 06 and 07, written
independently on September 23, 2026 (\cref{odg:sub:provenance}). The two are
twins: they share the order-free Gaussian system, the intersective polynomial
and the Pell divisibility argument, and each has decisive results the other
lacks. Source 06 is the base. It gives the Diophantine definition of $\Pi$,
the Diophantine graph of $\ct$, the homomorphism theorem and the
reconstruction package; source 07 gives the constant-term detector, the ideal
test, intermediate rings and number-field coefficients. Shared results are
printed once with both sources named. Labels of this material carry the
sub-prefix \fn{odg:def:}.

\subsection{General nonnegative-support rings}\label{odg:def:sub:setting}

Let $\Bbbk$ be a field of characteristic zero and $\Gamma$ an ordered abelian
group. For a set-sized $\Gamma$ these constructions take place in an
ordinary Hahn field. When $\Gamma=\No$, every series still has set-sized
support; the same finite algebra is interpreted under the class convention
below, rather than treating $\No$ as a set. The notation $\omega^\gamma$ is
formal for an abstract group $\Gamma$ and is a Conway monomial in the actual
surreal instances. Consider series $f=\sum_\gamma a_\gamma\omega^\gamma$ with
$a_\gamma\in\Bbbk$ and reverse-well-ordered support in $\Gamma$, added
coefficientwise and multiplied by convolution: the Hahn field with the
exponent order reversed, written in the $\omega$-convention of
\cref{odg:sub:notation} \src{06, 07}. Put
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal A_\Bbbk(\Gamma)&=\{f:\supp(f)\subseteq\Gamma^{\ge0}\},\qquad
 \Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)=\{f:\supp(f)\subseteq\Gamma^{>0}\},\\
 \mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)&=\mathfrak o+\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)
 \qquad\text{for a unital subring }\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk .
\end{align*}
As in \cref{odg:prop:ring}, the constant coefficient $\ct$ is a ring
retraction of $\mathcal A_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$ onto $\Bbbk$ with kernel
$\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$, restricting to a retraction of
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ onto $\mathfrak o$. Following source
07, an \emph{intermediate ring} for $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$ is a ring $A$
with
\begin{equation}\label{odg:def:eq:intermediate}
 \mathfrak o\subseteq A\subseteq\mathcal A_\Bbbk(\Gamma),\qquad
 A\cap\Bbbk=\mathfrak o ;
\end{equation}
no inclusion $\ct(A)\subseteq\mathfrak o$ is required. This is weaker than
being the full ring $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$. For example,
$A=\Z[\omega+\tfrac12]\subseteq\R[\omega]=\mathcal A_\R(\Z)$ has
$A\cap\R=\Z$: a nonconstant polynomial in $\omega+\tfrac12$ still has
positive degree. Nevertheless $\ct(\omega+\tfrac12)=\tfrac12\notin\Z$.
Thus the constant-term argument for $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}$ must not be
silently applied to every intermediate ring. The instances used in
this report are
\begin{gather*}
 \BR=\mathcal A_\R(\No),\qquad \BC=\mathcal A_\C(\No),\qquad
 \Pi=\Pi_\R(\No),\qquad \Pi_{\C}=\Pi_\C(\No),\\
 \Oz=\mathcal R_\Z(\R,\No),\qquad \Og=\mathcal R_{\Z[i]}(\C,\No),
\end{gather*}
and $\Oz_\Gamma=\mathcal R_\Z(\R,\Gamma)$ of \cref{odg:sec:formal}. For
$\Gamma=\Z$ every reverse-well-ordered subset of $\Z^{\ge0}$ is finite, so
$\mathcal A_\Bbbk(\Z)=\Bbbk[\omega]$ and
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Z)=\mathfrak o+\omega\Bbbk[\omega]$ \src{06}.
For $\Gamma=\R$ supports such as $\{1,\frac12,\frac13,\ldots\}$ occur, and no
proof below uses a least positive exponent. For $\Gamma=\{0\}$ the ideal
is zero and $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)=\mathfrak o$; the
statements in this section include that case.

\begin{lemma}[Degree, units and algebraic elements \src{06, 07}]\label{odg:def:lem:units}
For nonzero $f,g\in\mathcal A_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$,
$\ddeg(fg)=\ddeg f+\ddeg g$, and $\mathcal A_\Bbbk(\Gamma)^\times=\Bbbk^\times$.
If a finite product of elements of $\mathcal A_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$ is a nonzero
constant, every factor is a nonzero constant. If $Q\in\Bbbk[T]$ has degree
$n\ge1$ and $f\in\mathcal A_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$ is nonconstant, then
$\ddeg Q(f)=n\ddeg f>0$. Consequently every element of
$\mathcal A_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$ algebraic over $\Bbbk$ lies in $\Bbbk$, and a
polynomial over $\Bbbk$ with no root in $\mathfrak o$ has no root in any
intermediate ring for $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $\alpha=\ddeg f$ and $\beta=\ddeg g$. Any exponents contributing to
a coefficient of $fg$ satisfy $\gamma\le\alpha$ and $\delta\le\beta$.
Equality $\gamma+\delta=\alpha+\beta$ forces $\gamma=\alpha$ and
$\delta=\beta$, so the coefficient there is the nonzero product of the two
leading coefficients. No term of larger degree occurs. If $fg=1$, the
nonnegative degrees $\alpha,\beta$ have sum zero, hence both vanish. A
nonnegative-support form of degree zero is constant; conversely every
nonzero constant has a constant inverse. The same degree argument applies
to a finite product equal to a nonzero constant.

If $f$ is nonconstant, then $d=\ddeg f>0$. For
$Q(T)=a_nT^n+\cdots+a_0$ with $a_n\ne0$, the leading coefficient of $a_nf^n$
is $a_n\lc(f)^n\ne0$ at exponent $nd$. Every nonzero term $a_jf^j$ with
$j<n$ has degree $jd<nd$, so that coefficient cannot cancel. This proves
$\ddeg Q(f)=nd>0$ without ordering the coefficient field. An algebraic
nonconstant element would make such a nonzero polynomial vanish, a
contradiction. A root of a nonconstant polynomial over $\Bbbk$ in $A$ must
therefore lie in $A\cap\Bbbk=\mathfrak o$; a nonzero constant polynomial has
no root anywhere.
\end{proof}

In particular $\Og^\times=\{\pm1,\pm i\}$, while the units of
$\BC=\C\oplus\Pi_{\C}$ are all of $\C^\times$; the larger ring is the one in
which the Pell factorization below takes place \src{06}.

\paragraph{Conventions.} A subset of $A^n$ is \emph{Diophantine} if it is
defined by a formula
$\exists\mathbf y\ \bigwedge_{j=1}^mP_j(\mathbf x,\mathbf y)=0$ with
$P_j\in\Z[\mathbf X,\mathbf Y]$ \src{06, 07}. This is the finite-system
convention; single-polynomial presentations are stated explicitly, and
negative atoms such as $y\ne0$ are not allowed. The numeral $2$ is the ring
term $1+1$. No formula below names an order, a monomial, $\sqrt2$ or a
coefficient map. For set-sized $\Gamma$ the statements are ordinary
first-order model theory. For $\Oz$ and $\Og$ a formula is interpreted by
relativizing its quantifiers to the class, formula by formula; no uniform
satisfaction predicate for the class structure, no set of all surreals and no
class of all class automorphisms is formed, and a statement about arbitrary
automorphisms is a statement about each map individually \src{06, 07}.

\subsection{The purely infinite ideal is Diophantine}

\begin{theorem}[A quadratic definition of the ideal \src{06}]\label{odg:def:thm:ideal}
Let $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$ be a subring such that $\sqrt2\in\Bbbk$ but
$2$ is not a square in $\Frac(\mathfrak o)$. For
$x\in\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:def:eq:ideal}
 x\in\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists y\in\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)\quad x^2=2y^2 .
\end{equation}
In particular the parameter-free formula
$\mathsf{Inf}(x):\Leftrightarrow\exists y\,(x^2=2y^2)$ defines $\Pi$ in $\Oz$
and $\Pi_{\C}$ in $\Og$, and the same formula works for every $\Gamma$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $x\in\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$, then $y=x/\sqrt2$ has the same support, so it lies
in $\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$ and witnesses the equation. Conversely, apply $\ct$ to
a solution: $a=\ct(x)$ and $b=\ct(y)$ lie in $\mathfrak o$ and $a^2=2b^2$. If
$b\ne0$, then $(a/b)^2=2$ in $\Frac(\mathfrak o)$, which is excluded; so $b=0$
and then $a=0$, that is, $x\in\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$. For $\mathfrak o=\Z$ the
hypothesis is the irrationality of $\sqrt2$; for $\mathfrak o=\Z[i]$, a square
root of $2$ in $\Q(i)$ would lie in $\Q(i)\cap\R=\Q$.
\end{proof}

The full positive-support part is needed for the forward implication:
its closure under multiplication by $1/\sqrt2$ supplies the witness.
The conclusion does not hold for arbitrary intermediate rings. For example,
$\omega\in A\cap\Pi_\R(\Z)$ for $A=\Z[\omega]$, but no $y\in A$ satisfies
$\omega^2=2y^2$: degree forces $y$ to be linear and its leading integer
coefficient would have square $1/2$. The nonsquare hypothesis also matters:
in $\mathcal R_{\Z[\sqrt2]}(\R,\Gamma)$ the nonzero constant $x=\sqrt2$
satisfies the equation with $y=1$, although it is not in the ideal.

For example $\pi\omega^3-\sqrt3\,\omega+7$ does not satisfy $\mathsf{Inf}$,
because its constant term is $7$, while $\pi\omega^3-\sqrt3\,\omega$ and
$\sum_{n\ge1}\omega^{1/n}$ do; ``purely infinite'' includes $0$ and negative
elements such as $-\omega$ \src{06}. The formula is existential, has no
parameters and one witness, so it answers the first part of
\cref{odg:q:existentialPi} (\cref{odg:def:sub:settled}). It is compatible with
\cref{odg:prop:notdiophantine}: $\Pi$ is stable under $\ct$, since
$\ct(\Pi)=\{0\}\subseteq\Pi$, whereas the nonzero locus is not.

First-order definability of $\Z$ in $\Oz$ is older. In a MathOverflow comment
of 6 July 2018 the user \emph{nombre} recognized ordinary integers by the
absence of nonzero solutions of $x^2=2y^2$ in a bounded interval, and observed
the resulting failure of saturation \cite{Nombre}. Source 06 states that the
equation of \cref{odg:def:thm:ideal} belongs to the same obstruction and is
not an unrelated discovery; the new point is its positive-existential form.

\begin{corollary}[Homomorphisms preserve the ideal \src{06}]\label{odg:def:cor:idealhom}
Let $\Bbbk,\Bbbk'$ be fields of characteristic zero containing $\sqrt2$. Every
unital ring homomorphism
$\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)\to\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk',\Gamma')$ maps
$\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$ into $\Pi_{\Bbbk'}(\Gamma')$, and the same holds between
two rings $\mathcal R_{\Z[i]}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)\to\mathcal R_{\Z[i]}(\Bbbk',\Gamma')$.
No injectivity or size condition on the target is needed.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For $x$ in the source ideal, $x^2=2(x/\sqrt2)^2$ with $x/\sqrt2$ in the source
ring. A unital homomorphism fixes the integer numeral $2$, so its images
satisfy the same equation. Apply \cref{odg:def:thm:ideal} in the target.
The homomorphism need not fix the chosen square root, the coefficient field
or any monomial: the formula contains none of those parameters.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Other radicands \src{06}]\label{odg:def:rem:delta}
If a nonzero $\delta\in\mathfrak o$ has a square root in $\Bbbk$ but not in
$\Frac(\mathfrak o)$, the same proof shows that $\exists y\,(x^2=\delta y^2)$
defines $\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$ in $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$; the
formula is parameter-free when $\delta$ is an integer numeral. The proof uses
no ordering of $\Bbbk$, no divisibility of $\Gamma$ and no real closedness.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Pell rigidity and divisible Pell coordinates}

\begin{corollary}[Pell rigidity in intermediate rings \src{06, 07}]\label{odg:def:cor:pell}
Let $A$ be an intermediate ring for $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$. If $u,v\in A$
satisfy $u^2-2v^2=1$, then $u,v\in\mathfrak o$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Extend the coefficients to $\Bbbk(\sqrt2)$; this does not change supports. There
$(u+\sqrt2\,v)(u-\sqrt2\,v)=1$, so both factors are constants by
\cref{odg:def:lem:units}, and adding and subtracting them shows that $u,v$
are constants. Their coefficients lie in $\Bbbk$, so $u,v\in A\cap\Bbbk=\mathfrak o$.
Equivalently, apply \cref{odg:dec:thm:etale} to $E=\Q(\sqrt2)$ with basis
$1,\sqrt2$.
\end{proof}

Source 06 proves this in $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ with
$\sqrt2\in\Bbbk$, source 07 in intermediate rings by coefficient extension; the
argument is that of \cref{odg:cor:pell}. It does not estimate $u^2$ and $2v^2$
separately, so cancellation between their leading terms is harmless, and
$\sqrt2$ occurs in the proof only, not as a parameter. For $\mathfrak o=\Z[i]$
the conclusion is Gaussian constancy, not reality: $(u,v)=(i,i)$ solves
$u^2-2v^2=1$, and the Gaussian construction below uses only Gaussian
constancy \src{06}.

\begin{lemma}[Divisible Pell coordinates \src{06, 07}]\label{odg:def:lem:pelldiv}
Let $(X_k,Y_k)$ be the Pell sequence \eqref{odg:eq:pellsequence}. For every
ordinary $m\ge1$ there is $k$ with $1\le k\le m^2$ such that
$X_k\equiv1$ and $Y_k\equiv0\pmod m$; then $Y_k>0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $M=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}3&4\\2&3\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$.
Its inverse $\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}3&-4\\-2&3\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$ has
integer entries, so $M$ permutes $(\Z/m\Z)^2$. Among the $m^2+1$ points
$M^j(1,0)$ for $0\le j\le m^2$, two coincide, say at $j<\ell$. Applying
$M^{-j}$ gives $M^{\ell-j}(1,0)=(1,0)$, with
$1\le k=\ell-j\le m^2$ \src{07}. In the ordinary integer recurrence
$X_0=1$, $Y_0=0$, the first step gives $(3,2)$ and both coordinates remain
positive, so $Y_k>0$. This also covers $m=1$, whose residue set has one
point. Source 06 argues instead that $3+2T$ is a unit
of the ring $(\Z/m\Z)[T]/(T^2-2)$, with inverse $3-2T$. Division by the
monic polynomial $T^2-2$ gives a unique representative $a+bT$, so the ring
has $m^2$ elements; some positive power at most $m^2$ equals $1$.
Comparing the two coordinates gives the same congruences.
\end{proof}

No classification of Pell solutions, primitive-divisor theorem or
distribution result is used \src{06}. Pell equations have a long history in
Diophantine definability, from Davis's explicit work \cite{Davis} and Denef's
on polynomial rings \cite{Denef} to Miller and Shlapentokh's on rings of
functions \cite{MillerShlapentokh}. Sources 06 and 07 use only the elementary
fact that, after adjoining $\sqrt2$, every unit of the nonnegative-support
ring is constant; source 07 stresses that no theorem about arbitrary Hahn
supports is inferred from theorems about polynomial rings.

\begin{lemma}[Integers in principal ideals \src{07}]\label{odg:def:lem:intmultiple}
Let $\mathfrak o$ be a unital subring of a number field. For every nonzero
$a\in\mathfrak o$, the ideal $a\mathfrak o$ contains a nonzero ordinary
integer; for a Gaussian integer $a$ one may take $a\overline a$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Algebraicity supplies a nonzero integer polynomial vanishing at $a$ after
clearing the denominators of a rational relation. Choose one of least
degree, $A_na^n+\cdots+A_1a+A_0=0$. If $A_0=0$, cancellation of $a\ne0$
gives a nonzero relation of smaller degree. Thus $A_0\ne0$, and
$A_0=-a(A_1+A_2a+\cdots+A_na^{n-1})\in a\mathfrak o$.
The expression uses only integer coefficients and nonnegative powers of
$a$, which belong to the unital subring $\mathfrak o$; no algebraic-integrality
hypothesis is needed. Changing its sign if necessary gives a positive
ordinary integer in the same ideal.
\end{proof}

\subsection{An intersective polynomial}

A polynomial in $\Z[T]$ is \emph{intersective} if it has a root modulo every
positive integer. We use
\begin{equation}\label{odg:def:eq:Lambda}
 \Lambda(T)=(T^2-13)(T^2-17)(T^2-221)=T^6-251T^4+6851T^2-48841 .
\end{equation}
It is a classical example: L\^e and Spencer list it in \cite[Section 2,
Example 4]{LeSpencer} and attribute it to Borevich and Shafarevich
\cite[p.~3]{BorevichShafarevich}; Berend and Bilu study the general phenomenon
of polynomials with roots modulo every integer \cite{BerendBilu}. Sources 06
and 07 both reprove the properties they need, because the definitions below
use roots modulo \emph{all} integers; the proofs are printed once.

\begin{proposition}[The polynomial $\Lambda$ \src{06, 07}]\label{odg:def:prop:intersective}
\leavevmode
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=*]
\item For every ordinary $N\ge1$ there is $r\in\Z$ with
$\Lambda(r)\equiv0\pmod N$.
\item $\Lambda$ has no root in $\Q(i)$; hence none in $\Z$ or $\Z[i]$, and by
\cref{odg:def:lem:units} none in any intermediate ring for $\Z\subseteq\Bbbk$
or $\Z[i]\subseteq\Bbbk$.
\item For every nonzero $v\in\Z[i]$ there are $s\in\Z[i]$ and $t\in\Z$ with
$vs=\Lambda(t)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
(ii) A root in a field satisfies $T^2=d$ with $d\in\{13,17,221\}$. If
$(a+bi)^2=d$ with $a,b\in\Q$, then $2ab=0$; $a=0$ gives $d=-b^2\le0$, and
$b=0$ would make $d$ a rational square, which none of the three is.

(i) It suffices to find a root modulo each prime power $p^a$ dividing $N$
and to combine them by the Chinese remainder theorem; the chosen factor of
$\Lambda$ may depend on $p$. For $N=1$ every integer works. Let $p$ be odd. If $p\notin\{13,17\}$, then $13$
and $17$ are nonzero modulo $p$; if neither is a square, their product $221$
is, by multiplicativity of the Legendre symbol. For $p=13$ use
$17\equiv2^2$, and for $p=17$ use $13\equiv8^2$. In each case some factor
$T^2-d$ has a root $r\not\equiv0\pmod p$, and it lifts to every $p^a$: if
$r^2\equiv d\pmod{p^j}$, replacing $r$ by $r+hp^j$ changes $(r^2-d)/p^j$ by
$2rh$ modulo $p$, and $2r$ is invertible modulo $p$. For $p=2$ use $17$: the
root $1$ works modulo $2^a$ for $a\le3$, since $17\equiv1\pmod8$; if an odd
$r$ satisfies $r^2\equiv17\pmod{2^a}$ with $a\ge3$, then
$(r+2^{a-1})^2-r^2=2^ar+2^{2a-2}\equiv2^a\pmod{2^{a+1}}$, so exactly one of
$r$ and $r+2^{a-1}$ is a root modulo $2^{a+1}$. This step constructs a
root at each power of $2$; it need not preserve the previously chosen
residue modulo $2^a$, and no compatible sequence of such choices is needed.
For each prime-power divisor of $N$, choose one of the roots just
constructed. The Chinese remainder theorem gives an integer congruent to
all of them; its $\Lambda$-value is divisible by every such prime power,
hence by their product $N$.

(iii) Put $N=v\overline v\in\Z_{>0}$, choose $t$ by (i) with $\Lambda(t)=Nq$,
and take $s=q\overline v$.
\end{proof}

The equation $vs=\Lambda(t)$ is a positive-existential certificate of
$v\ne0$ only when $v$ is already known to be a Gaussian constant. It is not a
definition of the nonzero elements: for $v\in\Pi_{\C}$ it has no solution,
since applying $\ct$ would give a root of $\Lambda$ in $\Z[i]$ \src{06}.

\subsection{One order-free system defining \texorpdfstring{$\Z$}{Z} and \texorpdfstring{$\Z[i]$}{Z[i]}}

Let $\mathcal E(x;u,v,w,s,t)$ be the system
\begin{equation}\label{odg:def:eq:system}
 x(u^2-2v^2-1)=0,\qquad x(v-xw)=0,\qquad x\bigl(vs-\Lambda(t)\bigr)=0,
\end{equation}
and put $\Xi(x):\Leftrightarrow\exists u,v,w,s,t\ \mathcal E(x;u,v,w,s,t)$.

\begin{theorem}[{One system for $\Z$ and $\Z[i]$} \src{06, 07}]\label{odg:def:thm:constants}
Let $(\mathfrak o,\Bbbk)$ be $(\Z,\R)$ or $(\Z[i],\C)$, let $\Gamma$ be any
ordered abelian group, and let $A$ be an intermediate ring. Then $\Xi$ defines
$\mathfrak o$ in $A$. In particular $\Xi(\Oz)=\Z$ and $\Xi(\Og)=\Z[i]$: the same
parameter-free system of three equations, of total degrees $3$, $3$ and $7$,
with five witnesses, defines both.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $\mathcal E(x;u,v,w,s,t)$ holds with $x\ne0$. Cancelling $x$ in the
domain $A$ gives
\[
 u^2-2v^2=1,\qquad v=xw,\qquad vs=\Lambda(t).
\]
By \cref{odg:def:cor:pell}, $u,v\in\mathfrak o$. If $v=0$, then $\Lambda(t)=0$
with $t\in A$, which \cref{odg:def:prop:intersective}(ii) excludes. So $v$ is a
nonzero constant, and $xw=v$ forces $x$ and $w$ to be constants
(\cref{odg:def:lem:units}); hence $x\in A\cap\Bbbk=\mathfrak o$. If $x=0$ there
is nothing to prove.

Conversely let $x\in\mathfrak o$. For $x=0$ take all witnesses zero. For
$x\ne0$ let $m=|x|$, or $m=x\overline x$ in the Gaussian case, so that
$m\in x\mathfrak o$. By \cref{odg:def:lem:pelldiv} choose a Pell pair
$(u,v)=(X_k,Y_k)$ with $v>0$ and $m\mid v$; then $w=v/x\in\mathfrak o$. By
intersectivity choose $t\in\Z$ with $v\mid\Lambda(t)$ and put
$s=\Lambda(t)/v\in\Z$. These witnesses all lie in $\mathfrak o\subseteq A$: $u,v,s,t$ are
ordinary integers, while $w$ may be a nonreal Gaussian integer. They
satisfy the system.
\end{proof}

The proof uses the intermediate-ring condition, not a retraction
$A\to\mathfrak o$. It therefore applies, for example, to
$\Z[\omega+\tfrac12]$ above even though its constant coefficient need not be
an integer. In fact the same proof works with any characteristic-zero
coefficient field containing the indicated copy of $\Z$ or $\Z[i]$:
coefficient extension proves Pell rigidity, root exclusion uses
$A\cap\Bbbk=\mathfrak o$, and the witnesses are already in $\mathfrak o$.
The displayed theorem retains the two source pairs; the broader
number-field version is stated in \cref{odg:def:thm:numberfield}.

Source 07 states the theorem in this form, for both pairs and all
intermediate rings. Source 06 proves the identical system (with witnesses
$h,r$ for $s,t$) in $\mathcal R_{\Z[i]}(\C,\Gamma)$ and $\Og$, noting that the
root exclusion takes place after applying $\ct$, so that algebraic closedness
of the coefficient field is not needed. The factor $x$ in each equation admits
$x=0$ without a disjunction. For $x=1+i$ a witness is $u=3$, $v=2$, $w=1-i$,
$t=1$, $s=-21120$, because $\Lambda(1)=-42240$; for $x=\omega+i$ there is none,
since a nonzero Gaussian constant is not divisible by $\omega+i$ in $\BC$
\src{06, 07}. The formula contains no occurrence of $i$ and no distinguished
choice of it.

\paragraph{Why the certificate is needed.} The relation $v=xw$ alone would
allow $v=0$ and hence every $x$. Writing $v\ne0$ would give an existential
formula with an inequation, not a Diophantine one, and replacing it by
$vz=1$ would force $v$ to be a unit, which most Pell coordinates are not. The
equation $vs=\Lambda(t)$ excludes $v=0$ because $\Lambda$ has no root in $A$,
and accepts every nonzero ordinary $v$ because $\Lambda$ is intersective
\src{06, 07}. Over a ring containing $i$ the three equations cannot be
combined into one sum of squares ($1^2+i^2=0$), and no single-polynomial
Gaussian definition is claimed. In the proof one may take the Pell index at
most $m^2$ and $0\le t<v$; these are existence bounds, not claims of optimal
or practical complexity \src{07}. The ring restriction is essential: in a
field containing a root $r$ of $\Lambda$, $\Xi(x)$ holds for every $x$, with
$u=1$, $v=w=s=0$ and $t=r$; so $\Xi$ defines nothing in $\No$ or $\No[i]$
\src{07}.

\subsection{Single real polynomials and the natural numbers}

In the real case a conjunction of equations becomes one equation as a sum of
squares. The report already prints the quartic guards of sources 01 and 05
(\cref{odg:thm:guard,odg:thm:standarddef}), and source 07's quartic, which is
source 05's up to the substitution described in
\cref{odg:def:rem:quarticvariant}. Source 06 proposed a quintic as its
principal real formula; it is recorded here with the comparison.

\begin{remark}[Source 06's quintic, and a comparison \src{06}]\label{odg:def:rem:quintic}
Let $\Bbbk$ be an ordered field containing $\sqrt2$. In
$\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$, and in particular in $\Oz$,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:def:eq:quintic}
 x\in\Z\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \exists u,v,w,s_1,\ldots,s_4\quad
 x\Bigl[(u^2-2v^2-1)^2+(v-xw)^2+\Bigl(u-2-\sum_{j=1}^4s_j^2\Bigr)^2\Bigr]=0 .
\end{equation}
Indeed, for $x\ne0$ the three squares vanish; Pell rigidity makes $u,v$
integers, $u=2+\sum s_j^2\ge2$ excludes $v=0$, and then $xw=v\ne0$ forces
$x\in\Z$. Conversely, for $0\ne x\in\Z$ take a Pell pair with $|x|\mid Y_k$
(\cref{odg:def:lem:pelldiv}), $w=Y_k/x$, and four squares for $X_k-2\ge1$.
The polynomial has total degree exactly five (the monomial $xu^4$) and seven
witnesses. Source 06 tabulates complete ordinary witnesses for
$x\in\{1,2,\pm3,5,7\}$, for instance $u=17$, $v=12$, $w=4$,
$(s_1,\ldots,s_4)=(3,2,1,1)$ for $x=3$; for $x=\omega$ or $\omega+1$ there
are none in $\Oz$. It is dominated by \cref{odg:thm:guard} (degree four, five
witnesses, uniform in $n$) and by \cref{odg:thm:standarddef} and source 07's
\eqref{odg:def:eq:F4} (degree four, six witnesses); source 06's question on
the least degree is thereby partly answered (\cref{odg:def:sub:settled}). Its
mechanism, divisibility $v=xw$ of a nonzero Pell coordinate, is the one that
survives in the Gaussian system \cref{odg:def:thm:constants}. Source 06
restricts the quintic to the real case, correctly: over $\Og$ it accepts
$x=\omega$ \mergetag, with $u=1$, $v=0$, $w=1$, $s_1=i$,
$s_2=e^{-i\pi/4}\omega^{1/2}$, $s_3=s_4=0$. Then $u^2-2v^2-1=0$,
$v-xw=-\omega$ and $u-2-s_1^2-s_2^2=-1+1+i\omega=i\omega$, and
$(-\omega)^2+(i\omega)^2=0$. Source 07 records the analogous collapse of its
quartic (\cref{odg:def:rem:quarticvariant}).
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[The natural numbers \src{06, 07}]\label{odg:def:cor:naturals}
Let $\Std$ be any of the Diophantine definitions of $\Z$ in $\Oz$
(\cref{odg:thm:standarddef}, \cref{odg:def:thm:constants},
\eqref{odg:def:eq:quintic}). Then
$x\in\N\Leftrightarrow\Std(x)\wedge\exists r_1,\ldots,r_4\
x=r_1^2+r_2^2+r_3^2+r_4^2$, where $0\in\N$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The forward implication is Lagrange's theorem. Conversely $\Std(x)$ makes $x$
an ordinary integer, and a sum of squares in the ordered ring $\Oz$ is
nonnegative (alternatively, apply $\ct$).
\end{proof}

Source 07 restricts the $r_j$ by its constant-definition formula as well; in
$\Oz$ this is harmless and unnecessary. \Cref{odg:rem:ringinduction} gives a
variant in the language $\{0,1,+,\cdot\}$ of the nonnegative semiring, with
every witness in $\Oz_{\ge0}$, that defines $\N$ inside $\Oz_{\ge0}$.

\section{The constant term: detector, ideal test and graph}\label{odg:def:sec:ct}

\subsection{A root detector for augmentations}

The following algebraic statement, due to source 07, isolates why a single
polynomial can detect a nonzero constant term.

\begin{theorem}[Augmentation root detector \src{07}]\label{odg:def:thm:augdetector}
Let $\mathcal H$ be a commutative unital $\Bbbk$-algebra with a
$\Bbbk$-algebra homomorphism $\varepsilon:\mathcal H\to\Bbbk$ that is the
identity on $\Bbbk$. Let $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$ be a unital subring and
$A=\mathfrak o+\ker\varepsilon$. Let $f\in\mathfrak o[T]$ satisfy:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)},leftmargin=*]
\item $f$ has no root in $\mathfrak o$;
\item $f$ has a root $r\in\Bbbk$;
\item for every nonzero $c\in\mathfrak o$ there is $b\in\mathfrak o$ with
$f(b)\in c\mathfrak o$.
\end{enumerate}
Then for every $a\in A$,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:def:eq:augdetector}
 \varepsilon(a)\ne0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\exists s,t\in A\quad as=f(t).
\end{equation}
No hypothesis that $\mathcal H$ is a domain, reduced, complete or Henselian is
needed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Identify $\Bbbk$ with its image in $\mathcal H$; the retraction makes this
embedding injective. Then $A=\varepsilon^{-1}(\mathfrak o)$, since every
$z\in\mathcal H$ decomposes as $\varepsilon(z)+(z-\varepsilon(z))$, with the
second summand in the kernel.
If $as=f(t)$ and $\varepsilon(a)=0$, applying $\varepsilon$ gives
$f(\varepsilon(t))=0$ with $\varepsilon(t)\in\mathfrak o$, contrary to (a).
Conversely let $c=\varepsilon(a)\ne0$, choose $b$ by (c), and factor
$f(T)=(T-r)Q(T)$ in $\Bbbk[T]$. Put
\begin{equation}\label{odg:def:eq:witness}
 \alpha=\frac{b-r}{c},\qquad t=r+\alpha a,\qquad s=\alpha Q(t).
\end{equation}
Since $t-r=\alpha a$, we get $as=(t-r)Q(t)=f(t)$. Moreover
$\varepsilon(t)=r+\alpha c=b\in\mathfrak o$ and
$c\,\varepsilon(s)=\varepsilon(as)=f(b)\in c\mathfrak o$, so
$\varepsilon(s)\in\mathfrak o$; thus $s,t\in\mathfrak o+\ker\varepsilon=A$. Only
the nonzero scalar $c$ is inverted, in the field $\Bbbk$.
\end{proof}

The augmentation need not be part of the ring language: the conclusion is a
ring-language formula that detects its kernel \src{07}, with the coefficients
of $f$ as parameters. In the applications below $f\in\Z[T]$, so these are
integer numerals and the formula is parameter-free.

For a concrete example with zero divisors, take $\mathcal H=\R\times\R$,
embed $\R$ diagonally, and let $\varepsilon(u,v)=u$. Then
$A=\Z\times\R$. The zero divisor $a=(1,0)$ has nonzero augmentation and
admits the certificate
\[
 t=(0,\sqrt{13}),\qquad s=(-48841,0),\qquad
 as=(-48841,0)=\Lambda(t).
\]
Thus invertibility of $a$ is not required; the cancellation in the proof
takes place in $\Bbbk$, after applying $\varepsilon$.

\begin{theorem}[Detecting a nonzero constant term \src{07}]\label{odg:def:thm:detector}
Let $(\mathfrak o,\Bbbk)$ be $(\Z,\R)$ or $(\Z[i],\C)$ and
$A=\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$. For every $a\in A$,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:def:eq:detector}
 \Theta(a):\Longleftrightarrow\exists s,t\ \bigl(as=\Lambda(t)\bigr)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\ct(a)\ne0 .
\end{equation}
In particular this holds in $\Oz$ and $\Og$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{odg:def:thm:augdetector} with $\mathcal H=\mathcal A_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$,
$\varepsilon=\ct$ and $f=\Lambda$. Hypothesis (a) is
\cref{odg:def:prop:intersective}(ii); (b) holds with $r=\sqrt{13}$. For (c),
given $c\ne0$ in $\mathfrak o$ choose a positive integer $m\in c\mathfrak o$
($|c|$ or $c\overline c$) and $b\in\Z$ with $\Lambda(b)\in m\Z\subseteq
c\mathfrak o$.
\end{proof}

With $r=\sqrt{13}$ the witnesses \eqref{odg:def:eq:witness} are explicit:
$t=r+\alpha a$ and $s=\alpha(2r+\alpha a)(t^2-17)(t^2-221)$, with
$\ct(t)=b$ and $\ct(s)=\Lambda(b)/c$. Neither $r$ nor $\alpha$ need lie in $A$;
the witnesses do, because their constant coefficients are right \src{07}.

\begin{proposition}[Support control of the witnesses \src{07}]\label{odg:def:prop:support}
Let $S=\supp(a)$ and $S^\ast=S\cup\{0\}$. The witnesses above satisfy
$\supp(t)\subseteq S^\ast$ and $\supp(s)\subseteq\bigcup_{j=0}^5jS^\ast$,
where $jS^\ast$ is the $j$-fold sumset and $0S^\ast=\{0\}$. If $a$ is
nonconstant, $\ddeg t=\ddeg a$ and $\ddeg s=5\ddeg a$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The witness $t$ is affine in $a$ and $s$ is a polynomial of degree five in
$a$ with constant coefficients, so the support inclusions follow from finite
convolution. Finite sumsets of reverse-well-ordered supports are again
reverse well ordered, and each fixed exponent has only finitely many
contributions in each such convolution. All exponents remain in $\Gamma$;
the scalars $r,\alpha$ change coefficients only.
Since $b\in\mathfrak o$ and $r\notin\mathfrak o$, $\alpha\ne0$; the
leading coefficients of the two polynomials in $a$ are $\alpha$ and
$\alpha^6$, and \cref{odg:def:lem:units} gives the degrees.
\end{proof}

The construction divides no series by $a$, expands no $(1+h)^{-1}$ and takes
no limit; it applies unchanged to supports accumulating towards zero and to
exponent groups of any rank \src{07}.

The predicate $\Theta$ detects a nonzero constant term: it rejects the
nonzero element $\omega$ and accepts the nonunit $1+\omega$.

\begin{example}[Certificates \src{07}]\label{odg:def:ex:certificates}
For $a=\omega$ (or $a=\sum_{n\ge1}\omega^{1/n}$) there is no certificate: a
solution of $as=\Lambda(t)$ would give $\Lambda(\ct(t))=0$ in $\Z$. For
$a=1+\omega$ take $c=1$, $b=0$, so $\alpha=-\sqrt{13}$ and
\[
 t=-\sqrt{13}\,\omega,\qquad s=13(\omega-1)(13\omega^2-17)(13\omega^2-221),
 \qquad (1+\omega)s=\Lambda(t);
\]
the constant coefficients are $\ct(t)=0$ and $\ct(s)=-48841=\Lambda(0)$, and
the irrational coefficient $-\sqrt{13}$ of $t$ is allowed. For $a=2+\omega$ one
may take $b=1$, since $\Lambda(1)=-42240$ is even: then
$\alpha=(1-\sqrt{13})/2$, $t=1+\alpha\omega$, and $\ct(s)=-21120$.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[A root in the coefficient field is needed \src{07}]\label{odg:def:rem:rootneeded}
Let $A=\Z+\omega\Q[\omega]$, an intermediate ring for $\Z\subseteq\Q$ with
$\Gamma=\Z$. The proof of \cref{odg:def:thm:constants} still shows that $\Xi$
defines $\Z$ in $A$ (it needs only Pell rigidity and the absence of integer
roots of $\Lambda$). The detector fails: evaluation at $\omega=-1$ is a
surjective homomorphism $A\to\Q$ (the element $-q\omega$ maps to $q$) whose
kernel is $(1+\omega)A$, because division by $1+\omega$ in $\Q[\omega]$ leaves
a quotient with the same constant coefficient. So $A/(1+\omega)A\cong\Q$ has no
root of $\Lambda$, and $(1+\omega)s=\Lambda(t)$ has no solution although
$\ct(1+\omega)=1$. Here $\Q$ contains no root of $\Lambda$, so hypothesis (b)
of \cref{odg:def:thm:augdetector} fails. The detector is not asserted for
arbitrary intermediate rings, which need not contain the polynomial
witnesses. For example, take $A=\Z[\omega]\subseteq\R[\omega]$.
Here $\ct(A)\subseteq\Z$ and the ambient coefficient field contains
$\sqrt{13}$, but a certificate $(1+\omega)s=\Lambda(t)$ in $A$ would give
$\Lambda(t(-1))=0$ in $\Z$ upon evaluation at $\omega=-1$. Thus the full
pullback condition $A=\mathfrak o+\ker\varepsilon$ also matters.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The largest ideal without a root}

\begin{theorem}[One polynomial tests every ideal \src{07}]\label{odg:def:thm:idealtest}
Let $(\mathfrak o,\Bbbk)$ be $(\Z,\R)$ or $(\Z[i],\C)$,
$A=\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ and $I=\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$. For every
ideal $J$ of $A$,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:def:eq:idealtest}
 \bigl(\exists t\in A\quad\Lambda(t)\in J\bigr)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 J\not\subseteq I .
\end{equation}
Thus $\Lambda$ has no root in $A/J$ exactly when $J\subseteq I$, and $I$ is the
unique largest ideal of $A$ whose quotient has no root of $\Lambda$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $J\subseteq I$ and $\Lambda(t)\in J$, applying $\ct$ gives
$\Lambda(\ct(t))=0$ in $\mathfrak o$, which is impossible. If $J\not\subseteq I$,
choose $a\in J$ with $\ct(a)\ne0$; \cref{odg:def:thm:detector} gives
$as=\Lambda(t)$, which lies in $J$. Since $A/I\cong\mathfrak o$ has no root,
$I$ has the property and contains every ideal that has it.
\end{proof}

For a class ideal of a proper-class ring the displayed equivalence is the
primary formulation; no set of quotient classes is formed. The case $J=A$ is
allowed: the zero ring has roots of every polynomial \src{07}.

\begin{corollary}[A universal definition of the ideal \src{07}]\label{odg:def:cor:universal}
In the rings of \cref{odg:def:thm:idealtest},
$I=\{a:\forall s,t\ (as\ne\Lambda(t))\}$, and $\ct(a)=\ct(b)$ if and only if
$\forall s,t\ ((a-b)s\ne\Lambda(t))$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Negate \cref{odg:def:thm:detector}, and use additivity of $\ct$.
\end{proof}

As a definition of $I$ this universal formula is superseded by the
existential one of \cref{odg:def:thm:ideal}; it is kept as the negation of the
detector. Together, \cref{odg:def:thm:ideal,odg:def:thm:detector} make both $I$
and its complement Diophantine. Source 07 also notes the external description
$I=\bigcap_{n\ge1}nA$ for $\mathfrak o=\Z,\Z[i]$, of which
\eqref{odg:eq:divkernel} is the case of $\Oz$. Indeed, writing $x=d+u$ with
$d\in\mathfrak o$ and $u\in I$, we have $x\in nA$ exactly when
$d\in n\mathfrak o$: necessity follows by applying $\ct$, and sufficiency
uses $u/n\in I$. An ordinary integer divisible by every positive integer is
zero; applying this to both coordinates gives the same conclusion in
$\Z[i]$. If instead $\mathfrak o$ contains $\Q$, then $nA=A$ for every
$n\ge1$, so this intersection does not detect $I$.

\subsection{The graph of the constant term}

\begin{theorem}[The graph of $\ct$ is Diophantine \src{06}]\label{odg:def:thm:ctgraph}
Let $(\mathfrak o,\Bbbk)$ be $(\Z,\R)$ or $(\Z[i],\C)$ and
$A=\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$. The formula
\begin{equation}\label{odg:def:eq:ctgraph}
 \mathsf{CT}(x,n):\Longleftrightarrow\Xi(n)\ \wedge\ \exists y\
 \bigl((x-n)^2=2y^2\bigr)
\end{equation}
defines the graph of $\ct:A\to\mathfrak o$; for each $x$ there is exactly one
such $n$. It is Diophantine, with six witnesses. In $\Oz$, $\Xi$ may be
replaced by any Diophantine definition of $\Z$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The first clause says $n\in\mathfrak o$ (\cref{odg:def:thm:constants}); by
\cref{odg:def:thm:ideal} the second says $x-n\in I$. In the decomposition
$A=\mathfrak o\oplus I$ the two conditions together say exactly $n=\ct(x)$.
Existence is explicit: take $n=\ct(x)$, choose the five witnesses for
$\Xi(n)$, and put $y=(x-n)/\sqrt2\in I$. Uniqueness concerns the output
$n$, not the six existential witnesses: two possible outputs differ by an
element of $\mathfrak o\cap I=\{0\}$.
\end{proof}

Source 06 proves the theorem with its own predicates, the quintic in the real
case and the Gaussian system in the Gaussian case; its single real polynomial
$P(n,\ldots)^2+((x-n)^2-2y^2)^2$, with $P$ the quintic of
\eqref{odg:def:eq:quintic}, has total degree ten and eight witnesses. Using the
guard of \cref{odg:thm:guard} instead gives degree four:

\begin{corollary}[A quartic for the graph of $\ct$ \mergetag]\label{odg:def:cor:ctquartic}
Let $\Psi_1(X,U,V,W_1,W_2,W_3)$ be the guard polynomial
\eqref{odg:eq:guardpoly} with $n=1$. For $x,m\in\Oz$,
\[
 m=\ct(x)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists u,v,w_1,w_2,w_3,y\in\Oz\quad
 \Psi_1(m,u,v,w_1,w_2,w_3)+\bigl((x-m)^2-2y^2\bigr)^2=0 .
\]
The polynomial has integer coefficients, total degree four and six
witnesses. With the six-witness quartics \eqref{odg:eq:standardquartic} or
\eqref{odg:def:eq:F4} in place of $\Psi_1$ the same holds with seven
witnesses.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The polynomial is a sum of three squares of integer polynomials, since
$\Psi_1$ is itself a sum of two. In the ordered ring $\Oz$ it vanishes exactly
when the three squared polynomials vanish, that is, when
$\Psi_1(m,u,v,w_1,w_2,w_3)=0$ and $(x-m)^2=2y^2$. By \cref{odg:thm:guard},
witnesses for the first equation exist exactly when $m\in\Z$, and by
\cref{odg:def:thm:ideal}, a witness $y$ for the second exists exactly when
$x-m\in\Pi$. Both together say $m=\ct(x)$. Every term has degree at most
four, and the coefficient of $x^4$ is $1$, since $\Psi_1$ does not involve
$x$; hence the total degree is exactly four. The witnesses are
$u,v,w_1,w_2,w_3,y$. The variant follows in
the same way from \cref{odg:thm:standarddef} or
\cref{odg:def:rem:quarticvariant}.
\end{proof}

By \cref{odg:rem:abstractguard} and \cref{odg:def:cor:pell} the corollary
holds verbatim in $\mathcal R_\Z(\R,\Gamma)$ for every $\Gamma$. No single
polynomial is claimed in the Gaussian case.

\begin{corollary}[The canonical splitting is definable \src{06}]\label{odg:def:cor:splitting}
For the rings of \cref{odg:def:thm:ctgraph}, the exact sequence
$0\to I\to A\xrightarrow{\ct}\mathfrak o\to0$ has a parameter-free definable
retraction and a parameter-free definable section; its kernel and the graph of
the retraction are Diophantine.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The predicate $\Xi$ defines the embedded copy of $\mathfrak o$.
On that domain the section is the inclusion, with graph $x=n$.
The retraction has graph $\mathsf{CT}$, and its kernel is defined by
$\mathsf{Inf}$. These formulas have no parameters.
\end{proof}

The direct-sum decomposition is additive. In the coordinates
$x=n+u\leftrightarrow(n,u)$ its multiplication is
\[
 (n,u)(m,v)=(nm,nv+mu+uv),
\]
so the splitting does not assert a direct product of rings.
The existence of the sequence is elementary normal-form algebra; the content
is the definability of the actual splitting, not merely an interpretation of
$A/I$ \src{06}.

\begin{remark}[Source 07's prenex definitions; a second route \src{07}]\label{odg:def:rem:sigma2}
Source 07 defines the graph by $\mathsf{CT}^{\forall}(a,c):\Leftrightarrow
\Xi(c)\wedge\forall s,t\,((a-c)s\ne\Lambda(t))$, with the two prenex forms
\[
 \exists\mathbf u\ \forall s,t\ \bigl[\mathcal E(c;\mathbf u)\wedge
 (a-c)s\ne\Lambda(t)\bigr],\qquad
 \forall s,t\ \exists\mathbf u\ \bigl[\mathcal E(c;\mathbf u)\wedge
 (a-c)s\ne\Lambda(t)\bigr],
\]
where $\mathbf u$ is a quintuple of witnesses. In the first form the witnesses
for $\Xi(c)$ do not occur in the universal inequality; in the second, a
single pair, for example $(s,t)=(0,0)$, already produces witnesses for
$\Xi(c)$, and conversely a
fixed witness works for all $(s,t)$. Both are correct by
\cref{odg:def:thm:constants,odg:def:cor:universal}. Source 07 claimed neither
minimality nor the impossibility of an existential definition; source 06's
\cref{odg:def:thm:ctgraph} supplies one.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Homomorphisms preserve the constant term}

\begin{theorem}[All homomorphisms respect the constant term \src{06}]\label{odg:def:thm:homct}
Let $\Bbbk,\Bbbk'$ be fields of characteristic zero containing $\sqrt2$, and
$\Gamma,\Gamma'$ ordered abelian groups. Every unital ring homomorphism
$f:\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)\to\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk',\Gamma')$ satisfies
$\ct(f(x))=\ct(x)$. If $i\in\Bbbk\cap\Bbbk'$ and
$f:\mathcal R_{\Z[i]}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)\to\mathcal R_{\Z[i]}(\Bbbk',\Gamma')$ is a
unital ring homomorphism, then $\ct(f(x))=\sigma(\ct(x))$, where $\sigma$ is
the identity or complex conjugation on $\Z[i]$, according as $f(i)=i$ or
$f(i)=-i$. No size restriction on the target is imposed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $x=n+a$ with $n\in\mathfrak o$ and $a$ in the ideal. By
\cref{odg:def:cor:idealhom}, $f(a)$ lies in the target ideal. A unital
homomorphism fixes ordinary integers. In the Gaussian case $f(i)^2=-1$, and by
\cref{odg:def:lem:units} a root of $T^2+1$ in the target ring is a constant,
hence $\pm i$. Taking constant terms of $f(x)=f(n)+f(a)$ gives the claim.
\end{proof}

Source 06 states the theorem for the rings of its arithmetic sections; the
proof uses only that both coefficient fields contain $\sqrt2$. It generalizes
\cref{odg:prop:canonicalct} (endomorphisms of $\Oz$) and source 07's statement
that ring automorphisms of $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ preserve
$\mathfrak o$, $I$ and $\ct$ up to $\sigma$, which source 07 derives from the
parameter-free definitions. It does not say that homomorphisms kill the ideal:
the identity does not. Preservation of the ideal, annihilation of the ideal and
pointwise fixation of the real coefficients are three different assertions
\src{06}. For general homomorphisms the proof uses preservation of
equations and their existential witnesses; parameter-free first-order
definability alone would only ensure invariance under automorphisms.
For set-sized $\Gamma'$ it is consistent with
\cref{odg:fact:settarget}: every unital homomorphism
$\Oz\to\mathcal R_\Z(\R,\Gamma')$ is then $x\mapsto\ct(x)1$. Source 07's
version concerns ring automorphisms of the coefficient-restricted ring; it
does not classify field automorphisms of $\No$ or $\No[i]$, nor claim that an
arbitrary field automorphism preserves $\Oz$ or $\Og$.

\subsection{Number-field coefficient rings}

The polynomial $\Lambda$ cannot serve every number field, since a field
containing $\sqrt{13}$ has a root of it. A tailored polynomial of the same
shape exists for each fixed number field \src{07}.

\begin{lemma}[A tailored intersective polynomial \src{07}]\label{odg:def:lem:tailored}
For every number field $K$ there are distinct primes $p,q$ such that
$\Lambda_K(T)=(T^2-p)(T^2-q)(T^2-pq)$ has no root in $K$ and has a root modulo
every positive integer.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A number field has only finitely many quadratic subfields: it lies in a finite
Galois extension of $\Q$, whose Galois group has finitely many subgroups. By
Dirichlet's theorem choose a prime $p\equiv1\pmod8$ with
$\Q(\sqrt p)\not\subseteq K$, and then a prime $q\equiv1\pmod{8p}$ with
$\Q(\sqrt q),\Q(\sqrt{pq})\not\subseteq K$; only finitely many $q$ are
excluded, since distinct positive squarefree radicands give distinct
quadratic fields. Both arithmetic progressions have residue $1$ coprime
to their modulus, so Dirichlet supplies infinitely many candidates; also
$q\equiv1\pmod p$ ensures $q\ne p$. Then none of $p,q,pq$ is a square in $K$. For an odd prime
$\ell\notin\{p,q\}$, one of $p,q,pq$ is a nonzero square modulo $\ell$, as in
\cref{odg:def:prop:intersective}. At $\ell=p$ use $q\equiv1\pmod p$. At
$\ell=q$, quadratic reciprocity and $p\equiv1\pmod4$ give
$(p/q)=(q/p)=(1/p)=1$. The nonzero roots lift to all odd prime powers, and at
$2$ the number $p\equiv1\pmod8$ has a root modulo every $2^a$ by the lifting
step of \cref{odg:def:prop:intersective}. The Chinese remainder theorem
finishes the proof. Dirichlet's theorem and reciprocity are classical inputs
\cite{Apostol}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Coefficient rings in number fields \src{07}]\label{odg:def:thm:numberfield}
Let $\mathfrak o$ be a unital subring of a number field $K$, let $\Bbbk\supseteq
K$ be a field of characteristic zero, and let $A$ be an intermediate ring for
$\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$. Let $\Xi_K$ be $\Xi$ with $\Lambda$ replaced by
$\Lambda_K$. Then $\Xi_K$ defines $\mathfrak o$ in $A$. If moreover
$A=\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ and $\Bbbk$ contains a root of
$\Lambda_K$, then $\ct(a)\ne0\Leftrightarrow\exists s,t\ (as=\Lambda_K(t))$,
and the ideal test, the universal definition of the ideal and both prenex
definitions of the graph of $\ct$ hold with $\Lambda_K$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The proof of \cref{odg:def:thm:constants} used Pell rigidity
(\cref{odg:def:cor:pell}), the absence of roots of the polynomial in
$\mathfrak o$ (\cref{odg:def:lem:tailored,odg:def:lem:units}), a nonzero integer
in every $x\mathfrak o$ (\cref{odg:def:lem:intmultiple}) and roots modulo all
integers. More explicitly, for $x\ne0$ the system forces $u,v\in\mathfrak o$
by Pell rigidity and $v\ne0$ by root exclusion. Then $xw=v$ forces $x$ to
have degree zero, hence to lie in $A\cap\Bbbk=\mathfrak o$.
Conversely, if $0\ne x\in\mathfrak o$, choose $m>0$ in $x\mathfrak o$ and
ordinary Pell coordinates $u,v$ with $0<v\in m\Z$. Put $w=v/x\in\mathfrak o$,
choose $t\in\Z$ with $\Lambda_K(t)\in v\Z$, and put
$s=\Lambda_K(t)/v\in\Z$. These are witnesses already in $\mathfrak o$;
$x=0$ is admitted by the factors $x$ in the defining equations. No
integrality or finite-generation hypothesis on $\mathfrak o$ is used.
For the detector, given $c\ne0$ in $\mathfrak o$ choose a positive integer $m\in c\mathfrak o$ and $b\in\Z$ with
$\Lambda_K(b)\in m\Z$, and apply \cref{odg:def:thm:augdetector} with any root of
$\Lambda_K$ in $\Bbbk$. The ideal test and the graph use only the detector and
the constant definition.
\end{proof}

The theorem covers subrings of a number field that are not finitely
generated. It does not give one polynomial uniform in all number fields, and
does not apply as stated to algebraic extensions of $\Q$ of infinite degree:
no polynomial of this kind is root-free in a field containing all algebraic
numbers \src{07}.

\begin{remark}[Existential definitions over number-field coefficients \mergetag]\label{odg:def:rem:numberfieldideal}
The formula $\exists y\,(x^2=2y^2)$ of \cref{odg:def:thm:ideal} fails when
$\sqrt2\in\Frac(\mathfrak o)$, for instance for $\mathfrak o=\Z[\sqrt2]$.
In the setting of \cref{odg:def:thm:numberfield} choose a prime $\delta$ with
$\Q(\sqrt\delta)\not\subseteq K$; it exists because $K$ has finitely many
quadratic subfields and distinct primes give distinct fields
$\Q(\sqrt\delta)$. Then $\delta$ is not a square in $K\supseteq\Frac(\mathfrak o)$,
since $z^2=\delta$ with $z\in K$ would give $\Q(\sqrt\delta)=\Q(z)\subseteq K$.
If $\sqrt\delta\in\Bbbk$, \cref{odg:def:rem:delta} shows that
$\exists y\,(x^2=\delta y^2)$ defines $\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$ in
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$, and
$\Xi_K(n)\wedge\exists y\,((x-n)^2=\delta y^2)$ defines the graph of $\ct$:
the first clause says $n\in\mathfrak o$, by \cref{odg:def:thm:numberfield}
applied to the intermediate ring $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$, and
the second says $x-n\in\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$. Both definitions are Diophantine;
they improve source 07's universal and prenex formulas whenever
$\sqrt\delta\in\Bbbk$, and need no root of $\Lambda_K$ in $\Bbbk$.

In particular, the detector hypothesis in \cref{odg:def:thm:numberfield}
already suffices for these existential conclusions. If
$\Lambda_K(r)=0$ in the field $\Bbbk$, one of its three factors vanishes,
so $r^2=\delta$ for some $\delta\in\{p,q,pq\}$. Each of these radicands is
nonsquare in $K$, by \cref{odg:def:lem:tailored}. The preceding argument
therefore applies with this $\delta$, including the composite choice $pq$:
primality was only a way to find a suitable radicand. Thus the ideal has a
one-witness Diophantine definition, its complement has the two-witness
detector, and the graph of $\ct$ has a six-witness Diophantine definition
under exactly the stated detector hypotheses. This is a further consequence
of combining sources 06 and 07. The integer $\delta$ is chosen once for
the fixed coefficient fields; the formulas contain its numeral, not a
parameter naming $r$, and no uniform choice across all number fields is
asserted.
\end{remark}

\section{Coefficient reconstruction and logical consequences}\label{odg:def:sec:reconstruction}

\subsection{The multiplier ring of the ideal}

Throughout this subsection $\Gamma\ne0$, $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$ is a
unital subring, $\mathcal R=\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$,
$I=\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$, $\mathcal A=\mathcal A_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$, and
$\mathcal F=\Frac(\mathcal R)$, viewed inside the Hahn field of all series with
reverse-well-ordered support in $\Gamma$ \src{06}. Put
$(I:I)_{\mathcal F}=\{r\in\mathcal F:rI\subseteq I\}$.

\begin{lemma}[\src{06}]\label{odg:def:lem:ainfrac}
$\mathcal A\subseteq\mathcal F$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose $g\in\Gamma^{>0}$. For $a\in\mathcal A$ both $\omega^g$ and $a\omega^g$
lie in $I\subseteq\mathcal R$, and $\omega^g\ne0$, so
$a=(a\omega^g)/\omega^g\in\mathcal F$. This also covers constants
$c\in\Bbbk$: although $c$ need not belong to $\mathcal R$, the fraction
$(c\omega^g)/\omega^g$ does. No common positive lower bound on the support
of $a$ is needed.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The multiplier identity \src{06}]\label{odg:def:thm:multiplier}
Inside $\mathcal F$,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:def:eq:multiplier}
 (I:I)_{\mathcal F}=\mathcal A,\qquad
 \Bbbk=\{0\}\cup\bigl((I:I)_{\mathcal F}\bigr)^\times .
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A nonnegative and a strictly positive exponent add to a strictly positive
exponent, so $\mathcal AI\subseteq I$. Conversely let
$r\in\mathcal F\setminus\mathcal A$ and choose a negative exponent $h$ in
$\supp(r)$; it need not be the least, and there need not be a least. Then
$\omega^{-h}\in I$, and the coefficient of $r\omega^{-h}$ at exponent zero is
the nonzero coefficient of $\omega^h$ in $r$, so $r\omega^{-h}\notin I$.
Multiplication by a single monomial translates support injectively; no
other coefficient contributes at zero and no cancellation can remove it.
This witnesses the failure to be a multiplier. The second identity follows
from $\mathcal A^\times=\Bbbk^\times$
(\cref{odg:def:lem:units}).
\end{proof}

An integral domain interprets its fraction field by pairs $(a,b)$ with
$b\ne0$, where $(a,b)\sim(c,d)$ means $ad=bc$. Addition and multiplication
are represented by $(ad+bc,bd)$ and $(ac,bd)$, and the original ring by
$a\mapsto(a,1)$. The denominator $bd$ is nonzero because the ring is a
domain. For the proper classes
``quotient'' means this definable presentation, with set-valued
representatives supplied by the surreal quotient, and no proper class is used
as an element of another \src{06}. With $\mathsf{Inf}$ from
\cref{odg:def:thm:ideal}, put
\begin{align*}
 \mathsf{Mult}(a,b)&:\Longleftrightarrow b\ne0\wedge\forall x\bigl(\mathsf{Inf}(x)
 \Rightarrow\exists y\,(\mathsf{Inf}(y)\wedge ax=by)\bigr),\\
 \mathsf{Coeff}(a,b)&:\Longleftrightarrow b\ne0\wedge\bigl(a=0\vee(a\ne0\wedge
 \mathsf{Mult}(a,b)\wedge\mathsf{Mult}(b,a))\bigr).
\end{align*}
The first says that the fraction $a/b$ multiplies $I$ into $I$, and is
invariant under change of representative. Indeed $ax=by$ is equivalent in
$\mathcal F$ to $(a/b)x=y$; all $x,y$ in this formula range over the original
ring, where $\mathsf{Inf}$ defines $I$. The second formula handles zero
separately and otherwise requires both $a/b$ and its inverse $b/a$ to be
multipliers, so \cref{odg:def:thm:multiplier} identifies it with
$a/b\in\Bbbk$. It does not assert that every nonzero multiplier is a
coefficient: $\omega^g\in\mathcal A$ is not a unit there for $g>0$.

\begin{corollary}[Internal reconstruction \src{06}]\label{odg:def:cor:internal}
Assume $\Gamma\ne0$ and the hypotheses of \cref{odg:def:thm:ideal} (for
instance in the real and Gaussian rings). Then $\mathcal A$ and the actual
embedded coefficient field
$\Bbbk$ are parameter-free first-order definable in the interpreted fraction
field, and the coefficient map $\mathcal A\to\Bbbk$ has the definable graph
$\{(a,c):a\in\mathcal A,\ c\in\Bbbk,\ a-c\in I\}$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Use $\mathsf{Mult}$ and $\mathsf{Coeff}$ in the interpreted fraction field.
The last condition means that $a-c$ is the image of an element of the
original ring satisfying $\mathsf{Inf}$. Every $a\in\mathcal A$ has a unique
decomposition $a=c+u$ with $c\in\Bbbk$ and $u\in I$, so the displayed
relation is exactly the coefficient map. This recovers the embedded field,
not just an abstract isomorphic copy.
\end{proof}

The same reconstruction works whenever $I$ has a parameter-free definition
in $\mathcal R$: the multiplier argument imposes no condition on
$\mathfrak o$ beyond being a unital subring of $\Bbbk$. In particular, for
$\Gamma\ne0$ the integer-radicand definitions in
\cref{odg:def:rem:numberfieldideal} recover the coefficient field and map
under the number-field detector hypotheses, even when $2$ is a square in
$\Frac(\mathfrak o)$. Substitute that ideal predicate for $\mathsf{Inf}$ in
the two formulas above; no square root is named in them.

The formulas do not mention monomials; monomials are used only to prove that
they define the right sets, and $\sqrt2$ is not a hidden parameter. The
multiplier formula contains a universal quantifier, so the reconstruction is
first order and is not claimed positive existential \src{06}. Two boundaries
are necessary. If $\Gamma=0$, then $I=0$ and every fraction multiplies it, so
the formula recovers $\Frac(\mathfrak o)$ rather than $\Bbbk$. And $\mathcal F$
need not be the whole Hahn field: for $\Gamma=\Z$ it is the rational function
field $\Bbbk(\omega)$; the multiplier identity holds in $\mathcal F$ without any
such equality \src{06}. For example, in $\Z+\omega\R[\omega]$ the ideal
$\omega\R[\omega]$ is defined by $\mathsf{Inf}$, the fraction field is
$\R(\omega)$, the multiplier ring is $\R[\omega]$ and its units are $\R^\times$;
the reconstruction phenomenon is not an artifact of proper-class size. With
$\Gamma=\R$ and $f=\sum_{n\ge1}\omega^{1/n}$, the ideal formula is witnessed by
$f/\sqrt2$, and the multiplier proof uses one negative exponent, not a least
one \src{06}.

\subsection{Reconstructing the surreal field}

\Cref{odg:thm:fractions} gives $\Frac(\Oz)=\No$, with a common monomial
denominator for every set of surreals; applied to the set of real and
imaginary parts it gives $\Frac(\Og)=\No[i]$. Source 06 proves the same
statement (its common-denominator lemma), printed once as
\cref{odg:thm:fractions}; it uses bounds for sets of exponents only, and asserts
no common denominator for a proper class of inputs.

\begin{theorem}[Reconstruction of the real structure \src{06}]\label{odg:def:thm:realrecovery}
From the pure ring $\Oz$ one can parameter-free interpret the field $\No$ and
define in it the canonical subfield $\R$, the order, the ring
$\Ofin$ of finite elements, the ideal $\mathfrak m$ of infinitesimals and the
graph of the standard part $\st:\Ofin\to\R$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Interpret the fraction field, which is $\No$. By \cref{odg:def:cor:internal}
the predicate $\mathsf{Coeff}$ defines its subfield $\R$. Since $\No$ is real
closed \cite{Conway,Gonshor}, $x>0\Leftrightarrow x\ne0\wedge\exists z\,(z^2=x)$
defines the order, with $x<y$ obtained by applying it to $y-x$.
Here $z$ ranges over the interpreted field, not just $\Oz$.
Put $\Ofin(x):\Leftrightarrow\exists r\in\R\,(r>0\wedge-r\le x\le r)$ and
$\mathfrak m(x):\Leftrightarrow\forall r\in\R\,(r>0\Rightarrow-r<x<r)$. A
nonzero surreal is bounded by a real number exactly when its leading
exponent is nonpositive. For a finite surreal, removing the coefficient at
zero leaves either zero or a remainder with a largest, strictly negative
exponent; that remainder is
therefore smaller in absolute value than every positive real. This proves
existence of standard part, including zero by taking coefficient zero.
It is unique because a nonzero real is not infinitesimal. So
$\mathsf{ST}(x,r):\Leftrightarrow\Ofin(x)\wedge r\in\R\wedge\mathfrak m(x-r)$
defines the standard part.
\end{proof}

The two constant-term operations must not be confused
(\cref{odg:rem:notvaluation}): $\ct$ reads the constant term of a number with
nonnegative exponents, $\st$ that of a finite number, with nonpositive
exponents. For example $\ct(\omega+7)=7$ and $\st(7+\omega^{-1})=7$, but
$\omega+7$ is not finite \src{06}.

\begin{corollary}[The value group \src{06}]\label{odg:def:cor:valuegroup}
The natural ordered value group of $\No$ is interpretable from $\Oz$ as
$\No^\times/\Ofin^\times$, ordered by $v(x)\ge v(y)\Leftrightarrow x/y\in\Ofin$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For $x\ne0$, membership in $\Ofin^\times$ means that both $x$ and $x^{-1}$
are finite. Thus $x\sim y$ iff $x/y\in\Ofin^\times$ is definable, and
multiplication descends to the quotient. If the leading exponents of $x,y$
are $g,h$, then $x/y$ has leading exponent $g-h$. It is finite exactly
when $g\le h$, and a finite unit exactly when $g=h$. Consequently the
displayed relation is a well-defined total order on the quotient,
compatible with multiplication; in additive valuation notation
$v(xy)=v(x)+v(y)$ it corresponds externally to $v(x)=-g$.
\end{proof}

This recovers the value group as an interpreted quotient, not a canonical
cross-section of monomials, and does not show that the $\omega$-map, the
simplicity order, birthdays or the surreal exponential are definable
\src{06}. The external representative $-g$ also explains the proper-class
convention: it is a surreal number representing a valuation class, not a
proper class used as an element. Its use to verify the quotient does not
put a definable monomial cross-section into the interpreted language.
The fraction-field construction can be phrased as a canonical
bi-interpretation between the pure ring $\Oz$ and the field $\No$ with a
predicate for $\Oz$; the essential content is the reconstruction of the
\emph{canonical} coefficient field.

\subsection{Automorphisms of \texorpdfstring{$\Oz$}{Oz}}

\begin{theorem}[Automorphisms fix the real coefficients \src{06}]\label{odg:def:thm:autreal}
Every unital ring automorphism $f$ of $\Oz$ extends uniquely to a field
automorphism $\tilde f(a/b)=f(a)/f(b)$ of $\No$. It preserves the order, $\R$,
$\Ofin$ and $\mathfrak m$, fixes every real number, and commutes with $\st$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The extension is well defined by cross multiplication and unique because
every element of $\No$ is a fraction. It preserves the parameter-free
definable sets of \cref{odg:def:thm:realrecovery}, so it restricts to an
order-preserving automorphism of $\R$ fixing $\Q$. If $\tilde f(r)\ne r$, a
rational strictly between $r$ and $\tilde f(r)$ contradicts order
preservation. The definable graph of $\st$, with its real coordinate fixed,
gives $\st\circ\tilde f=\st$ on $\Ofin$.
\end{proof}

The statement concerns automorphisms. A universal multiplier formula need not
be preserved by a nonsurjective embedding, so the argument does not apply
unchanged to endomorphisms; \cref{odg:def:thm:homct} applies to all unital
homomorphisms \src{06}.

\begin{example}[Coefficient rigidity is not total rigidity \src{06}]\label{odg:def:ex:dilation}
The map $\mathsf S_2(\sum_ga_g\omega^g)=\sum_ga_g\omega^{2g}$ is a field
automorphism of $\No$ with inverse $\mathsf S_{1/2}$, since doubling exponents
is an order automorphism of $(\No,+)$; it preserves supports and $\Oz$, fixes
real coefficients and sends $\omega$ to $\omega^2$. Nonnegative exponents
remain nonnegative,
and the coefficient at zero is unchanged, so the inverse also preserves
$\Oz$. This is an exponent substitution, not the squaring map $x\mapsto x^2$;
for example $\mathsf S_2(1+\omega)=1+\omega^2$. Thus $\Aut(\Oz)$ is not
trivial.
\end{example}

The dilations $\mathsf S_a$ are studied in the repository's surcomplex
automorphism report (\fn{docs/surcomplex/surcomplex-field-automorphisms}), and
the single-dilation report (\fn{docs/surcomplex/single-dilation-hahn-support})
defines the coefficient field and the constant coefficient from one dilation of
a field; \cref{odg:def:thm:realrecovery} starts instead from the pure ring $\Oz$
and its Diophantine ideal. \Cref{odg:def:thm:autreal,odg:def:ex:dilation} are
also relevant to the questions on automorphisms of $\Oz$ studied in the
sibling report \fn{docs/surreal/set-sized-quotients-of-omnific-integers}.

\subsection{The Gaussian ring does not determine the real axis}

The Gaussian system and the multiplier theorem recover $\No[i]=\Frac(\Og)$ and
its actual coefficient field $\C$, but not the real form $\No$ \src{06}.

\begin{proposition}[All coefficient automorphisms occur \src{06}]\label{odg:def:prop:coeffaut}
Every field automorphism $\sigma$ of $\C$ extends coefficientwise to a field
automorphism $\hat\sigma(\sum_gc_g\omega^g)=\sum_g\sigma(c_g)\omega^g$ of
$\No[i]$ preserving $\Og$. Every automorphism of $\Og$ extends to $\No[i]$ and
preserves $\C$ setwise, so restriction to $\C$ is surjective and
$\sigma\mapsto\hat\sigma$ is a composition-preserving section.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A coefficient automorphism preserves supports and the finite sums and products
defining Hahn convolution; its inverse is $\widehat{\sigma^{-1}}$.
The coefficients of each product are finite sums, so no continuity or
analytic regularity of $\sigma$ is needed. It fixes $\Z$
and sends $i$ to $\pm i$, so it preserves $\Z[i]$ and hence $\Og$. An
automorphism of $\Og$ extends to the fraction field and preserves the
first-order coefficient predicate of \cref{odg:def:cor:internal}.
Finally $\widehat{\sigma\circ\rho}=\hat\sigma\circ\hat\rho$ coefficientwise,
and restriction of $\hat\sigma$ to $\C$ is $\sigma$, giving the stated
section.
\end{proof}

For proper-class fields this is a statement about individual maps and their
compositions, not a construction of a set of all class automorphisms.

Let $\eta:\No\to\R$ send a surreal to the coefficient of $\omega^0$ in its
normal form. It is additive on $\No$ but not multiplicative
($\eta(\omega)=\eta(\omega^{-1})=0$, $\eta(1)=1$), and is used only as an
additive map on the exponent group. Put
$\chi(g)=\exp(i\pi\eta(g)/2)\in\C^\times$, the ordinary complex exponential of
an ordinary real multiple of $i$; then $\chi(g+h)=\chi(g)\chi(h)$, $\chi(0)=1$
and $\chi(1)=i$.

\begin{lemma}[A phase twist preserving $\Og$ \src{06}]\label{odg:def:lem:twist}
The map $\tau(\sum_gc_g\omega^g)=\sum_gc_g\chi(g)\omega^g$ is an automorphism of
$\No[i]$ that fixes $\C$ pointwise, preserves every support and $\Og$, and
satisfies $\tau(\omega)=i\omega$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
All values of $\chi$ are nonzero, so supports are unchanged; additivity is
clear. If $a+b=g$, the contribution of $(a,b)$ to the coefficient at $g$ is
multiplied by $\chi(a)\chi(b)=\chi(g)$, so $\tau$ commutes with convolution;
$\chi^{-1}$ gives the inverse. Constants sit at exponent zero and are fixed. A
Gaussian omnific integer keeps its positive support and its Gaussian integer
constant term, since $\chi(0)=1$; the inverse has the same property.
These operations are coefficientwise on set-supported normal forms,
without an infinite analytic summation. The exponential in $\chi$ is only
the ordinary complex exponential of a real scalar.
\end{proof}

The character-twist construction and its use to move the real axis while
fixing complex coefficients are already in the repository's surcomplex
automorphism report (\fn{docs/surcomplex/surcomplex-field-automorphisms}); source
06 credits it there. The additional fact needed here is that the twist
preserves $\Og$.

\begin{theorem}[No definable real axis or conjugation \src{06}]\label{odg:def:thm:norealaxis}
The subfield $\No\subseteq\No[i]$ is not first-order definable in the structure
$(\No[i],\Og)$, even with every complex number named by a constant, and
neither is conjugation. The subring $\Oz\subseteq\Og$ is not definable in the
pure ring $\Og$, even with parameters from $\Z[i]$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The automorphism $\tau$ preserves the field, the predicate $\Og$ and every
complex constant, but sends $\omega\in\No$ to $i\omega\notin\No$; definable
sets are invariant under automorphisms fixing the parameters. Conjugation
does not commute with $\tau$: $\tau(\overline\omega)=i\omega$ while
$\overline{\tau(\omega)}=-i\omega$. Finally the restriction of $\tau$ to $\Og$
fixes $\Z[i]$ and moves $\omega\in\Oz$ outside $\Oz$.
Naming every element of $\C$ does not affect the first obstruction:
$\tau$ fixes each of them, and every individual formula uses only finitely
many names. For the restriction to $\Og$, the parameters $\Z[i]$ already
lie in that ring; arbitrary complex constants need not.
\end{proof}

There is no conflict with the recovery of $\C$: the twist fixes $\C$
pointwise. The coefficient field and a distinguished real form are different
structure. Naming a suitable involution would suffice, since its fixed ring
can be taken, but that gives the real structure explicitly instead of
reconstructing it \src{06}.

\subsection{Logical consequences}

The equational transfer of \cref{odg:thm:transfer} is proved again by source 06
(its retraction-transfer lemma), by source 07 (positive-existential
conservation) and by source 13 (equation-only specialization and the
Hilbert's-tenth-problem transfer), for all rings
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ with parameters in $\mathfrak o$ and,
in the Gaussian case, with $\Z[i]$ in place of $\Z$. It is printed once, as
\cref{odg:thm:transfer,odg:cor:H10}; the proof is the same for every
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$. Transfer does not make solutions
constant: $y=x$ has many nonconstant solutions \src{07}.
Both the parameters and the free tuple must be in the coefficient subring
for this two-way transfer. Existential witnesses may range over the entire
ring: applying $\ct$ supplies coefficient witnesses.

\begin{theorem}[Diophantine subsets of $\Z^n$ \src{06}]\label{odg:def:thm:ce}
Let $\Bbbk$ be an ordered field containing $\sqrt2$. A subset $S\subseteq\Z^n$,
regarded as a subset of $\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)^n$ (in particular of
$\Oz^n$), is Diophantine there with integer coefficients if and only if it is
computably enumerable.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $S$ is defined by equations in $\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$, its trace on
$\Z^n$, which is $S$, is defined by the same equations over $\Z$, by
transfer; enumerating integer witnesses shows that it is computably
enumerable. Conversely, by the MRDP theorem \cite{Matiyasevich} (in its
integer form, by sign coding and sums of squares) $S$ is defined over $\Z$ by
an integer system $\Phi_S$. In $\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ impose
$\Std(x_1)\wedge\cdots\wedge\Std(x_n)\wedge\Phi_S(\mathbf x)$, with $\Std$ the
quintic \eqref{odg:def:eq:quintic}; once the free tuple is standard, transfer
makes the remaining condition equivalent to $\Phi_S$ over $\Z$.
The guards exclude nonstandard free tuples; no guards on the auxiliary
witnesses of $\Phi_S$ are needed, since $\ct$ sends any such witnesses to
integer ones. In the real ring the conjunction can be written as one sum
of squares.
\end{proof}

Here $S$ is required to consist of standard tuples. No computability
classification of arbitrary subsets of the ring is claimed, and arbitrary
surreal parameters are not finite inputs to an algorithm \src{06}.

\paragraph{Generality of the classification \mergetag.}
With the finite-system convention for Diophantine definitions, the same
equivalence holds in $\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ for every characteristic-zero
field $\Bbbk$, without an ordering or a square root of $2$. Apply
\cref{odg:def:thm:numberfield} with $\mathfrak o=\Z$ and $K=\Q$ to obtain
the integer guard $\Xi_\Q$; its first assertion needs no root of
$\Lambda_\Q$ in $\Bbbk$. Replace $\Std$ by this guard in the proof, and use
the same retraction in both directions. All formula coefficients are
integers. A single sum-of-squares equation is not claimed over an
unordered field. This is a consequence of combining the order-free
definition with source 06's transfer argument.

\begin{corollary}[Standard arithmetic is definable \src{06, 07}]\label{odg:def:cor:arithmetic}
The structures $(\Z,+,\cdot,0,1)$ and $(\N,+,\cdot,0,1)$ are parameter-free
definable in the pure ring $\Oz$, and every arithmetic sentence has an
effective translation obtained by relativizing its quantifiers to $\Std$ and
to \cref{odg:def:cor:naturals}. For a set-sized intermediate ring $A$ for
$\Z\subseteq\R$, true arithmetic therefore reduces many-one to $\Th(A)$; so
$\Th(A)$ is undecidable and, being complete, is not recursively axiomatizable.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The translation replaces $\exists x$ by $\exists x(\Std(x)\wedge\cdots)$ and
$\forall x$ by $\forall x(\Std(x)\Rightarrow\cdots)$; induction on formulas
proves it correct. For $A$ use $\Xi$ (\cref{odg:def:thm:constants}) in place
of $\Std$, and the same four-square definition of $\N$, which is valid in $A$
because sums of squares are nonnegative in the ordered ring
$\mathcal A_\R(\Gamma)$. A recursive
axiomatization of a complete theory would decide each sentence by searching for
a proof of it or of its negation.
\end{proof}

For $\Oz$ the same holds sentence by sentence under the class convention;
no definable global satisfaction predicate for the class ring is asserted.
Source 07 credits the non-axiomatizability of omnific arithmetic to
Je\v r\'abek's 2011 MathOverflow answer \cite{JerabekMO}, and source 06 cites
the same answer for the logical limitations of $\Oz$; the undecidability of
integer-coefficient solvability over $\Oz$ follows from transfer alone
(\cref{odg:cor:H10}). None of these consequences is presented as new, and none
is a new undecidability theorem about $\Q$ or about number fields.

\begin{theorem}[What equations cannot define \src{06, 07}]\label{odg:def:thm:collapse}
Let $\Gamma\ne0$, let $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$ be a unital subring and
$\mathcal R=\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$. No positive-existential
formula with parameters in $\mathfrak o$ defines a subset of $\mathcal R$ that
contains a nonzero element of $\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$ and excludes $0$. In
particular $\mathcal R\setminus\{0\}$, $\mathcal R\setminus\mathfrak o$ and
$\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)\setminus\{0\}$ are not so definable, and in the real case
neither is the strictly positive cone.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The composite $\mathcal R\xrightarrow{\ct}\mathfrak o\hookrightarrow
\mathcal R$ is an endomorphism fixing $\mathfrak o$, and positive-existential
formulas with parameters fixed by an endomorphism are preserved by it (as in
\cref{odg:thm:transfer}). If such a formula held at a nonzero
$a\in\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$, it would hold at the image $\ct(a)=0$. Each displayed set
contains $\omega^g$ for $g\in\Gamma^{>0}$, which is positive in the real case,
and excludes $0$.
\end{proof}

The parameter restriction is essential to this endomorphism argument:
for a fixed nonzero $h\in I$, the equation $x=h$ defines $\{h\}$ with
parameter $h$, which is not fixed by $\ct$. In the real case ``strictly
positive'' means $x>0$, so zero is excluded.
This generalizes \cref{odg:prop:notdiophantine}. It is consistent with the
Diophantine definitions of $\Pi$ and of $\{\ct\ne0\}$: the first contains $0$,
and the second contains no element of $\Pi$. The detector avoids the
obstruction precisely because it defines $\ct(a)\ne0$, not $a\ne0$: $\omega$
is nonzero but divides no value $\Lambda(t)$ \src{07}. The set
$\mathcal R\setminus\{0\}$ has the quantifier-free definition $x\ne0$; what
fails is a positive-existential definition. Source 07 left open whether $\Pi$
itself has one; \cref{odg:def:thm:ideal} of source 06 shows that it has.

\begin{proposition}[A quantifier-free lower bound \src{06}]\label{odg:def:prop:notqf}
In $\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ with $\Gamma\ne0$, neither $\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$ nor
$\Z$ is definable by a quantifier-free ring formula in one variable, even with
arbitrary parameters; the same holds for the ideal and $\Z[i]$ in
$\mathcal R_{\Z[i]}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$. Hence the complete theory of such a
set-sized ring does not eliminate quantifiers in the ring language.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
In an infinite domain a nonzero one-variable polynomial has finitely many
roots. Each atomic equality is therefore true either everywhere (the
zero polynomial) or at finitely many points; finite Boolean combinations
of finite or cofinite sets remain finite or cofinite. This also covers
negated equalities and any finite tuple of ring parameters.
Fix $g\in\Gamma^{>0}$. The ideal contains the infinitely many distinct
monomials $\omega^{kg}$, $k\ge1$, and its complement contains the nonzero
ordinary integers. The coefficient subring is infinite and its complement
contains those same monomials. Neither set is finite or cofinite. If the
complete theory eliminated quantifiers, the coefficient-subring definition
from \cref{odg:def:thm:numberfield}, with $K=\Q$ or $\Q(i)$, would be
equivalent to a quantifier-free formula, contradicting what was just proved.
\end{proof}

This is a first lower bound on logical complexity only; it does not show that
degree four, or five witnesses, is optimal \src{06}.

\begin{theorem}[Omitted types \src{07; also 06}]\label{odg:def:thm:saturation}
Let $A$ be a set-sized intermediate ring for $(\Z,\R)$ or $(\Z[i],\C)$, or a
set-sized ring as in \cref{odg:def:thm:numberfield}, and let $\Xi_A$ be the
corresponding constant-definition formula. The computable parameter-free type
\[
 \{\Xi_A(x)\}\cup\{Q(x)\ne0:0\ne Q\in\Z[X]\}
\]
is finitely satisfiable and omitted; so $A$ is not recursively saturated. For
the full rings $\Oz$ and $\Og$ the same explicit type is finitely satisfiable
and omitted.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Use a fixed canonical syntax for integer polynomials, with signed integer
coefficients and no trailing zero coefficient. Testing that a formula is
$\Xi_A(x)$ or a displayed inequality for a nonzero polynomial is decidable;
this makes the type computable, not just countable. Its only coefficients
are integer numerals, including those of the fixed guard $\Xi_A$.
A finite part excludes the roots of finitely many nonzero polynomials.
If the sum of their degrees is $D$, at most $D$ of the ordinary integers
$0,1,\ldots,D$ are excluded; at least one satisfies every inequality and
$\Xi_A$. A realization of the whole type would lie in $\mathfrak o$, hence
be algebraic over $\Q$; clearing denominators in an annihilating rational
polynomial gives a nonzero integer polynomial vanishing at it, a
contradiction. Recursive saturation would realize this computable finitely
satisfiable type over no parameters.
\end{proof}

For $\mathfrak o=\Z$ the simpler countable type $\{\Std(x)\}\cup\{x\ne n:n\in\Z\}$
already shows that $\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ is not countably saturated, and
the Gaussian analogue holds with all Gaussian constants \src{06}. For the full
classes this is the class-relative formulation: no set-sized model carrier is
assumed. Nonsaturation of $\Oz$ has the 2018 precedent \cite{Nombre}. Even a
very rich exponent order does not make these rings recursively saturated; the
statement concerns the ring, not its order or a surrounding field \src{07}.

\begin{remark}[Transfer without elementarity \src{06; also 13}]\label{odg:def:rem:notelementary}
For $\Gamma\ne0$ the inclusion $\Z\hookrightarrow\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ is
not elementary, although it transfers positive-existential formulas: the
sentence $\exists x,y\,(x^2=2y^2\wedge y\ne0)$ is false in $\Z$ and in $\Z[i]$
