and holds in the ring, with $y=\omega^g$ and $x=\sqrt2\,\omega^g$ for any
$g>0$ (when $\sqrt2\in\Bbbk$). This is a second route to the non-elementarity
recorded after \cref{odg:thm:induction} and in \cref{odg:rem:ringinduction}.
The same non-elementarity conclusion for
$\Z\hookrightarrow\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ holds even when $\Bbbk$
does not contain $\sqrt2$: the sentence $\forall x\,\Xi_\Q(x)$, using the
guard above, holds in $\Z$ but fails at $\omega^g$ in the larger ring.
The guard defines $\Z$ in both structures by
\cref{odg:def:thm:numberfield}; this argument uses no ordering of $\Bbbk$.
A formula that defines the actual integers in this ring need not select the
standard copy in an elementary extension, where it defines an elementary
extension of the induced arithmetic; this is compatible with the omitted
types above \src{06}.
\end{remark}

\section{Infinite families, primitivity, and projective points}\label{odg:sec:primitive}

\subsection{Polynomial curves through integer points}

\begin{proposition}[Finite polynomial lifting \src{01, 02, 05}]\label{odg:prop:polylift}
Let $F_1,\ldots,F_r\in\R[X_1,\ldots,X_n]$, and let
$P_1(T),\ldots,P_n(T)\in\R[T]$ satisfy
\[
 F_j(P_1(T),\ldots,P_n(T))=0\quad(1\leq j\leq r),
 \qquad P_i(0)\in\Z\quad(1\leq i\leq n).
\]
Then $\mathbf P(t)\in\Oz^n$ solves the system for every $t\in\Pi$, with
constant tuple $\mathbf P(0)$. If some $P_i$ is nonconstant, every
$t\in\Pi\setminus\{0\}$ produces an infinite point.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Every nonconstant term of $P_i(t)$ lies in $\Pi$, since $\Pi$ is an ideal and
a real vector space, so the constant coefficient is exactly $P_i(0)$.
Polynomial identities hold under substitution into any field extension. If
$P_i$ has degree $d>0$ and $\ddeg(t)=\gamma>0$, its top term has degree
$d\gamma$, strictly larger than every other term's degree.
\end{proof}

No integrality is required of the nonconstant coefficients: for example
$(1+t^2/2,\,t^2/2,\,t)$ is omnific for every $t\in\Pi$ although
$1/2\notin\Oz$; it satisfies $X^2-Y^2-Z^2=1$ by expansion.
For $t<0$, leading degree still proves infinite size, though it need not
preserve the eventual signs of the polynomials as $T\to+\infty$.
The finiteness of the polynomials matters, however.
Evaluating an infinite real power series at an infinite omnific argument is
not licensed by this proposition; the Taylor expansion of $\sqrt{1+T}$ is not
an admissible way to assert that $\sqrt{1+\omega}$ lies in $\Oz$. Polynomial
maps supply a systematic sufficient condition for nontrivial fibers; they do
not exhaust all normal-form constructions, and no such exhaustion is assumed
(but see \cref{odg:thm:specialization} for finite supports). On smooth affine
curves they do decide existence. More precisely, for an affine $\Z$-model
with smooth geometrically integral generic curve, a nonordinary omnific
point exists exactly when the real fiber is isomorphic to $\Aff^1_\R$ and
an ordinary integer point exists. If $\phi:\Aff^1_\R\to C_\R$ is such an
isomorphism and $\mathbf n$ is that integer point, the entire fiber over
$\mathbf n$ is $\phi(\phi^{-1}(\mathbf n)+\Pi)$, bijectively
(\cref{odg:cr:cor:omnific,odg:cr:cor:polywitness}). The coordinates of $\phi$
are finite real polynomials, not necessarily integer polynomials, and the
model need not be the affine line over $\Z$. Taking a nonzero monomial
parameter gives a nonordinary point of finite support with that constant
term; arbitrary parameters in $\Pi$ need not have finite support.

\subsection{Infinite solutions of homogeneous equations}

\begin{theorem}[Homogeneous cone criterion \src{01}; part (b) \src{02}]\label{odg:thm:cone}
Let $F_1,\ldots,F_r\in\R[X_1,\ldots,X_n]$ be homogeneous of positive
degrees.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)},leftmargin=*]
\item Their common zero locus has a nonzero point in $\Oz^n$ if and only
if it has a nonzero point in $\R^n$.
\item If the $F_j$ have ordinary integer coefficients, the
equivalence in (a) persists with any fixed combination of coordinate sign and
nonvanishing conditions preserved by positive scaling, and these existence
problems are decidable.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(a) A real nonzero common zero $\mathbf a$ gives the omnific common zero
$\omega\mathbf a\in\Pi^n$ by homogeneity. Conversely, take a nonzero common
zero $\mathbf x\in\Oz^n$. If every coordinate is constant, it is a real
point. Otherwise let $\gamma>0$ be the maximum of the coordinates' leading
degrees and $r_i=[\omega^\gamma]x_i$; the vector $\mathbf r$ is nonzero. For
$F_j$ homogeneous of degree $d_j$, the coefficient at $\omega^{d_j\gamma}$
in $F_j(\mathbf x)$ is $F_j(\mathbf r)$, since a product attains that exponent
only by taking exponent $\gamma$ in every factor. So $\mathbf r$ is a nonzero
real common zero. This route uses no quantifier elimination.

(b) A real solution $\mathbf r$ gives $\omega\mathbf r$, which has the same
signs and zero pattern. Conversely an omnific solution lies, with $\R$, in a
set-sized real closed subfield of $\No$ (generate a field and take its real
closure inside $\No$). Quantifier elimination makes the inclusion of $\R$
elementary \cite{Tarski}, so the existential statement with its finite sign
conditions already holds over $\R$; quantifier elimination is an effective
decision procedure for this finite formula with integer coefficients.
\end{proof}

The leading-vector route of (a) does not preserve sign or nonvanishing
conditions on coordinates of lower degree: for example $(\omega^2,\omega)$
has both coordinates positive, but its highest-degree coefficient vector
is $(1,0)$. This is why (b) uses Tarski. Any
homogeneous solution over $\No$, not only over $\R$, can be multiplied by a
common positive monomial into $\Pi^n$ preserving signs and zero patterns
(\cref{odg:cor:projectiveclear}) \src{02, 05}. Neither statement decides
affine equations: homogenizing an affine equation and requiring the new
coordinate to be nonzero allows division in $\No$, not in $\Oz$.
For example $X^2-2Z^2=0$ has the omnific solution
$(X,Z)=(\sqrt2\,\omega,\omega)$ with $Z\ne0$. Its affine coordinate
$X/Z=\sqrt2$ is not omnific, and $x^2=2$ has no omnific solution by
\cref{odg:prop:univariate}.

\begin{example}[Fermat scaling \src{01, 02, 05}]\label{odg:ex:fermatscaling}
For every ordinary integer $n\geq2$ and $0<t\in\Pi$,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:eq:fermatscaling}
 x=t,\qquad y=t,\qquad z=2^{1/n}t
\end{equation}
is a positive infinite solution of $x^n+y^n=z^n$; for $t=\omega$ this is
$\omega^n+\omega^n=(2^{1/n}\omega)^n$. The irrational coefficient is
permitted by the definition of $\Oz$. All constant coefficients are zero,
consistent with \cref{odg:thm:transfer}, whose specialization is $(0,0,0)$;
it does not refute Fermat's Last Theorem about nonzero ordinary integers. The
coordinates are divisible by every ordinary integer and have a common
infinite monomial divisor (\cref{odg:thm:commondivisor}), so these solutions
are not primitive and must not be cited as resolving a primitive Fermat
problem.
\end{example}

\status{Source 02 states that the failure of the nonzero analogue of
Fermat's Last Theorem for omnific integers ``is already known'', citing
comments by Emil Je\v r\'abek (27 June 2018) and Timothy Chow (16 November
2021) under MathOverflow answer 303688 \cite{MOFermat}. This attribution
could not be verified when this report was assembled (the site was not
reachable), and it is recorded as source 02's, unverified. No source claims
novelty for the example.}

\subsection{Unimodular Pythagorean triples}

\begin{proposition}[Infinite unimodular Pythagorean triples \src{01, 05}]\label{odg:prop:pythagoras}
For every $t\in\Pi$, put $x=t^2-1$, $y=2t$, $z=t^2+1$. Then
$x^2+y^2=z^2$ and the triple is unimodular. For $t\ne0$ all three
coordinates are infinite, and for $t>0$ they are positive.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The identity is the ordinary polynomial identity
$(t^2-1)^2+4t^2=(t^2+1)^2$. Two unit-ideal witnesses are
\begin{equation}\label{odg:eq:pythbezout}
 \frac{t^2}{2}z-\Bigl(\frac{t^2}{2}+1\Bigr)x=1\ \src{01},\qquad
 \frac t2\,y-x=1\ \src{05},
\end{equation}
with $t^2/2,\,t/2\in\Pi\subseteq\Oz$. Positive leading degree gives the
assertion about infinite size.
\end{proof}

The constant tuple is $(-1,0,1)$, not the zero tuple; that is exactly where
the common-divisor obstruction for $\Pi^3$ stops applying. Infinite
solutions therefore need not arise by scaling an ordinary tuple, and
exponent two differs from exponents at least three
(\cref{odg:thm:unimodularfermat}). More generally
$(u^2-v^2)^2+(2uv)^2=(u^2+v^2)^2$ for all $u,v\in\Oz$ \src{02}.

\subsection{Which real projective directions have primitive representatives?}

A point $[r_0:\cdots:r_n]\in\mathbb P^n(\R)$ is \emph{rational} if
it has homogeneous coordinates in $\Q$, equivalently a nonzero integer
coordinate tuple.

\begin{theorem}[Rationality of primitive constant directions \src{01}]\label{odg:thm:realray}
For a real projective point $p=[r_0:\cdots:r_n]$, the following are
equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=*]
\item $p$ is rational;
\item $p$ has a unimodular representative in $\Oz^{n+1}$;
\item $p$ has a primitive representative in $\Oz^{n+1}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For (i)$\Rightarrow$(ii), choose an integer representative with ordinary gcd
one; the integer B\'ezout identity makes it unimodular over $\Oz$.
(ii)$\Rightarrow$(iii) is immediate. For (iii)$\Rightarrow$(i), write a
primitive representative as $z_i=\lambda r_i$ with $\lambda\in\No^\times$
and choose $r_j\ne0$. Since $z_j\in\Oz\subseteq\BR$ and division by the
nonzero real $r_j$ preserves $\BR$, $\lambda\in\BR$. Put $c=\ct(\lambda)$;
then $\ct(z_i)=cr_i\in\Z$ for all $i$. If $c=0$, every $z_i$ lies in $\Pi$,
so $2$ divides every coordinate, contradicting primitivity. Hence $c\ne0$ and
$(cr_0,\ldots,cr_n)$ is a nonzero integer representative of $p$.
\end{proof}

In fact every primitive representative of a real projective point is an
ordinary coprime integer tuple. To see this additional consequence, the
theorem first makes the point rational. Fix an ordinary representative
$\mathbf m$ with gcd one and integers $b_i$ such that $\sum_i b_i m_i=1$.
Every omnific representative has the form $\mathbf z=s\mathbf m$ for some
$s\in\No^\times$, and
\[
 s=\sum_i b_i z_i\in\Oz,\qquad
 (z_0,\ldots,z_n)=s\Oz.
\]
Indeed each $z_i$ is a multiple of $s$, and the displayed B\'ezout identity
puts $s$ in the coordinate ideal. Thus such a representative is primitive
if and only if $s$ is a unit, equivalently $s=\pm1$, and then it is also
unimodular. This equivalence is specific to representatives of real
directions; no general equivalence of primitivity and unimodularity over
$\Oz$ is asserted.

For example, $[1:\sqrt2]$ has omnific representatives such as
$(\omega,\sqrt2\,\omega)$ but no primitive, hence no unimodular,
representative; source 02 proved the unimodular half of this example
directly. The theorem concerns real projective points; it is not a
classification of all points of $\mathbb P^n(\No)$, and neither monomial
clearing nor the existence of a homogeneous solution answers the
primitive-representative question. \Cref{odg:frac:sec:fractions}, from sources
08 and 09, extends the classification to all points of $\mathbb P^n(\R(t))$
and $\mathbb P^n(\C(t))$ for $0\ne t\in\Pi$, and to the region $\Vreg$ of
$\mathbb P^1(\No)$ (\cref{odg:frac:thm:main,odg:frac:cor:curves,odg:frac:thm:dichotomy,odg:frac:thm:gaussian});
the Gaussian form of the theorem is \cref{odg:frac:cor:gaussiandirections}.
A classification of all points of $\mathbb P^n(\No)$ is still not claimed
(\cref{odg:frac:q:ideals,odg:frac:q:region}). On projective varieties that are rigid
over the one-sided rings, such as curves of positive genus, sources C12 and C14 show
that only rational points have invertible, and in particular unimodular, omnific
coordinates (\cref{odg:cr:thm:ozideal,odg:cr:cor:projectiveQ}).

\subsection{Finite supports specialize to polynomial arcs}

The rigidity theorems above do not depend on finite supports. Finite-support
expressions nevertheless admit an additional reduction \src{05}.

\begin{lemma}[Finite ordered specialization \src{05}]\label{odg:lem:specialization}
Let $H$ be a finitely generated subgroup of an ordered abelian group and
$E\subseteq H$ finite. There is an additive map $\ell:H\to\Z$ with
$\ell(a)>\ell(b)$ whenever $a,b\in E$ and $a>b$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A finitely generated torsion-free abelian group is isomorphic to $\Z^r$. In
such coordinates, form the finite set of vectors $a-b$ for $a,b\in E$ with
$a>b$. If this set is empty, $\ell=0$ works. Otherwise enumerate it as
$v_1,\ldots,v_s$; each vector represents a positive element in the original
group order.

We first show that $0$ is not in their ordinary real convex hull. If it
were, choose a solution of
\[
 \sum_j c_jv_j=0,\qquad \sum_j c_j=1,\qquad c_j\ge0
\]
with the smallest possible number of positive coefficients. The augmented
columns $(v_j,1)$ for those coefficients are linearly independent. Otherwise
a nonzero dependence $d_j$ has $\sum_j d_j=0$, hence both positive and
negative entries; moving the coefficient vector along this dependence
until one positive coefficient reaches zero preserves all constraints and
reduces its positive support. Independence gives a square nonsingular
minor with integer entries, so the positive $c_j$ are rational by solving
that subsystem. Clearing denominators yields a nonempty sum of positive
integer multiples of the $v_j$ equal to zero, impossible in an ordered group.

Let $C$ be their convex hull and choose $p\in C$ of least Euclidean norm.
Compactness supplies $p$, and $p\ne0$. For $v\in C$, minimality along the
segment $p+u(v-p)$, $0\le u\le1$, gives
$p\mathbin{\cdot}(v-p)\ge0$. Consequently
$p\mathbin{\cdot}v_j\ge\|p\|^2>0$ for every $j$.
A rational vector sufficiently close to $p$ keeps these finitely many
strict inequalities. Multiplying it by a positive common denominator
gives an integer vector $w$, and $\ell(h)=w\mathbin{\cdot}h$ is the required
additive map. All convexity here is in the ordinary finite-dimensional
space $\R^r$, not in a surreal topological space.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite-support solutions specialize to arcs \src{05}]\label{odg:thm:specialization}
Suppose $\mathbf x\in\Oz^n$ has finite normal-form supports and satisfies a
finite polynomial system over $\R$. There are real polynomials
$f_1(T),\ldots,f_n(T)$ satisfying the same polynomial identities with
$f_i(0)=\ct(x_i)$, and every nonconstant $x_i$ gives a nonconstant $f_i$. A
prescribed finite list of nonzero real-coefficient polynomial expressions
in $\mathbf x$ can
be kept nonzero, with the same eventual signs as $T\to+\infty$ as their
surreal signs.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $E$ contain zero, all exponents of the coordinates, and all exponents of
the prescribed expressions after expansion; it is finite. Let $H$ be its
integer span and choose $\ell$ by \cref{odg:lem:specialization}. The
group algebra $\R[H]$ identifies with the finite-support real normal forms
on $H$: distinct monomials are linearly independent, and their multiplication
adds exponents. Thus the original equations hold in this algebra. The map
$\R[H]\to\R[T,T^{-1}]$, $\omega^h\mapsto T^{\ell(h)}$, is a ring
homomorphism fixing every real coefficient. Positive coordinate exponents map to positive integers,
so coordinate images are polynomials with the same constant coefficients;
injectivity of $\ell$ on $E$ prevents cancellation of distinct supported
terms, in particular of leading terms. Polynomial identities are preserved,
and each prescribed expression keeps its leading coefficient and hence its
eventual sign for large positive $T$.
\end{proof}

Source 05 states the system over $\Z$; fixing real scalars in the same
homomorphism proves the extension above. Coefficients of the additional
expressions are specified as real for the same reason. The map need not be
injective or order preserving on all of $H$: it preserves the finite list
of comparisons used in its construction. For instance, on
$H=\Z+\Z\sqrt2$, the map $\ell(1)=1$, $\ell(\sqrt2)=2$ preserves the order
of $E=\{0,1,\sqrt2,1+\sqrt2\}$ but kills the nonzero group-algebra element
$\omega^{\sqrt2}-\omega^2$. To preserve that expression as well, its
supported exponent $2$ must be included in $E$ and a suitable map chosen
again. Equations need no such extra condition: every ring homomorphism
preserves them, even when it has a kernel.

With \cref{odg:prop:polylift} (substituting $t\in\Pi$ for $T$), this gives an
exact equivalence between the existence of a finite-support nonconstant
solution and of a nonconstant real polynomial arc with integer constant
coordinates. For the reverse implication choose $t=\omega$: the resulting
tuple has finite supports, and each nonconstant polynomial has a nonconstant
value. Positive $t\in\Pi\setminus\{0\}$ also preserves the prescribed signs,
because its positive leading term makes each polynomial's leading term
determine its sign. A single specialization therefore preserves any finite
list of polynomial equalities, disequalities and sign conditions. This does
not turn infinite-support solutions into polynomial arcs. For smooth affine curves,
however, every nonordinary point shares its constant term with a nonordinary point
of finite support (\cref{odg:cr:cor:polywitness}).

Finite-support B\'ezout certificates can be retained as well. If
$x_i,u_i\in\Oz$ all have finite supports and $\sum_i u_i x_i=1$, apply the
theorem to the combined tuple and include this equation. Its images satisfy
$\sum_i g_i(T)f_i(T)=1$, with integer constant coefficients in every $f_i$
and $g_i$. Evaluating at any $t\in\Pi$ gives an omnific B\'ezout certificate,
so the resulting family is unimodular. This argument requires finite
supports for the supplied witnesses $u_i$, not just for the coordinates
$x_i$; existence of arbitrary omnific witnesses does not supply that
additional hypothesis.

For a finite-support implementation one may store an element as a map from
exact exponents to nonzero exact real coefficients; the essential operations
are coefficientwise addition, exponent addition in convolution, combination
of equal exponents, and deciding integrality of the constant coefficient.
Rational exponents with real algebraic coefficients give a practical
decidable representation; general symbolic reals or arbitrary surreal
exponents need further equality and order oracles. Useful exact certificates
are polynomial identities, B\'ezout witnesses such as \eqref{odg:eq:pythbezout},
standard-modulus quotients, and finite normal-form obstructions such as the
first negative term in \eqref{odg:eq:binomial} \src{05}.

\section{Fractions, denominator ideals and unimodular presentations}\label{odg:frac:sec:fractions}

This section is built from sources 08 and 09 (\cref{odg:sub:provenance}), with
source 08 as the base. \Cref{odg:thm:fractions} makes every surreal a fraction
of omnific integers, but it says nothing about lowest terms, and
\cref{odg:thm:nogcd,odg:thm:realray} treat only the constant pairs $(t,rt)$ and
the constant real directions. The central theorem below
(\cref{odg:frac:thm:pullback}) removes the constancy. For every tuple that is
unimodular over $\BR$, the constant vector alone decides whether the admissible
scalar multipliers form $\Oz$ or $\Pi$. This classifies every omnific
representation of every point of $\mathbb P^n(\R(t))$ for $0\ne t\in\Pi$
(\cref{odg:frac:thm:main}). It fills the gap stated after \cref{odg:thm:realray}
and answers \cref{odg:q:homogeneous} in part (\cref{odg:frac:cor:homogeneous}).
Source 09 adds the region $\Vreg$ of points with a $\BR$-unimodular
presentation. It identifies the specialization fibers with the orbits of the
congruence kernel of $\SL_2(\Oz)\to\SL_2(\Z)$ and proves that these fibers are
dense. Source 08 adds a localization with residue field $\Q$, polynomial
models, the exact denominator defect of a scale extension with its $\Tor_1$
witness, and the Gaussian dichotomy. The Gaussian dichotomy contains the
Gaussian unimodular-direction criterion of manuscript 14 of the sibling
report (\cref{odg:frac:cor:gaussiandirections}). The proof review recorded in
\fn{RECONCILIATION.md} now covers this whole section, including the
localization, scale-extension, fixed-workspace and Gaussian arguments.
The later additions in \cref{odg:sec:formal,odg:sec:questions}, other
new material and remaining imported foundations still need review.

\subsection{Conventions}\label{odg:frac:sub:conventions}

Throughout, $t$ denotes a nonzero element of $\Pi$, as in
\cref{odg:prop:polylift,odg:prop:pythagoras}. It is not the Hahn variable
$t=\omega^{-1}$ of \cref{odg:sub:notation}. Source 08 writes $\tau$, which here
names the phase twist of \cref{odg:def:lem:twist}; source 09 uses only the
monomials $T=\omega^\alpha$ with $\alpha>0$. Polynomials are written in an
indeterminate $T$, and $p(t)$ is evaluation at $t$.

\paragraph{Coordinate order.} Numerators come first and the denominator last.
A tuple $(x_1,\ldots,x_n)$ is the point $[x_1:\cdots:x_n:1]$, and
$\mathbb P^1(\No)=\No\cup\{\infty\}$ through $[P:Q]\mapsto P/Q$ for $Q\ne0$ and
$[P:0]\mapsto\infty$. Source 09 uses this order, while source 08 puts the
denominator first. The specialized values, such as $f(0)$, are the same in
both orders. A point of $\mathbb P^1(\No)$ other than $\infty$ is called
\emph{affine}. Source 09 calls such points ``finite'', a word reserved here for
bounded surreals.

\paragraph{Primitivity.} ``Primitive'' and ``unimodular'' are as in
\cref{odg:def:primitive}. Over another domain, a tuple is primitive when its
only common divisors are units. Source 09's ``divisor-primitive'' presentations
and ``B\'ezout fractions'' are the primitive and the unimodular presentations
here. Unimodularity implies primitivity; the converse is not assumed over $\Oz$
and is proved below only for the classified representations.

\paragraph{New symbols.} The symbols $\Den$, $\spz$, $\Vreg$, $\Ureg$,
$\Kcong$, $\res$, $\Ex$, $\Pmod$, $\mathsf F_{x,b}$ and $\mathsf N_x$ are
introduced below. Most of them replace source symbols that collide with
symbols of this report (\cref{odg:tab:renames}). In this report $\Gamma$ is an exponent
group, $\sigma$ an automorphism, $\rho=\sqrt2-1$, $D$ the Pell parameter, $G$
the Gram matrix, $U_t$ the quadratic transvection, $A$ an intermediate ring,
$\mathcal A_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$ and $\mathcal F$ the rings of
\cref{odg:def:sec:arithmetic,odg:def:sec:reconstruction}, $B$ a bilinear form,
$\Delta$ a discriminant and $\V$ a zero set. The source symbols are therefore
renamed. The calligraphic $\Vreg$ below is a class of projective points, not a
zero set.

\subsection{Denominator ideals}\label{odg:frac:sub:ideals}

\begin{definition}[Denominator ideals \src{08, 09}]\label{odg:frac:def:den}
For surreals $x_1,\ldots,x_n$ put
\[
 \Den(x_1,\ldots,x_n)=\{b\in\Oz:bx_j\in\Oz\text{ for every }j\}.
\]
For a domain $R'$ in a field containing the $x_j$, $\Den_{R'}$ is defined in the
same way with $R'$ in place of $\Oz$. A \emph{representation} of
$(x_1,\ldots,x_n)$ is a vector $(bx_1,\ldots,bx_n,b)\in\Oz^{n+1}$ with $b\ne0$.
\end{definition}

Zero belongs to $\Den(x_1,\ldots,x_n)$. The actual common denominators are its
nonzero members, and a common one exists by applying
\cref{odg:thm:fractions} to the finite set of coordinates. Moreover
$\Den(x_1,\ldots,x_n)=\bigcap_j\Den(x_j)$, so it is an ideal as well.
For a general domain $R'$ in an ambient field, this ideal can instead be
zero: a nonzero denominator forces every $x_j$ to lie in $\Frac(R')$.
Conversely, if all coordinates lie in $\Frac(R')$, the product of finitely
many individual denominators is a nonzero common denominator.
Ideals of $\Oz$ here may be proper classes; their operations and generated
ideals use finite sums and products, as in \cref{odg:sub:notation}. Source 09 writes $\operatorname
D(x)$; the letter $D$ is the Pell parameter here.

\begin{proposition}[Calculus of denominator ideals \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:prop:calculus}
For every $x\in\No$, $\Den(x)$ is a nonzero ideal of $\Oz$, with
$\Den(0)=\Oz$ and $\Den(x)=\Oz\cap x^{-1}\Oz$ for $x\ne0$. For $z\in\Oz$,
\[
 \Den(x+z)=\Den(x),\qquad x\,\Den(x)=\Den(1/x)\quad(x\ne0).
\]
For $g=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in\GL_2(\Oz)$ and
$cx+d\ne0$,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:frac:eq:covariance}
 \Den\Bigl(\frac{ax+b}{cx+d}\Bigr)=(cx+d)\,\Den(x).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Closure under addition and under multiplication by $\Oz$ is direct, and
nonzero denominators exist by \cref{odg:thm:fractions}. For $h\in\Oz$,
$h(x+z)\in\Oz$ is equivalent to $hx\in\Oz$, by subtracting $hz$; this also
covers $x=0$. For $x\ne0$, multiplication by $x$ sends a denominator $h$ of
$x$ to $xh\in\Oz$, with $(xh)/x=h\in\Oz$. Its inverse sends a denominator
$k$ of $1/x$ to $k/x$. This proves the reciprocal identity.
For \eqref{odg:frac:eq:covariance} put
$y=(ax+b)/(cx+d)$. If $h\in\Den(x)$, then $k=(cx+d)h\in\Oz$ and
$yk=(ax+b)h\in\Oz$. Conversely let $k\in\Den(y)$ and write
$\delta_g=ad-bc\in\Oz^\times$ for the determinant. Since
$a-cy=\delta_g/(cx+d)$ and $dy-b=\delta_g x/(cx+d)$, the elements
\[
 h=\delta_g^{-1}(a-cy)k=\frac{k}{cx+d},\qquad
 xh=\delta_g^{-1}(dy-b)k
\]
lie in $\Oz$: both $k$ and $yk$ do, and $\delta_g^{-1}\in\Oz$.
Thus $h\in\Den(x)$ and $k=(cx+d)h$. In particular the right side of
\eqref{odg:frac:eq:covariance} lies in $\Oz$, even though the scalar
$cx+d$ itself need not lie there.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Least denominators \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:prop:least}
A nonzero ideal $\mathfrak a\subseteq\Oz$ is principal if and only if its positive
members have a least element. Hence $x\in\No$ has a least positive omnific
denominator if and only if $\Den(x)$ is principal. If $\Den(x)=b_0\Oz$ with
$b_0>0$, the representations of $x$ are exactly $(a_0h,b_0h)$ with
$a_0=b_0x$ and $0\ne h\in\Oz$, and $(a_0,b_0)$ is primitive.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $\mathfrak a=b_0\Oz$ with $b_0>0$, the positive members are $b_0h$ with
$h\ge1$ (\cref{odg:prop:units}), so $b_0$ is least. Conversely, if $b_0$ is the
least positive member and $a\in\mathfrak a$, ordered division
(\cref{odg:prop:division}) gives $a=qb_0+r$ with $0\le r<b_0$ and
$r\in\mathfrak a$, so $r=0$. The description of the representations follows
from the definition. If $d$ divides $a_0$ and $b_0$, then $b_0/d\in\Den(x)$, so
$b_0/d=b_0h$ with $h\in\Oz$, and cancellation of $b_0\ne0$ gives $dh=1$, so $d$ is a unit.
This uses one ordered division for each $a$; it does not assume termination
of a Euclidean algorithm in $\Oz$. The argument proves primitivity;
the stronger B\'ezout conclusion below comes from the multiplier theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Unimodular fractions \src{08, 09}]\label{odg:frac:lem:unimodular}
Let $R'$ be a domain and $(p_1,\ldots,p_n,q)\in R'^{\,n+1}$ unimodular with
$q\ne0$. Then $\Den_{R'}(p_1/q,\ldots,p_n/q)=qR'$. Every representation over
$R'$ is $h(p_1,\ldots,p_n,q)$ for a unique $0\ne h\in R'$, and $h$ is a gcd of
it. For $R'=\Oz$ and $q>0$, the vector itself is the only primitive
representation with positive denominator, and two unimodular representations
differ by a sign.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Take $u_1p_1+\cdots+u_np_n+u_0q=1$ in $R'$. If $b$ is a denominator, then
\[
 \frac bq=u_0b+\sum_ju_j\frac{bp_j}{q}\in R'.
\]
Thus $b=qh$, and the numerators are $p_jh$. Conversely every $h\in R'$ gives
a denominator, and $q\ne0$ makes the multiplier $h=b/q$ unique and nonzero
for an actual representation. A common divisor of $h(p_1,\ldots,p_n,q)$ divides the same
linear combination, which is $h$, and $h$ is a common divisor. For $R'=\Oz$ a
representation is primitive exactly when $h$ is a unit, that is, $h=\pm1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Ordinary denominators \src{08, 09}]\label{odg:frac:prop:ordinary}
Inside $\No$,
\[
 \Oz\bigl[(\Z\setminus\{0\})^{-1}\bigr]=\Q\oplus\Pi,
 \qquad
 0\longrightarrow\Pi\longrightarrow\Q\oplus\Pi\xrightarrow{\ \ct\ }\Q\longrightarrow0
\]
with the sequence split exact. The ring $\Q\oplus\Pi$ meets $\R$ in $\Q$, has
unit group $\Q^\times$, is not a field, and has $\Q$ as its only subfield. If
$x=y+m/n$ with $y\in\Pi$, $n>0$ and $\gcd(m,n)=1$, then $\Den(x)=n\Oz$, and
$x=(ny+m)/n$ is a unimodular presentation with least positive denominator $n$:
for integers $u,v$ with $um+vn=1$,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:frac:eq:ordinarybezout}
 u(ny+m)+(v-uy)\,n=1,\qquad v-uy\in\Oz .
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
If $a=k+y\in\Oz$ with $k\in\Z$, $y\in\Pi$, and $0\ne n\in\Z$, then
$a/n=k/n+y/n\in\Q\oplus\Pi$; conversely $y+m/n=(ny+m)/n$.
The constant-term map has kernel $\Pi$ and is split by the inclusion of
rational constants. The direct sum describes the additive decomposition;
it is not a direct product of rings, since rational constants act on $\Pi$
by scalar multiplication. A nonzero element of this ring has nonnegative
leading degree. If it and its inverse both belong to the ring, additivity
of leading degrees forces both degrees to be zero. Their supports are
nonnegative, so both are rational constants. Conversely every nonzero
rational is a unit. Also $\omega$ lies in the ring while $\omega^{-1}$ does not. For
$b\in\Oz$ the product $bx$ has no negative exponent, and its constant
coefficient is $(m/n)\ct(b)$. So $b\in\Den(x)$ exactly when $n\mid\ct(b)$,
which is $b\in n\Oz$ by \cref{odg:thm:finitequotients}. The identity
\eqref{odg:frac:eq:ordinarybezout} is direct, and a positive element of $n\Oz$
is at least $n$. Finally, a subfield contains $\Q$. A nonreal member of
$\Q\oplus\Pi$ is infinite, and its inverse is a nonzero infinitesimal outside
the ring. So every member of a subfield is real, hence rational.
\end{proof}

An element of $\No$ algebraic over $\Q$ is a real algebraic number.
Indeed, a nonzero polynomial in $\Q[T]$ factors over the real algebraic
numbers into linear factors and irreducible quadratics. After a nonzero
scalar is removed, each quadratic has the form $(T-a)^2+b^2$ with $b\ne0$,
and has no root in any ordered extension. A surreal root must therefore
annihilate a linear factor and be real algebraic. This uses only the real
closedness of the real algebraic numbers, not a transfer theorem for classes.
Hence the elements of $\Q\oplus\Pi$ algebraic over $\Q$ are exactly the
rationals, and those of $\Oz$ are the integers \src{09}. Fractions that
produce $\sqrt2$ need infinite omnific denominators. Also
$\BR\cap\Ofin=\R$ and $(\Q\oplus\Pi)\cap\Ofin=\Q$: $\ct$ discards the infinite
terms and $\st$ the infinitesimal ones (\cref{odg:rem:notvaluation}) \src{09}.
So there are four rings to keep apart \src{08}:
\[
 \Q\ \subsetneq\ \Q\oplus\Pi\ \subsetneq\ \Oz_\Pi\ \subsetneq\ \Frac(\Oz)=\No .
\]
The second admits only ordinary integer denominators. The third, the
localization of $\Oz$ at the prime ideal $\Pi$, admits every denominator with a
nonzero constant coefficient; it is studied in \cref{odg:frac:sub:residues}.
The strict inclusions have concrete witnesses: $\omega$ is not rational;
$1/(1+\omega)$ belongs to $\Oz_\Pi$ but is a nonzero infinitesimal and hence
is not in $\Q\oplus\Pi$; and $\sqrt2$ is an omnific fraction but cannot
have a denominator with nonzero integer constant coefficient, by
\cref{odg:frac:thm:realconstants}.

\begin{proposition}[No set of denominators \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:prop:nosetdenominators}
No set $S\subseteq\Oz\setminus\{0\}$ supplies a denominator for every surreal:
there is $0<x\in\No$ with $bx\notin\Oz$ for every $b\in S$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The leading exponents of the members of $S$ form a set. By
\cref{odg:lem:setbounds} choose $\eta>0$ exceeding every $\ddeg(b)$, and put
$x=\omega^{-\eta}$. Then $bx\ne0$ has negative leading exponent, so it is a
nonzero infinitesimal and does not lie in $\Oz$.
\end{proof}

By contrast, the class $\mathcal M=\{\omega^\beta:\beta\ge0\}$ of monomials does
serve: $\mathcal M^{-1}\Oz=\No$, with the localization read for the class of
denominators. This is \replabel{osq:prop:fractions}(iii) of the sibling
report, which source 09 proves again. A single monomial $\omega^\beta$ fails
at $x=\omega^{-\beta-1}$ \src{09}. Even a set-sized subfield of $\No$ has one
common omnific denominator for all its elements (\cref{odg:thm:fractions}),
but the whole class does not \src{08}.

\begin{theorem}[Real constants \src{08, 09}]\label{odg:frac:thm:realconstants}
For $r\in\R$,
\[
 \Den(r)=
 \begin{cases}
 q\,\Oz,& r=p/q\in\Q,\ \gcd(p,q)=1,\ q>0,\\
 \Pi,& r\notin\Q.
 \end{cases}
\]
For irrational $r$, every representation $r=a/b$ has $a,b\in\Pi$, every
nonzero ordinary integer divides both, and the pair has no gcd. For real
constants each of the following is equivalent to $r\in\Q$: a primitive
representation exists; a unimodular representation exists; $\Den(r)$ is
principal; $\Den(r)$ is generated by a set; a least positive omnific
denominator exists.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $b=k+y$ with $k\in\Z$ and $y\in\Pi$. Multiplication by a real introduces
no new exponents (and preserves support when the scalar is nonzero), so
$rb\in\Oz$ exactly when $rk\in\Z$. This includes $r=0$, whose reduced
denominator is $q=1$. For $r=p/q$ in lowest terms this
means $q\mid k$, and since $\Pi=q\Pi$ (\cref{odg:thm:finitequotients}) the ideal
is $q\Oz$. For irrational $r$ it forces $k=0$, giving $\Pi$.

For irrational $r$, then $a=rb\in\Pi$ as well. Dividing their coefficients
by any nonzero ordinary integer preserves positive support, so every such
integer divides both. In particular the pair is not primitive.
If $d$ is a common divisor of
$a$ and $b$, then $(a/d,b/d)$ is another representation of $r$, so both
quotients again lie in $\Pi$ and are divisible by $2$. Hence $2d$ is a common
divisor; it does not divide $d$, because $1/2\notin\Oz$. So no common divisor
is greatest. \Cref{odg:cor:Pglobal} excludes a set of generators, and halving
shows that there is no least positive denominator. In the rational case the
ordinary pair $(p,q)$ is unimodular, and \cref{odg:frac:lem:unimodular} gives
the remaining assertions.
\end{proof}

\status{Source 09 proves $\Den(r)=\Pi$ and the absence of a gcd for irrational
$r$ and credits the absence of a gcd to \cref{odg:thm:nogcd}; source 08
proves the whole theorem and credits the same result. The gcd part is
\cref{odg:thm:nogcd} for the pair $(b,rb)$. For irrational $r$ the ideal
$\Den(r)$ is the dual $\operatorname{Hom}_{\Oz}(L(M),\Oz)$ of the coefficient-lattice module
$L(M)=M+\Pi$ with $M=\Z+\Z r$, and the equality $\Den(r)=\Pi$ is the rank-two
case of \replabel{osq:tn:thm:duals} of the sibling report (manuscript 14
there). Hamkins discusses the failure of lowest terms and the omnific
fractions of irrational reals \cite{Hamkins}, as source 08 records. The
theorem does not erase the distinction between $\Q$ and $\R\setminus\Q$,
although $\Frac(\Oz)\cap\R=\R$ \src{08}.}

\begin{remark}[The halving route \mergetag]\label{odg:frac:rem:halving}
Sources 08 and 09 show that no gcd exists by replacing a common divisor $d$ by
$2d$. This differs from the shift $d\mapsto d\,\omega^\delta$ in the proof of
\cref{odg:thm:nogcd} and from the degree argument of \cref{odg:rem:nogcd01}. It
needs no new exponent, and so the conclusion of \cref{odg:thm:nogcd} holds in
every fixed workspace. \emph{Claim:} for every ordered abelian group
$\Gamma\ne0$, every $0\ne t\in\Pi_\Gamma$ and every $r\in\R\setminus\Q$, the pair
$(t,rt)$ has no gcd in the ring $\Oz_\Gamma$ of \cref{odg:sec:formal}.
Indeed, let $d$ be a common divisor, with $t=du$ and $rt=dv$. Then $v=ru$, and
$\ct(v)=r\ct(u)$ with both constants in $\Z$, so $u,v\in\Pi_\Gamma$. Dividing
coefficients by $2$ preserves supports, so $u/2,v/2\in\Pi_\Gamma$ and $2d$ is a
common divisor. It does not divide $d$, because $1/2\notin\Oz_\Gamma$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The multiplier theorem}\label{odg:frac:sub:multiplier}

We first isolate the algebraic mechanism. Throughout this subsection, $R$ is a
domain containing $\R$ with a ring retraction $c:R\to\R$ that fixes $\R$. Put
$I_R=\ker c$ and $R_\Z=c^{-1}(\Z)=\Z\oplus I_R$, and let $K$ be any field
containing $R$, possibly larger than $\Frac(R)$. The main example is
$(R,c)=(\BR,\ct)$, with $R_\Z=\Oz$ and $I_R=\Pi$. Another is the polynomial ring $R=\R[T]$ with evaluation at $T=0$;
its image $\R[t]$ under evaluation at the omnific parameter is treated in
\cref{odg:frac:sub:models}. Every element of $R$ decomposes uniquely as
$c(h)+(h-c(h))\in\R\oplus I_R$, and multiplication by a nonzero real
preserves $I_R$. No nonzero-kernel hypothesis is needed for the algebraic
theorem: when $I_R=0$, the irrational case simply has no nonzero
admissible scalar.

\begin{definition}[Rational directions \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:def:rational}
A nonzero $\mathbf v\in\R^{n+1}$ has \emph{rational projective direction} if
its line contains a nonzero vector of $\Q^{n+1}$, equivalently of $\Z^{n+1}$.
Such a line has a primitive integral generator, unique up to sign.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[The scalar lattice of a real line \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:lem:lattice}
For $\mathbf v\ne0$ put
$\mathcal L_{\mathbf v}=\{s\in\R:s\mathbf v\in\Z^{n+1}\}$. If the direction of
$\mathbf v$ is not rational, then $\mathcal L_{\mathbf v}=\{0\}$. If $\mathbf v$
is a primitive integral vector, then $\mathcal L_{\mathbf v}=\Z$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A nonzero $s$ in the first case would make the line rational. In the second
case choose integers $u_i$ with $\sum_iu_iv_i=1$. If every $sv_i$ is an
integer, so is $s=\sum_iu_i(sv_i)$. Conversely every integer $s$ works.
More generally, if $\lambda\mathbf v$ is primitive integral, then
$s\mathbf v\in\Z^{n+1}$ exactly when $s/\lambda\in\Z$, giving
$\mathcal L_{\mathbf v}=\lambda\Z$. Clearing ordinary denominators and
dividing by the positive integer gcd constructs this primitive generator;
the same lattice calculation shows that it is unique up to sign.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[All ambient scalar representations \src{08}; pairs \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:thm:pullback}
Let $\mathbf P=(P_0,\ldots,P_n)\in R^{n+1}$ be unimodular over $R$. Its residue
vector $\mathbf v=(c(P_0),\ldots,c(P_n))$ is nonzero, and
\begin{equation}\label{odg:frac:eq:scalars}
 \{h\in K:hP_i\in R_\Z\text{ for all }i\}
 =\{h\in R:c(h)\mathbf v\in\Z^{n+1}\}.
\end{equation}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)},leftmargin=*]
\item If $[\mathbf v]\in\mathbb P^n(\Q)$, multiply $\mathbf P$ by a nonzero real
scalar so that $\mathbf v$ becomes primitive integral. The set
\eqref{odg:frac:eq:scalars} is then exactly $R_\Z$, and the normalized vector is
unimodular over $R_\Z$.
\item If $[\mathbf v]\notin\mathbb P^n(\Q)$, the set \eqref{odg:frac:eq:scalars} is
exactly $I_R$, and this does not change under nonzero real rescaling of
$\mathbf P$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Choose $U_i\in R$ with $\sum_iU_iP_i=1$. Applying $c$ shows $\mathbf v\ne0$. If
every $hP_i$ lies in $R_\Z\subseteq R$, then
\[
 h=\sum_iU_i(hP_i)\in R .
\]
For $h\in R$, membership of $hP_i$ in $R_\Z$ is exactly $c(h)v_i\in\Z$. This
proves \eqref{odg:frac:eq:scalars}, and \cref{odg:frac:lem:lattice} gives the
two scalar sets. It remains to show that the normalized vector in (a) is
unimodular over $R_\Z$, not merely primitive. Here $P_i$ denotes the
normalized coordinate, and $U_i$ is a B\'ezout row for that normalized
vector: rescaling the original vector by $\lambda$ rescales its row by
$\lambda^{-1}$. The normalized coordinates $P_i$ belong to $R_\Z$
because their residues are integers. Choose $u_i\in\Z$ with
$\sum_iu_iv_i=1$, put $S=\sum_iu_iP_i$, so that $c(S)=1$, and define
\begin{equation}\label{odg:frac:eq:lift}
 V_i=u_i+(1-S)U_i .
\end{equation}
Each $V_i$ has residue $u_i\in\Z$, so $V_i\in R_\Z$, and
$\sum_iV_iP_i=S+(1-S)\sum_iU_iP_i=1$.
\end{proof}

The first identity of the proof forces $h$ back into $R$ even when $h$ is
allowed in a much larger field. For $R=\BR$ this is why numerators and
denominators with arbitrary infinite supports, or with scales outside the
supports of $\mathbf P$, cannot evade the classification; formal reduction of a
rational function would not show this \src{08, 09}.
The scalar set includes zero; nonzero scalars give the actual projective
representations. If $\lambda\mathbf v$ is primitive integral, the scalar
set for the \emph{original} vector is $\lambda R_\Z$, since
$c(h)\in\lambda\Z$ and $\lambda I_R=I_R$; it becomes $R_\Z$ after
normalization. Unimodularity over $R$ is essential. For instance the pair
$(\omega,\omega)$ over $\BR$ admits the scalar $\omega^{-1}\notin\BR$,
which takes it to $(1,1)$, but has no B\'ezout identity over $\BR$.

\begin{remark}[A second route to the lift \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:rem:lift}
Source 09 adjusts constants instead of using \eqref{odg:frac:eq:lift}. For a
pair $(A_1,A_0)$ normalized to the primitive constant vector $(m_1,m_0)$ with
$U_0A_1+V_0A_0=1$ over $R$, the real solutions $(y_1,y_0)$ of
$y_1m_1+y_0m_0=1$ differ by multiples of $(m_0,-m_1)$. Choose integers
$u_1,u_0$ with $u_1m_1+u_0m_0=1$ and the real $k$ with
$(u_1,u_0)=(c(U_0),c(V_0))+k(m_0,-m_1)$. Then $U_0+kA_0$ and $V_0-kA_1$ have
constants $u_1$ and $u_0$ and still form a B\'ezout row. With $n+1$ coordinates
and constant vector $\mathbf m$, let $\mathbf w=\mathbf u-c(\mathbf U)$ for an
integer row $\mathbf u$ with $\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf m=1$, and choose a real row
$\mathbf r$ with $\mathbf r\cdot\mathbf m=1$. Write $\mathbf A$ for the
normalized column and $e_j$ for the $j$th standard row. Add to $\mathbf U$
the row $\sum_{i,j}r_iw_j(A_ie_j-A_je_i)$, summing over all ordered pairs.
Each summand pairs to zero with $\mathbf A$, so the B\'ezout identity is
unchanged. Its constant row is
$(\mathbf r\cdot\mathbf m)\mathbf w-(\mathbf w\cdot\mathbf m)\mathbf r=\mathbf w$,
since $\mathbf w\cdot\mathbf m=0$. Source 08's formula \eqref{odg:frac:eq:lift}
is shorter.
\end{remark}

\status{The ring $R_\Z$ is the pullback of $c$ along $\Z\hookrightarrow\R$.
Neither source claims the pullback construction or the use of coefficient
retractions as new. Source 08 cites the retraction transfer lemma of
L'Innocente--Mantova \cite[Lemma 9.2.1]{LM} as a methodological precedent, and
source 09 cites Klawa \cite{Klawa} for background on $D+M$ rings. The sibling
report credits the $D+M$ context to Dobbs--Papick, and its computation of
scalar homomorphisms \replabel{osq:tn:thm:hom} (manuscript 14 there) uses the
same step: a scalar $q$ with $qm_0\in B$ satisfies $q=(qm_0)/m_0\in B$, and then
only the constant part matters. Source 09's pair version (its multiplier
theorem, with multipliers in $\Pi\oplus\{c\in\R:cp,cq\in\Z\}$) is the case
$n=1$, $R=\BR$ of \cref{odg:frac:thm:pullback}; its sketch for $n+1$
coordinates is contained in it.}

\subsection{The unimodular region and its specialization}\label{odg:frac:sub:region}

\begin{definition}[The unimodular region \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:def:region}
Let $\Vreg\subseteq\mathbb P^1(\No)$ be the class of points with a presentation
$[P:Q]$ such that $P,Q\in\BR$ and $UP+VQ=1$ for some $U,V\in\BR$. For such a
presentation put
\[
 \spz([P:Q])=[\ct(P):\ct(Q)]\in\mathbb P^1(\R).
\]
Let $\Ureg\subseteq\Vreg$ be the class of points with a presentation that is
unimodular over $\Oz$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Well-defined specialization \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:lem:welldefined}
Two $\BR$-unimodular presentations of the same point differ by a nonzero real
scalar, so $\spz:\Vreg\to\mathbb P^1(\R)$ is well defined. Moreover
$\mathbb P^1(\R)\subseteq\Vreg$ with $\spz(r)=r$, and $\BR\subseteq\Vreg$ with
$\spz(x)=\ct(x)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Applying $\ct$ to a B\'ezout identity shows that the two constants of a
unimodular pair are not both zero. If $(P',Q')=h(P,Q)$ with $h\in\No^\times$,
the B\'ezout identity of $(P,Q)$ gives $h=UP'+VQ'\in\BR$, and that of
$(P',Q')$ gives $h^{-1}\in\BR$. So $h\in\BR^\times=\R^\times$
(\cref{odg:prop:units}), and the constant vectors define the same point. Use
the pairs $(r,1)$, $(1,0)$ and $(x,1)$ for the last assertions.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Denominator dichotomy on the unimodular region \src{09}; \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:thm:dichotomy}
Let $x=P/Q$ be an affine point of $\Vreg$, with $(P,Q)$ unimodular over $\BR$ and
$Q\ne0$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=*]
\item If $\spz(x)\in\mathbb P^1(\Q)$, choose $\lambda\in\R^\times$ with
$(\lambda\ct P,\lambda\ct Q)$ a primitive integer vector and $\lambda Q>0$. Then
$\Den(x)=\lambda Q\,\Oz$. The presentation $x=\lambda P/\lambda Q$ is unimodular
over $\Oz$ and is the only primitive presentation with positive denominator, and
$\lambda Q$ is the least positive element of $\Den(x)$.
\item If $\spz(x)\notin\mathbb P^1(\Q)$, then $\Den(x)=Q\,\Pi$. This ideal is not
generated by any set. Every presentation has both entries in $\Pi$, none is
primitive, none has a gcd, and every positive denominator can be halved.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently $\Ureg=\spz^{-1}\bigl(\mathbb P^1(\Q)\bigr)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{odg:frac:thm:pullback} with $n=1$, $(R,c)=(\BR,\ct)$ and $K=\No$.
The representations of $x$ are the vectors $h(P,Q)$ with $h$ in the set
\eqref{odg:frac:eq:scalars}, and their denominators are $hQ$. In case (i),
$(\lambda P,\lambda Q)$ is unimodular over $\Oz$, and
\cref{odg:frac:lem:unimodular} gives the rest. In case (ii) the admissible
multipliers form $\Pi$, so $\Den(x)=Q\Pi$. Multiplication by $Q$ is an
$\Oz$-linear bijection $\Pi\to Q\Pi$, so \cref{odg:cor:Pglobal} excludes a set
of generators. Every representation is $h(P,Q)$ with $h\in\Pi$ and $P,Q\in\BR$,
so both entries lie in $\Pi$. If $d$ is a common divisor of a representation,
the quotient is again a representation, so its entries lie in $\Pi$ and $2d$ is
a common divisor that does not divide $d$. Halving $h\in\Pi$ keeps it in $\Pi$.
Finally, the constant vector of an $\Oz$-unimodular pair is a primitive integer
vector (apply $\ct$ to its B\'ezout identity), so $\Ureg$ lies in the rational
fibers. Conversely, (i) gives an $\Oz$-unimodular presentation of every affine
point with rational specialization, and $\infty=[1:0]$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The exact strength of the dichotomy \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:rem:strength}
For affine points of $\Vreg$ the following coincide: rational specialization,
unimodularity over $\Oz$, principality of the denominator ideal, a least
positive denominator, and a primitive presentation. The equivalence is
proved on $\Vreg\cap\No$ only. The point $\infty$ also has rational
specialization and the unimodular presentation $[1:0]$, but the affine
denominator ideal and its least positive element are not defined there.
No such equivalence is asserted outside $\Vreg$, and neither source claims that every
surreal lies in $\Vreg$ (\cref{odg:frac:q:region}). Source 09 notes that
L'Innocente--Mantova use ``reduced'' for a different, support-theoretic notion
\cite{LM}; the word is not used here for lowest terms. For $n+1$ coordinates
the analogue is \cref{odg:frac:thm:pullback} itself.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Rational functions of one parameter}\label{odg:frac:sub:rational}

\begin{lemma}[Evaluation at an omnific parameter \src{08}; monomials \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:lem:evaluation}
Evaluation at $t$ embeds $\R[T]$ in $\BR$ and extends to an embedding
$\R(T)\hookrightarrow\No$. For every polynomial $p$, $\ct(p(t))=p(0)$, and for
$p\ne0$, $\ddeg(p(t))=(\deg p)\,\ddeg(t)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Every power of $t$ has nonnegative support, so polynomial evaluation lands
in $\BR$. Applying the retraction and using $\ct(t)=0$ gives
$\ct(p(t))=p(0)$. Put $\delta=\ddeg(t)>0$; this holds for either sign of $t$.
The nonzero terms $a_jt^j$ have distinct leading exponents $j\delta$, so the
term of largest degree cannot cancel; this is the argument of
\cref{odg:prop:univariate}. Thus evaluation is injective. In particular
$q(t)\ne0$ for every nonzero $q\in\R[T]$, and the fraction-field extension
is well defined and injective by cross multiplication. For later use, if
$0\ne f=p/q\in\R(T)$, multiplicativity of leading exponents gives
\[
 \ddeg(f(t))=(\deg p-\deg q)\delta=(\deg f)\delta.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Representations of rational-function tuples \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:thm:main}
Let $p_1,\ldots,p_n,q\in\R[T]$ with $q\ne0$ and $\gcd(p_1,\ldots,p_n,q)=1$, put
$x_j=p_j(t)/q(t)$ and
\[
 \spz(\mathbf x)=[p_1(0):\cdots:p_n(0):q(0)]\in\mathbb P^n(\R).
\]
This point is defined. Every representation of $\mathbf x=(x_1,\ldots,x_n)$ is
uniquely of the form
\[
 h\,\bigl(p_1(t),\ldots,p_n(t),q(t)\bigr),\qquad h\ne0,
\]
and:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)},leftmargin=*]
\item If $\spz(\mathbf x)\in\mathbb P^n(\Q)$, rescale the polynomials by a
common nonzero real scalar so that their constant vector is primitive integral.
Then $h$ ranges over $\Oz\setminus\{0\}$, $\Den(\mathbf x)=q(t)\,\Oz$, and the
polynomial vector at $t$ is unimodular over $\Oz$.
\item If $\spz(\mathbf x)\notin\mathbb P^n(\Q)$, no normalization is needed.
Then $h$ ranges over $\Pi\setminus\{0\}$, and $\Den(\mathbf x)=q(t)\,\Pi$, an
ideal with no set of generators.
\end{enumerate}
These statements cover representations with arbitrary surreal supports.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The polynomial gcd gives a B\'ezout identity
$\sum_jU_j(T)p_j(T)+U_0(T)q(T)=1$ in $\R[T]$. By \cref{odg:frac:lem:evaluation}
its evaluation shows that the vector at $t$ is unimodular over $\BR$, with
residue vector the constant vector of the polynomials. Evaluating the same
identity at $0$ shows that this vector is nonzero. Equivalently, if all
constants vanished then $T$ would be a common polynomial factor. Apply
\cref{odg:frac:thm:pullback} with $(R,c)=(\BR,\ct)$ and $K=\No$. Every
representation has the unique multiplier $h=b/q(t)\ne0$, and every admissible
nonzero $h$ gives a representation. In (a), $q$ and the $p_j$ henceforth
mean the rescaled polynomials; for an original denominator $q_{\rm old}$
the ideal is $\lambda q_{\rm old}(t)\Oz$, where $\lambda$ is the common
normalizing scalar. The ideals are the classes of last coordinates, including
zero. In (b), multiplication by $q(t)\ne0$ is an $\Oz$-linear bijection
$\Pi\to q(t)\Pi$. A set of generators for its image would pull back to a
set of generators for $\Pi$, contrary to \cref{odg:cor:Pglobal}.
\end{proof}

The projective specialization is independent of the chosen reduced
polynomial vector. Indeed, two such evaluated vectors for the same tuple
differ by a nonzero surreal scalar. Their two $\BR$-B\'ezout identities
force both that scalar and its inverse into $\BR$, just as in
\cref{odg:frac:lem:welldefined}; hence the scalar is a nonzero real.
Taking constants gives the same projective point. This argument applies to
any number of coordinates and is compatible with $\spz$ on $\Vreg$.

\status{Source 09 proves the case $n=1$, $t=\omega^\alpha$ (its one-scale
classification), including the formula
$\spz(P(\omega^\alpha)/Q(\omega^\alpha))=[P(0):Q(0)]$ and the membership of
these points in $\Vreg$. It is the special case of \cref{odg:frac:thm:main} and
\cref{odg:frac:thm:dichotomy} and is not printed separately. Source 08's
theorem has any number of coordinates and any $t\in\Pi\setminus\{0\}$; a nonzero
$t$ that is merely infinite, with a nonintegral constant, is not allowed
\src{08}. The coprimality over $\R[T]$ is necessary: an uncancelled pair such as
$(T(T+r),T(T+1))$ has constant vector zero \src{09}.}

\begin{corollary}[Lowest terms are all or nothing \src{08}; \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:cor:lowest}
In \cref{odg:frac:thm:main}(a) every representation has a gcd, namely its
multiplier $h$ up to sign. The only primitive representations are the two signs
of the normalized polynomial vector, and both are unimodular. The least positive
common denominator is $|q(t)|$. In case (b) no representation is primitive or
has a gcd, every nonzero ordinary integer divides every coordinate of every
representation, every common divisor can be replaced by a strictly larger one
in the divisibility order, and there is no least positive common denominator.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Case (a) is \cref{odg:frac:lem:unimodular} with $\Oz^\times=\{\pm1\}$, and
$|h|\ge1$ for $0\ne h\in\Oz$. The denominator $|q(t)|$ is attained by
choosing $h=1$ or $h=-1$ according to the sign of $q(t)$.
In case (b) every coordinate is $h$ times an
element of $\BR$ with $h\in\Pi$, hence lies in $\Pi$. If $d$ is a common
divisor, dividing by $d$ gives another representation, whose multiplier $h/d$
lies in $\Pi$ by \cref{odg:frac:thm:main}; so $2d$ is a common divisor not
dividing $d$: otherwise cancellation of $d\ne0$ would put $1/2$ in $\Oz$.
Division by any nonzero ordinary integer preserves positive support, proving
the asserted ordinary divisibility of all coordinates. Replacing $h$ by
$h/2$ halves a positive denominator and preserves the whole representation.
\end{proof}

This strengthens \cref{odg:thm:nogcd}: there the pair is the constant point
$(t,rt)$, while here the absence of a gcd holds for every representation of
every point with irrational specialization, for example $h(t+r,t+1)$.

\begin{corollary}[Leading exponents of denominators \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:cor:spectrum}
The leading exponents of the nonzero common denominators of $\mathbf x$ in
\cref{odg:frac:thm:main} are exactly the surreals $\ddeg q(t)+\eta$ with
$\eta\ge0$ in case (a), and with $\eta>0$ in case (b).
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Use \cref{odg:lem:degree} and the two ideal formulas. The nonzero elements of
$\Oz$ have every nonnegative leading exponent, and those of $\Pi$ every
positive one; ordinary integers and monomials $\omega^\eta$ realize them.
\end{proof}

This measures the leading scale, not the support. In case (b) the endpoint is
missing although denominators exist at every larger leading exponent \src{08}.

\begin{corollary}[One rational function \src{08, 09}]\label{odg:frac:cor:onefunction}
Let $f=p/q\in\R(T)$ be reduced. Then $f(t)\in\Vreg$ with $\spz(f(t))=f(0)$, read
in $\R\cup\{\infty\}$. It has a primitive omnific representation if and only if
$f(0)\in\Q\cup\{\infty\}$; lowest terms are then unique once the denominator is
positive, and otherwise no representation has a gcd. The criterion does not
depend on $t$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For a reduced pair $p(0)$ and $q(0)$ are not both zero, and $[p(0):q(0)]$ is
rational exactly when $f(0)\in\Q\cup\{\infty\}$. Apply
\cref{odg:frac:thm:main,odg:frac:cor:lowest}. In particular a pole at $0$
means $q(0)=0$ and $p(0)\ne0$, giving the rational projective point
$[1:0]$; it does not mean that $q(t)=0$. The zero function uses the
reduced pair $(0,1)$ and has least denominator $1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[A formal specialization \src{08, 09}]\label{odg:frac:rem:formal}
The value $f(0)$ is a substitution in an ordinary rational function before
evaluation at $t$. It is not a surreal value of the infinite number $t$, nor
the valuation residue at infinity. There is no operation of ``setting
$\omega=0$'' on arbitrary normal forms, and no homomorphism $\No\to\R$
extending the substitution: a unital field homomorphism extending
$\ct:\Oz\to\Z$ would send the invertible element $\omega$ to $0$. The
criterion belongs to the rational-function subfield and the retraction on $\BR$.
The map $\spz$ is not a field homomorphism either: its domain is only $\Vreg$,
its values are projective, and field operations can make both constants of a
presentation vanish \src{09}.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Denominator ideals \src{08, 09}]\label{odg:frac:ex:table}
Let $t>0$ in $\Pi$ and let $r$ be an irrational real. Generators are given up
to sign.
\begin{center}
\small
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.15}
\begin{tabular}{@{}llllc@{}}
\toprule
$x$ & $\spz(x)$ & $\Den(x)$ & Lowest terms & Source\\
\midrule
$m/n$, $\gcd(m,n)=1$, $n>0$ & $m/n$ & $n\Oz$ & yes & 08, 09\\
$r$ & $r$ & $\Pi$ & no & 08, 09\\
$t+r$ & $r$ & $\Pi$ & no & 08\\
$rt+3/5$ & $3/5$ & $5\Oz$ & yes & 09\\
$1/t$ & $\infty$ & $t\,\Oz$ & yes & 08\\
$r/t$ & $\infty$ & $(t/r)\,\Oz$ & yes & 08\\
$(rt+1)/(t+1)$ & $1$ & $(t+1)\,\Oz$ & yes & 08\\
$(2t+3)/(5t+7)$ & $3/7$ & $(5t+7)\,\Oz$ & yes & 09\\
$(rt+2)/(t+3)$ & $2/3$ & $(t+3)\,\Oz$ & yes & 09\\
$(t+r)/(t+1)$ & $r$ & $(t+1)\,\Pi$ & no & 08, 09\\
$(t^2+1)/(t^2+r)$ & $1/r$ & $(t^2+r)\,\Pi$ & no & 09\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The constant-denominator rows are coprime automatically. In the nonconstant
rows involving $r$, a common root would force $r=1$, $r=2/3$ or $r=0$,
as applicable; irrationality excludes these possibilities. For
$(2T+3,5T+7)$, the combination $5(2T+3)-2(5T+7)=1$ is already a
B\'ezout identity. The denominator $t/r$ in the row $r/t$ comes from
rescaling $(r,T)$ by $1/r$, not from asserting that $r\in\Oz$. The
function $(rt+1)/(t+1)$ has irrational standard part $r$ but unique lowest
terms, while $(t+r)/(t+1)$ has standard part $1$ and none. So the standard part
does not decide cancellation \src{08}.
\end{example}

The class of surreals with a primitive omnific representation is not a
subring \src{08}. Both $t$ and $\sqrt2/t$ have one, but their product $\sqrt2$
does not. Both $1/t$ and $\sqrt2-1/t=(\sqrt2t-1)/t$ specialize to $\infty$, but
their sum $\sqrt2$ does not. These are statements about primitive
representations, not about the existence of fractions.

\begin{example}[Nearly equal values, opposite denominators \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:ex:close}
Let $r$ be irrational and $t>0$ in $\Pi$, and put
\[
 x_+=r+\frac1t=\frac{rt+1}{t},\qquad x_-=r+\frac1{t+1}=\frac{rt+r+1}{t+1}.
\]
Then $\spz(x_+)=\infty$, and $t$ is the least positive denominator of $x_+$,
with the certificate
\begin{equation}\label{odg:frac:eq:closebezout}
 (1-rt)(1+rt)+(r^2t)\,t=1
\end{equation}
over $\Oz$: $1-rt$ has integer constant $1$, while $r^2t\in\Pi$,
so the real coefficients do not obstruct membership of the witnesses.
But $\spz(x_-)=r+1\notin\Q$, so $\Den(x_-)=(t+1)\Pi$ and no
presentation of $x_-$ has a gcd. Yet $x_+-x_-=1/(t(t+1))$ and
$\st(x_+)=\st(x_-)=r$. The standard part does not detect the obstruction, even
at the next infinitesimal scale.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[A certificate procedure \src{08, 09}]\label{odg:frac:rem:procedure}
For $f_1,\ldots,f_n\in\R(T)$ the proofs give a finite procedure. (1) Choose a
common denominator, divide out the polynomial gcd, and compute a polynomial
B\'ezout identity. (2) Evaluate at $0$ and decide whether the direction is
rational. (3) If it is not, return $\Den=q(t)\Pi$. (4) If it is, normalize to a
primitive integer vector, compute an integer B\'ezout identity, and apply
\eqref{odg:frac:eq:lift} (or \cref{odg:frac:rem:lift}) to get an omnific
B\'ezout certificate. To perform step (2), choose a nonzero constant
coordinate $v_k$; the direction is rational exactly when every $v_j/v_k$
is rational. Clearing their denominators and then their integer gcd gives
the primitive vector and the scalar used in step (4).
(5) For the chosen parameter, choose the sign so that $q(t)>0$:
$\operatorname{sgn}(q(t))=\operatorname{sgn}(\lc(q))\operatorname{sgn}(t)^{\deg q}$.
The polynomial certificate works for every nonzero purely infinite parameter;
the positive-denominator sign may depend on that parameter. The ambient-scalar
identity makes the classification apply to representations with arbitrary
supports. The procedure is effective over coefficient fields with exact
arithmetic, decidable equality and rationality, and the required sign tests,
for example real algebraic number fields; it is not a uniform
algorithm for reals given by approximation oracles, and no factorization in
$\Oz$ is used \src{08, 09}.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Prescribed specialization near a given function \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:prop:localdense}
Give $\R(T)$ its topology at infinity: $g$ is $N$-close to $f$ if
$\deg(g-f)<-N$, where $\deg$ is the degree of a rational function
($\deg0=-\infty$). For every $f\in\R(T)$, every ordinary $N>0$ and every
$r\in\R$ there is $g\in\R(T)$, regular at $0$, with $g(0)=r$ and
$\deg(g-f)<-N$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Choose an ordinary integer $L>0$ larger than the pole order of $f$ at $0$
(take that order to be $0$ when $f$ is regular), and for an ordinary integer
$M\ge1$ put
\[
 H=(1+T^L)^{-M},\qquad g=(1-H)f+rH .
\]
The numerator of $1-H$ is $(1+T^L)^M-1$, whose lowest nonzero term is
$MT^L$, and its denominator is nonzero at $0$. Thus $(1-H)f$ is regular
and vanishes at $0$, while $H(0)=1$, proving $g(0)=r$. Moreover
$g-f=H(r-f)$. If $f=r$ this is $0$; otherwise its degree is
$\deg(r-f)-LM$. Choose $M\ge1$ with $LM>N+\deg(r-f)$ to obtain the
strict inequality. Degrees of rational functions add under multiplication,
even if numerator and denominator factors cancel.
\end{proof}

Source 09 states the conclusion ``unless $g=f$ exactly''; the clause is not
needed, since $g=f$ gives degree $-\infty$. This topology is not the topology
induced by the full surreal order. More explicitly \mergetag, fix $t$ and put
$\delta=\ddeg(t)>0$. The set-cut bound supplies $\eta>N\delta$ for every
ordinary $N\ge0$. Every nonzero $f(t)\in\R(t)$ has leading exponent
$m\delta$ for an integer $m$, by \cref{odg:frac:lem:evaluation}, and hence
$|f(t)|>\omega^{-\eta}$. The interval of radius $\omega^{-\eta}$ around
any member of $\R(t)$ therefore meets $\R(t)$ only there: the induced
topology is discrete. In contrast, the degree topology has nonzero
$T^{-M}$ arbitrarily close to zero. \Cref{odg:frac:thm:focusing} achieves
arbitrary surreal accuracy by choosing a new monomial after the target
interval \src{09}.

\subsection{Simultaneous denominators and rational curves}\label{odg:frac:sub:curves}

\begin{example}[No least common denominator \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:ex:nolcm}
Let $t>0$ in $\Pi$. Each of $x_1=1/t$ and $x_2=\sqrt2/t$ has unique lowest
terms, with $\Den(x_1)=t\,\Oz$ and $\Den(x_2)=(t/\sqrt2)\,\Oz$. But
\begin{equation}\label{odg:frac:eq:nolcm}
 \Den(x_1,x_2)=t\,\Oz\cap\frac t{\sqrt2}\,\Oz=t\,\Pi .
\end{equation}
This ideal has no set of generators and no least positive member, so the two
canonical denominators have no least common multiple in the divisibility order.
\end{example}
\begin{proof}
The common polynomial vector is $(1,\sqrt2,T)$, with specialization
$[1:\sqrt2:0]$, which is not rational. \Cref{odg:frac:thm:main} gives
\eqref{odg:frac:eq:nolcm}. An lcm would generate the intersection of the two
principal ideals: being a common multiple gives one inclusion, and dividing
every common multiple gives the other. This would make $t\Pi$ principal,
contrary to its lack of a set of generators. The individual assertions are
\cref{odg:frac:cor:onefunction}.
\end{proof}

The two fractions are already reduced; the obstruction is the irrational
relation between their residues. Every finite tuple in $\R(t)$ has either a
principal common denominator ideal or a scalar copy of $\Pi$, with no
intermediate case \src{08}. For monomial $t=\omega^g$, the identity
\eqref{odg:frac:eq:nolcm} is the intersection formula
$X^gA\cap cX^gA=X^g\Pi$ of \replabel{osq:tn:thm:syzygy}(iii) in the sibling
report, with $c=1/\sqrt2$.

\begin{corollary}[Primitive coordinates on rational curves \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:cor:curves}
Let $p_0,\ldots,p_n\in\R[T]$ have gcd one, and let
$\varphi=[p_0:\cdots:p_n]$. For every $0\ne t\in\Pi$, the point $\varphi(t)$ has
primitive omnific homogeneous coordinates if and only if
$\varphi(0)\in\mathbb P^n(\Q)$. Such coordinates are then unimodular and, after
the common real normalization of the $p_i$, equal to
$\pm(p_0(t),\ldots,p_n(t))$. Otherwise no omnific coordinate vector of
$\varphi(t)$ has a gcd.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The polynomial gcd gives a B\'ezout identity, so the vector at $t$ is
$\BR$-unimodular with residue vector $(p_0(0),\ldots,p_n(0))$. Apply
\cref{odg:frac:thm:pullback} and the argument of \cref{odg:frac:cor:lowest}. No
affine chart is needed.
\end{proof}

For constant $p_i$ this is \cref{odg:thm:realray} of source 01. The corollary
replaces a constant vector by any reduced polynomial parametrization; the
criterion concerns the specialization at $T=0$, not the direction at
infinity \src{08}. Source 08's illustration, the conic
$(1-t^2,2t,1+t^2)$ with certificate
$(1+\tfrac{t^2}2)(1-t^2)+\tfrac{t^2}2(1+t^2)=1$ and specialization $[1:0:1]$, is
\cref{odg:prop:pythagoras} with source 01's witness \eqref{odg:eq:pythbezout},
up to the sign of the first coordinate; source 08 presents it as an
illustration, not as a new construction.

\begin{example}[A curve without primitive coordinates \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:ex:noprimitive}
The vector $(1,\sqrt2+t,t^2)$ has specialization $[1:\sqrt2:0]$, which is not
rational. Its entries are not all omnific, but $h(1,\sqrt2+t,t^2)$ is omnific
for every $0\ne h\in\Pi$, and by \cref{odg:frac:thm:pullback} these are all the
omnific coordinate vectors of the point. None is primitive, although the first
unscaled coordinate is $1$: the unscaled vector does not lie in $\Oz^3$.
\end{example}

\begin{corollary}[Solutions on rational curves \src{08} \mergetag]\label{odg:frac:cor:homogeneous}
Let $F_1,\ldots,F_r\in\R[X_0,\ldots,X_n]$ be homogeneous, and let
$p_0,\ldots,p_n\in\R[T]$ have gcd one, satisfy
$F_j(p_0(T),\ldots,p_n(T))=0$ for all $j$, and have
$[p_0(0):\cdots:p_n(0)]\in\mathbb P^n(\Q)$. After the normalization of
\cref{odg:frac:cor:curves}, for every $0\ne t\in\Pi$ the vector
$(p_0(t),\ldots,p_n(t))$ is a unimodular omnific solution of the system, and it
has an infinite coordinate when some $p_i$ is nonconstant. If instead
$[p_0(0):\cdots:p_n(0)]\notin\mathbb P^n(\Q)$, no point $\varphi(t)$ of the curve
with $0\ne t\in\Pi$ has a primitive, in particular a unimodular, omnific
coordinate vector.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A common real rescaling by $\lambda\ne0$ preserves each equation:
homogeneity gives $F_j(\lambda p_0,\ldots,\lambda p_n)
=\lambda^{\deg F_j}F_j(p_0,\ldots,p_n)=0$ for nonzero $F_j$, and zero
equations are automatic. Evaluation then preserves these identities.
\Cref{odg:frac:cor:curves} gives the unimodular vector in the rational case
and excludes primitive coordinates in the irrational case. If $p_i$ has
positive degree, \cref{odg:frac:lem:evaluation} gives
$\ddeg(p_i(t))=(\deg p_i)\ddeg(t)>0$, so that coordinate is infinite in
absolute value; rescaling by a nonzero real does not change its degree.
\end{proof}

This answers \cref{odg:q:homogeneous} in part: a homogeneous system whose
projective zero locus contains such a rational curve has unimodular omnific
solutions, and along the curve the points with primitive coordinates are
exactly those of rational specialization. It says nothing about points off
rational curves, nor about the dependence on an integral model asked there
(\cref{odg:frac:sub:settled}). The corollary is the merge's formulation of
source 08's \cref{odg:frac:cor:curves}. \Cref{odg:prop:polylift} asks for
integer constants of the $p_i$ and gives omnific solutions, not unimodularity;
here only its projective direction needs to be rational, and the output
after normalization is unimodular. The polynomial gcd condition is also
essential for this stronger conclusion.

\subsection{Specialization fibers, congruence orbits and density}\label{odg:frac:sub:orbits}

Apply $\ct$ entrywise to matrices. It gives a split exact sequence
\[
 1\longrightarrow\Kcong\longrightarrow\SL_2(\Oz)\xrightarrow{\ \ct\ }\SL_2(\Z)\longrightarrow1,
 \qquad \Kcong=\ker\bigl(\ct:\SL_2(\Oz)\to\SL_2(\Z)\bigr),
\]
split by the integer matrices. The kernel of $\ct:\SL_2(\BR)\to\SL_2(\R)$ is the
same group $\Kcong$: determinants are polynomial expressions, so taking
constants commutes with determinants and matrix multiplication, and a matrix
over $\BR$ with constant matrix $I$ has integer
constants, hence entries in $\Oz$ \src{09}. Source 09 writes $\Gamma$ for
$\Kcong$.

\begin{lemma}[Equivariance \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:lem:equivariance}
The class $\Vreg$ is stable under $\SL_2(\BR)$, and
$\spz(gx)=\ct(g)\,\spz(x)$. In particular $\Kcong$ preserves $\spz$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
An invertible matrix carries a unimodular column to a unimodular column: apply
its inverse to transport a B\'ezout row. Explicitly, if a row $u$ and a
column $v$ satisfy $uv=1$, then $(ug^{-1})(gv)=1$ with entries still in
$\BR$. Taking constants commutes with matrix multiplication, giving
$\ct(gv)=\ct(g)\ct(v)$ and the asserted projective formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Specialization fibers are congruence orbits \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:thm:orbits}
For every $r\in\mathbb P^1(\R)$, $\spz^{-1}(r)=\Kcong\cdot r$. Hence
\[
 \Vreg=\coprod_{r\in\mathbb P^1(\R)}\Kcong\cdot r,\qquad
 \Ureg=\coprod_{r\in\mathbb P^1(\Q)}\Kcong\cdot r,
\]
disjoint unions of classes, and every $\Kcong$-orbit in $\Vreg$ contains exactly
one constant real projective point (including $\infty$).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
One inclusion is \cref{odg:frac:lem:equivariance}. For the other let $(P,Q)$
be a $\BR$-unimodular column representing $x$, with $UP+VQ=1$, and put
\[
 M=\begin{pmatrix}P&-V\\Q&U\end{pmatrix}\in\SL_2(\BR),\qquad
 M_0=\ct(M)\in\SL_2(\R),\qquad g=MM_0^{-1}.
\]
Then $M\infty=x$, $\ct(g)=I$, so $g\in\Kcong$, and $g(M_0\infty)=x$ with
$M_0\infty=\spz(x)$. Distinct real projective points have distinct specializations, and the
description of $\Ureg$ follows from \cref{odg:frac:thm:dichotomy}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Modular equivalence \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:cor:modular}
For $r,s\in\mathbb P^1(\R)$, $s\in\SL_2(\Oz)\cdot r$ if and only if
$s\in\SL_2(\Z)\cdot r$. Moreover $\Ureg=\SL_2(\Oz)\cdot\infty$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
If $s=gr$, then $s=\spz(s)=\ct(g)r$ by \cref{odg:frac:lem:equivariance}; the
converse uses integer matrices. An $\Oz$-unimodular column completes to a
determinant-one matrix over $\Oz$ as in the proof of \cref{odg:frac:thm:orbits}.
\end{proof}

The orbits carry a canonical real projective label, and the rational labels
select exactly the fibers whose affine points have lowest terms \src{09}.
The fiber labeled $\infty$ has affine points too, such as $1/t$.

Here and below the topology on $\mathbb P^1(\No)$ is the ordered-field
topology in its two affine charts. Around a surreal point use order
intervals. Around $\infty$ use the reciprocal chart $[P:Q]\mapsto Q/P$:
basic neighborhoods are $\{\infty\}\cup\{z\in\No:|z|>1/\varepsilon\}$
for $\varepsilon>0$. In particular every nonempty projective open
neighborhood contains an affine interval. These are class-defined
neighborhoods; no set of all surreal open subsets is assumed.

\begin{lemma}[Focusing matrices \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:lem:focusmatrix}
For $x\in\No$ and $b\in\Pi$ with $bx,bx^2\in\Pi$ put
\[
 \mathsf F_{x,b}=\begin{pmatrix}1+bx&-bx^2\\ b&1-bx\end{pmatrix}
 =I+b\,\mathsf N_x,\qquad
 \mathsf N_x=\begin{pmatrix}x&-x^2\\1&-x\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Then $\mathsf N_x^2=0$, $\mathsf F_{x,b}\in\Kcong$, $\mathsf F_{x,b}$ fixes $x$,
$\mathsf F_{x,b}\mathsf F_{x,c}=\mathsf F_{x,b+c}$ when both are defined,
$\mathsf F_{x,b}^{-1}=\mathsf F_{x,-b}$, and
\begin{equation}\label{odg:frac:eq:focusaction}
 \mathsf F_{x,b}(z)=x+\frac{z-x}{1+b(z-x)},\qquad
 \mathsf F_{x,b}(\infty)=x+\frac1b\quad(b\ne0),
\end{equation}
the first formula where its denominator is nonzero. For $b=0$ the matrix is
the identity and fixes $\infty$. For $b\ne0$, the pole $x-1/b$ is sent
to $\infty$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The entries other than the constant diagonal lie in $\Pi$, and
$\det\mathsf F_{x,b}=(1+bx)(1-bx)+b^2x^2=1$. The identities follow from
$\mathsf N_x^2=0$. The hypotheses also hold for $b+c$ and $-b$ because
$\Pi$ is an additive group, which justifies the composition and inverse
inside $\Kcong$. The action on a column $(z,1)$ has denominator
$1+b(z-x)$; subtracting $x$ gives \eqref{odg:frac:eq:focusaction}.
The image of $(1,0)$ is $(1+bx,b)$, giving the stated infinity formula
when $b\ne0$ and the fixed point when $b=0$. At $z=x-1/b$ the numerator
is $-1/b\ne0$, so the projective image is indeed $\infty$.
\end{proof}

Source 09 calls $\mathsf F_{x,b}$ an omnific transvection. It is unrelated to
the quadratic isometry of \cref{odg:lem:transvection}.

\begin{theorem}[Set-wise focusing \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:thm:focusing}
Let $S\subseteq\mathbb P^1(\No)$ be a set, $x\in\No$ and $\varepsilon>0$. There is
a monomial $b=\omega^\beta$ with $\beta>0$ such that $\mathsf F_{x,b}\in\Kcong$ and
\[
 \mathsf F_{x,b}(S)\subseteq(x-\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon).
\]
In particular no point of $S$ is sent to $\infty$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The set $\{1,x,x^2\}$ can be cleared by one monomial (\cref{odg:thm:fractions}),
and any larger exponent also clears it. The positive surreals $2/\varepsilon$
and $2/|z-x|$, for affine $z\in S\setminus\{x\}$, form a set, so by
\cref{odg:lem:setbounds} we may also take $\beta$ larger than all their leading
exponents, as well as larger than the clearing exponent and zero. A
positive monomial with strictly greater leading exponent dominates each
specified positive bound regardless of its real leading coefficient.
Then $b,bx,bx^2\in\Pi$, $b>2/\varepsilon$, and $b|z-x|>2$ for those
$z$. For such $z$ put $w=z-x$; then $|1+bw|\ge|bw|-1>\tfrac12|bw|>0$ and
\[
 |\mathsf F_{x,b}(z)-x|=\frac{|w|}{|1+bw|}<\frac2b<\varepsilon .
\]
The point $x$ is fixed, and $\infty$ goes to $x+1/b$, in the same interval.
\end{proof}

The conclusion holds for every set-sized family, not only for finite ones. It
fails for the proper class $\mathbb P^1(\No)$, since an invertible matrix acts
bijectively; the restriction to sets is essential \src{09}. In this explicit
family its pole $x-1/b$ always maps to infinity. The bound $b|z-x|>2$
ensures that this pole is outside the chosen set $S$.

\begin{corollary}[Dense fibers \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:cor:dense}
Every $\Kcong$-orbit in $\mathbb P^1(\No)$ is dense in the topology of its
ordered-field charts. In particular every fiber $\spz^{-1}(r)$, $r\in\mathbb P^1(\R)$, is dense, and
$\Vreg$ contains continuum many pairwise disjoint dense fibers.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{odg:frac:thm:focusing} to a singleton and a nonempty interval;
every nonempty open set of $\mathbb P^1(\No)$ contains an interval. Then use
\cref{odg:frac:thm:orbits}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Both denominator types in every interval \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:cor:bothtypes}
Every nonempty open interval of $\No$ contains a fraction with a least positive
omnific denominator and a unimodular presentation, and a fraction whose
denominator ideal is not generated by any set and none of whose presentations
has a gcd. The first can be taken in any prescribed rational fiber, the second
in any prescribed irrational fiber.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Combine \cref{odg:frac:cor:dense,odg:frac:thm:dichotomy}.
\end{proof}

Explicitly \src{09}, fix $x\in\No$, $\varepsilon>0$ and an irrational real
$r$. Apply \cref{odg:frac:thm:focusing} with $S=\{\infty,r\}$, choosing
$b$ with the bounds in its proof. The point with specialization $\infty$ is
\[
 y=x+\frac1b=\frac{1+bx}{b},\qquad (1-bx)(1+bx)+(bx^2)\,b=1,
\]
so $b$ is the least positive denominator of $y$, and $|y-x|<\varepsilon$. For the
already chosen irrational real $r$, the point
$y=\mathsf F_{x,b}(r)=x+(r-x)/(1+b(r-x))$ also satisfies $|y-x|<\varepsilon$
and has
$\Den(y)=\bigl(1+b(r-x)\bigr)\Pi$, since the transformed $\BR$-unimodular column
has constant vector $(r,1)$. Its second entry is $1+b(r-x)$, and its
first is $r+bx(r-x)$; applying a determinant-one matrix preserves
$\BR$-unimodularity. The bound in the focusing proof excludes a zero
denominator. For $r=x$ this is $y=r$ and the denominator factor is $1$.

\begin{corollary}[Nowhere continuity \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:cor:discontinuous}
The map $\spz:\Vreg\to\mathbb P^1(\R)$ is continuous at no point, for the
relative topology from the ordered-field charts on $\mathbb P^1(\No)$ and
the usual topology on $\mathbb P^1(\R)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
At $y\in\Vreg$ choose $r\ne\spz(y)$ and a neighbourhood of $\spz(y)$ omitting
$r$. Every relative neighborhood of $y$ is the intersection with $\Vreg$
of a projective open neighborhood, and the latter meets the dense fiber
$\spz^{-1}(r)\subseteq\Vreg$. Thus no such neighborhood maps into the
chosen target neighborhood. The same argument uses the reciprocal chart
when $y=\infty$.
\end{proof}

Denominator arithmetic is thus invisible to order approximation of any
accuracy. This does not conflict with the continuity of the field operations.
The dense fibers are invariant arithmetic classes; they are not open and not
separated components \src{09}.

\begin{remark}[Elementary matrices do not suffice \mergetag]\label{odg:frac:rem:elementary}
The matrices $\mathsf N_x$ are the transposes of the matrices $Q_\alpha$ of the
non-elementary unipotents in \fn{docs/surreal/omnific-groups-and-lattices}
(\replabel{ogl:el:thm:nonel}), at $\alpha=x$. That report proves that
$h\cdot\infty$ has rational standard part whenever $h\in E_2(\Oz)$ and
$h\cdot\infty$ is finite (\replabel{ogl:el:prop:cuspresidue}), where $E_2$ is
the group generated by elementary matrices. Two consequences follow.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=*]
\item If $x$ is finite with $\st(x)\notin\Q$, for example an irrational real,
then $\mathsf F_{x,b}\notin E_2(\Oz)$ for every $b$ as in
\cref{odg:frac:lem:focusmatrix} with $b\ne0$. Indeed
$\mathsf F_{x,b}(\infty)=x+1/b$ is finite with standard part $\st(x)$.
\item The orbit $E_2(\Oz)\cdot\infty$ is not dense. It misses the interval
$(\sqrt2-\omega^{-1},\sqrt2+\omega^{-1})$, whose points are finite with standard
part $\sqrt2$. But $\Kcong\cdot\infty=\spz^{-1}(\infty)$ is dense
(\cref{odg:frac:cor:dense}), and $\Ureg=\SL_2(\Oz)\cdot\infty$ strictly contains
$E_2(\Oz)\cdot\infty$. For example $\sqrt2+\omega^{-2}$ lies in that interval and
in $\Ureg$, with the unimodular pair $(1+\sqrt2\,\omega^2,\omega^2)$ and the
certificate $(1-\sqrt2\,\omega^2)(1+\sqrt2\,\omega^2)+2\omega^2\cdot\omega^2=1$.
\end{enumerate}
So the density of the orbit of $\infty$ needs matrices outside $E_2(\Oz)$, and
source 09 rightly works with $\Kcong$ rather than with the elementary kernel of
that report. Source 09 never claims $\Kcong\subseteq E_2(\Oz)$; the remark adds
the comparison.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The localization at the purely infinite ideal}\label{odg:frac:sub:residues}

The ideal $\Pi$ is prime, since $\Oz/\Pi\cong\Z$ is a domain. Let
\[
 \Oz_\Pi=\bigl(\Oz\setminus\Pi\bigr)^{-1}\Oz
 =\Bigl\{\frac ab:a,b\in\Oz,\ \ct(b)\ne0\Bigr\}\subseteq\No ,
\]
the localization at $\Pi$, not the quotient $\Oz/\Pi$. Its denominators
are closed under multiplication because their nonzero integer constant
coefficients multiply. Fractions are realized inside $\No$, so this notation
does not require a set containing all pairs of omnific integers. Source 08
writes $\mathcal A$ and $\rho$ for $\Oz_\Pi$ and $\res$.

\begin{proposition}[A local ring with residue field $\Q$ \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:prop:localization}
The map $\res:\Oz_\Pi\to\Q$, $\res(a/b)=\ct(a)/\ct(b)$, is a well-defined
surjective ring homomorphism. The ring $\Oz_\Pi$ is local with maximal ideal
$\ker\res=(\Oz\setminus\Pi)^{-1}\Pi$, its real elements are exactly $\Q$, and it
is neither a field nor a valuation ring of $\No$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
If $a/b=a'/b'$ with $\ct(b),\ct(b')\ne0$, then $ab'=a'b$; taking constant
coefficients proves well-definedness, and the fraction rules give a surjective
homomorphism: ordinary fractions already realize every rational value.
Its kernel consists exactly of fractions with numerator in $\Pi$.
If $\res(a/b)\ne0$, then $\ct(a)\ne0$ and $b/a\in\Oz_\Pi$, so $a/b$
is a unit; an element of $\ker\res$ is not a unit, because its image in
the nonzero field $\Q$ is zero. Thus all nonunits form this proper ideal.
