Every proper ideal contains no unit and hence lies in it, proving that it
is the unique maximal ideal. For $r\in\R$, a
representation with denominator outside $\Pi$ exists exactly when $\Den(r)$ has
an element with nonzero constant, which by \cref{odg:frac:thm:realconstants}
happens exactly for $r\in\Q$. So neither $\sqrt2$ nor $1/\sqrt2$ lies in
$\Oz_\Pi$. Its fraction field is $\No$, since
$\Oz\subseteq\Oz_\Pi\subseteq\No$ and $\Frac(\Oz)=\No$. A valuation ring
of that field would contain $\sqrt2$ or its inverse, so this ring is not one. Finally $t\in\ker\res$ is a nonzero nonunit.
\end{proof}

This is consistent with the sibling report's list of the prime ideals with
set-sized quotient: $\Pi$ is prime with $\Oz/\Pi=\Z$ and fraction field $\Q$
(\replabel{osq:cor:smallprimes}), and $\res$ restricts to $\ct$ on $\Oz$.

\begin{proposition}[Rational functions in the localization \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:prop:localeval}
Under $\R(T)\cong\R(t)$,
\[
 \Oz_\Pi\cap\R(t)=\{f(t):f\in\R(T)\text{ regular at }0,\ f(0)\in\Q\},
\]
and $\res(f(t))=f(0)$ on this ring.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Write $f=p/q$ in reduced form. If $f(0)$ is irrational, every denominator lies in
$q(t)\Pi\subseteq\Pi$ (\cref{odg:frac:thm:main}). If $f$ has a pole at $0$, then
$\Den(f(t))=\lambda q(t)\Oz$ with $q(0)=0$, and again all denominators lie in
$\Pi$. In both cases $f(t)\notin\Oz_\Pi$. If $f$ is regular at $0$ with
$f(0)\in\Q$, normalize $(p(0),q(0))$ to a primitive integer pair; its second
entry is nonzero, so the normalized denominator lies outside $\Pi$, and
$\res(f(t))=f(0)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Independent real and rational residues \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:thm:tworesidues}
Let $\Oz_\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}=\Oz_\Pi\cap\Ofin$. The map
$(\st,\res):\Oz_\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}\to\R\times\Q$ is surjective, its two kernels
are comaximal, and
$\Oz_\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}/(\ker\st\cap\ker\res)\cong\R\times\Q$. For $r\in\R$,
$q\in\Q$ and $t>0$ in $\Pi$, the element
\[
 x_{r,q}=\frac{rt+q}{t+1}=r+\frac{q-r}{t+1}
\]
lies in $\Oz_\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}$ with $\st(x_{r,q})=r$ and $\res(x_{r,q})=q$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
With $q=m/n$, $x_{r,q}=(nrt+m)/(nt+n)$ has numerator and denominator in $\Oz$
and denominator constant $n\ne0$, so $\res(x_{r,q})=m/n$; the second expression
differs from $r$ by an infinitesimal. The elements $u=t/(t+1)$ and $v=1/(t+1)$
lie in $\Oz_\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}$, satisfy $u+v=1$, and have
$(\st,\res)(u)=(1,0)$ and $(\st,\res)(v)=(0,1)$. So the kernels are comaximal, and
the map to the product has kernel $\ker\st\cap\ker\res$. Thus two
elements have the same pair of residues exactly when their difference lies
in that intersection. This gives the asserted quotient isomorphism through
ordinary pairs in $\R\times\Q$ as representatives, without treating
proper-class cosets as set elements.
\end{proof}

The standard part sees the behaviour at an infinite parameter and $\res$ the
constant-coefficient specialization. Both fix $\Q$ \src{08}, but they can
also agree on nonrational elements. In fact \mergetag, on their common domain,
\[
 \{x\in\Oz_\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}:\st(x)=\res(x)\}
   =\Q\oplus(\ker\st\cap\ker\res)
\]
as an additive decomposition. If the common value is $q$, it lies in $\Q$
and $x-q$ lies in both kernels; conversely every such sum has both residues
$q$. The intersection meets $\Q$ only in zero. It is nonzero: for $t>0$ in
$\Pi$, the element $t/(t+1)^2$ has both residues zero and is a positive
infinitesimal, hence is not rational. The earlier phrase ``agree only on
$\Q$'' was therefore too strong.
Neither map is a retraction of $\No$, and their domains differ.
The positive element $u$ above has rational residue zero. To see that
$\res$ even fails to preserve weak order, take $r>0>q$: then $x_{r,q}>0$
has rational residue $q<0$. For nonzero reduced $f=p/q$,
\cref{odg:frac:lem:evaluation} makes $f(t)$ finite exactly when
$\deg p\le\deg q$. With strict inequality it is infinitesimal. With equal
degrees, subtracting the ratio $\lc(p)/\lc(q)$ lowers the numerator degree,
so that ratio is its standard part. The zero function is finite with both
residues zero. So $\Oz_\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}\cap\R(t)$ consists of the
rational functions regular at $0$ and at $\infty$ with a rational value at $0$
and any real value at $\infty$. The surjection onto $\R\times\Q$ is a set-sized
quotient of the class ring $\Oz_\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}$. That ring meets $\Oz$ in $\Z$
and contains no nonzero element of $\Pi$, so the sibling report's
\replabel{osq:prop:fractions}(iv), about localizations in which an element of
$\Pi$ becomes a unit, does not apply. There is no contradiction.

\subsection{Polynomial models and scale extensions}\label{odg:frac:sub:models}

For an indeterminate $X$ write $\Pmod[X]=\Z+X\R[X]$; source 08 writes $A_\tau$,
$A_0$, $A_m$ and source 09 writes $O_\Z$. These are set-sized rings.

\begin{proposition}[The omnific part of a one-parameter field \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:prop:intersection}
$\BR\cap\R(t)=\R[t]$ and $\Oz\cap\R(t)=\Z+t\R[t]$, the image $\Pmod_t$ of
$\Pmod[T]$ under $T\mapsto t$. Moreover $\Frac(\Pmod_t)=\R(t)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Polynomial division writes $f=p/q$ as $a+b/q$, with $a,b,q\in\R[T]$
and either $b=0$ or $\deg b<\deg q$. By \cref{odg:frac:lem:evaluation},
a nonzero $b(t)/q(t)$ is infinitesimal. If $f(t)\in\BR$, then
$b(t)/q(t)=f(t)-a(t)\in\BR$ as well, since $a(t)\in\BR$. That ring has
no nonzero infinitesimal, so $b=0$. This proves the first intersection.
For a polynomial $a$, the identity $\ct(a(t))=a(0)$ gives the second.
The fraction field contains $t$ and every real $r=(rt)/t$, since both
$rt$ and $t$ lie in $\Pmod_t$ and $t\ne0$. Conversely it lies in
$\R(t)$, proving equality.
\end{proof}

Real constants are absent from $\Pmod_t$ except for $\Z$ but reappear in its
fraction field. Transport evaluation at $T=0$ from $\R[T]$ to $\R[t]$
through the injective evaluation map $T\mapsto t$. With this retraction,
\cref{odg:frac:thm:pullback} gives, in the notation of \cref{odg:frac:thm:main},
\begin{equation}\label{odg:frac:eq:modelden}
 \Den_{\Pmod_t}(\mathbf x)=q(t)\,\Pmod_t\quad\text{or}\quad q(t)\cdot t\R[t],
\end{equation}
according as $\spz(\mathbf x)$ is rational or not, with the same normalization.
The primitive or nonprimitive status is the same in the model and in $\Oz$,
although the ideal of all denominators becomes much larger.

\begin{proposition}[Generator counts \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:prop:generators}
The ideal $t\R[t]$ of $\Pmod_t$ needs exactly $\mathfrak c=|\R|$ generators, and so
does every $q(t)\,t\R[t]$ with $q\ne0$. The corresponding ideal $q(t)\Pi$ of $\Oz$
has no set of generators.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
With $I=t\R[t]$, every product of two members has degree at least two,
and $t^2a(t)=t\cdot ta(t)$ for $a\in\R[T]$, so $I^2=t^2\R[t]$.
The coefficient of $t$ gives $I/I^2\cong(\R,+)$. The action of
$\Pmod_t$ on this quotient factors through $\Pmod_t/I=\Z$.
If a family of cardinality $\kappa<\mathfrak c$ generated $I$, its
images would generate $(\R,+)$ as an abelian group. But finite integer
linear combinations of such a family number at most
$\max(\kappa,\aleph_0)<\mathfrak c$, a contradiction; this uses no
continuum hypothesis. Conversely $\{rt:r\in\R\}$ generates $I$, because
every monomial $rt^j$, $j\ge1$, is $(rt)t^{j-1}$ with
$t^{j-1}\in\Pmod_t$, and polynomials have finite support.
Multiplication by $q(t)\ne0$ is a module isomorphism onto the scaled
ideal. Multiplication by the same scalar identifies $\Pi$ with $q(t)\Pi$,
so \cref{odg:cor:Pglobal} gives the last assertion even without a specified
rational-function tuple.
\end{proof}

For an ideal $J$ of $\Pmod_t$ let $J\Oz$ be its extension to $\Oz$:
finite sums of products $ja$ with $j\in J$ and $a\in\Oz$.

\begin{theorem}[Enlargement defect \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:thm:enlargement}
In the notation of \cref{odg:frac:thm:main},
$\Den_{\Pmod_t}(\mathbf x)\,\Oz=q(t)\,\Oz=\Den(\mathbf x)$ in the rational case,
and $\Den_{\Pmod_t}(\mathbf x)\,\Oz=q(t)\,t\,\BR\subsetneq q(t)\,\Pi=\Den(\mathbf x)$
otherwise.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Only the second case needs an argument. The extension $(t\R[t])\Oz$ contains $rt$
for real $r$ and $tu$ for $u\in\Pi$, hence $t\BR$; conversely a product of an
element of $t\R[t]$ and an omnific integer lies in $t\BR$. For strictness choose
$\varepsilon=\tfrac12\ddeg(t)>0$: then $\omega^\varepsilon\in\Pi$, but
$\omega^\varepsilon/t$ has negative leading exponent, so it is a nonzero
infinitesimal outside $\BR$. Multiplication by $q(t)$ preserves the strict
inclusion.
\end{proof}

Enlarging the available scales and recomputing a denominator ideal can thus
reveal denominators not generated by those of the smaller model. The primitive
or nonprimitive answer is stable, but the module of all denominators is not
obtained by extending scalars \src{08}.

In the rest of this subsection $m\ge2$, the rings are $\Pmod[T]\subseteq\Pmod[U]$
with $T=U^m$, embedded in $\Oz$ by $T=\omega$ and $U=\omega^{1/m}$, and
$I_T=T\R[T]$, $I_U=U\R[U]$.

\begin{theorem}[Scale-extension defect \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:thm:scale}
Let $p_1,\ldots,p_n,q\in\R[T]$ have gcd one, $q\ne0$, $\mathbf f=(p_j/q)_j$ and
$\mathbf s=[p_1(0):\cdots:p_n(0):q(0)]$. If $\mathbf s\in\mathbb P^n(\Q)$, then
$\Den_{\Pmod[U]}(\mathbf f(U^m))=\Den_{\Pmod[T]}(\mathbf f(T))\,\Pmod[U]$. If
$\mathbf s\notin\mathbb P^n(\Q)$, then
\[
 \Den_{\Pmod[U]}(\mathbf f(U^m))=q(U^m)\,U\R[U],\qquad
 \Den_{\Pmod[T]}(\mathbf f(T))\,\Pmod[U]=q(U^m)\,U^m\R[U],
\]
and division by $q(U^m)$ induces an isomorphism of the quotient of the first by
the second with $U\R[U]/U^m\R[U]$, which has the real basis
$U,U^2,\ldots,U^{m-1}$ and real dimension $m-1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A polynomial B\'ezout identity persists under $T=U^m$, and the constant vector is
unchanged; apply \cref{odg:frac:thm:pullback} in both polynomial rings. In the
rational case both ideals are the same principal ideal. Otherwise they are
$q(T)I_T$ and $q(U^m)I_U$, and $I_T\Pmod[U]=U^m\R[U]$: the inclusion $\subseteq$ is
by degrees, and every $rU^m$ comes from $rT$, while multiplication by $U^k$ gives
the higher monomials. Multiplication by the nonzero polynomial $q(U^m)$
identifies $U\R[U]$ with the new denominator ideal and $U^m\R[U]$ with
the extended old one. Its inverse therefore induces the stated quotient
isomorphism, which is $\Pmod[U]$-linear. Every class has a unique
representative $\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}r_jU^j$, giving its real basis.
\end{proof}

The dimension counts real coefficient layers, not module length \src{08}.
The $\Pmod[U]$-action on the displayed quotient is multiplication followed
by truncation modulo $U^m$. In fact this module has infinite length
\mergetag: on its top layer $\R U^{m-1}$, positive-degree terms of
$\Pmod[U]$ act as zero and constants act as integers. Thus
\[
 \Z U^{m-1}\subsetneq\tfrac12\Z U^{m-1}
 \subsetneq\tfrac14\Z U^{m-1}\subsetneq\cdots
\]
is a strictly increasing chain of $\Pmod[U]$-submodules. Finite real
dimension here does not imply finite module length.

\begin{theorem}[An integral extension that is neither finite nor flat \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:thm:nonflat}
For every $m\ge2$ the inclusion $\Pmod[T]\hookrightarrow\Pmod[U]$ is integral, but
$\Pmod[U]$ is neither finitely generated nor flat as a $\Pmod[T]$-module.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Each $rU^k$ with $k\ge1$ is a root of the monic polynomial
$X^m-r^mT^k$ over $\Pmod[T]$: its constant term belongs to $I_T$ even
when $r$ is not an integer. An element of $\Pmod[U]$ is an integer plus
a finite sum of such monomials. Adjoining these finitely many integral
elements gives a finite module, spanned by products of bounded powers;
the determinant argument then gives a monic equation for their sum.
This proves integrality. If $\Pmod[U]$ were a finite $\Pmod[T]$-module,
then
$\Pmod[U]/I_T\Pmod[U]\cong\Z\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{m-1}\R U^j$ would be finitely
generated over $\Pmod[T]/I_T=\Z$, which it is not: its projection onto
the coefficient of $U$ is the uncountable group $(\R,+)$.

For flatness put $r=\sqrt2$ and consider the row
$(rT,T):\Pmod[T]^2\to\Pmod[T]$. The equation $rTa+Tb=0$ implies
$b=-ra$ by cancellation of $T\ne0$. For $a\in\Pmod[T]$, both $a$ and
$ra$ belong to that ring exactly when its integer constant $\ct(a)$ is
zero, since $r$ is irrational. Thus the old kernel is
$L=\{(h,-rh):h\in I_T\}$. After substitution $T=U^m$, the identical
argument gives the new kernel $\{(h,-rh):h\in I_U\}$.
The image of $L\otimes_{\Pmod[T]}\Pmod[U]$ in $\Pmod[U]^2$ consists of
finite sums of old relations multiplied by new scalars, hence is exactly
$\{(h,-rh):h\in I_T\Pmod[U]=U^m\R[U]\}$.
The relation $(U,-rU)$ is in the new kernel but not in this image because
$m\ge2$. Flatness would make these two modules equal by preservation of
exactness at the middle of the row sequence
\cite[Tag 00HK]{StacksFlat}. Therefore $\Pmod[U]$ is not flat.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The defect as a Tor group \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:prop:tor}
Let $J=(\sqrt2T,T)\subseteq\Pmod[T]$ and $C=\Pmod[T]/J$. Then
$\Tor_1^{\Pmod[T]}(C,\Pmod[U])\cong U\R[U]/U^m\R[U]$ as $\Pmod[U]$-modules.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Put $A_0=\Pmod[T]$ and $A_m=\Pmod[U]$ for this proof, with the specified
embedding $T\mapsto U^m$. From $0\to J\to A_0\to C\to0$, the long exact
sequence of $\Tor$ and $\Tor_1^{A_0}(A_0,A_m)=0$ identify
\[
 \Tor_1^{A_0}(C,A_m)
   \cong\ker(J\otimes_{A_0}A_m\longrightarrow A_m)
\]
(see \cite[Section 10.75]{StacksFlat}). Let
$L=\ker((\sqrt2T,T):A_0^2\to J)$. Right exactness of tensoring gives
\[
 J\otimes_{A_0}A_m\cong
 A_m^2/\operatorname{im}(L\otimes_{A_0}A_m\longrightarrow A_m^2).
\]
Under this presentation the map to $A_m$ is induced by the row
$(\sqrt2U^m,U^m)$. Its kernel is therefore the new relation module
modulo the image of the old one, both computed in
\cref{odg:frac:thm:nonflat}. Taking the first coordinate identifies this
quotient with $I_U/(I_TA_m)=U\R[U]/U^m\R[U]$, $A_m$-linearly.
In particular $(U,-\sqrt2U)$ represents a nonzero class. This argument
uses right exactness, which holds without flatness, and does not assume
that $L\otimes_{A_0}A_m\to A_m^2$ is injective.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Scope of the scale computations \src{08}; \mergetag]\label{odg:frac:rem:cores}
Integrality, nonflatness and non-finiteness of pullback extensions are familiar;
the point is the exact denominator-sensitive defect realized by a finer surreal
monomial, and the same $m-1$ layers witness the failure of flatness. All tensor
products and $\Tor$ groups here are over set-sized rings \src{08}. The tower
$\Pmod[T]\subseteq\Pmod[T^{1/2}]\subseteq\Pmod[T^{1/6}]\subseteq\cdots$ (exponents
$1/m!$) has union the set-sized core $A_1\cong T_\Q$ of the sibling report
(\replabel{osq:hd:lem:cores}), with $(D,K)=(\Z,\R)$. The steps have exponent
group $\Z$ and are not among the cores $T_\Gamma$ there, which need a divisible
$\Gamma$; their ideals $I_T$ are not idempotent ($I_T/I_T^2\cong\R$), whereas
$\Pi^{\mathrm{fin}}_\Gamma$ is. No statement of that report is contradicted.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Monomial denominators and fixed workspaces}\label{odg:frac:sub:monomial}

For $x\in\No$ let $\Ex(x)=\{\beta\in\No:\beta\ge0,\ \omega^\beta x\in\Oz\}$, the
class of exponents of monic monomial denominators. The corresponding
denominators $\omega^\beta$ have coefficient $1$; ordinary integers such as
$2$ are not of this form. Source 08 writes $M(x)$
and source 09 $E(x)$.

\begin{proposition}[Monomial denominators \src{08, 09}]\label{odg:frac:prop:monomial}
Let $x=\sum_{\gamma\in S}r_\gamma\omega^\gamma\ne0$ and $r_\eta=0$ for
$\eta\notin S$. Then $\beta\in\Ex(x)$ if and only if $\beta\ge0$,
$\gamma+\beta\ge0$ for all $\gamma\in S$, and $r_{-\beta}\in\Z$. The class
$\Ex(x)$ is nonempty and upward closed, and $\omega^{\beta'}x\in\Pi$ whenever
$\beta'>\beta\in\Ex(x)$. It has a least member exactly when $x\in\Oz$ (least
member $0$) or $S$ has a negative least element $a$ with $r_a\in\Z$ (least
member $-a$). Also $\Ex(0)=\No^{\ge0}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Multiplication by $\omega^\beta$ shifts supports by $\beta$ and keeps
coefficients, which gives the criterion; nonemptiness is \cref{odg:thm:fractions},
and a strictly larger exponent makes every shifted exponent positive. Let
$\beta>0$ be least. If the shifted support were entirely positive, a positive
$\varepsilon$ below $\beta$ and below every shifted exponent, which exists by
\cref{odg:lem:setbounds}, would make $\beta-\varepsilon$ admissible. So $0$ is
in the shifted support and is its minimum; hence $-\beta$ is a negative least
element of $S$ with integral coefficient. Conversely such an element makes $-a$
admissible and forbids smaller exponents. The case $\beta=0$ is $x\in\Oz$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Monomial and algebraic minimization differ \src{08, 09}]\label{odg:frac:ex:monomial}
(i) The rational $1/2$ has least omnific denominator $2$, but
$\Ex(1/2)=\{\beta:\beta>0\}$ has no least member \src{08}. (ii) The number
$x=\tfrac12\omega^{-1}=1/(2\omega)$ has least positive denominator $2\omega$, by
the unimodular pair $(1,2\omega)$, but $\Ex(x)=\{\beta:\beta>1\}$, since at
$\beta=1$ the numerator is $1/2$ \src{09}. (iii) For irrational $r$,
$\Ex(r)=\{\beta:\beta>0\}$, and here even unrestricted denominators have no least
member (\cref{odg:frac:thm:realconstants}) \src{09}. (iv) The geometric series
$x=1/(\omega-1)=\sum_{n\ge1}\omega^{-n}$ has least positive denominator
$\omega-1$, but $\Ex(x)$ is the class of $\beta$ exceeding every ordinary integer,
with no least member: if $\beta$ exceeds every ordinary integer, so does
$\beta-1$ \src{08}. The unrestricted least denominator follows from the
unimodular pair $(1,\omega-1)$. Straightforward monomial clearing therefore needs
an exponent above every integer, where a two-term denominator of leading exponent
$1$ suffices; support bounds cannot replace denominator-ideal arithmetic, and a
preferred monomial denominator need not be the arithmetically preferred one.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[One fixed workspace \src{08, 09}]\label{odg:frac:ex:workspace}
Let $K=\R((\omega^{-1}))$, the Laurent series in $\omega^{-1}$. A
reverse-well-ordered set of nonnegative integers is finite, so
$K\cap\Oz=\Pmod_\omega=\Z+\omega\R[\omega]$, and
\[
 \{\omega^n:n\in\N\}^{-1}\Pmod_\omega=\R[\omega,\omega^{-1}]
 \ \subsetneq\ \Frac(\Pmod_\omega)=\R(\omega)\ \subsetneq\ K .
\]
For the localization equality, multiplying any Laurent polynomial by a
sufficiently high power of $\omega$ makes every exponent positive,
putting it in $\omega\R[\omega]$ even if its coefficients are not integral.
Conversely dividing an element of $\Pmod_\omega$ by a power of $\omega$
gives a Laurent polynomial. The fraction-field equality follows from
$r=(r\omega)/\omega$ for every $r\in\R$ and the reverse containment
$\Pmod_\omega\subseteq\R(\omega)$.
The first inclusion is strict because $1/(\omega-1)=\sum_{n\ge1}\omega^{-n}$ is not
a Laurent polynomial \src{09}. The second is strict by either of two elements.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=*]
\item $E=\sum_{n\ge0}\omega^{-n}/n!$ \src{08}. If $\sum_nz^n/n!$ were rational, a
nonzero $\sum_{j=0}^dq_jz^j$ would give $\sum_jq_j/(n-j)!=0$ for all large $n$;
multiplying by $n!$ for $n\ge d$ gives
$\sum_jq_jn(n-1)\cdots(n-j+1)=0$ for infinitely many $n$.
This polynomial is zero, and the falling factorials have distinct degrees
and leading coefficient one, forcing every $q_j=0$, a contradiction.
\item $F=\sum_{n\ge1}\omega^{-n!}$ \src{09}. If $F=A(u)/B(u)$ in $u=\omega^{-1}$ with
$B(0)\ne0$ after cancellation (the series is regular at zero), write
$B=\sum_{j=0}^d b_j u^j$ and $F=\sum c_nu^n$. Comparing coefficients
in $BF=A$ gives
$b_0c_n=-\sum_{j=1}^d b_jc_{n-j}$ for $n>\deg A$, with $n\ge d$.
Since $b_0\ne0$, a block of $d$ consecutive zero coefficients beyond
this bound forces all later ones to vanish by induction. The factorial
gaps give such blocks followed by a coefficient $1$, a contradiction.
A constant $B$ would make $F$ a polynomial, also impossible.
\end{enumerate}
In $\No$ the monomial $\omega^\omega$ clears $F$, with exponents $\omega-n!>0$, but
$\omega^\omega\notin K$, since $K$ has only integer exponents \src{09}.
\end{example}

The example is consistent with source 06's non-claim that the fraction field of a
fixed Hahn ring need not be the whole Hahn field ($\Gamma=\Z$ gives
$\Bbbk(\omega)$), and with the cofinality criterion of L'Innocente--Mantova
\cite[Proposition 2.4.5]{LM}. The small common divisor of
\cref{odg:thm:commondivisor} may likewise need an exponent outside a workspace.
Even where the needed shifts exist, the dichotomy in a workspace requires a
unimodular pair over its nonnegative-support ring; the polynomial B\'ezout
certificate of \cref{odg:frac:thm:main} supplies it, and a formal cancellation in
an ambient field does not \src{09}.

\subsection{Gaussian omnific fractions}\label{odg:frac:sub:gaussian}

Here $\BC=\C\oplus\Pi_{\C}$ and $\Og=\Z[i]\oplus\Pi_{\C}$ (\cref{odg:def:rings}),
with $\ct:\BC\to\C$ a retraction and $\Og=\ct^{-1}(\Z[i])$.

\begin{proposition}[Gaussian fractions \src{08}; \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:prop:gaussianfrac}
$\Og[(\Z\setminus\{0\})^{-1}]=\Q(i)\oplus\Pi_{\C}$. The units of $\Og$ are
$\pm1,\pm i$, and $\Pi_{\C}$ is divisible by every nonzero ordinary integer and
not generated by any set as an ideal of $\Og$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Clearing the two rational constants gives the first identity. By
\cref{odg:lem:degree} a unit of $\BC$ is a complex constant, and the units of
$\Z[i]$ are the four displayed. Division by a nonzero integer preserves
positive support. For a set of proposed generators, the union of their
real and imaginary supports is a set of positive exponents. Choose
$\varepsilon>0$ strictly below every exponent in this union, using
\cref{odg:lem:setbounds}. Multiplication by an element of $\Og$ cannot
introduce exponents below $\varepsilon$, since its exponents are
nonnegative; neither can a finite sum. Thus $\omega^{\varepsilon/2}$
lies outside the generated ideal, proving the last assertion.
\end{proof}

The identity $\Frac(\Og)=\No[i]$, proved by sources 08 and 09 through clearing
the real and imaginary parts with one monomial, is already stated with
source 06 in \cref{odg:def:sec:reconstruction}, and the units of $\Og$ are
recorded in \cref{odg:def:sub:setting}. Source 09 gives no complex lowest-term
classification; source 08 does.

\begin{theorem}[Gaussian representation dichotomy \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:thm:gaussian}
Let $t\in\Pi$ be nonzero and let $p_1,\ldots,p_n,q\in\C[T]$, $q\ne0$, have gcd
one. \Cref{odg:frac:thm:main} holds with $(\R,\Z,\Q,\BR,\Oz,\Pi)$ replaced by
$(\C,\Z[i],\Q(i),\BC,\Og,\Pi_{\C})$. In particular primitive homogeneous
coordinates exist exactly when $[p_1(0):\cdots:p_n(0):q(0)]\in\mathbb P^n(\Q(i))$;
they are then unimodular and unique up to a Gaussian unit. Otherwise no
representation has a gcd, and the denominator ideal is $q(t)\,\Pi_{\C}$, with no
set of generators.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Evaluation at $t$ is injective on $\C[T]$: a nonzero polynomial of degree
$d$ has leading exponent $d\,\ddeg(t)$ and nonzero leading complex
coefficient. In particular $q(t)\ne0$. The evaluated vector is unimodular
over $\BC$ by a complex polynomial B\'ezout identity, and the identity
forces every ambient scalar giving Gaussian omnific coordinates into
$\BC$. For a nonzero complex residue vector $\mathbf v$, the
complex $s$ with $s\mathbf v\in\Z[i]^{n+1}$ are $0$ unless the line is defined over
$\Q(i)$. In that case choose $\lambda\in\C^\times$ so that
$\lambda\mathbf v$ is a primitive Gaussian integral vector. Replace all
polynomials by their $\lambda$ multiples and the B\'ezout row by its
$\lambda^{-1}$ multiple. Since $\Z[i]$ is Euclidean, the normalized
constant vector has a Gaussian B\'ezout identity; as in
\cref{odg:frac:lem:lattice}, its admissible constant scalars are exactly
$\Z[i]$. This description is for the normalized vector; before
normalization the set is $\lambda\Z[i]$.
Formula \eqref{odg:frac:eq:lift} gives a Gaussian omnific B\'ezout certificate.
The normalized scalar sets are therefore $\Og$ or $\Pi_{\C}$. In the
first case the lifted identity shows that a common divisor of the
coordinates of a scalar multiple divides that scalar; primitive multiples
are precisely unit multiples. The four units give the stated uniqueness.
In the second case every representing tuple has all entries in $\Pi_{\C}$.
If $d$ is any common divisor, dividing by $d$ gives another representing
tuple, so its entries again lie in $\Pi_{\C}$ by the scalar
classification. They remain Gaussian omnific after division by two.
Thus $2d$ is still a common divisor, but $2d\mid d$ would require
$1/2\in\Og$, a contradiction to the existence of a gcd.
The generator assertion is \cref{odg:frac:prop:gaussianfrac}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Constant directions over $\Z$ and $\Z{[i]}$ \src{08, 14}]\label{odg:frac:cor:gaussiandirections}
Let $(D,k,A_D)$ be $(\Z,\R,\Oz)$ or $(\Z[i],\C,\Og)$. For a point
$[r_0:\cdots:r_n]\in\mathbb P^n(k)$ the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=*]
\item it lies in $\mathbb P^n(\Frac D)$;
\item it has a unimodular representative in $A_D^{\,n+1}$;
\item it has a primitive representative in $A_D^{\,n+1}$.
\end{enumerate}
The primitive representatives are then the unit multiples of one primitive
tuple in $D^{n+1}$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Choose a nonzero coordinate and permute it into the denominator position.
Apply \cref{odg:frac:thm:gaussian} (or \cref{odg:frac:thm:main} for $D=\Z$)
to these constant polynomials: the chosen denominator is nonzero, and
their polynomial gcd is one because at least one constant is nonzero.
In the rational-direction case normalization gives a primitive tuple in
$D^{n+1}$, and the classification gives exactly its unit multiples as
primitive representatives. Otherwise no representative has a gcd.
Undoing the permutation preserves every divisibility and B\'ezout property.
\end{proof}

\status{The real case is \cref{odg:thm:realray} of source 01. Manuscript 14 of
the sibling report proves the criterion (i)$\Leftrightarrow$(ii) for both pairs
$(D,k)$, using only that $D$ is a principal ideal domain: taking constants in a
B\'ezout identity makes the constant multiplier nonzero, and a primitive tuple
over $D$ has a B\'ezout identity over $D$. That report records that the Gaussian
and principal-ideal form is printed here with \cref{odg:thm:realray}; this
corollary is that statement. Manuscript 14 notes that the criterion concerns
coordinate representatives, not all points of a projective scheme over a ring,
and it credits only the example $[1:\sqrt2]$ of the present report, not
\cref{odg:thm:realray}.}

For constants $z\in\C$, ordinary Gaussian rationality $z\in\Q(i)$ is thus exactly
the existence of a primitive Gaussian omnific fraction, although Gaussian
omnific fractions produce every surcomplex number \src{08}. The localization of
$\Og$ at the elements with nonzero constant has residue field $\Q(i)$, and on its
finite part (both real and imaginary coordinates finite), the complex
standard part and Gaussian-rational residue are prescribed independently
in $\C\times\Q(i)$ by $(zt+w)/(t+1)$ with $z\in\C$, $w\in\Q(i)$ and
$0<t\in\Pi$. A common nonzero ordinary denominator for the two parts
of $w$ puts this fraction in the localization. Its constant-term residue
is $w$, while subtracting $z$ leaves $(w-z)/(t+1)$ with infinitesimal
real and imaginary parts. This is the proof of
\cref{odg:frac:thm:tworesidues} coordinatewise \src{08}.

\subsection{What is and is not classified}\label{odg:frac:sub:scope}

The slogan that all surreals are rational over the omnific integers is correct
only for membership in the fraction field. It does not give ordinary rationality,
lowest terms, or a rational specialization map \src{08}. The results classify
every tuple in $\R(t)$ and $\C(t)$ for $0\ne t\in\Pi$, and every point of
$\Vreg$. They do not classify $\Den(x)$ for arbitrary $x\in\No$, and they do not
show that $\Vreg=\mathbb P^1(\No)$; density is weaker than equality, as the
disjoint dense fibers show \src{09}. A multivariate polynomial gcd of one does
not give a B\'ezout identity ($T_1$ and $T_2$ have gcd one in $\R[T_1,T_2]$ but
generate an ideal annihilated by evaluation at $(0,0)$), so the proofs
do not extend directly to several
parameters \src{08}. The scale computation is one explicit test case, not a
general flatness or normalization theorem for Hahn integer parts \src{08}. The
questions left by the two sources are
\cref{odg:frac:q:ideals,odg:frac:q:region,odg:frac:q:orbits,odg:frac:q:parameters,odg:frac:q:scales}.

\section{Differential rigidity over one-sided Hahn rings}\label{odg:cr:sec:principle}

This section and the next two form the part \emph{Curve and differential
rigidity}. They are built from five manuscripts written independently on
September 23, 2026, tagged \src{C10}--\src{C14} after their file prefixes
\fn{10-} to \fn{14-} (\cref{odg:sub:provenance}); the tags \src{13} and
\src{14} without the letter keep their meaning as manuscripts of the sibling
report. All five prove the same spine. A smooth geometrically integral affine
curve with constant coefficients has nonconstant points over a one-sided Hahn
ring with nonzero exponent group exactly when it is the affine line
(\cref{odg:cr:thm:affine}); in particular every omnific solution of a
nonsingular Weierstrass equation is an ordinary integer solution
(\cref{odg:cr:cor:weierstrass}). The elementary route gives a B\'ezout
certificate for squarefree equations $y^m=P(x)$. The geometric
\emph{two-ring} argument treats more general targets: properness and the
Euler derivations force the contractions of every global differential
form at a point to vanish. Source
C14 is the base because it has the weakest hypotheses on the shared theorems:
any coefficient field of characteristic zero and any ordered exponent group.
For the affine-curve classification, sources C10 and C12 use an
algebraically closed field, C11 an algebraically closed field or $\R$,
and C13 the fields $\R$, $\C$. The abstract proper-rigidity results of
C11 and C13 already allow every characteristic-zero coefficient field;
C13 assumes a divisible exponent group. The results answer \cref{odg:q:affine} for
smooth curves and its Weierstrass test completely, and they answer
\cref{odg:q:search,odg:q:homogeneous} in part (\cref{odg:cr:sub:settled}).
They contain \cref{odg:thm:separated,odg:cor:mordell} as special cases.
Labels of this material carry the sub-prefix \fn{odg:cr:}. The proof review in
\fn{RECONCILIATION.md} covers this section's ring and Euler prerequisites,
squarefree certificate and two-ring differential principle, through symmetric
forms and inheritance. It also covers the curve classification in
\cref{odg:cr:sub:positivegenus,odg:cr:sub:affine}, including arbitrary coefficient
descent and the logarithmic proof, and the arithmetic fibers, polynomial witnesses
and separated-model descent in \cref{odg:cr:sub:arith}. It also covers projective
coordinates and their ideal obstruction in \cref{odg:cr:sub:projective}.
The review also covers \cref{odg:cr:sub:singular} and the singular-curve
criterion through \cref{odg:sg:cor:fibre}. The repeated-root and later
geometric applications remain outside it; imported foundations are identified
separately.
A sixth manuscript, source C15
(prefix \fn{15-}), reproves much of the part independently and extends the two-ring
argument to logarithmic forms in every dimension; its new results form
\cref{odg:log:sub:log}, with the sub-prefix \fn{odg:log:}, and its other results are
credited where they are printed. A seventh, source C16 (prefix \fn{16-}), extends the curve
classification to singular curves; its new results form \cref{odg:sg:sub:singular}, with
the sub-prefix \fn{odg:sg:}, and its other results are credited where they are printed.

\subsection{Conventions}\label{odg:cr:sub:conventions}

Throughout the part, $\Bbbk$ is a field of characteristic zero, $\bar\Bbbk$ an
algebraic closure, and $\Gamma$ a set-sized ordered abelian group; $\Gamma$ is
\emph{not} assumed divisible, and $\Gamma_\Q=\Gamma\otimes_\Z\Q$ is its ordered
divisible hull, into which $\Gamma$ embeds because it is torsion free. We use the
$\omega$-convention and the rings of \cref{odg:def:sub:setting}: the series
$\sum_\gamma a_\gamma\omega^\gamma$ with $a_\gamma\in\Bbbk$ and reverse-well-ordered
support form the Hahn field $\crH$, and inside it
\begin{align*}
 \crA&=\{f:\supp f\subseteq\Gamma^{\ge0}\},&
 \crP&=\{f:\supp f\subseteq\Gamma^{>0}\},\\
 \crO&=\{f:\supp f\subseteq\Gamma^{\le0}\},&
 \crm&=\{f:\supp f\subseteq\Gamma^{<0}\}.
\end{align*}
For $0\ne f\in\crH$ the valuation is $v(f)=-\ddeg f$, with $v(0)=+\infty$; so
$\crO$ is its valuation ring and $\crm$ the maximal ideal. For a subring
$\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$ the arithmetic ring is
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)=\mathfrak o+\crP$. In the surreal instances
$\Gamma\subseteq\No$, $\crA$ is the part of $\BR$ or $\BC$ supported in $\Gamma$,
$\mathcal R_\Z(\R,\Gamma)=\Oz_\Gamma$. For $\Bbbk=\R$, $\crO$ is the
workspace form of the finite surreals $\Ofin$, with $\crm$ its infinitesimals;
for $\Bbbk=\C$, both real and imaginary coordinates must be finite,
and both are infinitesimal in $\crm$. The two rings $\crA$
and $\crO$ point in opposite support directions. For $\Gamma\ne0$ they
are distinct: $\crA$ is not the valuation ring or the ring of finite
elements (\cref{odg:rem:notvaluation}). For $\Gamma=0$ both reduce to
the coefficient field.

\paragraph{The $t$-convention of three sources.} Sources C11, C12 and C14 write
$\Bbbk((t^\Gamma))$ with well-ordered supports and $t^\gamma=\omega^{-\gamma}$, as
in \cref{odg:sub:notation}. Their ring $B_\Gamma(k)$ of series supported on
$\Gamma_{\le0}$ is $\crA$ with exponents negated, their ideal of series supported
on $\Gamma_{<0}$ ($P_\Gamma(k)$ in C11, $I$ in C12) is $\crP$, and their
valuation ring ($\mathcal O$ in C11 and C14, $V$ in C12) is $\crO$. Sources C10
and C13 use the $\omega$-convention of this report. Every statement below is
translated; no normalization changes.

\paragraph{Points and the class convention.} For a $\Bbbk$-scheme $X$ and a
commutative $\Bbbk$-algebra $S$, $X(S)=\Hom_\Bbbk(\Spec S,X)$. A point is
\emph{constant} if it comes from $X(\Bbbk)$. The retraction $\ct$ acts on points
functorially. All schemes, modules of differentials and valuation rings in this
part are set-sized. For the class rings $\BR$, $\BC$, $\Oz$ and $\Og$ we never
form a spectrum: an affine point is a finite tuple satisfying the equations,
and for a separated $\Bbbk$-scheme of finite type a point over $\BR$ means a point
over some $\mathcal A_\R(\Gamma)$, $\Gamma\subseteq\No$ set-sized, two such being
identified after passage to a common larger workspace \src{C10, C12, C13}. A
projective point over a ring is given by an invertible quotient; its finite
presentation data, like a unit-ideal witness, fit into one workspace \src{C12}.
By \cref{odg:lem:workspace} every finite datum lies in some $\mathcal A_\R(\Gamma)$
or $\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma)$, and the theorems below hold for arbitrary $\Gamma$
subject to their stated hypotheses, so
either the rational span of the supports \src{C10, C12, C13} or the group they
generate \src{C11, C14} may be used.

\paragraph{Letters.} In this part $\partial_\lambda$ is an Euler derivation (the
sources' $D_\lambda$) and $\mathcal D$ a family of derivations; $C$ is a curve,
$\overline C$ its smooth projective completion, $\Sigma=\overline C\setminus C$ its
boundary and $g$ the genus; $A$ is an abelian variety (not an intermediate ring
as in \cref{odg:def:sec:arithmetic}), $G$ an algebraic group, $T$ a torus and $U$
a unipotent group; $S$ is a commutative $\Bbbk$-algebra of coefficients (as
elsewhere, a set-sized ring); $\langle\mathbf a\rangle$ is the ideal generated by
the coordinates of a tuple $\mathbf a$. A calligraphic $\mathcal O$ with a scheme
as subscript is a structure sheaf, while $\crO$ is the valuation ring above. The
sources write the valuation ring with a macro \texttt{\textbackslash OO} (source C12
writes $V$), affine space with \texttt{\textbackslash A}, and use
\texttt{\textbackslash dd} for the degree (C10) or for the differential (C11, C13).
In this report \texttt{\textbackslash OO} is the orthogonal group
$\OO$ and \texttt{\textbackslash V} a zero set $\V$, and the omnific groups report
uses \texttt{\textbackslash A} for a ring; so the new material writes $\crO$,
$\Aff$, $\ddeg$ and $\dif$ instead. The complete list of renamed symbols is in
\cref{odg:tab:renames}.

\subsection{Two opposite rings in one Hahn field}\label{odg:cr:sub:rings}

\begin{lemma}[Intersections, units and degree \src{C14; also C10--C13, C15--C17}]\label{odg:cr:lem:rings}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)},leftmargin=*]
\item $\crA$ is a $\Bbbk$-subalgebra of $\crH$, $\crP$ is an ideal of it,
$\crA=\Bbbk\oplus\crP$, and $\ct$ is a ring retraction of $\crA$ onto $\Bbbk$ with
kernel $\crP$. Likewise $\crO=\Bbbk\oplus\crm$ is the valuation ring of $v$, with
fraction field $\crH$, maximal ideal $\crm$ and residue map $\ct$.
\item $\crA\cap\crO=\Bbbk$, $\crA\cap\crm=\{0\}$ and $\crP\cap\crO=\{0\}$.
\item $\crA^\times=\Bbbk^\times$, and for a subring $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$,
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)\cap\Bbbk=\mathfrak o$ and
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)^\times=\mathfrak o^\times$.
\item If $P\in\Bbbk[X]$ has degree $d\ge1$ and $x\in\crA\setminus\Bbbk$, then
$P(x)\ne0$ and $\ddeg P(x)=d\,\ddeg x$. Every element of $\crA$ algebraic over
$\Bbbk$ lies in $\Bbbk$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Parts (a), (c) for $\crA$ and (d) are \cref{odg:def:lem:units} and its proof.
On $\crO$, addition and multiplication preserve nonpositive support;
in a product exponent zero can arise only from two zero exponents.
Thus $\ct$ is a ring homomorphism onto $\Bbbk$, with kernel $\crm$.
For a nonzero $f\in\crH$, $v(f)\ge0$ means $f\in\crO$, while
$v(f)<0$ implies $v(f^{-1})>0$ and $f^{-1}\in\crm$.
This proves the valuation-ring property and also $\Frac\crO=\crH$:
in the latter case $f=1/f^{-1}$. A nonzero $f\in\crO$ is a unit
exactly when $v(f)=0$, since $v(f^{-1})=-v(f)$. Its nonunits are
therefore precisely $\crm$, which is the maximal ideal, and
$\crO/\crm\cong\Bbbk$ via $\ct$.
Part (b) compares supports: a support in $\Gamma^{\ge0}\cap\Gamma^{\le0}$ is
contained in $\{0\}$, and one in $\Gamma^{\ge0}\cap\Gamma^{<0}$ or
$\Gamma^{>0}\cap\Gamma^{\le0}$ is empty. A unit of
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ is a unit of $\crA$, hence a nonzero
constant, and a constant lies in $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ exactly
when it lies in $\mathfrak o$; this gives (c).
\end{proof}

For $\Gamma\ne0$, the ring $\crA$ is not a valuation ring. To see this,
choose $\gamma>0$ and put $q=\omega^\gamma/(1+\omega^\gamma)$.
Both $q$ and $q^{-1}=1+\omega^{-\gamma}$ have degree zero and are
nonconstant, whereas every degree-zero element of $\crA$ is constant.
Neither belongs to $\crA$, violating the valuation-ring criterion.
If $\Gamma=0$, instead $\crA=\crO=\crH=\Bbbk$ is a field and hence
a valuation ring. Nothing below assumes that $\crA$ is
Noetherian, normal, a gcd domain, or that $\Frac\crA=\crH$
\src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14}. It is not integrally closed in general
(\cref{odg:cr:sub:singular}). For $\Gamma=\Z$ it is the polynomial ring
$\Bbbk[\omega]$ (\cref{odg:def:sub:setting}); for $\Gamma=\Q$ it contains
$\sum_{n\ge1}\omega^{1/n}$, whose support has no least element, and for
lexicographically ordered $\Q^2$ it contains $\sum_{n\ge1}\omega^{(1,1/n)}$, whose
fine structure lies below the first Archimedean scale \src{C11, C12, C13, C14}.
No proof below replaces such a support by a finite truncation.

\subsection{Euler derivations on both rings}\label{odg:cr:sub:euler}

For a $\Q$-linear map $\lambda:\Gamma_\Q\to\Bbbk$, equivalently an additive map
$\Gamma\to(\Bbbk,+)$, put
\begin{equation}\label{odg:cr:eq:euler}
 \partial_\lambda\Bigl(\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}a_\gamma\omega^\gamma\Bigr)
 =\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\lambda(\gamma)a_\gamma\omega^\gamma .
\end{equation}
For a divisible $\Gamma=H\subseteq\No$ and a real $\lambda$ this is
\eqref{odg:eq:euler}; here neither divisibility nor real values are needed, and
values in $\Q\subseteq\Bbbk$ already suffice for detection
\src{C11, C12, C13}. In the $t$-convention the rule reads
$\partial(t^\gamma)=\lambda(-\gamma)t^\gamma$; the sources C11, C12 and C14 define
their family by $t^\gamma\mapsto\lambda(\gamma)t^\gamma$, which is the same family
with $\lambda$ replaced by $-\lambda$. As in \cref{odg:sec:derivations}, these are
local, freely chosen derivations; they are not a canonical surreal derivation,
not the Berarducci--Mantova derivation, and not derivatives in the surreal
order topology \src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14}.

\begin{lemma}[Euler derivations on the two rings \src{C12, C13; also C10, C11, C14, C15, C16, C17}]\label{odg:cr:lem:euler}
Each $\partial=\partial_\lambda$ is a $\Bbbk$-derivation of $\crH$ with
$\supp\partial f\subseteq\supp f$, hence $v(\partial f)\ge v(f)$, and
\begin{equation}\label{odg:cr:eq:tworings}
 \partial\bigl(\crA\bigr)\subseteq\crP,\qquad
 \partial\bigl(\crO\bigr)\subseteq\crm .
\end{equation}
For $u\in\crH^\times$ one has $\partial u/u\in\crO$, and $\partial u/u\in\crm$
when $u\in\crO^\times$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A subset of a reverse-well-ordered support is reverse well ordered, so
\eqref{odg:cr:eq:euler} defines a series, and additivity is coefficientwise. At a
fixed exponent the product has finitely many contributions, and
$\lambda(\alpha+\beta)=\lambda(\alpha)+\lambda(\beta)$ gives the Leibniz rule
coefficientwise, as in the proof of \cref{odg:lem:derivation}; $\lambda(0)=0$
kills $\Bbbk$. Deleting the exponent $0$ from a support in $\Gamma^{\ge0}$ leaves one
in $\Gamma^{>0}$, and from a support in $\Gamma^{\le0}$ one in $\Gamma^{<0}$; this is
\eqref{odg:cr:eq:tworings}. Finally $v(\partial u/u)=v(\partial u)-v(u)\ge0$, and if
$v(u)=0$ then $\partial u\in\crm$ by \eqref{odg:cr:eq:tworings}, so
$\partial u/u\in\crm$ \src{C12}.
\end{proof}

The stronger support inclusion $\partial(\crA)\subseteq\crP$ is proved by sources C11, C12
and C13, and appears already in the collection, as a derivation
$\Oz\to\Pi$ in \replabel{osq:prop:classder} and as a map
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(k,\Gamma)\to\Pi_k(\Gamma)$ in \replabel{opa:par:prop:Euler}.
Sources C10 and C14 use only $\partial(\crA)\subseteq\crA$. The inclusion
$\partial(\crO)\subseteq\crm$ on the \emph{opposite} ring is the new ingredient of
all five sources; it is the bridge to properness.

These image inclusions need not be proper. For example, when $\Gamma=\Z$
and $\lambda(n)=n$, the ring $\crA=\Bbbk[\omega]$ has
$\partial(\crA)=\omega\Bbbk[\omega]=\crP$: the monomial
$b\omega^n$, $n>0$, is the derivative of $(b/n)\omega^n$.
Likewise $v(\partial f)\ge v(f)$ need not be a strict inequality.
For any $u\ne0$, write $u=a\omega^\alpha(1+\varepsilon)$ with
$a\in\Bbbk^\times$, $\alpha=\ddeg u$ and $\varepsilon\in\crm$.
Leibniz gives the more precise formula
\[
 \frac{\partial_\lambda u}{u}
 =\lambda(\alpha)+\frac{\partial_\lambda\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon},
 \qquad
 \ct\!\left(\frac{\partial_\lambda u}{u}\right)=\lambda(\alpha).
\]
The second summand belongs to $\crm$, since its numerator does and its
denominator is a unit of $\crO$. This explains both logarithmic-derivative
clauses: an arbitrary monomial can give a nonzero constant, whereas a
unit of $\crO$ has $\alpha=0$ \mergetag.

\begin{lemma}[Joint constants and leading detection \src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14, C15, C16, C17}]\label{odg:cr:lem:separation}
The common kernel of all Euler derivations of $\crH$ is $\Bbbk$. If
$x\in\crA\setminus\Bbbk$ has $\ddeg x=\alpha$, there is $\lambda$ with
$\lambda(\alpha)=1$, and then $\partial_\lambda x\ne0$ and
$\ddeg\partial_\lambda x=\alpha$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $f$ has a nonzero coefficient at $\gamma\ne0$, extend $\{\gamma\}$ to a basis of
the $\Q$-vector space $\Gamma_\Q$ and let $\lambda$ be the coordinate functional
taking $\gamma$ to $1\in\Q\subseteq\Bbbk$; then $\partial_\lambda f$ has the same
nonzero coefficient at $\gamma$. Only choice for a set-sized vector space is used.
For the last assertion take $\gamma=\alpha$: no new exponent is created and the
leading coefficient survives. This is \cref{odg:lem:detection} for an arbitrary
$\Gamma$ and $\Bbbk$.
\end{proof}

As after \cref{odg:lem:detection}, a single Euler derivation can kill a
nonconstant series ($\lambda(a+b\sqrt2)=a$ kills $\omega^{\sqrt2}$ on
$\Q+\Q\sqrt2$); every constancy argument below uses the whole family
\src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14, C15}.

\begin{corollary}[Relatively algebraically closed constants \src{C10, C14, C15, C17}]\label{odg:cr:cor:algclosed}
Every element of $\crH$ algebraic over $\Bbbk$ lies in $\Bbbk$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Let $p$ be the minimal polynomial of $f$ over $\Bbbk$. In characteristic zero it
is separable, so $p'(f)\ne0$, and $0=\partial(p(f))=p'(f)\partial f$ for every
Euler derivation. More explicitly, coprimality of $p$ and $p'$ gives
$a p+b p'=1$ in $\Bbbk[X]$; evaluating at $f$ makes $p'(f)$ invertible.
The chain rule therefore forces $\partial f=0$ for every member of the
family. Thus $f$ is a joint constant, and
\cref{odg:cr:lem:separation} applies.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The squarefree certificate}\label{odg:cr:sub:squarefree}

\begin{lemma}[Differential division \src{C14; also C10, C11, C12, C13, C15}]\label{odg:cr:lem:division}
Let $m\ge1$ be an ordinary integer, let $P\in\Bbbk[X]$ be squarefree,
and choose $U,W\in\Bbbk[X]$ with
$UP+WP'=1$. In independent variables $x,y,\xi,\upsilon$ put
\[
 F=y^m-P(x),\quad G=my^{m-1}\upsilon-P'(x)\xi,\quad
 H=U(x)y\xi+mW(x)\upsilon .
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{odg:cr:eq:universal}
 \xi-y^{m-1}H+U(x)\xi F+W(x)G=0 .
\end{equation}
Consequently, if $x,y$ lie in a commutative $\Bbbk$-algebra with $y^m=P(x)$ and
$\partial$ is a $\Bbbk$-derivation of it,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:cr:eq:division}
 \partial x=y^{m-1}\bigl(U(x)y\,\partial x+mW(x)\,\partial y\bigr).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Characteristic zero makes $P$ coprime to $P'$, so such $U,W$ exist.
Expanding in $\Bbbk[x,y,\xi,\upsilon]$, the terms in $y^m\xi$ and in
$y^{m-1}\upsilon$ cancel and
$\xi\bigl(1-U(x)P(x)-W(x)P'(x)\bigr)=0$ remains. Substituting
$\xi=\partial x$, $\upsilon=\partial y$ makes $F$ and $G$ vanish, since the
derivative of $y^m=P(x)$ is $G=0$ \src{C12, C13}.
\end{proof}

The identity is a finite certificate in the ideal generated by the equation and
its derivative; it replaces the unique factorization or the quotient-ring step of
\cref{odg:thm:separated} and needs no cancellation in the ring
\src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14}.

\begin{theorem}[Squarefree superelliptic rigidity \src{C12, C13, C15; also C10, C11, C14}]\label{odg:cr:thm:squarefree}
Let $P\in\Bbbk[X]$ be squarefree of degree $d\ge2$ and $m\ge2$ an ordinary integer.
Every solution of $y^m=P(x)$ in $\crA^2$ lies in $\Bbbk^2$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $x\in\Bbbk$, then $y$ is algebraic over $\Bbbk$ and lies in $\Bbbk$ by
\cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}(d). Suppose $\alpha=\ddeg x>0$. By
\cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}(d), $P(x)\ne0$ has degree $d\alpha$, so $y\ne0$ and
$\beta=\ddeg y$ satisfies $m\beta=d\alpha$; in particular $\beta>0$. Choose
$\partial$ with $\ddeg\partial x=\alpha$ (\cref{odg:cr:lem:separation}) and put
$h=U(x)y\,\partial x+mW(x)\,\partial y$, so that $\partial x=y^{m-1}h$ by
\eqref{odg:cr:eq:division}. The factors $\partial x$ and $\partial y$ lie in the
ideal $\crP$ (\cref{odg:cr:lem:euler}), and their coefficients lie in $\crA$, so
$h\in\crP$; and $h\ne0$ because $\partial x\ne0$. A nonzero element of $\crP$ has
positive degree, so
\[
 \alpha=\ddeg\partial x=(m-1)\beta+\ddeg h>(m-1)\beta .
\]
Multiplying by $m$ gives $m\alpha>(m-1)d\alpha$, that is
$\bigl(m-(m-1)d\bigr)\alpha>0$. But $m-(m-1)d\le0$ for all ordinary $m,d\ge2$, and
$\alpha>0$, a contradiction. Only multiplication of group elements by ordinary
integers is used, so $\Gamma$ need not be divisible.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The unit route at the corner $(2,2)$ \src{C10, C11, C14}]\label{odg:cr:rem:unitroute}
Sources C10, C11 and C14 use only $h\in\crA$. Then $\ddeg h\ge0$ and the same
computation gives $\bigl(m-(m-1)d\bigr)\alpha\ge0$, a contradiction whenever
$(m-1)d>m$; since $(m-1)d-m=(m-1)(d-1)-1$, this excludes every pair except
$m=d=2$. That corner they treat by units: extend coefficients to $\bar\Bbbk$,
write $P(X)=c(X-r_1)(X-r_2)$ with $r_1\ne r_2$, choose $q^2=c$ and put
$u=x-(r_1+r_2)/2$; then $(y-qu)(y+qu)=-c(r_1-r_2)^2/4\in\bar\Bbbk^\times$, so both
factors are units of $\mathcal A_{\bar\Bbbk}(\Gamma)$, hence constants, and so are
$x,y$, whose coefficients lie in $\Bbbk$. This is the constant-product mechanism
of \cref{odg:lem:constantproduct,odg:thm:binary}. The support inclusion
$h\in\crP$ of sources C12 and C13 removes the corner without it; it is printed
as the primary proof, and this unit argument is kept as the second route.
\end{remark}

The hypotheses are sharp \src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14, C15}. For $d=1$ the curve is an
affine line: $y^m=ax+b$ with $a\ne0$ has the points $(\,(u^m-b)/a,\,u)$ for all
$u\in\crA$. For $m=1$ the graph $y=P(x)$ has the evident families. Squarefreeness
is needed: the cusp $y^2=x^3$ has $(u^2,u^3)$ (\cref{odg:cr:prop:singular}).
Characteristic zero is needed (\cref{odg:cr:sub:charp}).

\Cref{odg:cr:thm:squarefree} contains \cref{odg:thm:separated}: for
$a,b,c\in\C^\times$ and $m,n\ge2$, the equation $ax^m+by^n=c$ is
$y^n=P(x)$ with $P(X)=(c-aX^m)/b$, which is squarefree of degree $m$ because its
derivative vanishes only at $0$, where $P(0)=c/b\ne0$. So
\cref{odg:cr:thm:squarefree} with $\Bbbk=\C$ and $\Gamma\subseteq\No$ reproves
\cref{odg:thm:separated,odg:cor:mordell} without the binary-form step, and the
case $d=2$ includes the Pell-type equations $y^2=Dx^2+c$ with $Dc\ne0$
(\cref{odg:cor:pell}) \src{C13}. The separated-power proof of
\cref{odg:sec:derivations} remains a valid second proof of its case.

\begin{proposition}[A cubic certificate \src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14, C15}]\label{odg:cr:prop:cubic}
For $P(X)=X^3+aX+b$ and $\Delta_0=4a^3+27b^2$,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:cr:eq:cubic}
 (27b-18aX)\,P(X)+(6aX^2-9bX+4a^2)\,P'(X)=\Delta_0
\end{equation}
in $\Z[a,b,X]$. For $a,b\in\Bbbk$, the polynomial $P$ is squarefree if and
only if $\Delta_0\ne0$. In every commutative $\Bbbk$-algebra, on
$y^2=P(x)$ and for a derivation $\partial$ killing $a,b$,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:cr:eq:cubicdiv}
 \Delta_0\,\partial x=y\bigl((27b-18ax)\,y\,\partial x+2(6ax^2-9bx+4a^2)\,\partial y\bigr),
\end{equation}
whether or not $\Delta_0$ vanishes. If $\Delta_0\ne0$, set
$U=(27b-18aX)/\Delta_0$ and $W=(6aX^2-9bX+4a^2)/\Delta_0$. Then the
differential $\dif x/y$, initially defined where $y\ne0$, extends regularly
to the whole affine curve by
\begin{equation}\label{odg:cr:eq:dxy}
 \frac{\dif x}{y}=U(x)\,y\,\dif x+2W(x)\,\dif y .
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Expansion proves \eqref{odg:cr:eq:cubic}. If $\Delta_0\ne0$, dividing this
identity by $\Delta_0$ proves coprimality of $P$ and $P'$. A repeated root
satisfies $3r^2+a=0$ and $r^3+ar+b=0$, whence $b=2r^3$ and
$\Delta_0=4(-3r^2)^3+27(2r^3)^2=0$. Conversely, if $\Delta_0=0$ and $a=0$,
then $b=0$ and $r=0$ is repeated. If $a\ne0$, put $r=-3b/(2a)$;
then $r^2=-a/3$ and $r^3=b/2$, so $P(r)=P'(r)=0$.

To obtain \eqref{odg:cr:eq:cubicdiv} without dividing by $\Delta_0$,
multiply \eqref{odg:cr:eq:cubic} at $x$ by $\partial x$ and substitute
$P(x)=y^2$ and $P'(x)\partial x=2y\partial y$.
Now assume $\Delta_0\ne0$ and write
$\eta=U(x)y\,\dif x+2W(x)\dif y$. This polynomial expression is a global
regular differential. The relation $2y\,\dif y=P'(x)\dif x$ gives
$y\eta=(U(x)P(x)+W(x)P'(x))\dif x=\dif x$.
Thus $\eta=\dif x/y$ where $y\ne0$. Where $P'(x)\ne0$, substituting
$\dif x=2y\,\dif y/P'(x)$ instead gives $\eta=2\dif y/P'(x)$.
These two open sets cover the curve because $U(x)y^2+W(x)P'(x)=1$;
in particular the second formula explains regularity at every point
with $y=0$ \src{C12, C13}.
\end{proof}

The right side of \eqref{odg:cr:eq:dxy} is regular on the whole affine curve,
including its points with $y=0$, and the element $h$ in the proof of
\cref{odg:cr:thm:squarefree}, for $m=2$ and these $U,W$, is exactly its
contraction with $\partial$. The half-form $\dif x/(2y)$ instead contracts
to $h/2$; this is the normalization used in the direct proof below.
This is the bridge between the elementary certificate and the geometric argument
below: the certificate exhibits one global regular differential of the curve
\src{C12, C13}. The classical discriminant of $P$ is $-\Delta_0$ and the elliptic
discriminant is $-16\Delta_0$; only the nonvanishing matters \src{C10, C12}.

\begin{corollary}[Nonsingular short Weierstrass equations \src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14, C15}]\label{odg:cr:cor:weierstrass}
For $a,b\in\Bbbk$ with $4a^3+27b^2\ne0$, every solution of $y^2=x^3+ax+b$ in
$\crA^2$ is constant. In particular, for $a,b\in\Z$ with the same
nonvanishing condition,
\[
 \{(x,y)\in\Oz^2:y^2=x^3+ax+b\}=\{(x,y)\in\Z^2:y^2=x^3+ax+b\},
\]
and for $a,b\in\Z[i]$ satisfying that condition the solutions in $\Og^2$
are those in $\Z[i]^2$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The first assertion is \cref{odg:cr:thm:squarefree} with $(m,d)=(2,3)$ and
\cref{odg:cr:prop:cubic}. Directly: if $\ddeg x=\alpha>0$ then $2\ddeg y=3\alpha$,
and with $\ddeg\partial x=\alpha$ the element
$g=\tfrac12U(x)y\,\partial x+W(x)\,\partial y\in\crP$ satisfies $2yg=\partial x$.
Here $g\ne0$ since $\partial x\ne0$, so
$\ddeg g=\alpha-\tfrac32\alpha<0$ in $\Gamma_\Q$, impossible \src{C13, C14}.
For omnific coordinates put both in one workspace $\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma)$
(\cref{odg:lem:workspace}); they become complex constants, and
$\Oz\cap\C=\Z$, $\Og\cap\C=\Z[i]$.
\end{proof}

So $y^2=x^3-x+1$ ($\Delta_0=23$), $y^2=x^3+2x+3$ ($\Delta_0=275$) and, by
\cref{odg:cr:thm:squarefree}, $y^2=x^5-x+1$ (whose gcd with $5x^4-1$ is one)
have only their ordinary omnific points \src{C12, C13}; source C15 works out the
certificate for $\Delta_0=23$ and also names $y^2=x^5+x+1$, $y^3=x^4+x+1$ and $y^5=x^2-2$,
whose right sides are squarefree. No complete list of those ordinary points is claimed
\src{C12, C13, C15}.

\begin{corollary}[General Weierstrass equations \src{C13}, for every $\Bbbk$]\label{odg:cr:cor:generalweierstrass}
Let $a_1,a_2,a_3,a_4,a_6\in\Bbbk$ and suppose the projective cubic of
\begin{equation}\label{odg:cr:eq:generalweierstrass}
 y^2+a_1xy+a_3y=x^3+a_2x^2+a_4x+a_6
\end{equation}
is nonsingular. Every solution in $\crA^2$ is constant. If the $a_j$ lie in $\Z$
(or $\Z[i]$), every solution in $\Oz^2$ (or $\Og^2$) is ordinary.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Put $Y=2y+a_1x+a_3$. Then $Y^2=Q(x)$ with
\[
 Q(X)=4X^3+(a_1^2+4a_2)X^2+(2a_1a_3+4a_4)X+(a_3^2+4a_6),
\]
because $Y^2-Q(x)$ is four times the difference of the two sides of
\eqref{odg:cr:eq:generalweierstrass}. The change of variables is invertible over
$\Bbbk$ and extends to the invertible projective linear change
$[x:y:z]\mapsto[x:2y+a_1x+a_3z:z]$. A repeated root $r\in\bar\Bbbk$ of $Q$
would give the affine point $(r,0)$ on $Y^2=Q(x)$ where both partial
derivatives $-Q'(r)$ and $2Y$ vanish, so its tangent space has dimension
two rather than one (the plane-curve Jacobian criterion
\cite[Section 53.9]{StacksPlane}). This contradicts nonsingularity of
the projective cubic. Hence $Q$ is squarefree of degree three.
\Cref{odg:cr:thm:squarefree} makes
$x$ and $Y$ constant, hence $y=(Y-a_1x-a_3)/2$. Division by $2$ takes place in
$\crA$. For omnific coordinates use a common complex workspace and then
intersect the constant coordinates with $\Oz$ or $\Og$, as in
\cref{odg:cr:cor:weierstrass}; division by $2$ in the integer coefficient
ring is not required \src{C13}.
\end{proof}

Source C13 states this over $\Z$ and $\Z[i]$ with the fields $\R$ and $\C$; the
proof uses only characteristic zero.

\begin{proposition}[Singular cubics \src{C10, C11, C12, C14, C15}]\label{odg:cr:prop:singular}
If $4a^3+27b^2=0$, put $r=-3b/(2a)$ when $a\ne0$ and $r=0$ when $a=0$. Then
$a=-3r^2$, $b=2r^3$, and
\begin{equation}\label{odg:cr:eq:nodefamily}
 x=s^2-2r,\qquad y=s^3-3rs
\end{equation}
solves $y^2=x^3+ax+b$ in every commutative $\Bbbk$-algebra. If $a,b\in\Z$, then
$r\in\Z$, and \eqref{odg:cr:eq:nodefamily} gives omnific solutions for every
$s\in\Oz$, nonordinary when $s$ is. Likewise the node $y^2=x^2(x+1)$ carries
$(h^2-1,\,h(h^2-1))$ and the cusp $y^2=x^3$ carries $(h^2,h^3)$ \src{C11}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
If $a=0$, then $b=0$ and the claims hold with $r=0$. If $a\ne0$,
the identity $4a^3=-27b^2$ gives $r^2=9b^2/(4a^2)=-a/3$ and
$r^3=r\cdot r^2=b/2$. In both cases
$X^3-3r^2X+2r^3=(X-r)^2(X+2r)$, and at $x=s^2-2r$ the two factors are
$(s^2-3r)^2$ and $s^2$, whose product is $y^2$. For integers $a,b$, the number $r$
is a rational root of the monic integer polynomial $X^3+aX+b$, hence an integer
\src{C12}; equivalently, $r=p/q$ in lowest terms and $-3p^2/q^2=a\in\Z$ force
$q=1$ \src{C14}. A nonconstant $s$ gives $\ddeg x=2\ddeg s>0$. The node and cusp
identities are $(h(h^2-1))^2=(h^2-1)^2h^2$ and $(h^3)^2=(h^2)^3$.
\end{proof}

The case $r=0$ is the cusp and $r=1$ the node $y^2=x^3-3x+2$ with
$(s^2-2,\,s^3-3s)$ \src{C12, C14}. For positive infinite $s\in\Oz$ the map
$s\mapsto s^2-2r$ is injective, so every singular integral short Weierstrass cubic has a
proper class of distinct omnific points, while a nonsingular one has only its set
of ordinary points \src{C12, C14}. This is a polynomial family, not a claim that
every point of a singular curve has a parameter in the same ring \src{C14}. Thus
the test equation of \cref{odg:q:affine} has two sharply different outcomes:
every nonsingular equation is rigid, and every singular one has polynomial
families \src{C10, C12}.

\subsection{The two-ring contraction principle}\label{odg:cr:sub:tworing}

\begin{definition}[Differential separation data \src{C14; also C10, C11, C13}]\label{odg:cr:def:data}
Let $L/\Bbbk$ be a field extension, $R\subseteq L$ a $\Bbbk$-subalgebra, and
$V\subseteq L$ a valuation ring containing $\Bbbk$, with fraction field $L$ and
maximal ideal $\mathfrak m_V$. A family $\mathcal D$ of $\Bbbk$-derivations of $L$
is \emph{separating} if
\begin{equation}\label{odg:cr:eq:data}
 \partial(R)\subseteq R,\qquad \partial(V)\subseteq\mathfrak m_V
 \quad(\partial\in\mathcal D),\qquad R\cap\mathfrak m_V=\{0\},\qquad
 \bigcap_{\partial\in\mathcal D}\ker\partial=\Bbbk .
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

No inclusion between $R$ and $V$ is required, and $R$ is not assumed to be a
valuation ring, normal, finitely generated or of finite transcendence degree;
$L$ may be larger than $\Frac R$ \src{C10, C11, C14}. By
\cref{odg:cr:lem:rings,odg:cr:lem:euler,odg:cr:lem:separation},
$(L,R,V,\mathcal D)=(\crH,\crA,\crO,\{\partial_\lambda\})$ is separating data, with
the stronger inclusion $\partial(\crA)\subseteq\crP$.

Here is the meaning of differentiating a point. For a $\Bbbk$-algebra $S$,
an $S$-point $p_S$ and a $\Bbbk$-derivation $\partial:S\to M$, where $M$ is
an $S$-module, compose the natural pullback map with the universal map:
\[
 p_S^*\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk}\longrightarrow\Omega^1_{S/\Bbbk}
 \xrightarrow{\ \iota_\partial\ }M,
 \qquad \dif f\longmapsto\partial(f(p_S)).
\]
For $S=L$ and $M=L$ this is an $L$-linear functional on the cotangent
fiber, denoted $\partial p_L$. On an affine open with coordinate algebra
$B$, the corresponding tangent derivation of $B\otimes_\Bbbk L$ is
$f\otimes a\mapsto a\,\partial(f(p_L))$: it annihilates the new scalar factor
$L$. Thus it is a tangent vector to $X_L$ at $p_L$, although the original
map $\partial:L\to L$ is only $\Bbbk$-linear.

\begin{lemma}[Equality from an ambient field \src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14, C15}]\label{odg:cr:lem:separated}
Let $X$ be a separated scheme and $\sigma_1,\sigma_2\in X(S)$. If $S\to T_i$ are
ring maps whose kernels have zero intersection, and the images of
$\sigma_1,\sigma_2$ in each $X(T_i)$ agree, then $\sigma_1=\sigma_2$. In particular
$X(S)\to X(T)$ is injective for an injective ring map $S\hookrightarrow T$, such as
$S\subseteq L$ with $L$ a field; $L=\Frac S$ is not needed.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The equalizer of $\sigma_1,\sigma_2$ is the inverse image of the closed diagonal,
a closed subscheme of $\Spec S$ with ideal $J$. Agreement over $T_i$ puts $J$ in
the kernel of $S\to T_i$ for every $i$, so $J=0$ \src{C11}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Two-ring annihilation \src{C13}; one-forms \src{C10, C11, C12, C14}]\label{odg:cr:thm:annihilation}
Let $L,R,V,\mathfrak m_V$ and $\mathcal D$ satisfy the first three conditions of
\eqref{odg:cr:eq:data}. Let $X$ be proper over $\Bbbk$ and $p\in X(R)$, with
$p_L\in X(L)$ its image. For every $r\ge1$, every global covariant tensor
$s\in H^0\bigl(X,(\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk})^{\otimes r}\bigr)$ and all
$\partial_1,\ldots,\partial_r\in\mathcal D$,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:cr:eq:annihilation}
 s_{p_L}(\partial_1p_L,\ldots,\partial_rp_L)=0 ,
\end{equation}
and every global symmetric differential of positive degree $r$ vanishes on
the diagonal $(\partial p_L,\ldots,\partial p_L)$. Smoothness of $X$ is not needed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A $\Bbbk$-derivation $\partial:S\to M$ into an $S$-module factors uniquely through
an $S$-linear map $\iota_\partial:\Omega^1_{S/\Bbbk}\to M$,
$\sum a_j\,\dif b_j\mapsto\sum a_j\partial b_j$; the sums are finite, and no finite
generation of $S$ is needed \cite[Tag 00RM]{StacksDiff}. Pull $s$ back along
$p$, apply the natural maps to $(\Omega^1_{R/\Bbbk})^{\otimes r}$, and
contract with the restrictions $\partial_j|_R:R\to R$. The resulting global
function on $\Spec R$ is an element $a_R\in R$. By the valuative criterion
for properness, which holds for arbitrary valuation rings and not only discrete
ones \cite[Tag 0BX5]{StacksProper}, $p_L$ extends to $q\in X(V)$. The restrictions
$\partial_j|_V$ take values in the $V$-module $\mathfrak m_V$; pulling $s$ back along
$q$ and contracting gives a finite sum of products of $r$ elements of
$\mathfrak m_V$ with coefficients in $V$. More precisely,
$a_V\in\mathfrak m_V^r\subseteq\mathfrak m_V$, since $r\ge1$.

For either $S=R$ or $S=V$, restriction of the contraction to $L$ is the
composition of $p_L^*\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk}\to\Omega^1_{L/\Bbbk}$ with the
same maps $\iota_{\partial_j}$. Indeed, the natural map
$\Omega^1_{S/\Bbbk}\otimes_S L\to\Omega^1_{L/\Bbbk}$ takes $\dif b$ to
$\dif b$, and both contractions take it to $\partial_j b$. Tensor products
and multiplication preserve this compatibility. No claim that this
natural map is an isomorphism, or that $L=\Frac R$, is needed. Hence
$a_R$ and $a_V$ have the same image in $L$, even when $p$ and $q$ use
different affine charts, and that image lies in $R\cap\mathfrak m_V=0$
\src{C13, C14}.

For a symmetric differential, use one derivation in every factor. The map
$(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_r)\mapsto
\prod_j\iota_\partial(\alpha_j)$ is symmetric and therefore descends to
$\Sym^r\Omega^1$. Its value over $V$ is still in $\mathfrak m_V^r$.
This uses the quotient definition of the symmetric power; no lift of a
global symmetric section to a global tensor section is assumed.
\end{proof}

The positive-degree restriction matters: in degree zero the global section
$1$ evaluates to $1$, and $\mathfrak m_V^0=V$ gives no vanishing. The joint
constant condition in \eqref{odg:cr:eq:data} is used only in the later
descent from zero tangent vectors to constant points.

The derivations are relative to $\Bbbk$, not to $L$: an $L$-linear derivation of
$L$ is zero and would give nothing. The forms are global and regular; a rational
form with a pole at the centre of $q$ need not contract into $\mathfrak m_V$
\src{C14}. The argument compares two extensions of one field-valued point; it
does not assert a ring map $R\to V$ compatible with the embeddings in $L$, and
such a map generally does not exist \src{C10}. For $X\subseteq\mathbb P^n_\Bbbk$
the valuative step is concrete: write $p_L=[a_0:\cdots:a_n]$, choose $j$ with $a_j\ne0$
of least valuation among the nonzero coordinates, and use $a_i/a_j\in V$.
The $j$th coordinate is $1$, so these ratios define a $V$-point of projective
space. The homogeneous equations of $X$ vanish in $L$ and hence in $V$,
so the point lies in $X$. This uses only the minimum of a finite set of
values, with no rank-one or discreteness condition \src{C13}. A regular
differential thus has two descriptions at the point, one with no strictly
negative exponent and one, after an Euler derivation, with only strictly negative
exponents; properness supplies the second even when the affine coordinates of
the point are infinite \src{C14}. Nothing here is an order-topological or
transfinite-limit argument.

\begin{lemma}[From vanishing tangents to a constant point \src{C13, C14; also C10, C11, C12}]\label{odg:cr:lem:constantpoint}
Let $(L,R,V,\mathcal D)$ be separating data, $X$ a separated $\Bbbk$-scheme of
finite type and $p\in X(R)$. If $\partial(f(p_L))=0$ for every $\partial\in\mathcal D$
and every regular function $f$ on some affine open $W\ni p_L$, then $p$ comes from
a unique point of $X(\Bbbk)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The algebra map $\Bbbk[W]\to L$ of $p_L$ has image in the joint constants $\Bbbk$,
so it defines $p_0\in W(\Bbbk)\subseteq X(\Bbbk)$ whose extension to $L$ is $p_L$.
The constant $R$-point of $p_0$ and $p$ agree over $L$, hence over $R$ by
\cref{odg:cr:lem:separated}. Uniqueness follows from $\Bbbk\hookrightarrow L$. The
point $p$ need not factor through $W$ before this is proved \src{C13}.
\end{proof}

It is enough to test finitely many algebra generators of $\Bbbk[W]$:
the derivations kill coefficients in $\Bbbk$ and satisfy the sum and
product rules. This finite test concerns the chosen affine chart; it
does not require a finitely generated subalgebra of $L$ stable under
all the derivations.

\begin{theorem}[Proper differential rigidity \src{C14; also C10, C11, C12, C13}]\label{odg:cr:thm:proper}
Let $(L,R,V,\mathcal D)$ be separating data and $X$ a smooth proper $\Bbbk$-scheme
whose cotangent sheaf $\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk}$ is generated by global sections. Then
$X(\Bbbk)\to X(R)$ is a bijection.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $p\in X(R)$ and $\partial\in\mathcal D$. Differentiation of $p_L$ defines an
$L$-linear map $p_L^*\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk}\to L$. Global generation makes
$H^0(X,\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk})\otimes_\Bbbk L\to p_L^*\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk}$ surjective:
pull back the surjective evaluation map
$H^0(X,\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk})\otimes_\Bbbk\mathcal O_X\to\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk}$
along $p_L$. Pullback is right exact, even when $p_L$ is not a closed
point. By \cref{odg:cr:thm:annihilation}, the tangent functional vanishes
on the image of this evaluation map, hence on the whole cotangent fiber. So
$\partial(f(p_L))=0$ for every regular $f$ on an affine open through $p_L$, and
\cref{odg:cr:lem:constantpoint} makes $p$ constant. Injectivity follows from
$\Bbbk\hookrightarrow L$.
\end{proof}

In fact this proof also works for a proper, possibly singular $X$ whose
K\"ahler differential sheaf is globally generated: neither its local
freeness nor smoothness was used. Smoothness in the stated theorem
provides the cotangent-bundle setting used in the applications below.

\begin{corollary}[Hahn realization \src{C14; also C10, C11, C12, C13}]\label{odg:cr:cor:hahnproper}
For every field $\Bbbk$ of characteristic zero, every set-sized ordered abelian
group $\Gamma$ and every smooth proper $\Bbbk$-scheme $X$ with globally generated
cotangent sheaf, $X(\crA)=X(\Bbbk)$, and $\ct$ is inverse to the inclusion of
constant points. For $\Bbbk=\R$ or $\C$ the same holds over $\BR$ or $\BC$ in the
class convention of \cref{odg:cr:sub:conventions}.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{odg:cr:thm:proper} to the data $(\crH,\crA,\crO,\{\partial_\lambda\})$.
The retraction fixes constant points \src{C10}. A class point lies over some
$\mathcal A_\R(\Gamma)$ or $\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma)$.
\end{proof}

The proof uses only that global forms span the cotangent space at the particular
field-valued point $p_L$ \src{C11, C12}. Hence if $Z\subseteq X$ is the locus where
global forms fail to generate, every nonconstant $\crA$-point has its $\crH$-point
in $Z$, and every Euler tangent direction of a point lies in the common
annihilator of the global one-forms there \src{C10, C11}. This is an obstruction,
not a classification of varieties with a nonempty failure locus, and it does not
prove that surviving directions integrate to nonconstant Hahn points.

\begin{definition}[Tangent detection by symmetric differentials \src{C13}]\label{odg:cr:def:detect}
Global symmetric differentials \emph{detect tangent directions} on a smooth proper
$\Bbbk$-scheme $X$ if for every field extension $L/\Bbbk$, every $x\in X(L)$ and
every nonzero tangent vector $u\in T_xX_L$ there are $r\ge1$ and
$s\in H^0(X,\Sym^r\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk})$ with $s_x(u,\ldots,u)\ne0$.
\end{definition}

The quantification over extension fields is needed because the point $p_L$ is
generally not classical. For $\mathcal F=\Sym^r\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk}$, flat base
change gives
$H^0(X_L,\mathcal F_L)=H^0(X,\mathcal F)\otimes_\Bbbk L$
\cite[Tag 02KH]{StacksBaseChange}; here
$(\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk})_L=\Omega^1_{X_L/L}$, with the base field changed
along with the scheme. Thus a detecting section after field
extension is a finite $L$-linear combination of base-field sections; if
its value is nonzero, at least one of those sections has nonzero value.
This justifies using sections over $\Bbbk$ in the definition \src{C13}.

\begin{theorem}[Symmetric-differential rigidity \src{C13, C15}]\label{odg:cr:thm:symmetric}
If $X$ is smooth and proper over $\Bbbk$ and its global symmetric differentials
detect tangent directions, then $X(\crA)=X(\Bbbk)$ for every $\Gamma$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If some $\partial_\lambda p_L$ were nonzero, a detecting symmetric differential
would have nonzero diagonal value on it, contrary to
\cref{odg:cr:thm:annihilation}. So all Euler tangent vectors vanish and
\cref{odg:cr:lem:constantpoint} applies.
\end{proof}

The same proof works for any separating data $(L,R,V,\mathcal D)$:
tangent detection at its field $L$ gives $X(R)=X(\Bbbk)$. The Hahn
realization supplies one such family, rather than an additional
geometric ingredient.

\begin{corollary}[A semiample tautological bundle \src{C13}; ample case also \src{C15}]\label{odg:cr:cor:semiample}
Let $X$ be smooth and projective over $\Bbbk$, and let
$\mathcal L=\mathcal O_{\mathbb P(\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk})}(1)$, with $\mathbb P(E)$
parametrizing one-dimensional quotients of $E$. If some positive tensor power of
$\mathcal L$ is globally generated, then $X(\crA)=X(\Bbbk)$. This holds in
particular when $\mathcal L$ is ample, which is what ``$\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk}$ is
ample'' means here, and when $\Sym^r\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk}$ is globally generated for
some $r\ge1$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A nonzero tangent vector $u$ at $x\in X(L)$ is a nonzero $L$-linear map
$x^*\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk}\to L$, hence a surjection. It defines a point
$[u]\in\mathbb P(\Omega^1)(L)$ over $x$. If $\mathcal L^{\otimes r}$ is
globally generated, its evaluation at $[u]$ is surjective onto a
one-dimensional $L$-space; at least one base-field section is nonzero
there. Since $\Omega^1$ is locally free, the projective-bundle identity is
$H^0(\mathbb P(\Omega^1),\mathcal L^{\otimes r})=H^0(X,\Sym^r\Omega^1)$
\cite[Tag 01OA]{StacksProjBundle}. Under the quotient identification
provided by $u$, the value of that section is exactly
$s_x(u,\ldots,u)\ne0$. An ample line
bundle on a projective scheme has a globally generated power
\cite[Tag 01PR]{StacksAmple}, and global generation of $\Sym^r\Omega^1$ gives that of
$\mathcal L^{\otimes r}$ through the universal quotient. Apply
\cref{odg:cr:thm:symmetric}.
\end{proof}

Conventions for semiample vector bundles vary, so the hypothesis is stated for
the tautological line bundle. No implication from nefness, bigness or general
type is asserted \src{C13}.

For a smooth projective $X$, tangent detection is in fact equivalent to
this tautological semiampleness condition. To see the converse, put
$P=\mathbb P(\Omega^1)$ and suppose detection holds. Applying it over
the residue field of each point of $P$ shows that the nonvanishing loci
of the sections of all positive powers of $\mathcal L$ cover $P$.
Quasi-compactness gives a finite subcover, from sections $s_i$ of degrees
$r_i\ge1$. For a common positive multiple $N$ of the $r_i$, the sections
$s_i^{N/r_i}$ of $\mathcal L^{\otimes N}$ still have no common zero,
so they generate that line bundle. If $P$ is empty the conclusion is
vacuous. This equivalence concerns tangent detection; it does not assert
that every rigid variety satisfies either condition.

\begin{proposition}[Inheritance \src{C13; also C10, C11, C14, C15}]\label{odg:cr:prop:inherit}
Let $X$ be a separated $\Bbbk$-scheme of finite type with $X(\crA)=X(\Bbbk)$. Every
locally closed subscheme $Y\subseteq X$ has $Y(\crA)=Y(\Bbbk)$. For a morphism
$f:Z\to X$, every $\crA$-point $z$ of $Z$ satisfies $f(z)=f(\ct z)$; so if every
fiber of $f$ over a $\Bbbk$-point is rigid, then $Z(\crA)=Z(\Bbbk)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $y\in Y(\crA)$ its image in $X$ is constant, equal to the image of
$y_0=\ct(y)\in Y(\Bbbk)$; an immersion is a monomorphism, so $y=y_0$. For $f$, the
point $f(z)$ is constant with constant term $x_0=f(\ct z)$, so the
universal property of $Z\times_X\Spec\Bbbk$, formed using $x_0$, puts
$z$ in that fiber as a $\crA$-point. If the fiber is rigid, this point
comes from a $\Bbbk$-point of the fiber and hence of $Z$. Conversely,
constant points always extend, and the retraction makes their extension
injective. The immersion argument also works for singular or nonreduced
$Y$; no smoothness is used here.
\end{proof}

Properness and global generation in \cref{odg:cr:thm:proper} cannot in general
be dropped \src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14}. For the counterexamples choose
$\gamma>0$ in $\Gamma$ (when $\Gamma=0$, $\crA=\Bbbk$ already).
The affine line $\Ga$ has trivial cotangent sheaf,
generated by $\dif T$, but is not proper, and $\Ga(\crA)=\crA$: the point
$T=\omega^\gamma$ does not extend to a $\crO$-point of that chart. The projective
line is proper but has no nonzero global one-form, and $[\omega^\gamma:1]$ is a
nonconstant point of $\mathbb P^1(\crA)$ for $\gamma>0$. Properness supplies the
valuation-ring point and global generation the detection; they are separate
hypotheses. Nor is finite generation a shortcut: a finite tuple of Hahn series
generates a finitely generated algebra, but an Euler derivation need not preserve
it, so one cannot infer that the tuple comes from a polynomial arc; polynomial
arcs appear only after the classification has forced the affine line
(\cref{odg:cr:cor:polywitness}) \src{C14}.

For an explicit failure of stability, put $t=\omega^\gamma$, choose
$\partial t=t$, and set $f=t+t^2$. Then $\partial f=2f-t$ does not belong
to the finitely generated algebra $\Bbbk[f]$: otherwise $t$ would belong
to it, whereas every nonconstant polynomial in $f$ has degree at least
$2\gamma>\gamma=\ddeg t$. The same point $[t:1]$ also illustrates why
regularity of the form is essential. On $\mathbb P^1$ the rational form
$\dif T$ contracts to $t$, which is not in $\crm$; at the valuation
centre, with coordinate $u=T^{-1}$, it is $-u^{-2}\dif u$ and has a pole.

\section{Smooth curves and their omnific points}\label{odg:cr:sec:curves}

\subsection{Positive genus}\label{odg:cr:sub:positivegenus}

\begin{lemma}[Canonical forms have no common zero \src{C13, C14; also C10, C11, C12}]\label{odg:cr:lem:canonical}
Let $\overline C$ be a smooth proper geometrically integral curve of genus $g\ge1$
over $\Bbbk$. Then $\Omega^1_{\overline C/\Bbbk}$ is generated by its global sections.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Global sections of a coherent sheaf on a proper scheme commute with field
extension \cite{StacksBaseChange}, and surjectivity descends along the faithfully
flat extension $\Bbbk\subseteq\bar\Bbbk$, so assume $\Bbbk$ algebraically closed.
Let $P$ be a closed point, hence a $\Bbbk$-rational point.
A nonconstant rational function with at most a simple pole at $P$ would be a
degree-one morphism $\overline C\to\mathbb P^1$, an isomorphism of smooth projective
curves, contradicting $g\ge1$; so $h^0(\overline C,\mathcal O_{\overline C}(P))=1$.
Riemann--Roch and duality \cite{StacksRR} give
$h^0\bigl(\overline C,\Omega^1(-P)\bigr)=h^0(\mathcal O_{\overline C}(P))+g-2=g-1$,
while $h^0(\overline C,\Omega^1)=g$. So some global differential does not vanish at
$P$ and spans the one-dimensional cotangent fiber there: the kernel of evaluation
is exactly $H^0(\overline C,\Omega^1(-P))$. Nakayama's lemma makes the evaluation
map surjective at every closed point. Its coherent cokernel is therefore zero,
since a nonempty closed support on a finite-type scheme over a field has a closed
point. This proves global generation on the whole curve.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Positive-genus curves \src{C11, C13, C14; also C10, C12}]\label{odg:cr:thm:positivegenus}
Let $\overline C$ be a smooth proper geometrically integral curve of genus at least
one over $\Bbbk$. Then $\overline C(\crA)=\overline C(\Bbbk)$, and every locally closed
subscheme of $\overline C$, in particular every open subcurve, has only constant
$\crA$-points.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{odg:cr:lem:canonical,odg:cr:cor:hahnproper,odg:cr:prop:inherit}.
\end{proof}

Sources C11, C13 and C14 prove this over every field of characteristic zero,
source C11 by extending coefficients to $\bar\Bbbk$ and comparing a point with its
constant term through \cref{odg:cr:lem:separated}; sources C10 and C12 assume $\Bbbk$
algebraically closed. No rational point is selected in advance: a genus-one torsor
without a $\Bbbk$-point has no $\crA$-point at all \src{C14}. Sources C10 and C15 note that
the projective statement also follows from an Abel--Jacobi embedding and
\cref{odg:cr:thm:abelian}; the proof above avoids that construction.

\begin{remark}[One nonzero form suffices for curves \src{C11, C12, C15}]\label{odg:cr:rem:oneform}
For curves the base-point-free statement can be bypassed. Let $p$ be a
nonconstant $\crA$-point of a smooth geometrically integral curve $X$ of positive
genus (proper, or an open subcurve of a proper one). If the image of the generic
point of $\Spec\crA$ were a closed point, its residue field would be finite over
$\Bbbk$ and embedded in $\crH$, hence equal to $\Bbbk$ by
\cref{odg:cr:cor:algclosed}, and $p$ would be constant by
\cref{odg:cr:lem:separated}. So $p$ gives an embedding $F=\Bbbk(X)\hookrightarrow\crH$.
A nonzero global regular form $\eta$ on the completion spans the one-dimensional
$F$-space $\Omega^1_{F/\Bbbk}$, and its contraction with every $\partial_\lambda$
vanishes by \cref{odg:cr:thm:annihilation}, applied after composing $p$ with the
inclusion into the proper completion. All contractions are taken in $\crH$
through the displayed embedding; the derivations need not preserve $F$.
Writing $\dif f=h_f\eta$ gives
$\partial_\lambda f=h_f\,\eta(\partial_\lambda)=0$ for all $f\in F$ and all $\lambda$,
so $F\subseteq\Bbbk$ by \cref{odg:cr:lem:separation}, contradicting
$\trdeg_\Bbbk F=1$. Source C11 gives this for affine curves over algebraically
closed $\Bbbk$ and source C12 for projective ones; the closed-point step for a
general $\Bbbk$ uses \cref{odg:cr:cor:algclosed}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The smooth affine classification}\label{odg:cr:sub:affine}
