\begin{lemma}[Two punctures force constancy \src{C10, C11, C13, C14, C15}]\label{odg:cr:lem:punctures}
Let $\Bbbk$ be algebraically closed. If $U=\mathbb P^1_\Bbbk\setminus\Sigma$ for a
finite set $\Sigma$ of at least two closed points, then $U(\crA)=U(\Bbbk)$.
More generally, an integral affine curve
$C$ over $\Bbbk$ whose coordinate ring has a nonconstant unit has
$C(\crA)=C(\Bbbk)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $\varphi:\Bbbk[C]\to\crA$ be a point and $u$ a nonconstant unit. Then
$\varphi(u)\in\crA^\times=\Bbbk^\times$, say $\varphi(u)=c$. The kernel of $\varphi$
contains $u-c\ne0$; it is a prime of the one-dimensional finitely generated domain
$\Bbbk[C]$, hence maximal with residue field $\Bbbk$ by the Nullstellensatz, and
$\varphi$ factors through $\Bbbk$ \src{C13}. For $U$, a coordinate $z$ with its zero
and pole at two removed points is such a unit: both $z$ and $z^{-1}$ are regular
on $U$ \src{C10, C11, C14}.
\end{proof}

This extends the torus remark of \cref{odg:sec:rigidity}
(``An algebraic-geometric formulation''). There the coordinate ring is
generated by units; here a single nonconstant unit suffices because the
coordinate ring has dimension one. It need not be generated by units.
In either argument units must specialize to nonzero constants.

\begin{lemma}[Forms of the affine line \src{C14; also C16}; real case \src{C10, C11, C13}]\label{odg:cr:lem:formA1}
If $C$ is a smooth geometrically integral affine curve over $\Bbbk$ with
$C_{\bar\Bbbk}\cong\Aff^1_{\bar\Bbbk}$, then $C\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The smooth projective completion $\overline C$ exists over the perfect field
$\Bbbk$ \cite{StacksCurveCompletion}; its base change is the smooth projective
completion of $C_{\bar\Bbbk}$, hence $\mathbb P^1$ by uniqueness
\cite{StacksCurves}. Its reduced boundary is finite \'etale over $\Bbbk$ and has
exactly one geometric point. Its degree is therefore one, so it consists of a
$\Bbbk$-rational point $P$. A smooth proper genus-zero curve with a
rational point is $\mathbb P^1_\Bbbk$ \cite{StacksGenusZero}: by Riemann--Roch,
$h^0(\mathcal O_{\overline C}(P))=2$, and a function with a single simple pole at
$P$ is a degree-one map to $\mathbb P^1_\Bbbk$ sending $P$ to $\infty$. Removing
$P$ gives $C\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$.
\end{proof}

For $\Bbbk=\R$ the unique geometric boundary point is fixed by complex
conjugation; sources C10, C11 and C13 prove this case. The affine unit circle
$x^2+y^2=1$ instead has two geometric boundary points, $[1:i:0]$ and $[1:-i:0]$
on $x^2+y^2=z^2$, exchanged by conjugation. Its complexification is a projective
line minus two points, so its rigidity follows from
\cref{odg:cr:lem:punctures} and the coefficient descent below. It is not a form
of the affine line. Source C13 includes it in the full real-descent argument;
the one-boundary-point step alone does not apply to it.

\begin{theorem}[Smooth affine curves \src{C14; also C10, C11, C12, C13, C15, C16}]\label{odg:cr:thm:affine}
Let $\Gamma\ne0$ and let $C$ be a smooth geometrically integral affine curve over
a field $\Bbbk$ of characteristic zero. Then
\[
 C(\crA)\ne C(\Bbbk)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad C\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk ,
\]
and in that case an isomorphism identifies $C(\crA)$ with $\crA$. The following
are equivalent: $C\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$; $C_{L}\cong\Aff^1_{L}$ for some (or every)
field extension $L/\Bbbk$; there is a nonconstant morphism $\Aff^1_\Bbbk\to C$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
First let $\Bbbk$ be algebraically closed. Let $\overline C$ be the smooth projective
completion and $\Sigma=\overline C\setminus C$; it is finite and nonempty, because a
positive-dimensional proper integral curve is not affine
\cite{StacksCurveCompletion}. If
$\overline C$ has positive genus, use \cref{odg:cr:thm:positivegenus}. Otherwise
$\overline C\cong\mathbb P^1_\Bbbk$ \cite{StacksGenusZero}; if $|\Sigma|\ge2$ use
\cref{odg:cr:lem:punctures}, and if $|\Sigma|=1$ then $C\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$.

For general $\Bbbk$, the coefficientwise inclusion
$\crA\hookrightarrow\mathcal A_{\bar\Bbbk}(\Gamma)$ preserves supports. Unless
$C_{\bar\Bbbk}\cong\Aff^1_{\bar\Bbbk}$, a point $\Bbbk[C]\to\crA$ becomes constant over
$\bar\Bbbk$, so its image lies in $\crA\cap\bar\Bbbk=\Bbbk$ and it is a $\Bbbk$-point
\src{C14}. If $C_{\bar\Bbbk}\cong\Aff^1_{\bar\Bbbk}$, then $C\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$ by
\cref{odg:cr:lem:formA1}, whose points are the elements of $\crA$; for
$0<\gamma\in\Gamma$ the element $\omega^\gamma$ is nonconstant.

For an arbitrary field extension $L/\Bbbk$, if $C_L\cong\Aff^1_L$, then
$\overline C_L\cong\mathbb P^1_L$ and its reduced boundary has degree one.
Genus and the degree of the finite \'etale boundary are unchanged by field
extension. Thus $\overline C$ has genus zero and a boundary consisting of one
$\Bbbk$-rational point. The Riemann--Roch argument in
\cref{odg:cr:lem:formA1} gives $C\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$. This does not require an
embedding of $L$ into $\bar\Bbbk$. The converse holds by base change.

A nonconstant morphism $\Aff^1\to C$ is dominant, since its image cannot be
contained in a closed point. It therefore induces an injection
$\Bbbk[C]\hookrightarrow\Bbbk[T]$. Composing with
$\Bbbk[T]\hookrightarrow\crA$, $T\mapsto\omega^\gamma$, gives a nonconstant
point: distinct powers have distinct exponents, so substitution is injective.
Conversely $\Aff^1\cong C$ is such a morphism \src{C12}. The assumption
$\Gamma\ne0$ is needed for these nonconstant examples; when $\Gamma=0$,
$\crA=\Bbbk$ and every point is constant.
\end{proof}

\begin{center}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.15}
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.38\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.52\textwidth}}
\toprule
Completion and boundary (over $\bar\Bbbk$) & Mechanism \src{C14}\\
\midrule
Positive genus, any boundary & Global regular differentials force constancy.\\
Genus zero, at least two missing points & An invertible coordinate forces constancy.\\
Genus zero, one missing point & The affine line takes every value in $\crA$.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The criterion is intrinsic to the affine curve, not to a plane equation: the
parabola $Y=X^2$ is an affine line and has the points $(u,u^2)$, whereas the
hyperbola $XY=1$ is $\mathbb P^1$ minus two points. Its $\crA$-points are precisely
$(c,c^{-1})$ for $c\in\Bbbk^\times$; imposing real omnific integer coordinates
leaves only $(1,1)$ and $(-1,-1)$ \src{C10, C11, C12}. Boundedness is not the mechanism:
$x^2+y^2=1$ is rigid already by \cref{odg:thm:bounded}, and over $\BC$ by
$(x+iy)(x-iy)=1$, but $y^2=x^3+x$ is unbounded in its real locus and is rigid by
\cref{odg:cr:prop:cubic} or by its global differential \src{C14}. The classification
uses no finite generation, rank or order-type hypothesis on supports
\src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14, C15}.

The rigid direction has a second proof, by logarithmic differentials, due to
source C12. We print it over an algebraically closed field, where its local
computation takes place; \cref{odg:cr:thm:affine} then descends as above.

\begin{lemma}[Logarithmic forms \src{C12, C15}]\label{odg:cr:lem:logdim}
Let $\Bbbk$ be algebraically closed, $\overline C$ a smooth projective integral curve of genus
$g$ and $\Sigma=P_1+\cdots+P_r$ a nonempty reduced divisor. Then
$h^0\bigl(\overline C,\Omega^1(\Sigma)\bigr)=g+r-1$, which vanishes only for $g=0$,
$r=1$, that is, for $\overline C\setminus\Sigma\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$. If $u$ is a local
parameter at a closed point $Q$, a rational differential with at most a simple pole at $Q$
has the form $c\,\dif u/u$ with $c\in\mathcal O_{\overline C,Q}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Riemann--Roch for $K_{\overline C}+\Sigma$, where $K_{\overline C}$ is a canonical
divisor of degree $2g-2$, gives $h^0(\Omega^1(\Sigma))-h^0(\mathcal O(-\Sigma))=g+r-1$,
and $h^0(\mathcal O(-\Sigma))=0$ because a regular function on the proper integral
curve is constant and vanishes at a point of $\Sigma$ only if it is zero. A curve of
genus zero is $\mathbb P^1_\Bbbk$. The local form holds because
$\mathcal O_{\overline C,Q}$ is a discrete valuation ring and $\dif u$ generates
its rank-one module of differentials: its class spans the cotangent fiber at
the smooth $\Bbbk$-point $Q$, so Nakayama's lemma applies. Writing $\eta=f\,\dif u$,
the simple-pole bound says $uf\in\mathcal O_{\overline C,Q}$, giving $c=uf$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The logarithmic route \src{C12; also C15}, closed centre \mergetag]\label{odg:cr:thm:logroute}
Let $\Bbbk$ be algebraically closed and $C$ a smooth integral affine curve over
$\Bbbk$ with $C\not\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$. Then $C(\crA)=C(\Bbbk)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $p:\Bbbk[C]\to\crA$ be a nonconstant point. Its kernel is a prime; a nonzero
prime of the one-dimensional domain $\Bbbk[C]$ is maximal with residue field $\Bbbk$,
which would make $p$ constant. So $p$ is injective and extends to an embedding
$F=\Bbbk(C)\hookrightarrow\crH$. By \cref{odg:cr:lem:logdim} there is a nonzero
$\eta\in H^0\bigl(\overline C,\Omega^1(\Sigma)\bigr)$. Its restriction to the affine
curve $C$ is regular, a finite sum $\sum_ja_j\,\dif b_j$ with $a_j,b_j\in\Bbbk[C]$,
so for every Euler derivation $\partial$
\[
 \eta(\partial)=\sum_jp(a_j)\,\partial\bigl(p(b_j)\bigr)\in\crP
\]
by \cref{odg:cr:lem:euler}. On the other hand the $\crH$-point of $\overline C$
extends, by properness, to a $\crO$-point with centre $Q$. The centre is a closed
point: if it were the generic point, then $F\subseteq\crO$ and the residue map would
give an injective $\Bbbk$-algebra map $F\to\Bbbk$, impossible since
$\trdeg_\Bbbk F=1$ \mergetag. In fact $Q$ lies in the boundary: if $Q\in C$,
every element of $\Bbbk[C]$ would map into $\crO\cap\crA=\Bbbk$, contradicting
nonconstancy. The map $\mathcal O_{\overline C,Q}\to\crO$ is local, so it sends
a local parameter $u$ to $\crm$; its image is nonzero because the map is the
restriction of the embedding $F\hookrightarrow\crH$. Since $\eta$
has at most a simple pole at $Q$, \cref{odg:cr:lem:logdim} and
\cref{odg:cr:lem:euler} give $\eta(\partial)=c\,\partial u/u\in\crO$. As
$\crP\cap\crO=0$, $\eta(\partial)=0$. Here the logarithmic derivative need only
lie in $\crO$, not in its maximal ideal. Both computations evaluate the same
rational form in $\crH$ under the same embedding of $F$.
Now $\Omega^1_{F/\Bbbk}$ is one-dimensional over
$F$ and spanned by $\eta$, so $\dif f=h_f\eta$ gives $\partial f=h_f\,\eta(\partial)=0$
for all $f\in F$ and all $\partial$, and $F\subseteq\Bbbk$ by
\cref{odg:cr:lem:separation}, contradicting $\trdeg_\Bbbk F=1$.
\end{proof}

Source C12 writes ``let $Q$ be its centre'' without showing that $Q$ is closed;
the step marked \mergetag{} supplies it. Source C15's proof of the same statement
avoids the step, because its local computation (\cref{odg:log:lem:valuation}) works
wherever the centre lies (\cref{odg:log:sub:log}). This route needs the support inclusion
$\partial(\crA)\subseteq\crP$: with $\partial(\crA)\subseteq\crA$ alone one would only get
$\eta(\partial)\in\crA\cap\crO=\Bbbk$. It also avoids the unit lemma: $\Gm$ has the
logarithmic form $\dif x/x$, a genus-one affine curve has regular forms even with
one point removed, and the affine line has no nonzero logarithmic forms:
every nonzero regular form $P(x)\,\dif x$ has a pole of order $\deg P+2\ge2$
at infinity. These are three values of $g+r-1$ \src{C12}.

\begin{corollary}[Curve subalgebras \src{C11, C12}]\label{odg:cr:cor:subalgebra}
Let $\Bbbk$ be algebraically closed. A finitely generated $\Bbbk$-subalgebra
$A_0\subseteq\crA$ that is a smooth (equivalently, normal) domain of transcendence
degree one is $\Bbbk[u]$ for some $u\in\crA\setminus\Bbbk$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A one-dimensional Noetherian domain is normal exactly when it is regular;
over the perfect field $\Bbbk$ its curve is then smooth
\cite{StacksCurveCompletion}. The inclusion is a nonconstant point of
$\Spec A_0$. Transcendence degree one excludes $\Gamma=0$, which would give
$\crA=\Bbbk$. Thus $\Spec A_0\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$ by
\cref{odg:cr:thm:affine}, and $u$ is the image of the coordinate.
\end{proof}

Smoothness of the algebra cannot be dropped: for $\gamma>0$, put $t=\omega^\gamma$.
The cusp algebra $\Bbbk[t^2,t^3]\subseteq\crA$ is not normal: $t=t^3/t^2$ lies
in its fraction field and is integral over it, but it is not in the algebra,
whose polynomial exponents belong to $\{0,2,3,4,\ldots\}$. The corollary does not say that every finitely
generated subalgebra of $\crA$ is a polynomial ring; higher transcendence degree is
outside it \src{C11}.

\begin{corollary}[All smooth curves \src{C13; also C15}, for every $\Bbbk$]\label{odg:cr:cor:allcurves}
Let $\Gamma\ne0$ and let $C$ be a smooth geometrically integral separated curve of
finite type over $\Bbbk$. Then $C$ has a nonconstant $\crA$-point if and only if
$C\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$ or $C\cong\mathbb P^1_\Bbbk$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A separated integral finite-type curve that is not proper is affine
\cite{StacksCurveCompletion}, and
\cref{odg:cr:thm:affine} applies. A proper curve of positive genus is rigid by
\cref{odg:cr:thm:positivegenus}. A proper curve of genus zero with an $\crA$-point
has the $\Bbbk$-point obtained by $\ct$, hence is $\mathbb P^1_\Bbbk$
\cite{StacksGenusZero}. Conversely, choose $\gamma>0$; $\omega^\gamma$ on
$\Aff^1$ and $[\omega^\gamma:1]$ on $\mathbb P^1$ are nonconstant points.
\end{proof}

Source C13 proves the corollary for $\Bbbk=\R$ and $\C$; its proof uses only
the curve classification and the rational point supplied by $\ct$, so the
statement holds over every field of characteristic zero. It is the only one of sources
C10--C14 to include the projective line; source C15 includes it too, over an
algebraically closed field, and descends the rigid direction to every field of
characteristic zero.

\subsection{Arithmetic models and exact fibers}\label{odg:cr:sub:arith}

The geometric classification controls points in $\crA=\Bbbk\oplus\crP$.
For arithmetic points we use its subring $R=\mathfrak o\oplus\crP$, where
$\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$. The distinction matters: coefficients of a
parametrization may lie anywhere in $\Bbbk$, while the constant coordinates
of an $R$-point must lie in $\mathfrak o$. We use closed affine presentations,
so a point is a tuple satisfying the defining polynomial equations.

\begin{theorem}[Exact fibers over an arbitrary coefficient ring \src{C11}, for every $\Bbbk$]\label{odg:cr:thm:arith}
Let $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$ be a subring, $\mathcal U\subseteq\Aff^n_{\mathfrak o}$
a closed subscheme such that $U=\mathcal U_\Bbbk$ is a smooth
geometrically integral curve, and $R=\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=*]
\item If $U\not\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$, then $\mathcal U(R)=\mathcal U(\mathfrak o)$.
\item If $\phi:\Aff^1_\Bbbk\to U$ is an isomorphism, then for every
$\mathbf n\in\mathcal U(\mathfrak o)$, with $c_{\mathbf n}=\phi^{-1}(\mathbf n)\in\Bbbk$,
\[
 \crP\longrightarrow\{\mathbf x\in\mathcal U(R):\ct(\mathbf x)=\mathbf n\},\qquad
 s\longmapsto\phi(c_{\mathbf n}+s),
\]
is a bijection, and $\phi(c_{\mathbf n}+s)$ is constant only for $s=0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(i) When $\Gamma=0$, $R=\mathfrak o$ and the assertion is immediate.
Otherwise a point of $\mathcal U(R)$ becomes a point of $U(\crA)$, constant by
\cref{odg:cr:thm:affine}; its coordinates lie in $R\cap\Bbbk=\mathfrak o$.
They still satisfy the defining equations, giving the claimed ordinary point.
The reverse inclusion is induced by $\mathfrak o\hookrightarrow R$.

(ii) Write $\mathcal U=\Spec(\mathfrak o[X_1,\ldots,X_n]/J)$ and
$\phi=(\phi_1,\ldots,\phi_n)$ with $\phi_j\in\Bbbk[T]$. Each
$\phi_j(c_{\mathbf n}+s)-\phi_j(c_{\mathbf n})$ is a polynomial in $s$ without constant
term, hence lies in $\crP$, and $\phi_j(c_{\mathbf n})=n_j\in\mathfrak o$; so the point
lies in $R^n$ and has constant term $\mathbf n$. For every $F\in J$,
$F(\phi(T))=0$ in $\Bbbk[T]$. After substitution it vanishes in $\crA$, hence
in $R$, since $R\hookrightarrow\crA$ is injective. This proves that the tuple
is a point of the original model, even if that model is not flat over
$\mathfrak o$. The inverse
of $\phi$ is a regular function on $U$, represented by a polynomial $q$ with
coefficients in $\Bbbk$, and evaluation commutes with $\ct$. For $\mathbf x$ in the
fiber, $t=q(\mathbf x)\in\crA$ has $\ct(t)=q(\mathbf n)=c_{\mathbf n}$, so
$t=c_{\mathbf n}+s$ with a unique $s\in\crP$, and $\mathbf x=\phi(t)$. If
$\phi(c_{\mathbf n}+s)$ is constant, it equals its constant term
$\mathbf n=\phi(c_{\mathbf n})$, and injectivity gives $s=0$ \src{C10}.
\end{proof}

The parametrization need not be defined over $\mathfrak o$: every nonconstant term
lies in $\crP$, and only the constant term must lie in $\mathfrak o$
\src{C11, C13}. Source C11 states the theorem for $\Bbbk$ algebraically closed or
$\Bbbk=\R$; it holds for every $\Bbbk$ of characteristic zero by
\cref{odg:cr:thm:affine}. Only the geometric fiber over $\Bbbk$ is assumed smooth:
the model may have bad reduction at primes of $\mathfrak o$ \src{C11, C13, C14}. No
integer-part assertion is made for an arbitrary $\mathfrak o$ or $\Gamma$ \src{C11}.

For example the model $2Y=0$ in $\Aff^2_\Z$ has generic fiber an affine line
and fiber $\Aff^2_{\mathbb F_2}$ at the prime $2$. Its coordinate ring has
nonzero $\Z$-torsion, so it is not flat over $\Z$. Nevertheless its omnific
points are exactly $(x,0)$, $x\in\Oz$, and the fiber over $(n,0)$ is
$\{(n+s,0):s\in\Pi\}$. The theorem uses the generic geometry and the injective
coefficient maps, not smoothness or flatness of the integral model.

\begin{corollary}[Omnific points on smooth affine curves \src{C10, C11, C13, C14}]\label{odg:cr:cor:omnific}
Let $\mathcal C\subseteq\Aff^n_\Z$ be a closed subscheme whose generic
fiber $C=\mathcal C_\Q$ is a smooth geometrically integral curve (equivalently, whose
real or complex fiber is one).
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)},leftmargin=*]
\item If $C_\C\not\cong\Aff^1_\C$ (equivalently $C\not\cong\Aff^1_\Q$, or
$C_\R\not\cong\Aff^1_\R$), then $\mathcal C(\Oz)=\mathcal C(\Z)$ and
$\mathcal C(\Og)=\mathcal C(\Z[i])$.
\item If $C\cong\Aff^1_\Q$, then $\mathcal C(\Oz)\ne\mathcal C(\Z)$ if and only if
$\mathcal C(\Z)\ne\varnothing$. For an isomorphism $\phi:\Aff^1_\R\to C_\R$ and
$\mathbf n\in\mathcal C(\Z)$, the fiber of $\ct$ over $\mathbf n$ is exactly
$\{\phi(\phi^{-1}(\mathbf n)+s):s\in\Pi\}$, parametrized bijectively; the Gaussian
fiber is parametrized by $\Pi_\C$.
\item Accordingly, either $\mathcal C(\Oz)$ is empty, or it equals the set
$\mathcal C(\Z)$, or every nonempty fiber of $\ct$ on it is a proper class.
\end{enumerate}
The same holds for a model over $\Z[i]$ and its points in $\Og$, with the complex
fiber in place of $C$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Smoothness and geometric integrality are invariant under extension of fields of
characteristic zero, in both directions; the affine-line condition descends
across arbitrary extensions by \cref{odg:cr:thm:affine}.
A finite tuple of omnific integers lies in
$\mathcal R_\Z(\R,\Gamma)$ for a set-sized $\Gamma$ (\cref{odg:lem:workspace}); apply
\cref{odg:cr:thm:arith} with $(\mathfrak o,\Bbbk)=(\Z,\R)$ or $(\Z[i],\C)$. The
formula of \cref{odg:cr:thm:arith}(ii) is finite polynomial evaluation, so it makes
sense for every $s\in\Pi$; every given point lies in a workspace where its
parameter exists uniquely. Since $\ct$ is a retraction, an omnific point has an
ordinary constant term, so $\mathcal C(\Z)=\varnothing$ forces
$\mathcal C(\Oz)=\varnothing$. A nonempty fiber in case (b) contains the distinct
points for $s=\omega^\alpha$, $\alpha$ ranging over the positive ordinals, so it is a
proper class \src{C10, C11, C13}. This argument uses an injective class-indexed
family, not a scheme over a proper-class ring. For Gaussian fibers choose a
complex affine-line isomorphism and replace $\Pi$ by $\Pi_\C$; the same real
monomials already witness proper-class size.
\end{proof}

Source C14 states (a) and (b) with the generic fiber over $\Q$, source C10 with
the complex fiber, and sources C11 and C13 with the real fiber; the conditions agree
by \cref{odg:cr:lem:formA1}. In the rigid case the retraction
$\mathcal C(\Oz)\to\mathcal C(\Z)$ is a bijection, not merely a surjection, and in
the affine-line case it is surjective onto the integer points with explicitly
parametrized fibers. The equality $\mathcal C(\Oz)=\mathcal C(\Z)$ is not an
algorithm for the integer points and does not assert that there are finitely many
\src{C10, C11, C13, C14}. The rigidity and fiber formulas also hold in one fixed
workspace, with $\Pi$ replaced by $\Pi_\R(\Gamma)$ \src{C10, C13}. That fiber is
a set: the proper-class conclusion uses all surreal supports. A nonzero fixed
$\Gamma$ still gives infinitely many distinct points, using
$s=\omega^{m\gamma}$ for $m\ge1$ and a fixed $\gamma>0$; for $\Gamma=0$ each
fiber is a singleton.

\begin{example}[Complete fibers \src{C10, C11, C13}]\label{odg:cr:ex:fibers}
On $Y=X^2$ the fiber over $(n,n^2)$ is $\{(n+s,\ n^2+2ns+s^2):s\in\Pi\}$. On
$Y=X^2-X+3$ with $\phi(T)=(2+T,\,5+3T+T^2)$, the fiber over $(2,5)$ is
$\{(2+u,\ 5+3u+u^2):u\in\Pi\}$; $u=\omega$ gives a point of finite support and
$u=\sum_{n\ge1}\omega^{1/n}$ one of infinite support, both by the same formula.
\end{example}

\begin{lemma}[Integer polynomial arcs \src{C14}]\label{odg:cr:lem:arcs}
Let $\mathcal C\subseteq\Aff^n_\Z$ be a closed subscheme with generic fiber
isomorphic to $\Aff^1_\Q$.
Through every $\mathbf n\in\mathcal C(\Z)$ there are polynomials $q_i(S)\in\Z[S]$
with $q_i(0)=n_i$ such that $\mathbf q:R\to\mathcal C(R)$ is injective for every
domain $R\supseteq\Z$ of characteristic zero. Over $\Oz$ every nonordinary parameter
has a nonordinary image, and parameters in $\Pi$ stay in the fiber of $\ct$ over
$\mathbf n$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write an isomorphism $\Aff^1_\Q\to\mathcal C_\Q$ as $\mathbf p(T)$ with
$p_i\in\Q[T]$, and its inverse as a polynomial $u$ with rational coefficients, so
that $u(\mathbf p(T))=T$. Put $t_0=u(\mathbf n)\in\Q$, expand
$p_i(t_0+T)=\sum_jc_{ij}T^j$ with $c_{i0}=n_i$, and choose an ordinary $N>0$
clearing the denominators of all $c_{ij}$ with $j\ge1$. The coefficient of
$S^j$ is $N^jc_{ij}=N^{j-1}(Nc_{ij})\in\Z$, while the constant coefficient
is already $n_i$. Thus $q_i(S)=p_i(t_0+NS)\in\Z[S]$ and $q_i(0)=n_i$.
The defining polynomials of $\mathcal C$ vanish on
$\mathbf q(S)$ over $\Q$, hence in $\Z[S]$. If $\mathbf q(s)=\mathbf q(s')$ in $R$,
apply $u$ in $\Frac R$ to get $t_0+Ns=t_0+Ns'$. Since $N\ne0$ in this
characteristic-zero field, $s=s'$. Some $q_i$ is nonconstant, since
$u(\mathbf q(S))=t_0+NS$, and by \cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}(d) its value at a
nonordinary omnific parameter is nonordinary. For $s\in\Pi$,
$\ct(\mathbf q(s))=\mathbf q(0)=\mathbf n$.
\end{proof}

The nonemptiness condition is needed: the model $2X=1$ with a free coordinate $Y$
has generic fiber $\Aff^1_\Q$ but neither integer nor omnific points \src{C14}.

The injection actually works for any $\Z$-torsion-free algebra $R$: if $D>0$
clears the coefficients of $u$ and $t_0$, the inverse identity becomes the
identity $(Du)(\mathbf q(S))=Dt_0+DN S$ in $\Z[S]$. Equality of two images
then gives $DN(s-s')=0$, and torsion-freeness gives $s=s'$. Characteristic
zero alone would not suffice for this argument. For the affine line and
the allowed choice $q(S)=2S$, the characteristic-zero ring
$\Z\times\mathbb F_2$ has distinct elements $(0,0)$ and $(0,1)$ with the
same image. The original domain hypothesis excludes such torsion.

\begin{proposition}[A congruence criterion \src{C14}]\label{odg:cr:prop:congruence}
Keep $\mathbf p$ and $u$ from \cref{odg:cr:lem:arcs}. Let $N_1>0$ clear the
denominators of the coefficients of $u$, and $N_2>0$ satisfy
$P_i(V)=N_2\,p_i(V/N_1)\in\Z[V]$ for all $i$. Then $n\mapsto\mathbf p(n/N_1)$ is a
bijection from
\[
 \{n\in\Z:P_i(n)\equiv0\pmod{N_2}\text{ for all }i\}
\]
onto $\mathcal C(\Z)$. This set is a union of residue classes modulo $N_2$, and
whether it is empty is decided by checking $0\le n<N_2$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $\mathbf x\in\mathcal C(\Z)$, $u(\mathbf x)\in N_1^{-1}\Z$; write it $n/N_1$. Then
$x_i=p_i(n/N_1)=P_i(n)/N_2$ are integers, so $n$ satisfies the congruences.
Conversely the congruences make the coordinates integral, and the defining
polynomials vanish identically on $\mathbf p$. The inverse identity gives the
bijection, with inverse $\mathbf x\mapsto N_1u(\mathbf x)$. Finally,
$P_i(n+N_2m)-P_i(n)$ is divisible by $N_2$ for every integer $m$, so the
congruences depend only on the residue class of $n$. The finite test includes
$N_2=1$, when its sole residue is $0$ and all parameters are admissible.
\end{proof}

This is an algorithm only after an affine-line isomorphism and its inverse are
supplied; it is not an algorithm for integer points of arbitrary curves
\src{C14}. For $3Y=X^2-X$ take $p_1=T$, $p_2=(T^2-T)/3$ and $u=X$: then $N_1=1$,
$N_2=3$, the admissible residues are $0$ and $1$ modulo $3$, and the arcs through
$(0,0)$ and $(1,0)$ are $(3S,\,3S^2-S)$ and $(1+3S,\,3S^2+S)$, valid for every
omnific parameter. In the letters above, $N$, $N_1$, $N_2$ are the sources' $M$,
$D$, $L$ (\cref{odg:tab:renames}).

Thus, once $\mathbf p$ and $u$ are supplied, the ordinary point set is either
empty or countably infinite: every admissible residue class contains infinitely
many integers, and the displayed parametrization is injective. This statement
concerns the affine-line branch only. Moreover the constructed integer arc
$\mathbf q$ parametrizes the entire omnific fiber over $\mathbf n$ when its
parameter ranges over $\Pi$: multiplication by the nonzero ordinary integer
$N$ is a bijection of $\Pi$. In the exact fiber formula,
$\mathbf p(t_0+s)=\mathbf q(s/N)$, and $s/N\in\Pi$. Thus denominator clearing
retains the full fiber, including its infinite-support points.

\begin{corollary}[Polynomial witnesses \src{C13}; also \src{C12}]\label{odg:cr:cor:polywitness}
For $\mathcal C$ as in \cref{odg:cr:cor:omnific}, with real fiber $C_\R$, the
following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=*]
\item $\mathcal C$ has a nonordinary omnific point;
\item $C_\R\cong\Aff^1_\R$ and $\mathcal C(\Z)\ne\varnothing$;
\item $C_\R$ has a nonconstant real polynomial arc through an integer point;
\item $\mathcal C$ has a nonordinary omnific point all of whose coordinates have
finite support.
\end{enumerate}
Every nonordinary omnific point has the same constant term as some nonordinary
point of finite support.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
(i) and (ii) are equivalent by \cref{odg:cr:cor:omnific}. Given (ii) and
$\mathbf n=\phi(t_0)$, the arc $T\mapsto\phi(t_0+T)$ gives (iii). Given (iii),
translate the real parameter so that the integer point occurs at $T=0$, then
substitute $T=\omega^\gamma$ with $\gamma>0$ (\cref{odg:prop:polylift}); the
coordinates have finite support and integer constants, and some coordinate has
positive degree. Clearly (iv) implies (i). Given a nonordinary point, (i) gives
the affine-line case; translating its isomorphism to the constant term of that
point and making the same substitution proves the last assertion.
\end{proof}

For smooth affine curves this answers the existence part of \cref{odg:q:search}:
no nonordinary point exists only because of infinite supports
(\cref{odg:cr:sub:settled}). It is stronger than \cref{odg:thm:specialization},
which starts from a finite-support point, and it removes the finite-support
hypothesis there for smooth curves \src{C12, C13}. It is not asserted for singular
curves or in higher dimension, and it gives no algorithm for the integer points
\src{C13}. Source C12 states the geometric part: over an algebraically closed
field, $C(\crA)\ne C(\Bbbk)$ exactly when there is a nonconstant morphism
$\Aff^1\to C$, which is the last equivalence of \cref{odg:cr:thm:affine}; this
classifies which curves acquire points, not the shape of an arbitrary
infinite-support point. Status (batch 31): for singular curves source C16 proves the Gaussian form
and the real form at integer points with a real preimage under the normalization
(\cref{odg:sg:cor:gaussian,odg:sg:prop:arithfamily}); the remaining real case is
\cref{odg:sg:q:real}.

In the setting of \cref{odg:cr:cor:polywitness}, the affine-line isomorphism
also descends to $\Q$. Applying \cref{odg:cr:lem:arcs} at the same integer point
therefore gives a stronger choice of witness: every coordinate can be taken in
$\Z[\omega^\gamma]$ for one $\gamma>0$. The finite-support witness may have
integer coefficients even when the original point has arbitrary real
coefficients and infinite support. This does not put the original point on a
polynomial arc with a finite-support parameter.

\begin{theorem}[Descent for separated models \src{C12}]\label{odg:cr:thm:descent}
Let $\mathcal X$ be a separated scheme of finite type over $\Z$ and
$X=\mathcal X_\C$. Suppose $X(\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma))=X(\C)$ for every set-sized
$\Gamma\subseteq\No$. Then inclusion and $\ct$ are inverse bijections
$\mathcal X(\Z)\cong\mathcal X(\Oz_\Gamma)$ for every such $\Gamma$, and
$\mathcal X(\Oz)=\mathcal X(\Z)$ in the class convention.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $p\in\mathcal X(\Oz_\Gamma)$ and $p_0=\ct(p)\in\mathcal X(\Z)$, and denote
the constant extension of $p_0$ to $\Oz_\Gamma$ by $\widetilde p_0$.
The image of $p$
in $X(\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma))$ is constant, and since
$\Oz_\Gamma\to\Z\to\C$ and $\Oz_\Gamma\to\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma)\to\C$ are compatible
with $\ct$, it is the image of $p_0$. So $p$ and $\widetilde p_0$ agree over the field
$\mathbb H_\C(\Gamma)$, in which the domain $\Oz_\Gamma$ embeds, hence over
$\Oz_\Gamma$ by \cref{odg:cr:lem:separated}. Explicitly, their equalizer is
a closed subscheme of $\Spec\Oz_\Gamma$ whose ideal maps to zero in that
field; injectivity makes the ideal zero. Specialization of a constant point
returns $p_0$, proving both inverse identities. In the class convention a
point is represented in a set-sized workspace, so the same equality applies
to each representative and remains true after enlarging the workspace.
\end{proof}

No smoothness of $\mathcal X$ at any prime and no flatness of
$\Oz_\Gamma\to\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma)$ is used; smoothness enters only through the
rigidity of the complex fiber, and separatedness recovers the integral point from
the ambient field \src{C12}. The hypothesis holds when $X$ is a smooth affine curve
other than $\Aff^1_\C$, a smooth projective curve of positive genus, an abelian or
semiabelian variety, a smooth proper variety with globally generated cotangent
sheaf, or a separated scheme with a quasi-finite map to one of these
(\cref{odg:cr:thm:affine,odg:cr:thm:positivegenus,odg:cr:thm:abelian,odg:cr:thm:semiabelian,odg:cr:cor:hahnproper,odg:cr:thm:quasifinite}),
so all of these have only ordinary omnific points on every separated integral
model \src{C12}. This is a reduction theorem, not an algorithm for the ordinary
points, and it gives no height bound \src{C12}.

\subsection{Projective coordinates}\label{odg:cr:sub:projective}

A nonzero homogeneous tuple over a field determines a projective point. Over a
domain $S$, a tuple $\mathbf a$ generates the coordinate ideal
$J=\sum_i Sa_i$. If $J$ is invertible, the surjection $S^{N+1}\to J$ defines
a projective $S$-point, as proved below. Here invertibility means that
$JJ'=S$ for a fractional ideal $J'\subseteq\Frac S$; it does not mean
$J=S$. Every nonzero principal ideal is invertible, including a proper one.
The special case $J=S$ is a unimodular tuple
\src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14}. By \cref{odg:cor:projectiveclear},
every point of $\mathbb P^n(\No)$ has coordinates in $\Pi^{n+1}$, and such
coordinates generate a proper ideal; sources C10 and C12 reprove this clearing,
which may fail when the clearing exponent is confined to a fixed workspace
\src{C10}. So raw homogeneous solutions can be abundant while unimodular ones are
constant; there is no conflict.

\begin{theorem}[Unimodular coordinates \src{C14}; for $\mathfrak o=\Bbbk$ \src{C10, C13}]\label{odg:cr:thm:unimodular}
Let $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$ be a subring, $R=\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$,
and $X\subseteq\mathbb P^N_\Bbbk$ a closed subscheme with $X(\crA)=X(\Bbbk)$. If a
unimodular tuple $\mathbf a\in R^{N+1}$ satisfies the homogeneous equations of $X$,
then $\mathbf a=\ct(\mathbf a)\in\mathfrak o^{N+1}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Choose $b_i\in R$ with $\sum_ib_ia_i=1$ and put $c_i=\ct(a_i)$, $d_i=\ct(b_i)$, so
$\sum_id_ic_i=1$. In particular $\mathbf c$ is nonzero and unimodular over
$\mathfrak o$. The original unit-ideal identity remains valid in $\crA$, so
the tuple defines a point of $X(\crA)$, which is
constant; applying $\ct$ identifies it with $[c_0:\cdots:c_N]$. So
$a_ic_j=a_jc_i$ in $\crH$ for all $i,j$. Put $s=\sum_jd_ja_j\in R$. Then
$sc_i=\sum_jd_ja_jc_i=a_i\sum_jd_jc_j=a_i$, and $1=\sum_ib_ia_i=s\sum_ib_ic_i$, so
$s\in R^\times=\mathfrak o^\times$ (\cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}). As $\ct(s)=\sum d_jc_j=1$
and $s$ is constant, $s=1$ and $a_i=c_i$. This last step upgrades equality of
projective points to equality of the given tuples: the residual scalar is
forced to be the constant unit $1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Invertible coordinate ideals \src{C12}]\label{odg:cr:lem:coordinatepoint}
Let $S$ be a domain contained in a field $L$, $X\subseteq\mathbb P^N_S$ a closed
subscheme, and $\mathbf a\in S^{N+1}$ a tuple with $[\mathbf a]\in X(L)$. If
$\langle\mathbf a\rangle$ is invertible, the tuple defines a morphism
$\Spec S\to X$ with that $L$-point.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Put $J=\langle\mathbf a\rangle$. The surjection $S^{N+1}\to J$, sending the
$i$th basis vector to $a_i$, is an invertible quotient. It defines a morphism
$f:\Spec S\to\mathbb P^N_S$ \cite[Tag 01ND]{StacksProjSpace}. Under the natural
identification $J\otimes_S L\cong L$, its generating sections become precisely
the coordinates $a_i$, so $f_L=[\mathbf a]$. The inverse image of $X$ is a
closed subscheme of $\Spec S$, say with ideal $I$. Since $f_L$ factors through
$X$, every element of $I$ maps to zero in $L$. Injectivity of $S\hookrightarrow L$
gives $I=0$, proving that $f$ factors through $X$.
\end{proof}

Only this sufficient direction is used; no claim is made that every projective
point over such a ring has a global unimodular coordinate tuple \src{C12}.

\begin{theorem}[Coordinate ideals on rigid targets \src{C12}]\label{odg:cr:thm:ideals}
Let $X\subseteq\mathbb P^N_\Bbbk$ be projective with $X(\crA)=X(\Bbbk)$, and let a nonzero
$\mathbf a\in\crA^{N+1}$ represent $p\in X(\crH)$. Then $\langle\mathbf a\rangle$ is
invertible if and only if it is principal, if and only if $p\in X(\Bbbk)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $\langle\mathbf a\rangle$ is invertible, \cref{odg:cr:lem:coordinatepoint} gives an
$\crA$-point, which is constant. If $p=[c_0:\cdots:c_N]$ with $c_i\in\Bbbk$, then
$a_i=\mu c_i$ with $\mu=a_j/c_j\in\crA$ for some $c_j\ne0$, and the constants $c_i$
generate the unit ideal, so $\langle\mathbf a\rangle=\mu\crA$. Here $\mu\ne0$
because $\mathbf a\ne0$. Its inverse fractional ideal is
$\mu^{-1}\crA$, proving invertibility without requiring $\mu$ to be a unit.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Omnific coordinate ideals \src{C12}]\label{odg:cr:thm:ozideal}
Let $\mathcal X\subseteq\mathbb P^N_\Z$ be closed with complex fiber $X$ satisfying
$X(\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma))=X(\C)$ for every set-sized $\Gamma$. Let
$\mathbf a\in\Oz^{N+1}$ be nonzero with $[\mathbf a]\in\mathcal X(\No)$. Then
$\langle\mathbf a\rangle$ is invertible if and only if it is principal, if and only if
$[\mathbf a]\in\mathcal X(\Q)$. If $\langle\mathbf a\rangle=\Oz$, every $a_i$ is an
ordinary integer.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $J=\sum_i\Oz a_i$. Invertibility gives finitely many
$b_i\in\No$ such that
\[
 \sum_i a_i b_i=1,\qquad a_jb_i\in\Oz\quad\text{for all }i,j.
\]
Indeed, expand a finite expression of $1$ in $JJ'$ in terms of the given
generators $a_i$, absorbing its coefficients into $b_i\in J'$.
Write each $b_i$ as a fraction of omnific integers. Put their numerators and
nonzero denominators, all $a_i$, and all products $a_jb_i$ into one workspace.
Then $b_i\in\Frac(\Oz_\Gamma)$ and
$J'_{\Gamma}=\sum_i\Oz_\Gamma b_i$ is a fractional ideal there (the product
of the chosen denominators clears it). The displayed identities give
$J_\Gamma J'_\Gamma=\Oz_\Gamma$. This uses finitely many witnesses, not a
Noetherian hypothesis or a spectrum of a proper-class ring. By
\cref{odg:cr:lem:coordinatepoint} the tuple is a point of $\mathcal X(\Oz_\Gamma)$,
which is ordinary by \cref{odg:cr:thm:descent}; so $[\mathbf a]$ is rational. If
$[\mathbf a]$ is rational, choose a primitive integer representative $\mathbf c$ and
integers $r_i$ with $\sum r_ic_i=1$; then $\mathbf a=\mu\mathbf c$ with
$\mu=\sum r_ia_i\in\Oz$ and $\langle\mathbf a\rangle=\mu\Oz$ (as after
\cref{odg:thm:realray}). A nonzero principal ideal is invertible. If
$\langle\mathbf a\rangle=\Oz$, then $\mu\in\Oz^\times=\{\pm1\}$, giving the
ordinary tuple $\mathbf a=\pm\mathbf c$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Rational projective points \src{C14; also C10, C12, C13, C15}]\label{odg:cr:cor:projectiveQ}
Let integer homogeneous equations define $\mathcal X\subseteq\mathbb P^N_\Z$ whose
complex fiber is smooth and proper with globally generated cotangent sheaf, for
example a smooth projective curve of positive genus or a projectively embedded
abelian variety. Every unimodular omnific solution tuple is an ordinary integer
tuple, and likewise with $\Og$ and $\Z[i]$. For a smooth projective curve of
positive genus over $\Q$ in such an embedding, the points with a unimodular omnific
representative are exactly the points of $\mathcal X(\Q)$, their unimodular
representatives are $\pm$ the primitive integer representatives, and every other
point of $\mathcal X(\No)$ represented in $\Oz^{N+1}$ has a coordinate ideal that is
neither invertible nor principal.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Put the tuple and a unit-ideal witness into one workspace and apply
\cref{odg:cr:cor:hahnproper,odg:cr:thm:unimodular} with $(\mathfrak o,\Bbbk)=(\Z,\C)$
or $(\Z[i],\C)$; the constant tuple lies in $\Z^{N+1}$ or $\Z[i]^{N+1}$. A rational
point has a primitive integer representative with an ordinary B\'ezout witness,
hence unimodular over $\Oz$ \src{C14}. The last assertion is
\cref{odg:cr:thm:ozideal} \src{C12}.
\end{proof}

For the Gaussian analogue, a $\Q(i)$-point has a primitive $\Z[i]$-tuple
because $\Z[i]$ is a Euclidean domain. Its B\'ezout identity makes it
unimodular over $\Og$. Conversely the first assertion makes any unimodular
$\Og$-tuple Gaussian integral. Two primitive Gaussian representatives of
the same point differ by one of $1,-1,i,-i$. Thus in the same rigid setting
the Gaussian unimodular representatives are exactly the primitive ordinary
Gaussian tuples, up to those units \src{C14}.

An ordinary integer tuple is unimodular over $\Oz$ exactly when its coordinates
generate $\Z$: apply $\ct$ to an omnific witness, and use an ordinary B\'ezout
identity for the converse \src{C13}. For the Fermat curve of exponent $n\ge3$,
\cref{odg:cr:thm:unimodular} with $\mathfrak o=\Bbbk=\C$ gives a second route to
\cref{odg:thm:unimodularfermat}, without its nonvanishing hypothesis on the
coordinates; excluding particular ordinary
points still needs the theorem of Wiles and Taylor--Wiles (\cref{odg:cor:flt})
\src{C13, C14, C15}. This is a specialization, not a new Fermat result. The unimodular
Pythagorean family of \cref{odg:prop:pythagoras} shows that positive genus cannot be
dropped \src{C13}. The corollary uses actual unit-ideal witnesses; absence of a
common nonunit divisor is a different condition in the non-B\'ezout ring $\Oz$, and
\cref{odg:q:primitivefermat} is not answered \src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14}. It is
complementary to \cref{odg:frac:cor:curves}: there, on rational curves, primitive
coordinates exist exactly at points of rational specialization; here, on rigid
projective targets, only constant points have unimodular or invertible coordinates.
The rationality conditions are different: the earlier result tests the
constant-term specialization, whereas the rigid-target result tests the
projective point itself. For example $[\omega:1]\in\mathbb P^1(\No)$ has
coordinate ideal $\Oz$ and specialization $[0:1]\in\mathbb P^1(\Q)$, but
is not a rational point. Thus a principal coordinate ideal alone does not
force rationality on a rational curve. For a constant projective direction,
the two criteria agree by \cref{odg:thm:realray}.

\begin{example}[A noninvertible coordinate ideal \src{C12}]\label{odg:cr:ex:denominator}
Let $\mathcal E:\ Y^2Z=X^3-XZ^2+Z^3$, whose affine cubic has $\Delta_0=23$.
It is squarefree, so the affine curve is smooth; its only point at infinity
is $[0:1:0]$, where the derivative of the defining polynomial with respect
to $Z$ is $1$. Thus the projective complex fiber is a smooth genus-one cubic.
In $\No$
put $x=\omega^2$ and
\[
 y=\omega^3\bigl(1-\omega^{-4}+\omega^{-6}\bigr)^{1/2}
 =\omega^3-\tfrac12\omega^{-1}+\tfrac12\omega^{-3}-\tfrac18\omega^{-5}
 +\tfrac14\omega^{-7}-\tfrac3{16}\omega^{-9}+\cdots,
\]
so $y^2=x^3-x+1$ and $y\notin\BR$. With $M=\omega^\omega$ the tuple
$(Mx,My,M)$ is purely infinite: the exponents of $y$ are ordinary integers,
and $\omega+n>0$ for every ordinary integer $n$. Multiplication by $M$ shifts
these exponents by $\omega$. The tuple
represents the nonrational point $[x:y:1]$. By \cref{odg:cr:thm:ozideal} the ideal
$\langle Mx,My,M\rangle=\langle My,M\rangle$ is neither invertible nor principal. The
monomial $M$ is not a common divisor in $\Oz$, since $My/M=y\notin\Oz$, but
$\omega^{\omega/2}$ is: dividing each coordinate by it leaves only exponents
$\omega/2+n>0$, with $n$ ordinary. This divisor is a nonunit, so the tuple is
not primitive and does not bear on the primitive question. The ideal equality
uses $Mx=xM$ and $x=\omega^2\in\Oz$; it does not assert that $M$ divides $My$.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[Field points outside the ring \src{C10; also C12, C13, C14, C15}]\label{odg:cr:rem:fieldpoints}
The theorems concern points over the support-restricted rings, not over $\No$ or
$\No[i]$. For real $a,b$ with $4a^3+27b^2\ne0$, the point
$x=\omega$, $y=\omega^{3/2}\bigl(1+a\omega^{-2}+b\omega^{-3}\bigr)^{1/2}$ of
$y^2=x^3+ax+b$ lies in $\No$; its first correction term is
$\tfrac a2\omega^{-1/2}$ if $a\ne0$ and $\tfrac b2\omega^{-3/2}$ otherwise, so
$y\notin\BR$ \src{C10}. Likewise $x=\omega^2$,
$y=\omega^3(1+\omega^{-4})^{1/2}=\omega^3+\tfrac12\omega^{-1}-\tfrac18\omega^{-5}+\cdots$
on $y^2=x^3+x$ \src{C14}, the point of \cref{odg:cr:ex:denominator}
\src{C12, C13}, and, for real $a,b$, any positive infinite $x$ with the positive square
root of $x^3+ax+b$ in the real closed field $\No$ \src{C15}. This last existence
statement by itself does not locate a negative tail: for example $a=b=0$,
$x=\omega^2$ gives $y=\omega^3$ in $\Oz$, on a singular cubic. For the explicit
nonsingular examples above the displayed first negative coefficient proves
failure of membership in $\BR$. The binomial expansions are support-controlled
Hahn sums: powers of the infinitesimal inside the square root have
reverse-well-ordered union of supports and finitely many contributions at
each exponent. Truncating
the tail changes the equation: replacing $y$ by $\omega^3$ in
\cref{odg:cr:ex:denominator} turns $\omega^6-\omega^2+1$ into $\omega^6$
\src{C12, C14}. Clearing such a point by \cref{odg:cor:projectiveclear} gives an
omnific tuple with a proper coordinate ideal, not an affine omnific point
\src{C10}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Singular curves and normalization}\label{odg:cr:sub:singular}

The classification of \cref{odg:cr:thm:affine} is for smooth curves. The short Weierstrass cubics are settled
completely (\cref{odg:cr:cor:weierstrass,odg:cr:prop:singular}) \src{C12}. The node
and the cusp have normalizations isomorphic to $\Aff^1$ but are not affine lines
themselves, so smoothness cannot be replaced by integrality in
\cref{odg:cr:thm:affine} \src{C14}. In the other direction a singular curve that is a
locally closed subscheme of an abelian variety (\cref{odg:cr:thm:abelian}) or has a
quasi-finite map to a rigid target (\cref{odg:cr:thm:quasifinite}) is rigid; the
embedding or map is a substantial extra hypothesis, and not every singular curve
has one \src{C13, C14}. Sources C10--C15 leave reducible affine curves
unclassified. A map from their coordinate ring to the domain $\crA$ kills the
nilradical and has prime kernel, hence factors through an irreducible component;
that component may be singular \src{C12}. Source C16 treats this case over
algebraically closed fields in \cref{odg:sg:thm:allcurves}.

A normalization lift over $\crA$ cannot be assumed merely from integrality.
For a nonconstant point of an integral constant curve the coordinate map is
injective, as proved before \cref{odg:sg:lem:place}, and identifies its function
field with a subfield of $\Frac\crA$. The lift exists exactly when the image
of the normalized coordinate ring lies in $\crA$. Thus normality of $\crA$
in its own fraction field would suffice, by the universal property of
normalization \cite[Lemma 29.55.5(4)]{StacksNormal}. Failure of normality
removes that sufficient hypothesis; it does not by itself produce a constant
curve point without a lift.

For the full class non-normality is \cref{odg:prop:notnormal}, with the witness
$\sqrt{\omega^2+1}$ of sources 01, 02 and 05. Sources C10 and C13 give the same
witness in a fixed workspace, and source C16 gives it again: for
$\Gamma=\Q+\Q\omega\subseteq\No$ (C13 allows any $\Q+\Q H$ with $H$ above every ordinary
integer, and C16 any $0<e<H$ in $\Gamma$ with $ne<H$ for every ordinary $n$, with
$\omega^e$ in place of $\omega$),
$q=\sqrt{\omega^2+1}=\omega+\tfrac12\omega^{-1}-\tfrac18\omega^{-3}+\cdots$ is integral
over $\mathcal A_\R(\Gamma)$ and not in it, while $\omega^\omega q\in\mathcal A_\R(\Gamma)$
because all its exponents $\omega+1-2n$ are positive for ordinary $n\ge0$;
their decreasing sequence is a valid reverse-well-ordered support, so
$q\in\Frac\mathcal A_\R(\Gamma)\setminus\mathcal A_\R(\Gamma)$. Source C12 gives the
same phenomenon for $\Gamma=\Z^2$ ordered lexicographically, with $g=(0,1)$,
$h=(1,0)$, $z=\omega^g(1+\omega^{-2g})^{1/2}$ and $\omega^hz\in\crA$. Only these
fixed-workspace forms are new.

The dominating scale matters: an integral element of
the larger field $\crH$ need not lie in $\Frac\crA$ \src{C13}. For $\Gamma=\Z$,
$\crA=\Bbbk[\omega]$ is integrally closed, and
$z=\omega(1+\omega^{-2})^{1/2}$ is integral over it but not in it; source C14 uses
this only to show that integrality in the ambient field does not keep a lift in the
ring, and explicitly does not claim non-normality. Source C15 gives the same element, as
$t^{-1}(1+t^2)^{1/2}$ in the $t$-convention, and concludes that normality is unavailable
even for $\Gamma=\Z$. That conclusion is not correct: for $\Gamma=\Z$ the ring
$\crA=\Bbbk[\omega]$ is a polynomial ring, hence normal, and the element, whose square
$\omega^2+1$ is not a square in $\Bbbk(\omega)$, lies outside $\Frac\crA$; it shows only
that $\crA$ is not integrally closed in the larger field $\crH$. It is
not an obstruction to the normalization lift of a nonconstant curve point:
for $\Gamma=\Z$, that lift does exist because $\Bbbk[\omega]$ is normal
in its own fraction field. Indeed $\omega^2+1$ has simple roots over
$\bar\Bbbk$, whereas the order of a zero or pole of a rational square is
even; thus its square root is not in $\Bbbk(\omega)$
(\cref{odg:app:provenance}). Source C15's
statement that $\crA$ is not assumed normal and that such a lift is not available as an
unstated step is correct. Nor is a non-normality example
by itself a point of a constant curve that fails to lift \src{C13}.

For an arbitrary
morphism $f:X\to G$ to a rigid target, \cref{odg:cr:prop:inherit} gives only
$f(x)=f(\ct x)$; applied to an Albanese map this confines nonconstant directions to
its fibers, and positive-dimensional fibers need not be rigid \src{C10}. The
remaining problem is \cref{odg:cr:q:singular}. Status (batch 31): source C16 classifies the
geometrically integral affine curves, singular or not, and the affine schemes of dimension
at most one over an algebraically closed field (\cref{odg:sg:thm:main,odg:sg:thm:allcurves},
\cref{odg:sg:sub:singular}); what remains of the question is the lifting problem
\cref{odg:sg:q:lifting} and the real fibers \cref{odg:sg:q:real}.

\subsection{Singular curves: normalization and conductor certificates}\label{odg:sg:sub:singular}

This subsection is built from a seventh manuscript on the subject of the part,
\emph{Singular Curves over Omnific Integers: Conductor differentials, a complete
Hahn-rigidity criterion, and polynomial witnesses at every surreal scale}, tagged
\src{C16} after its file prefix \fn{16-singular-curves-} (\cref{odg:sub:provenance}).
It pins \texttt{bcac55a}. At that pin sources C10--C14 were placed, with their audits,
but their material was not yet in this article, and source C15 was not yet placed; source
C16 read the audits of sources C13 and C14, which leave singular curves outside the
smooth classification, and saw none of the other curve manuscripts. It answers the first
two clauses of \cref{odg:cr:q:singular}: over every field of characteristic zero and for
every $\Gamma\ne0$, a geometrically integral affine curve, singular or not, has a
nonconstant $\crA$-point exactly when its normalization is the affine line
(\cref{odg:sg:thm:main}). The proof does not assume a normalization lift
over $\crA$: its existence cannot be inferred from integrality alone
(\cref{odg:cr:sub:singular}). A single
conductor element of the curve clears every power of a contracted regular differential
after a fixed finite multiplication (Seidenberg's identity), and the discrete valuation at
the one boundary point that a nonconstant point selects turns this into a contradiction
after finitely many derivatives. Labels of this subsection carry the sub-prefix
\fn{odg:sg:}. The proof review in \fn{RECONCILIATION.md} now covers
the criterion, its conductor and boundary-place arguments, and the structural
consequences through \cref{odg:sg:cor:fibre}; the later applications remain
outside that review. Throughout this subsection $\Gamma\ne0$ unless a
statement explicitly treats the trivial case. If $\Gamma=0$, then
$\crA=\Bbbk$ and every point is constant; an existence criterion for
nonconstant points must exclude this case.

Some statements of source C16 are already printed above and are not reprinted; source C16
is added to their credits. Its ring lemma (units, the retraction, transcendence of
nonconstant elements, no nonzero element of positive valuation) is
\cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}; its Euler derivations and their detection property are
\cref{odg:cr:lem:euler,odg:cr:lem:separation}, including the caution that one derivation
need not detect every nonconstant series; its non-normality example is the
fixed-workspace witness of \cref{odg:cr:sub:singular}; its descent from $\bar\Bbbk$ to
$\Bbbk$ is the argument of \cref{odg:cr:lem:formA1}; its smooth case is
\cref{odg:cr:thm:affine}, for which its proof is a third route to the rigid direction
(\cref{odg:sg:rem:thirdroute}); and its elliptic B\'ezout certificate is
\eqref{odg:cr:eq:cubic} at $(a,b)=(-1,1)$. Three short additions carry the tag
\mergetag{} and have complete proofs.

\paragraph{Translation and letters.} Source C16 uses the $t$-convention
$t^\gamma=\omega^{-\gamma}$. Its ring $B_{k,\Gamma}$ of Hahn series with nonpositive
$t$-exponents is $\crA$ with exponents negated; it is not the valuation ring, and the
italic $B$ is a bilinear form here. Its field $K=k((t^\Gamma))$, valuation ring $O$ and
maximal ideal $\mathfrak m$ are $\crH$, $\crO$ and $\crm$. Its valuation
$v(f)=\min\supp_t f$ is the valuation $v=-\ddeg$ of \cref{odg:cr:sub:conventions},
unchanged. Its purely infinite parts $\mathcal J_\R$, $\mathcal J_\C$ are $\Pi$, $\Pi_{\C}$,
and its $\Oz[i]$ is the ring $\Og$ of this report, unchanged. Its Euler derivation $D_\lambda$ multiplies $t^\gamma$ by
$\lambda(\gamma)$; it is $\partial_{-\lambda}$ in the notation of \eqref{odg:cr:eq:euler},
the same family. Its letter $D$ also names a general derivation, written $\partial$ here,
and a coefficient subring, written $\mathfrak o$ ($D$ is the Pell parameter). The
following letters are renamed because source C16 uses each twice or because the part
reserves them: its coordinate ring $A$ and normalization $\widetilde A$ are
$\Bbbk[C]$ and $\Bbbk[\widetilde C]$ ($A$ is an abelian variety in this part); its smooth
projective model $X$ is $\overline C$, the smooth projective model of the function field
$F=\Frac\Bbbk[C]$, which completes $\widetilde C$; its boundary is
$\Sigma=\overline C\setminus\widetilde C$ and its centre $p$ is $Q$, as in
\cref{odg:cr:thm:logroute}; its conductor element $d$ is $\kappa$ ($d$ is a degree and
$\dif$ the differential); its length $m$ of a differential presentation is $\ell$ ($m$ is
the superelliptic exponent); its contraction $s=\alpha(D)$ is $\alpha(\partial)$, as
$\eta(\partial)$ in \cref{odg:cr:thm:logroute}; its formal variable $z$ and Taylor map $E$
are $w$ and $\mathcal T$ ($z$ is a coordinate and $E$ an elliptic curve in
\cref{odg:sg:sub:example}); in \cref{odg:sg:sub:super} its number $r$ of distinct roots
is $k$ and $r_\infty$ is $n_\infty$ ($r$ is the vanishing order of a differential in
\cref{odg:sg:sub:seidenberg}), and its cofactors $Q$, $S$ are $P_1$, $P_{\mathrm{odd}}$
($Q$ is the centre); its B\'ezout polynomial $V$ is $W$ ($V$ is a valuation ring); its
points $P$, $P_0$ are $\mathbf x$, $\mathbf x_0$; and its purely infinite parameter $h$ and
exponent $a$ in \cref{odg:sg:sub:arith} are $s$ and $\beta$. In this subsection $E$ is the
affine elliptic curve of \cref{odg:sg:sub:example} and nothing else, and $P_1$ is a
polynomial, not a boundary point as in \cref{odg:cr:lem:logdim}. The complete list is in
\cref{odg:tab:renames}.

Two readings are tempting and false. ``The normalization of $C$ is $\Aff^1$'' does not
mean ``$C\cong\Aff^1$'': the node and the cusp are singular, have normalization $\Aff^1$
and carry polynomial families (\cref{odg:cr:prop:singular}). And ``$C$ has a nonconstant
$\crA$-point'' does not mean that every nonconstant point lifts to the normalization, or
is a polynomial family evaluated at a Hahn parameter; the criterion is about existence.

\subsubsection{The criterion}\label{odg:sg:sub:criterion}

\begin{theorem}[Singular curve criterion \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:thm:main}
Let $\Bbbk$ be a field of characteristic zero, $\Gamma\ne0$, and $C$ a geometrically
integral affine curve of finite type over $\Bbbk$, with normalization
$\nu:\widetilde C\to C$. The following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=*]
\item $C(\crA)$ contains a point not in $C(\Bbbk)$;
\item $\widetilde C\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$;
\item there is a nonconstant $\Bbbk$-morphism $\Aff^1_\Bbbk\to C$;
\item there is a nonconstant point in $C(\Bbbk[T])$.
\end{enumerate}
When they hold, for every $0<\gamma\in\Gamma$ there is a nonconstant point whose
coordinates have finite support in $\{0,\gamma,2\gamma,\ldots,n\gamma\}$ for some ordinary
$n$.
\end{theorem}

Here $C(\Bbbk[T])$ is a polynomial family, not a single ordinary point: for a closed
embedding $C\hookrightarrow\Aff^N_\Bbbk$, condition (iv) asks for polynomials
$p_1(T),\ldots,p_N(T)$, not all constant, that satisfy the equations of $C$ identically.
The theorem does not say that every nonconstant point is such a family evaluated at a
Hahn parameter. For a smooth curve it is \cref{odg:cr:thm:affine}. The node, the cusp and
the singular short Weierstrass cubics (\cref{odg:cr:prop:singular}) are its first
singular exceptional cases, and it removes every singularity restriction from the
smooth classification while keeping arbitrary $\Gamma$. The proof, in
\cref{odg:sg:sub:proof}, has four steps: a nonconstant point makes the function field a
subfield of $\crH$; the conductor gives a fixed denominator for all powers of a contracted
differential (\cref{odg:sg:thm:conductor}); a regular differential has a positive
valuation at the boundary place (\cref{odg:sg:lem:place}); and a fixed denominator
against a positive discrete valuation is a contradiction (\cref{odg:sg:cor:jetbound}).

The generalized-series setting and the normal forms of $\Oz$ are classical
\cite{Conway,Gonshor,LM}; the finite identity used here is Seidenberg's
\cite{Seidenberg}; normalization, Riemann--Roch and Riemann--Hurwitz are standard
\cite{StacksNormal,StacksCurves,StacksRR,StacksRH}. Source C16 names as its contribution
the combination that removes all singularity restrictions from the affine-curve
dichotomy at arbitrary rank, the finite-order certificate and the repeated-root
consequences; it does not attribute Seidenberg's identity, smooth rigidity or
Riemann--Hurwitz to itself. ``Complete criterion'' refers to the class of curves and
points of \cref{odg:sg:thm:main}, not to a named problem of the literature. Its targeted
comparison did not find the combined singular statement, and it is not a priority
search. Van den Dries \cite{vdDries} concerns infinite points in \emph{some} discretely
ordered ring, not in the fixed ring $\Oz$; source C16 checked only its record and
abstract (\cref{odg:cr:sub:dor}).

Source C16's reason for the next step is the one recorded in \cref{odg:cr:sub:singular}:
a point cannot be lifted through the normalization by an appeal to normality, because
$\crA$ need not be integrally closed even in its fraction field. Its witness is the
fixed-workspace example printed there: for $e,H\in\Gamma$ with $0<e$ and $ne<H$ for every
ordinary $n$, the element $\omega^e(1+\omega^{-2e})^{1/2}$ has exponents $(1-2n)e$, is
integral over $\crA$ and not in it, and becomes an element of $\crA$ after multiplication
by $\omega^H$ \src{C10, C13, C16}.

\begin{remark}[Why discreteness is needed \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:rem:discrete}
The same scales show why almost integrality alone does not force a
positive-valued element to vanish. Put $a=\omega^{-e}$. Then $v(a)=e>0$,
but $\omega^H a^n=\omega^{H-ne}\in\crA$ for every ordinary $n\ge0$.
No finite multiple of $e$ exceeds $H$. In the proof below the conductor
lies in a one-variable function field whose restricted value group is
$\gamma\Z$ (\cref{odg:sg:lem:place}). The contracted differential may
lie outside that function field: the Euler derivation need not preserve it.
Instead, the choice of derivation gives $v(\partial u)=v(u)=\gamma$,
and $\alpha(\partial)=h\,\partial u$ with $h\in F$ then gives
$v(\alpha(\partial))\in\gamma\Z$. These two valuations, rather than
two field memberships, justify the finite comparison. This is not an
assumption that $\Gamma$ has rank one. The hypothesis $ne<H$ for all $n$ is essential to
the example, in accordance with \cref{odg:cr:sub:singular}: for $\Gamma=\Z$ the ring is
$\Bbbk[\omega]$ and normal.
\end{remark}

Every finite tuple of surreals lies in a set-sized workspace
(\cref{odg:lem:workspace}); scheme theory and derivations are used only there, and no
spectrum of a proper-class ring is formed \src{C16}. In the surreal instances
$\crA$ is the part of $\BR=\R\oplus\Pi$ or $\BC=\C\oplus\Pi_{\C}$ supported in $\Gamma$,
and $\Oz=\Z\oplus\Pi$, $\Og=\Z[i]\oplus\Pi_{\C}$ \cite{Conway,Gonshor,LM}.

\subsubsection{Seidenberg's identity and conductor certificates}\label{odg:sg:sub:seidenberg}

Let $C$ be an integral affine curve over a field. Its normalization
$\Bbbk[\widetilde C]$ in $F=\Frac\Bbbk[C]$ is a finite $\Bbbk[C]$-module
\cite{StacksFiniteNormal}; write finitely many module generators as fractions
$c_i/d_i$ with $c_i,d_i\in\Bbbk[C]$ and $d_i\ne0$. Their product
$\kappa=\prod_i d_i$ is nonzero and clears every module generator, hence
every element of the normalization. It is a \emph{conductor element}
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:conductor}
 0\ne\kappa\in\Bbbk[C],\qquad \kappa\,\Bbbk[\widetilde C]\subseteq\Bbbk[C],
 \qquad\text{hence}\qquad \kappa b^j\in\Bbbk[C]\quad(b\in\Bbbk[\widetilde C],\ j\ge0).
\end{equation}
No principal conductor or greatest common divisor is needed \src{C16}.

\begin{lemma}[Seidenberg's power identity \src{C16}; after \cite{Seidenberg}]\label{odg:sg:lem:seidenberg}
Let $R$ be a domain containing $\Q$ in a field $L$ with a derivation $\partial$ such that
$\partial(R)\subseteq R$. If $\kappa,b\in L$ and $\kappa b^j\in R$ for $0\le j\le n$, then
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:seidenberg}
 \kappa^2(\partial b)^n
 =\frac1{n!}\sum_{j=0}^n(-1)^{n-j}\binom nj\,(\kappa b^{n-j})\,\partial^n(\kappa b^j)\in R .
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $w$ be an independent formal variable and $\mathcal T:L\to L[[w]]$,
$\mathcal T(f)=\sum_{i\ge0}\partial^i(f)w^i/i!$; the Leibniz rule makes $\mathcal T$ a ring
homomorphism: the coefficient of $w^i$ in $\mathcal T(fg)$ agrees with
that in $\mathcal T(f)\mathcal T(g)$ by
$\partial^i(fg)=\sum_{j=0}^i\binom ij\partial^j(f)\partial^{i-j}(g)$.
By the binomial theorem and multiplicativity,
\[
 \kappa\,\mathcal T(\kappa)\bigl(\mathcal T(b)-b\bigr)^n
 =\sum_{j=0}^n(-1)^{n-j}\binom nj(\kappa b^{n-j})\,\mathcal T(\kappa b^j).
\]
Since $\mathcal T(b)-b=(\partial b)w+O(w^2)$ and $\mathcal T(\kappa)=\kappa+O(w)$, the
coefficient of $w^n$ on the left is $\kappa^2(\partial b)^n$, and on the right it is the
sum in \eqref{odg:sg:eq:seidenberg}. This includes $n=0$, when both
sides equal $\kappa^2$. Each $\kappa b^{n-j}$ and each
$\partial^n(\kappa b^j)$ lies in $R$, and $1/n!\in\Q\subseteq R$.
\end{proof}

The variable $w$ is an ordinary formal variable, separate from the monomials
$\omega^\gamma$; only one coefficient, involving finitely many derivatives, is used, and
nothing is substituted for $w$. An element $b\in\Frac R$ is almost integral over $R$ when
$\kappa b^n\in R$ for all $n$ and a fixed $0\ne\kappa\in R$; the lemma is the finite form
of Seidenberg's theorem that derivations preserve complete integral closure, also for
non-Noetherian $\Q$-algebras \cite[Section 3]{Seidenberg}, \cite[Section 2.5]{Ciuperca}.
Here the actual multiplier $\kappa^2$ is kept \src{C16}.

\begin{theorem}[Conductor contraction certificate \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:thm:conductor}
Let $\Bbbk$ have characteristic zero, let $\Bbbk[C]\subseteq R\subseteq L$ be
$\Bbbk$-algebras in a field $L$, where $\Bbbk[C]$ is the coordinate ring of an integral
affine curve with normalization $\Bbbk[\widetilde C]\subseteq F\subseteq L$, and let
$\partial$ be a $\Bbbk$-derivation of $L$ with $\partial(R)\subseteq R$. Choose $\kappa$ as
in \eqref{odg:sg:eq:conductor}. For
\[
 \alpha=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}a_j\,\dif b_j\in\Omega^1_{\Bbbk[\widetilde C]/\Bbbk},
 \qquad a_j,b_j\in\Bbbk[\widetilde C],\quad \ell\ge1,
\]
put $\alpha(\partial)=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}a_j\,\partial b_j\in L$. Then for every ordinary
$n\ge0$
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:contract}
 \kappa^{2\ell+1}\alpha(\partial)^n\in R ,
\end{equation}
and for fixed $n$ the proof uses derivatives of order at most $n$ of finitely many
elements of $\Bbbk[C]$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Expand $\alpha(\partial)^n$ by the multinomial theorem. Apart from an integer coefficient,
a term is $\bigl(\prod_ja_j^{e_j}\bigr)\bigl(\prod_j(\partial b_j)^{e_j}\bigr)$ with
$e_j\ge0$ and $\sum_je_j=n$. The first factor lies in $\Bbbk[\widetilde C]$, so $\kappa$
times it lies in $\Bbbk[C]\subseteq R$. For each $j$, all $\kappa b_j^i$ lie in $\Bbbk[C]$,
so \cref{odg:sg:lem:seidenberg} with $n=e_j$ puts $\kappa^2(\partial b_j)^{e_j}$ in $R$.
Multiplying these $\ell+1$ memberships puts $\kappa^{2\ell+1}$ times the term in $R$, and
summing proves \eqref{odg:sg:eq:contract}. The exponent $2\ell+1$
depends on the chosen presentation of $\alpha$, but is independent of $n$;
this uniformity is the point of multiplying the memberships before summing.
Terms with $e_j=0$ cause no exception, since the identity at order zero gives
$\kappa^2\in R$. Only the derivatives $\partial^{e_j}$,
$e_j\le n$, of the elements $\kappa b_j^i$ occur.
\end{proof}

The ring $R$ may be all of $\crA$: no finite generation, normality or equality
$\Frac R=L$ is hidden in the statement. Only $\Bbbk[\widetilde C]$ is normalized, and its
elements $b_j$ may lie outside $R$ \src{C16}.

\begin{corollary}[A finite derivative-order bound \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:cor:jetbound}
In \cref{odg:sg:thm:conductor} take $R=\crA\subseteq L=\crH$ and $\partial$ an Euler
derivation. Suppose that for some $\gamma>0$ and ordinary $\delta,r\ge0$
\[
 v(\kappa)=-\delta\gamma,\qquad v\bigl(\alpha(\partial)\bigr)=(r+1)\gamma .
\]
These data are impossible; the contradiction occurs at
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:jetbound}
 n=\Bigl\lfloor\frac{(2\ell+1)\delta}{r+1}\Bigr\rfloor+1 .
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The element \eqref{odg:sg:eq:contract} is nonzero, lies in $\crA$ and has valuation
$\bigl(n(r+1)-(2\ell+1)\delta\bigr)\gamma>0$; but $\crA\cap\crm=\{0\}$
(\cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}).
\end{proof}

This is a finite certificate in differential algebra. The bound \eqref{odg:sg:eq:jetbound}
is the least $n$ for which that valuation is positive, for the given parameters; it is not
claimed optimal over the choices of conductor, presentation and differential, and no
algorithm is given for producing a conductor and a regular differential from an
arbitrary presentation (\cref{odg:sg:q:order,odg:sg:q:engine}) \src{C16}.

\subsubsection{The place at infinity}\label{odg:sg:sub:place}

Let $C=\Spec\Bbbk[C]$ be an integral affine curve and $\varphi:\Bbbk[C]\to\crA$ a
$\Bbbk$-algebra map. If its kernel is nonzero, then $\Bbbk[C]/\ker\varphi$ is a field
finite over $\Bbbk$, and its image in $\crA$ is algebraic over $\Bbbk$, hence equal to
$\Bbbk$ (\cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}(d)); the point is constant. So a nonconstant point gives
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:generic}
 \Bbbk[C]\hookrightarrow\crA\subseteq\crH,\qquad F=\Frac\Bbbk[C]\hookrightarrow\crH,
\end{equation}
and some element of $\Bbbk[C]$ has negative valuation \src{C16}.

\begin{lemma}[The place at infinity \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:lem:place}
Let $\Bbbk$ be algebraically closed and let \eqref{odg:sg:eq:generic} come from a
nonconstant point. There are a closed point $Q\in\Sigma=\overline C\setminus\widetilde C$, a
uniformizer $u\in F$ at $Q$ and $\gamma=v(u)>0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:place}
 v(f)=\operatorname{ord}_Q(f)\,\gamma\qquad(f\in F^\times).
\end{equation}
In particular $v(F^\times)=\gamma\Z$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The ring $V=F\cap\crO$ is a valuation ring of $F$ containing $\Bbbk$, nontrivial because
some element of $\Bbbk[C]$ has negative valuation. By the valuative criterion of
properness \cite[Tag 0BX5]{StacksProper}, the generic point extends to
$\Spec V\to\overline C$. The centre $Q$ is not the generic point, for then $F\subseteq V$;
so it is closed, and $\mathcal O_{\overline C,Q}$ is a discrete valuation ring
\cite{StacksCurves}. The local inclusion $\mathcal O_{\overline C,Q}\to V$ sends a
uniformizer $u$ into the maximal ideal of $V$, so $\gamma=v(u)>0$. Every $f\in F^\times$ is
$u^nw$ with $w\in\mathcal O_{\overline C,Q}^\times$; both $w$ and $w^{-1}$ lie in $V$, so
$v(w)=0$ and \eqref{odg:sg:eq:place} holds. If $Q$ were in $\widetilde C$, every element of
$\Bbbk[C]\subseteq\Bbbk[\widetilde C]\subseteq\mathcal O_{\overline C,Q}$ would have
nonnegative valuation, hence lie in $\crA\cap\crO=\Bbbk$, and the point would be
constant.
\end{proof}

This is the centre of \cref{odg:cr:thm:logroute}, with the discreteness of the induced
valuation on $F$ made explicit. The smooth projective model used here exists over any
algebraically closed, hence perfect, field, and the lemma does not use the
characteristic; the next proposition uses that \src{C16}.

\begin{proposition}[Only one point may be missing \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:prop:oneplace}
Let $\Bbbk$ be algebraically closed of any characteristic, with $\crA$ defined by the same
formula. If an integral affine curve $C$ over $\Bbbk$ has a nonconstant $\crA$-point, then
$\Sigma=\overline C\setminus\widetilde C$ consists of exactly one point.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $Q$ be the centre of \cref{odg:sg:lem:place}, which is in $\Sigma$, and suppose that
$q\ne Q$ is also in $\Sigma$. For $N\ge g(\overline C)+1$ Riemann--Roch gives
$h^0(\overline C,\mathcal O(Nq-Q))\ge N-g\ge1$ \cite{StacksRR}; a nonzero $f$ in this space
is regular on $\widetilde C$, vanishes at $Q$ and has its only possible pole at $q$. So
$f\in\Bbbk[\widetilde C]$ and $v(f)>0$. For a conductor element $\kappa$ every $\kappa f^n$
lies in $\Bbbk[C]\subseteq\crA$, while by \eqref{odg:sg:eq:place} both $v(\kappa)\le0$ and
$v(f)>0$ are integer multiples of $\gamma$; for large ordinary $n$, $v(\kappa f^n)>0$,
contradicting $\crA\cap\crm=\{0\}$. More explicitly, put
$\delta=-\operatorname{ord}_Q(\kappa)\ge0$ and
$r=\operatorname{ord}_Q(f)\ge1$. Any integer $n>\delta/r$ gives the
contradiction, even if $\Gamma$ has higher rank. Hence $\Sigma=\{Q\}$.
\end{proof}

The argument uses no derivation and no normality of the target ring; it is the part of
the obstruction that survives in positive characteristic. In characteristic zero
regular differentials add the restriction of genus zero \src{C16}. The two
positive-characteristic examples of \cref{odg:cr:sub:charp} satisfy it, as they must: the
affine curve $y^2+y=x^3$ of \cref{odg:cr:prop:char2} and the affine line $y^p=x^p-x$ of
\cref{odg:cr:prop:charp} each have one point at infinity \mergetag. So a
characteristic-free statement can only be one-place, not genus zero.

\subsubsection{Proof of the criterion}\label{odg:sg:sub:proof}

\begin{theorem}[Positive genus across singularities \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:thm:genus}
Let $\Bbbk$ be algebraically closed of characteristic zero. If the smooth projective model
$\overline C$ of an integral affine curve $C$ over $\Bbbk$ has genus at least one, then
$C(\crA)=C(\Bbbk)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Suppose a nonconstant point exists, and take $Q$, $u$ and $\gamma$ from
\cref{odg:sg:lem:place}. As $g\ge1$, there is a nonzero
$\alpha\in H^0(\overline C,\Omega^1_{\overline C/\Bbbk})$, a space of dimension $g$
\cite{StacksRR}. Its restriction to the smooth affine curve $\widetilde C$ lies in
$\Omega^1_{\Bbbk[\widetilde C]/\Bbbk}$ and has a finite presentation
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:alpha}
 \alpha=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}a_j\,\dif b_j,\qquad a_j,b_j\in\Bbbk[\widetilde C].
\end{equation}
Since $v(u)=\gamma$, the leading exponent of $u$ is $-\gamma\ne0$; choose $\lambda$ with
$\lambda(-\gamma)=1$ (\cref{odg:cr:lem:separation}) and put $\partial=\partial_\lambda$. The
leading term of $u$ survives, so $\partial u\ne0$ and $v(\partial u)=\gamma$. At $Q$ the
differential $\dif u$ freely generates the module of differentials, since $\overline C$ is
smooth and $\kappa(Q)=\Bbbk$ is separable over $\Bbbk$
\cite[Lemma 53.12.3]{StacksRH}; write
$\alpha=h\,\dif u$ with $0\ne h\in\mathcal O_{\overline C,Q}$ and $r=\operatorname{ord}_Q(h)\ge0$.
The universal property of K\"ahler differentials, applied to the $\Bbbk$-derivation
$F\to\crH$ obtained by restricting $\partial$, gives
\[
 \alpha(\partial)=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}a_j\,\partial b_j=h\,\partial u ,
\]
which is nonzero with valuation $(r+1)\gamma$ by \eqref{odg:sg:eq:place}. A conductor
element $\kappa\in\Bbbk[C]\subseteq\crA$ has $v(\kappa)=-\delta\gamma$ with an ordinary
$\delta\ge0$. \Cref{odg:sg:thm:conductor,odg:sg:cor:jetbound} give the contradiction.
\end{proof}

The derivation is defined on $F$ with values in $\crH$; it need not preserve $F$, and it
is never identified with an endomorphism of the function field. The equality
$\alpha(\partial)=h\,\partial u$ takes place in $\crH$ and is justified by the relative
module $\Omega^1_{F/\Bbbk}$ \src{C16}.

\begin{remark}[A third route in the smooth case \mergetag]\label{odg:sg:rem:thirdroute}
For smooth $C$ one may take $\kappa=1$ and $\delta=0$; then \eqref{odg:sg:eq:jetbound} gives $n=1$, that
is, $\alpha(\partial)\in\crA\cap\crm=\{0\}$ directly, and
\cref{odg:sg:prop:oneplace} replaces the unit argument of \cref{odg:cr:lem:punctures}
by Riemann--Roch. So the rigid direction of \cref{odg:cr:thm:affine} has a third proof,
after the descent proof of source C14 and the logarithmic proof of source C12
(\cref{odg:cr:thm:logroute}): positive genus by one regular differential and its
valuation at the centre, and genus zero with two or more boundary points by a function
with a single pole.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{The algebraically closed case.} Suppose $\Bbbk$ is algebraically closed and $C$
has a nonconstant point. By \cref{odg:sg:thm:genus} the model $\overline C$ has genus zero,
and by \cref{odg:sg:prop:oneplace} the affine normalization omits exactly one point; a
smooth projective genus-zero curve over an algebraically closed field is $\mathbb P^1$
\cite{StacksGenusZero}, so $\widetilde C\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$. Conversely, if
$\widetilde C\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$, the normalization map in a closed embedding
$C\hookrightarrow\Aff^N_\Bbbk$ is a tuple $(p_1(T),\ldots,p_N(T))$ of polynomials, not all
constant, and for $0<\gamma\in\Gamma$ the substitution $T=\omega^\gamma$ gives a point of
$C(\crA)$ with support in $\{0,\gamma,\ldots,n\gamma\}$, $n=\max\deg p_i$. It is
nonconstant because $\omega^\gamma$ is transcendental over $\Bbbk$ \src{C16}.

\paragraph{Descent to every field of characteristic zero.} Coefficientwise inclusion
$\crA\subseteq\mathcal A_{\bar\Bbbk}(\Gamma)$ preserves nonconstancy, and $C_{\bar\Bbbk}$ is
integral because $C$ is geometrically integral; so a nonconstant point over $\Bbbk$ gives
one over $\bar\Bbbk$, and the normalization of $C_{\bar\Bbbk}$ is $\Aff^1_{\bar\Bbbk}$. Over
the perfect field $\Bbbk$ the normalization $\widetilde C$ is smooth and geometrically
integral. To see the latter assertion, use the dense open where normalization
is an isomorphism: after coefficient extension it is an integral dense open
from $C_{\bar\Bbbk}$, while smoothness makes the whole base change normal
and reduced. The base change of $\widetilde C$ is consequently the
normalization of $C_{\bar\Bbbk}$: the morphism stays finite and birational
and its source is normal. So $\widetilde C$ is a form of the
affine line, and $\widetilde C\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$ by \cref{odg:cr:lem:formA1}: its completion
has genus zero and one geometric boundary point, which is rational because in
characteristic zero a closed point of higher degree splits into several geometric points
\src{C16}. This proves (i)$\Rightarrow$(ii) of \cref{odg:sg:thm:main}. The normalization map
gives (ii)$\Rightarrow$(iii); (iii) and (iv) are the same statement about the affine
functor; and evaluating a nonconstant polynomial family at $\omega^\gamma$ gives
(iv)$\Rightarrow$(i) with the stated finite support.

\paragraph{What the proof does not use.} No analyticity, no countability of supports, no
divisibility of $\Gamma$, no saturation, and no statement that an element integral over
$\crA$ lies in $\crA$; the proof works for $\Gamma=\Z$, for dense rank-one groups and for
every set-sized rank. The smooth normalization is a theorem about finite-type curves over
a perfect field, not a hypothesis on $C$: nodes, cusps, several branches and non-planar
singularities are allowed. The finite-type and constant-coefficient hypotheses are
substantive \src{C16}.

\subsubsection{Structural consequences}\label{odg:sg:sub:structure}

\begin{corollary}[Independence of the scale \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:cor:scale}
For a geometrically integral affine curve $C$ over a field of characteristic zero, whether
$C(\crA)=C(\Bbbk)$ does not depend on the nonzero ordered group $\Gamma$, and it holds
exactly when $C(\Bbbk[T])=C(\Bbbk)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Each statement is equivalent to $\widetilde C\not\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$ by
\cref{odg:sg:thm:main}.
\end{proof}

So transfinite supports never create the first nonconstant point on a constant-coefficient
affine curve; they can create further points, and complicated fibers, on the exceptional
curves. The corollary is an existence reduction, not a description of all points
\src{C16}.

\begin{corollary}[Finite birational invariance \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:cor:birational}
Let $C'\to C$ be a finite birational morphism of geometrically integral affine curves over a
field of characteristic zero. Then $C'(\crA)=C'(\Bbbk)$ if and only if $C(\crA)=C(\Bbbk)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
$\Bbbk[C']$ is integral over $\Bbbk[C]$ with the same fraction field, so both have the same
normalization; apply \cref{odg:sg:thm:main}.
\end{proof}

The affine normalization, including which points at infinity are removed, must be
retained: arbitrary birational equivalence is not enough, since $\Aff^1$ is flexible and
$\Gm$ rigid with the same function field \src{C16}.

\begin{theorem}[Affine schemes of dimension at most one \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:thm:allcurves}
Let $\Bbbk$ be algebraically closed of characteristic zero, let $\Gamma\ne0$,
and let $X$ be an affine $\Bbbk$-scheme of finite type and dimension at most one,
possibly reducible or nonreduced. Then
$X(\crA)=X(\Bbbk)$ if and only if no one-dimensional irreducible component of
$X_{\mathrm{red}}$ has affine normalization $\Aff^1_\Bbbk$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A map $\Bbbk[X]\to\crA$ kills nilpotents, since $\crA$ is a domain, and its kernel $P$ is
prime. If $X$ is empty, both point sets are empty and the criterion is
vacuous. For a point of nonempty $X$, if $\dim\Bbbk[X]/P=0$, the image is a domain algebraic over $\Bbbk$, hence $\Bbbk$
(\cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}(d)), and the point is constant. Otherwise $P$ is a minimal prime: a strict inclusion of a minimal prime
in $P$, followed by a strict prime inclusion above $P$, would give a chain
of length two in a ring of dimension at most one. Thus
$V(P)$ is a one-dimensional irreducible component of $X_{\mathrm{red}}$, integral and,
over the algebraically closed $\Bbbk$, geometrically integral, and the point is a
nonconstant point of it; \cref{odg:sg:thm:main} applies. Conversely the normalization of
an exceptional component gives a nonconstant polynomial map into $X$, which
$T=\omega^\gamma$ turns into a nonconstant point.
\end{proof}

The nonzero-group hypothesis is necessary: for $\Gamma=0$ and
$X=\Aff^1_\Bbbk$, the left side is true because $\crA=\Bbbk$, whereas
the stated condition on the normalization is false.

This classifies the reducible affine curves over an algebraically closed field of
characteristic zero, which \cref{odg:cr:sub:singular} records as not classified by sources
C10--C15; over other fields components need not be geometrically integral, and no
statement is made there \src{C16}.

\begin{corollary}[Polynomial witnesses in every fiber \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:cor:fibre}
Let $\Bbbk$ be algebraically closed of characteristic zero and $\widetilde C\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$.
For every $\mathbf x_0\in C(\Bbbk)$ and every $0<\gamma\in\Gamma$ there is a nonconstant
finite-support $\mathbf x\in C(\crA)$ with $\ct(\mathbf x)=\mathbf x_0$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The finite normalization map is surjective; choose $c\in\Bbbk$ over $\mathbf x_0$ and write
the map as $(p_i(T))_i$. The coordinates $p_i(c+\omega^\gamma)$ lie in $\crA$, have constant
terms $p_i(c)$. At least one $p_i$ is nonconstant, and translation by $c$
preserves its degree. Evaluation at the transcendental monomial
$\omega^\gamma$ is injective on $\Bbbk[T]$, so that coordinate is not
in $\Bbbk$. The resulting supports are finite subsets of
$\{0,\gamma,\ldots,n\gamma\}$, where $n=\max_i\deg p_i$.
\end{proof}

Over a field that is not algebraically closed the same construction works at every
$\mathbf x_0$ with a $\Bbbk$-rational preimage under the normalization, in particular at
