every nonsingular $\Bbbk$-point. It is not replaced by the existence of a $\Bbbk$-point on
the singular curve; the distinction matters at real isolated singularities
(\cref{odg:sg:q:real}) \src{C16}.

\subsubsection{Repeated-root superelliptic equations}\label{odg:sg:sub:super}

Let $\Bbbk$ be algebraically closed of characteristic zero, $m\ge2$, and
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:superpoly}
 P(X)=c\prod_{i=1}^{k}(X-a_i)^{e_i},\qquad c\ne0,\quad e_i\ge1,
\end{equation}
with distinct $a_i$ and $d=\deg P=\sum_ie_i$. Assume throughout this theorem that
$Y^m-P(X)$ is irreducible, equivalently $\gcd(m,e_1,\ldots,e_k)=1$.
Indeed, $P$ is a $p$th power in $\Bbbk(X)$ exactly when $p$ divides every $e_i$:
necessity follows from the orders at the $a_i$, and sufficiency from taking a
$p$th root of $c$. For completeness, put $K=\Bbbk(X)$ and $b^m=P$.
Since $K$ contains all $m$th roots of unity, $K(b)/K$ is cyclic Galois of degree
$r\mid m$, and $b^r\in K$ \cite{StacksKummer}. If $r<m$, then
$P=(b^r)^{m/r}$ is a $p$th power for some prime $p\mid m/r$.
Conversely, such a power makes $Y^m-P$ reducible by a difference-of-powers
factorization. Gauss's lemma transfers irreducibility between $K[Y]$ and
$\Bbbk[X,Y]$. Thus irreducibility means that for every prime $p\mid m$,
\emph{some} $e_i$ is not divisible by $p$ \src{C16}.

\begin{theorem}[Superelliptic flexibility criterion \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:thm:super}
Assume $Y^m-P(X)$ is irreducible, in addition to the hypotheses above.
Then $Y^m=P(X)$ has a nonconstant $\crA$-point if and only if exactly one
index $i$ has $m\nmid e_i$, and for that index $\gcd(m,e_i)=1$. Equivalently, after
extracting polynomial $m$th powers,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:exceptional}
 P(X)=c\,P_1(X)^m(X-a)^e,\qquad 1\le e<m,\quad\gcd(m,e)=1,
\end{equation}
and then a normalization parameter is
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:superparam}
 X=a+T^m,\qquad Y=\eta\,P_1(a+T^m)\,T^e,\qquad \eta^m=c .
\end{equation}
In every other irreducible case all solutions in $\crA^2$ are constant.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $\overline C$ be the smooth projective model of this degree-$m$ cover of
the $X$-line. At $a_i$ put $s=X-a_i$, and at infinity put $s=1/X$.
Locally $P=s^a U(s)$ with $U\in\Bbbk[[s]]^\times$, where $a=e_i$ at a finite
root and $a=-d$ at infinity. The unit $U$ has an $m$th root: take a root of
its constant coefficient and expand the remaining unit by the binomial series.
After dividing $Y$ by this root, the local equation is $w^m=s^a$.
Put $h=\gcd(m,|a|)$. It has $h$ branches, each with
$s=u^{m/h}$ and $w=\zeta u^{a/h}$ for an appropriate constant $\zeta$.
The relatively prime integers $m/h$ and $a/h$ generate $u$ in the branch
field, so its completed normalization is $\Bbbk[[u]]$ and its ramification
index is $m/h$. At finite nonroots $a=0$, giving $m$ unramified branches.
Thus a root contributes $m-\gcd(m,e_i)$ to the ramification sum, and
infinity has $n_\infty=\gcd(m,d)$ points and contributes $m-n_\infty$.
Characteristic zero makes this ramification tame, so Riemann--Hurwitz
\cite{StacksRH} gives
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:RH}
 2g-2=-2m+\sum_{i=1}^k\bigl(m-\gcd(m,e_i)\bigr)+m-\gcd(m,d).
\end{equation}
The coordinate ring is integral over $\Bbbk[X]$ because the equation is
monic in $Y$. Its normalization is the integral closure of $\Bbbk[X]$ in
the same function field: integrality in either direction follows from
transitivity and from inclusion of the base ring. It is finite over
$\Bbbk[X]$ and identifies with the inverse image of the affine $X$-line
in $\overline C$. Hence it omits exactly the $n_\infty$ points over infinity. By
\cref{odg:sg:thm:main} (with \cref{odg:sg:thm:genus,odg:sg:prop:oneplace}), flexibility is
equivalent to $g=0$ and $n_\infty=1$, which by \eqref{odg:sg:eq:RH} give
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:deficit}
 \sum_{i=1}^k\bigl(m-\gcd(m,e_i)\bigr)=m-1 .
\end{equation}
A nonzero summand is at least $m/2$, since a proper divisor of $m$ is at most $m/2$; so
there is exactly one nonzero summand, and its gcd is one. Conversely that pattern gives
$d\equiv e\pmod m$, so $\gcd(m,d)=1$, and \eqref{odg:sg:eq:deficit}, hence $g=0$ and one
point at infinity. Finally \eqref{odg:sg:eq:superparam} satisfies the equation; $T$ is
integral over the coordinate ring since $T^m=X-a$; and $T^m$ and $T^e=Y/(\eta P_1(X))$
generate $T$ in the function field: choose integers $a_0,b_0$ with
$a_0m+b_0e=1$ and use $T=(T^m)^{a_0}(T^e)^{b_0}$ there. Negative powers
are allowed in the function field, not asserted to lie in the coordinate ring.
So $\Bbbk[T]$ is a finite
birational normal extension of the coordinate ring, that is, its normalization.
\end{proof}

The irreducibility assumption is essential. Without it, $Y^2=X^2$ has the
nonconstant polynomial families $(T,\pm T)$ although no multiplicity is odd.
The quadratic corollary below includes such reducible cases separately.

Repeated factors, and so arbitrarily singular affine models, are governed by the same test;
the Riemann--Hurwitz computation itself is classical. Many repeated roots do not make a
curve flexible: only the residues of the multiplicities modulo $m$ matter \src{C16}. The
theorem contains \cref{odg:cr:thm:squarefree} over an algebraically closed field, where
all $e_i=1$ and $k=d\ge2$ gives at least two indices with $m\nmid e_i$, and it agrees with
\cref{odg:cr:prop:singular}: the singular cubic $(X-r)^2(X+2r)$, $r\ne0$, and the cusp $X^3$
have exactly one odd multiplicity. Over any field $\Bbbk$ of characteristic zero, if
$Y^m-P(X)$ is irreducible over $\bar\Bbbk$, the test applied to the factorization over
$\bar\Bbbk$ decides flexibility over $\Bbbk$: by \cref{odg:sg:thm:main} flexibility over
$\Bbbk$ means $\widetilde C\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$, which holds exactly when
$\widetilde C_{\bar\Bbbk}\cong\Aff^1_{\bar\Bbbk}$ by \cref{odg:cr:lem:formA1} and base
change \mergetag.

For $P\in\C[X]\setminus\{0\}$ write $P=cP_1^2P_{\mathrm{odd}}$ with $P_1$, $P_{\mathrm{odd}}$
monic, $P_{\mathrm{odd}}$ squarefree and $c\ne0$; $P_{\mathrm{odd}}$ is the product of the
irreducible factors of odd multiplicity. The same factorization is used over $\R$.

\begin{corollary}[The quadratic-root test \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:cor:hyper}
For $P\in\C[X]\setminus\{0\}$, the equation $Y^2=P(X)$ has a nonconstant
$\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma)$-point if and only if $\deg P_{\mathrm{odd}}\le1$; if
$\deg P_{\mathrm{odd}}\ge2$, every solution is constant. For $P\in\R[X]\setminus\{0\}$ and the
same factorization over $\R$, there is a nonconstant $\mathcal A_\R(\Gamma)$-point if and only
if $\deg P_{\mathrm{odd}}=1$, or $\deg P_{\mathrm{odd}}=0$ and $c>0$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Over $\C$, a nonconstant $P_{\mathrm{odd}}$ makes the curve irreducible and
\cref{odg:sg:thm:super} applies with $m=2$; if $P_{\mathrm{odd}}=1$ the two components
$Y=\pm\sqrt c\,P_1(X)$ are polynomial graphs. Over $\R$, $\deg P_{\mathrm{odd}}\ge2$ is rigid
after complexification. If $P_{\mathrm{odd}}=X-a$, then $X=a+cT^2$, $Y=cP_1(a+cT^2)\,T$ is a
real polynomial family for either sign of $c$. If $P_{\mathrm{odd}}=1$ and $c>0$ the graphs
are real. Evaluating any of these nonconstant polynomial families at
$T=\omega^\gamma$, $\gamma>0$, gives a nonconstant finite-support Hahn point.
If $P_{\mathrm{odd}}=1$ and $c<0$, the ordering of the real Hahn field forces
$Y=0$ and $P_1(X)=0$, and the roots of a real polynomial in an ordered extension of $\R$
are real, so the points are constant.
\end{proof}

The excluded $P=0$ has the family $(T,0)$. For example $Y^2=X^2(X-1)$ is flexible and
$Y^2=X^2(X-1)(X-2)$ is rigid, although both are singular at the origin; for
$\deg P_{\mathrm{odd}}=2$ the obstruction is two points at infinity, not positive genus, and
for $\deg P_{\mathrm{odd}}\ge3$ genus contributes as well \src{C16}.

\subsubsection{A pinched elliptic curve with a seventh-order certificate}\label{odg:sg:sub:example}

Consider
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:pinched}
 Z^2=X^2(X^3-X+1),
\end{equation}
singular at the origin. Put $f(X)=X^3-X+1$, of discriminant $-23$, so the affine elliptic
curve $E:\ Y^2=f(X)$ is smooth; it is the curve with $\Delta_0=23$ of
\cref{odg:cr:cor:weierstrass,odg:cr:ex:denominator}. The map $(X,Y)\mapsto(X,Z=XY)$ is the
normalization of \eqref{odg:sg:eq:pinched} and identifies $(0,1)$ with $(0,-1)$.
Indeed, $y$ is integral by $y^2=f(x)$ and is the fraction $z/x$, while the
smooth ring $\Bbbk[x,y]/(y^2-f(x))$ is normal. In ring
terms $\Bbbk[C]=\Bbbk[x,xy]\subseteq\Bbbk[\widetilde C]=\Bbbk[x,y]$ with $y^2=f(x)$, and
$x\,\Bbbk[\widetilde C]\subseteq\Bbbk[C]$: every element of the larger ring
is $A(x)+B(x)y$, whose product with $x$ is $xA(x)+B(x)z$. Thus
$\kappa=x$ is a conductor element \src{C16}.

\begin{theorem}[A pinched elliptic curve \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:thm:example}
Over every field of characteristic zero every solution of \eqref{odg:sg:eq:pinched} in
$\crA^2$ is constant. In particular
\[
 \{(x,z)\in\Oz^2:z^2=x^2(x^3-x+1)\}=\{(x,z)\in\Z^2:z^2=x^2(x^3-x+1)\},
\]
and likewise with $\Og$ and $\Z[i]$. A nonconstant solution is excluded by derivatives of
order at most seven.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Rigidity follows from \cref{odg:sg:thm:main}, since the normalization $E$ has genus one.
Here is the explicit certificate. Suppose $(x,z)\in\crA^2$ is nonconstant. If $x\in\Bbbk$,
then $z$ is algebraic over $\Bbbk$ and constant; so $x\notin\Bbbk$, $x\ne0$, and
$y=z/x\in\crH$ satisfies $y^2=f(x)$. Since a nonconstant element of $\crA$
has negative valuation, $v(x)<0$. The term $x^3$ has strictly smaller
valuation than both $-x$ and $1$, so it cannot cancel. Thus
$2v(y)=3v(x)$, and $\gamma=v(x/y)\in\Gamma$
satisfies $\gamma>0$, $v(x)=-2\gamma$ and $v(y)=-3\gamma$: these are the pole orders two and
three at the point at infinity of $E$. Choose $\lambda$ with $\lambda(-\gamma)=1$, so
$\lambda(\ddeg x)=-2\ne0$, and put $\partial=\partial_\lambda$ and
$\alpha(\partial)=\partial x/(2y)$; the leading term of $x$ survives, so
$v(\alpha(\partial))=\gamma$. The polynomials
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:bezout}
 U(X)=\frac{18X+27}{23},\qquad W(X)=\frac{-6X^2-9X+4}{23},\qquad Uf+Wf'=1,
\end{equation}
are those of \eqref{odg:cr:eq:cubic} at $(a,b)=(-1,1)$, divided by $\Delta_0=23$. With
$2y\,\partial y=f'(x)\,\partial x$ they give
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:sdecomp}
 \alpha(\partial)=\frac{U(x)\,y}{2}\,\partial x+W(x)\,\partial y .
\end{equation}
For every $j\ge0$, $xy^j\in\Bbbk[C]$: explicitly,
$xy^{2a}=xf(x)^a$ and $xy^{2a+1}=zf(x)^a$. Applying
\cref{odg:sg:lem:seidenberg} with conductor $x$ and element $y$ gives
$x^2(\partial y)^j\in\crA$ for all $j$, including $j=0$.
In the binomial expansion of the $n$th power of \eqref{odg:sg:eq:sdecomp},
the summand indexed by $i+j=n$, after multiplication by $x^3$, is
\[
 \binom ni \frac{U(x)^iW(x)^j}{2^i}
 (\partial x)^i\,(xy^i)\,\bigl(x^2(\partial y)^j\bigr).
\]
Every displayed factor lies in $\crA$; in particular, division by $2^i$
is division by a nonzero constant. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:ellipticcert}
 x^3\alpha(\partial)^n\in\crA\qquad(n\ge0).
\end{equation}
At $n=7$ this nonzero element has valuation $3(-2\gamma)+7\gamma=\gamma>0$, a contradiction.
For the term indexed by $j\le7$, Seidenberg's identity uses at most $j$
derivatives of $xy^a$ and powers of $x$, with $0\le a\le j$.
The latter derivatives are polynomial expressions in derivatives of $x=xy^0$.
Thus only derivatives of order at most seven of the elements $xy^a$,
$0\le a\le7$, occur. No assertion that $y$ itself belongs to $\crA$ is used. The
omnific statements follow in a common workspace, as in \cref{odg:cr:cor:weierstrass}.
\end{proof}

The certificate is not a squarefree equation in disguise: $y=z/x$ is \emph{not} assumed to
be in $\crA$, and the fixed multiplier $x^2$ for all powers of $\partial y$ replaces that
missing statement. The theorem is also \cref{odg:sg:cor:hyper} with
$P_{\mathrm{odd}}=X^3-X+1$. It classifies the additional omnific points by proving that
there are none; it does not enumerate the ordinary integer points of the curve or of $E$
\src{C16}. The general bound \eqref{odg:sg:eq:jetbound}, with $\ell=2$, $\delta=2$ and
$r=0$ for the differential $\dif x/(2y)$, which does not vanish at infinity, gives eleven;
seven improves it, and no optimality is claimed (\cref{odg:sg:q:order}).

\subsubsection{Omnific and Gaussian omnific points}\label{odg:sg:sub:arith}

Let $\mathcal C\subseteq\Aff^N_\Z$ be an affine scheme given by finitely many integer
equations whose real fiber $C_\R$ is a geometrically integral curve; the coordinate
embedding is fixed.

\begin{theorem}[Omnific rigidity for singular curves \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:thm:omnific}
If the affine normalization of $C_\R$ is not $\Aff^1_\R$, then
$\mathcal C(\Oz)=\mathcal C(\Z)$. Likewise, for an affine $\Z[i]$-model with geometrically
integral complex fiber $C_\C$ whose affine normalization is not $\Aff^1_\C$,
$\mathcal C(\Og)=\mathcal C(\Z[i])$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A candidate tuple lies in a set-sized real or complex workspace (\cref{odg:lem:workspace});
there the curve is rigid by \cref{odg:sg:thm:main}, so the coordinates are constants, and
$\Oz\cap\R=\Z$, $\Og\cap\C=\Z[i]$. The reverse inclusions are clear.
\end{proof}

The argument works for any coefficient subring $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$ and the ring
$\mathfrak o\oplus\ker(\ct)=\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$; it uses the constant-term
condition, not an order on $\mathfrak o$. This is \cref{odg:cr:thm:arith}(i) and
\cref{odg:log:thm:descent} for singular curves. For reducible complex models
\cref{odg:sg:thm:allcurves} gives the componentwise test \src{C16}.

If $\nu(T)=(p_1(T),\ldots,p_N(T))$, $p_i\in\Bbbk[T]$, parametrizes the normalization of an
exceptional curve, then for $c\in\Bbbk$ and every purely infinite $s$ with coefficients in
$\Bbbk$,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:sg:eq:arithfamily}
 \ct\bigl(p_i(c+s)\bigr)=p_i(c),
\end{equation}
since the positive powers of $s$ are purely infinite \src{C16}.

\begin{proposition}[Arithmetic families above liftable points \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:prop:arithfamily}
Let $\widetilde C\cong\Aff^1_\Bbbk$ with $\Bbbk=\R$ or $\C$, and $\mathfrak o=\Z$ or $\Z[i]$
respectively. If $\mathbf x_0\in\mathcal C(\mathfrak o)$ has a $\Bbbk$-rational preimage $c$
under the normalization, then $\nu(c+\omega^\beta)\in\mathcal C(\mathfrak o\oplus\Pi_\Bbbk)$
for every positive surreal $\beta$, where $\Pi_\R=\Pi$. These points are nonconstant, have
finite support and constant term $\mathbf x_0$, and they form a proper class of distinct
points.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Write $p_i(c+S)=p_i(c)+\sum_{j=1}^{d_i}b_{ij}S^j$.
At $S=\omega^\beta$ the exponents $j\beta$ are positive and distinct.
The constant coefficient is $p_i(c)\in\mathfrak o$; the other coefficients
need only belong to $\Bbbk$, as allowed in the omnific ring. Every defining
equation vanishes identically after substitution of the polynomial tuple
$\nu(T)$, so it still vanishes at $c+\omega^\beta$.
At least one coordinate polynomial has degree $e\ge1$, because the
normalization map is not constant. Its leading exponent is $e\beta$ with
nonzero coefficient, so the tuple is nonconstant. Multiplication by the
positive integer $e$ is injective on the ordered exponent group; distinct
$\beta$ therefore give distinct tuples. Each tuple has finite support.
Finally the positive surreal exponents form a proper class, proving the
claimed size of this family in the standing class convention.
\end{proof}

Over $\C$ every ordinary point has a complex preimage under the finite surjective
normalization: its fiber is a nonempty finite $\C$-scheme, and any closed
point of that fiber has residue field a finite extension of $\C$, hence $\C$.
Thus the existence dichotomy is complete for Gaussian points.

\begin{corollary}[The Gaussian existence dichotomy \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:cor:gaussian}
Let $\mathcal C$ be an affine $\Z[i]$-model with geometrically integral complex fiber $C_\C$.
Nonordinary $\Og$-points exist if and only if $\widetilde C_\C\cong\Aff^1_\C$ and
$\mathcal C(\Z[i])\ne\varnothing$. Then every ordinary Gaussian point is the constant term of
a proper class of finite-support nonordinary points.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A nonordinary tuple lies in a complex Hahn workspace and is nonconstant
there, because $\Og\cap\C=\Z[i]$. It forces the normalization condition by
\cref{odg:sg:thm:main}. Constant extraction is a ring homomorphism fixing
$\Z[i]$, so applying it to the defining equations gives an ordinary
Gaussian point. The converse is
\cref{odg:sg:prop:arithfamily}.
\end{proof}

For real omnific points the converse holds at every nonsingular ordinary point and at every
singular ordinary point with a real preimage under the normalization. A real point of a
singular curve can have only nonreal preimages, so the complex argument does not transfer,
and the real singular fibers are not classified (\cref{odg:sg:q:real}) \src{C16}.
For example, $Y^2=X^2(X-1)$ has affine-line normalization
$(X,Y)=(T^2+1,T(T^2+1))$, but the ordinary point $(0,0)$ has only the
preimages $T=\pm i$. Thus the real polynomial-family construction does not
apply at that point; this observation alone does not rule out all possible
Hahn points in its constant-term fiber. For
smooth curves the fibers are known exactly (\cref{odg:cr:cor:omnific}); for singular
exceptional curves \cref{odg:sg:prop:arithfamily} gives families in the fibers, not the
entire fibers: no lifting theorem for every point is proved
(\cref{odg:sg:q:lifting}).

The criterion decides whether a curve supports nonconstant points once its affine
normalization is known, and for superelliptic equations \cref{odg:sg:thm:super} makes this a
multiplicity computation. It does not decide whether ordinary integer points exist; the
implication $\mathcal C(\Oz)\ne\varnothing\Rightarrow\mathcal C(\Z)\ne\varnothing$ already
follows from the retraction. What it decides is whether \emph{additional} nonordinary
solutions can occur \src{C16}.

\subsubsection{Boundaries of the criterion}\label{odg:sg:sub:boundaries}

\paragraph{The field is different.} The Hahn field contains
$\omega^\gamma(1+\omega^{-2\gamma})^{1/2}$ for every $\gamma>0$, while $\crA$ does not, and
similar expansions give nonconstant field points on many curves of positive genus
(\cref{odg:cr:rem:fieldpoints}). The criterion concerns $\crA$-points, not $\crH$-points;
the prohibition of negative $\omega$-exponents is indispensable \src{C16}.

\paragraph{Constant coefficients.} If a coefficient may be a chosen element of the Hahn
ring, a prescribed tuple can be forced to solve a relation
(\cref{odg:cr:sub:boundaries}). The proof needs the Euler derivations to kill the
coefficients, and the function field to have transcendence degree one over the constant
field \src{C16}.

\paragraph{Characteristic zero is used twice.} Seidenberg's identity divides by $n!$, and an
Euler derivation must detect a nonzero valuation. In characteristic $p$ an additive map
$\Gamma\to\Bbbk$ kills $p\Gamma$ and vanishes on a $p$-divisible group
(\cref{odg:cr:sub:charp}). Seidenberg also treats Hasse--Schmidt operators, but that alone
does not supply a valuation-detecting operator. \Cref{odg:sg:prop:oneplace} survives in
every characteristic; the genus-zero conclusion is not asserted in positive
characteristic \src{C16}.

\paragraph{Higher dimension.} For a curve a nontrivial valuation trivial on constants is
controlled by one discrete place, and one nonzero regular differential has a scalar local
expression $h\,\dif u$ whose contraction is nonzero with known positive valuation. In
higher dimension valuations need not be discrete, the derivation can annihilate chosen
global forms, and a centre may have several tangent directions. The conductor identity
holds for finite birational extensions of domains in any dimension, but the geometric step
needs new hypotheses. The logarithmic and symmetric-differential criteria of
\cref{odg:cr:thm:symmetric,odg:log:cor:criterion} are not subsumed by this curve argument
(\cref{odg:sg:q:birational}) \src{C16}.

\paragraph{Normalization lifting.} The criterion concerns the \emph{existence} of a
nonconstant $\crA$-point and of a polynomial arc. It does not say that a given nonconstant
point of a singular exceptional curve factors through an $\crA$-point of the normalization;
\cref{odg:sg:thm:conductor} deliberately allows $b_j\notin\crA$. So the complete existence
dichotomy and the open lifting problem (\cref{odg:sg:q:lifting}) are consistent \src{C16}.

\subsubsection{Source C16's own audit}\label{odg:sg:sub:audit}

Source C16 ends with a table of the pitfalls it checked. Support reversal: $t^\gamma=
\omega^{-\gamma}$ throughout, and its ring has nonpositive $t$-support. Normality of $\crA$:
never assumed, with an explicit counterexample in $\Frac\crA$. The conductor: it comes from
the finite normalization of the finite-type curve, not from properties of $\crA$. Infinite
differential closure: only the finite identity \eqref{odg:sg:eq:seidenberg} is used, with no
Noetherian hypothesis on a differential hull. High rank: the conductor and the local
parameter come from one function field, so their valuations are integer multiples of one
$\gamma$. The derivation may leave $F$; the contraction uses $\Omega^1_{F/\Bbbk}$ and takes
values in $\crH$. Nonzero contraction: $\alpha=h\,\dif u$ with $h\ne0$, and the chosen
derivation keeps the leading term of $u$. No hidden infinite sum: the only auxiliary series
is formal in a new variable, and one coefficient is used. Arithmetic and geometry: ordinary
integer points and real normalization fibers are kept separate from the geometric
criterion. Proper classes: all algebraic geometry takes place in a set-sized workspace
containing the finite tuple. Novelty: a proposed extension of the inspected baseline, with
no exhaustive priority or peer-review claim.

Its script checks the finite Taylor identity, Euler derivations on finite rational
supports, the multiplicity and genus arithmetic of \cref{odg:sg:sub:super}, explicit
normalization substitutions, the elliptic B\'ezout certificate and the derivative-order
inequalities, with exact arithmetic (\cref{odg:app:verification}). These are regression
checks for finite parts of the argument, not proofs of Hahn summability, normalization,
Riemann--Roch or the universally quantified theorems; proofs, finite checks and a clean
build are different kinds of evidence \src{C16}. Its summary: the smooth result extends
through arbitrary affine-curve singularities, not by lifting a point into a normal ring but
by keeping one conductor of the original curve to bound all powers of a contracted
differential, which the one-dimensional place turns into a finite contradiction; the
only escape is normalization by the affine line, and transfinite supports or larger
scales cannot make a rigid constant curve flexible. What remains concerns the exceptional
fibers, higher dimension and effective verification (\cref{odg:sg:q:lifting,odg:sg:q:real,odg:sg:q:order,odg:sg:q:engine,odg:sg:q:charp,odg:sg:q:birational,odg:sg:q:images,odg:sg:q:lean}).

\section{Group varieties, coefficient algebras and sharpness}\label{odg:cr:sec:groups}

Until the explicit change of characteristic in \cref{odg:cr:sub:charp},
$\Bbbk$ has characteristic zero. The exponent group $\Gamma$ may be zero;
then $\crA=\Bbbk$ and $\crP=0$. For a $\Bbbk$-scheme $X$, the notation
$\ct:X(\crA)\to X(\Bbbk)$ denotes the map induced by the ring retraction.
A constant point in $X(\crA)$ is obtained by extending scalars along
$\Bbbk\hookrightarrow\crA$.

\subsection{Abelian and semiabelian varieties}\label{odg:cr:sub:abelian}

\begin{lemma}[Invariant differentials \src{C11, C12, C13, C14, C15}]\label{odg:cr:lem:invariant}
For a smooth algebraic group $G$ over $\Bbbk$ with identity $e$, translation gives an
isomorphism $\Omega^1_{G/\Bbbk}\cong\mathcal O_G\otimes_\Bbbk e^*\Omega^1_{G/\Bbbk}$; in
particular $\Omega^1_{G/\Bbbk}$ is generated by global invariant one-forms.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Differentiating the multiplication $G\times G\to G$ in the second factor along
$G\times\{e\}$ gives a morphism of vector bundles $G\times T_eG\to TG$,
$(g,v)\mapsto d(L_g)_e(v)$, whose inverse comes from translation by $g^{-1}$ and is
algebraic because inversion is a morphism. Its fiber at each $g$ is an
isomorphism with inverse $d(L_{g^{-1}})_g$, so it trivializes $TG$;
dualizing gives the displayed cotangent isomorphism. A basis at the
identity extends to global left-invariant forms spanning every fiber
\src{C12, C13}. The statement is
also \cite[Tag 047I]{StacksGroups} and, for abelian varieties,
\cite[Chapter IV, Proposition 6.4(b)]{MilneAV}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Abelian varieties \src{C10, C11, C13, C14, C15; also C12}]\label{odg:cr:thm:abelian}
For every abelian variety $A$ over $\Bbbk$, $A(\crA)=A(\Bbbk)$ as groups, with $\ct$
inverse to the inclusion. Every locally closed subscheme of $A$, singular or not,
has only constant $\crA$-points.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
An abelian variety is smooth and proper, and \cref{odg:cr:lem:invariant} gives
global generation; apply \cref{odg:cr:cor:hahnproper,odg:cr:prop:inherit}. The
maps come from ring maps and preserve the group law.
\end{proof}

This concerns scheme-valued points over the ring, including points not given by a
unimodular tuple in a fixed projective embedding; it is not the statement
$A(\crH)=A(\Bbbk)$, which is false, and it is not restricted to torsion points
\src{C10, C11, C13}. For $\Gamma=\Z$ it is the familiar constancy of polynomially
parametrized points of an abelian variety; for other groups the extra infinite
supports create no new points \src{C10}. Source C12 proves the theorem over
algebraically closed $\Bbbk$ and for closed subvarieties.

\begin{lemma}[Tori \src{C14; also C10, C11, C12, C13, C15}]\label{odg:cr:lem:torus}
For every $\Bbbk$-torus $T$, $T(\crA)=T(\Bbbk)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Over $\bar\Bbbk$ the torus is $\Gm^r$, whose points over
$\mathcal A_{\bar\Bbbk}(\Gamma)$ are tuples of units, hence constants.
Let $t\in T(\crA)$ and $t_0=\ct(t)$. Coefficient extension commutes with
constant extraction, so the image of $t$ is the constant extension of the
image of $t_0$. The two points agree after the injective coefficientwise
map $\crA\hookrightarrow\mathcal A_{\bar\Bbbk}(\Gamma)$.
Since $T$ is affine, equality of the extended coordinate homomorphisms
and injectivity give $t=t_0$ already over $\crA$. Equivalently, the
coordinate map takes values in $\crA\cap\bar\Bbbk=\Bbbk$ \src{C14}.
\end{proof}

Source C10 descends instead along a finite splitting field $L/\Bbbk$: coefficientwise
scalar extension gives $\crA\otimes_\Bbbk L\cong\mathcal A_L(\Gamma)$, because a finite
union of reverse-well-ordered supports is reverse well ordered. More explicitly,
choose a finite $\Bbbk$-basis of $L$ and split every coefficient in that
basis. The resulting finitely many component series have supports contained
in the original support; conversely their union is admissible. Linear
independence of the basis proves injectivity, so this is the natural
tensor-product isomorphism. The faithfully flat extension
$\crA\to\crA\otimes_\Bbbk L$ then reflects equality of points. Source C13
does the same from $\R$ to $\C$, as in \replabel{ogl:alg:lem:torus}, and source C11
descends from $\bar\Bbbk$ through \cref{odg:cr:lem:separated}; source C12 treats split
tori over an algebraically closed field. Source C15 descends along a finite splitting
field $L/\Bbbk$ using only the injectivity of the coefficientwise map
$\crA\to\mathcal A_L(\Gamma)$ and the affineness of $T$.

\begin{theorem}[Semiabelian varieties \src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14, C15}]\label{odg:cr:thm:semiabelian}
If $1\to T\to G\xrightarrow{\pi}A\to0$ is an extension of an abelian variety by a
torus over $\Bbbk$, then $G(\crA)=G(\Bbbk)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $\sigma\in G(\crA)$ put $\sigma_0=\ct(\sigma)\in G(\Bbbk)$. By
\cref{odg:cr:thm:abelian}, $\pi(\sigma)$ is constant with constant term
$\pi(\sigma_0)$, so $\pi(\sigma)=\pi(\sigma_0)$ and $\sigma_0^{-1}\sigma$ lies in the
scheme-theoretic kernel $T(\crA)$. By \cref{odg:cr:lem:torus} it is constant, and its
constant term is the identity; so $\sigma=\sigma_0$.
\end{proof}

No surjectivity of $G(\Bbbk)\to A(\Bbbk)$ is used: the lift $\sigma_0$ is the constant
term of the given point \src{C10, C13}. Properness is not assumed for $G$; the torus
part is handled by the unit lemma, and a trivial cotangent sheaf alone is not
enough, since $\Ga(\crA)=\crA$ \src{C11, C14}.

\begin{theorem}[Quasi-finite maps \src{C11, C15; also C10, C12, C14}]\label{odg:cr:thm:quasifinite}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a quasi-finite morphism of separated $\Bbbk$-schemes of finite type
with $Y(\crA)=Y(\Bbbk)$. Then $X(\crA)=X(\Bbbk)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A $\Bbbk$-scheme $Z$ of finite type and dimension zero is affine and
finite over $\Bbbk$ \cite[Lemma 33.20.2]{StacksZeroDim}. Write $Z=\Spec E$.
For any $\Bbbk$-algebra map $E\to\crA$, every image element is algebraic
over $\Bbbk$, hence belongs to $\Bbbk$ by \cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}(d).
Thus the map factors through $\Bbbk$, necessarily by its own constant
term, and $Z(\crA)=Z(\Bbbk)$. This includes nonreduced $Z$: its
nilpotents map to zero in the domain $\crA$ \src{C11, C14}.
For $x\in X(\crA)$ write $y=\ct(f(x))\in Y(\Bbbk)$.
Rigidity of $Y$ says $f(x)$ is the constant extension of $y$, so the
fiber-product property factors $x$ through $X_y$. Quasi-finiteness makes
this a zero-dimensional finite-type scheme over $\Bbbk$, and the preceding
argument makes $x$ constant. No finiteness or surjectivity of $f$ is needed.
\end{proof}

Thus locally closed subschemes and finite covers of semiabelian varieties, of
positive-genus curves and of their finite products are rigid, and so are finite
covers of products of positive-genus curves \src{C10, C11, C12, C14}. Source C14
states the finite case and source C10 the quasi-finite case over semiabelian and
other rigid separated targets; source C10 proves the zero-dimensional step by
\cref{odg:cr:cor:algclosed}. Adjoining a free affine-line factor destroys rigidity:
a product $X\times\Aff^1$ has nonconstant points through each $\Bbbk$-point of $X$
\src{C12}.

\begin{theorem}[The nonconstant part of a commutative group \src{C13}, for every $\Bbbk$]\label{odg:cr:thm:groupkernel}
Let $G$ be a smooth connected commutative algebraic group over $\Bbbk$, let $U$ be its
maximal connected unipotent affine subgroup, and fix an isomorphism
$U\cong\Ga^d$. Then $\ct$ gives a split exact sequence of abelian groups
\[
 0\longrightarrow\crP^{\,d}\longrightarrow G(\crA)
 \xrightarrow{\ \ct\ }G(\Bbbk)\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad\text{so}\qquad G(\crA)\cong G(\Bbbk)\oplus\crP^{\,d}.
\]
The kernel is canonically $\ker\bigl(U(\crA)\to U(\Bbbk)\bigr)$; its identification with
$\crP^{\,d}$ depends on the chosen coordinates. For $\Gamma=\No$ in the class
convention the kernel is $\Pi^d$ over $\R$ and $\Pi_\C^d$ over $\C$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By the Barsotti--Chevalley theorem there is a connected affine normal subgroup
$N\subseteq G$ with $G/N$ an abelian variety; since $G$ is commutative and $\Bbbk$ is
perfect, $N\cong T\times U$ with $T$ a torus and $U$ unipotent, and in
characteristic zero the commutative unipotent group $U$ is a vector group
\cite[Theorem 8.27, Corollaries 14.33, 16.15]{MilneAG}.
Here the product decomposition is applied to the \emph{affine} group $N$;
it is not a decomposition of $G$ itself. The largest connected affine
subgroup contains every connected unipotent affine subgroup of $G$, so
the $U$ in this decomposition is the subgroup in the statement.
Thus $G/U$ is an extension of
$G/N$ by $N/U\cong T$, a semiabelian variety. For $\sigma\in G(\crA)$ with
$\sigma_0=\ct(\sigma)$, the image of $\sigma$ in $(G/U)(\crA)$ is constant by
\cref{odg:cr:thm:semiabelian} and equals that of $\sigma_0$; so
$\sigma-\sigma_0\in U(\crA)$ has constant term $0$, that is, lies in $\crP^{\,d}$ in the
chosen coordinates. Conversely $\crP^{\,d}\subseteq U(\crA)$ lies in the kernel of $\ct$.
Let $\iota:U\hookrightarrow G$ denote inclusion, using the chosen vector
coordinates on $U$. The splitting and its inverse are explicitly
\[
 (a,s)\longmapsto a+\iota(s),\qquad
 \sigma\longmapsto\bigl(\ct(\sigma),
     \iota^{-1}(\sigma-\ct(\sigma))\bigr).
\]
The preceding kernel calculation justifies the second coordinate.
These maps are mutually inverse group homomorphisms because $G$ is
commutative, proving the direct-sum assertion, also when $d=0$ or $\Gamma=0$.
\end{proof}

Source C13 states and proves the theorem for $\Bbbk=\R$ and $\C$. Its structure inputs
are stated in its reference for perfect fields and for characteristic zero, and the
torus and semiabelian steps hold over every field of characteristic zero
(\cref{odg:cr:lem:torus,odg:cr:thm:semiabelian}); so the statement is printed for every
such $\Bbbk$. The cited statements have now been checked in the 2017 edition,
on printed pages 154, 289 and 329; in the last, the affine-group convention
is essential. The splitting is a splitting of groups of points
supplied by the retraction, not an algebraic splitting of $G$: an extension of an
abelian variety by a vector group can be nontrivial. It is not a formal-completion
statement either: $\crP$ is not nilpotent, and its nonzero elements are infinite, not
infinitesimal \src{C13}. For instance $\Ga(\crA)=\Bbbk\oplus\crP$, $\Gm(\crA)=\Bbbk^\times$
and $A(\crA)=A(\Bbbk)$. No analogous claim is made for noncommutative groups:
$\SL_2(\crA)$ and $\GL_2(\crA)$ contain the nonconstant unipotent matrices
$\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&u\\0&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$, $u\in\crP$
\src{C12, C13}.

\begin{remark}[Consistency with the omnific groups report \mergetag]\label{odg:cr:rem:ogl}
The report \fn{docs/surreal/omnific-groups-and-lattices} decides rigidity for closed
subgroup schemes of $\GL_N$: over $\BR$ exactly when the real fiber has no
nonidentity unipotent element, over $\BC$ exactly when the identity component is a
torus (\replabel{ogl:alg:thm:real}, \replabel{ogl:alg:thm:complex}), which answers
\cref{odg:q:affine} for group schemes (\replabel{ogl:alg:rem:affine}). The two
answers agree where they overlap. (1) In dimension one, $\Ga$ is the affine line and
is nonrigid in both; $\Gm$, which is $\mathbb P^1$ minus two points, and the
anisotropic torus $\SO(2)$, the conic $x^2+y^2=1$ with two conjugate points at
infinity, are rigid in both. (2) For a commutative unipotent group the
Baker--Campbell--Hausdorff product of \replabel{ogl:alg:prop:bch} is addition, and
its kernel $\exp(\mathfrak u\otimes\Pi_\Bbbk)$ is the group $\crP^{\,d}$ of
\cref{odg:cr:thm:groupkernel} in vector coordinates; the latter theorem extends the
description to commutative groups that are not affine. (3) Semiabelian rigidity
extends the torus freezing of \replabel{ogl:alg:lem:torus} beyond linear groups. (4)
For smooth curves real and complex rigidity coincide
(\cref{odg:cr:cor:omnific}(a)), but for groups they do not: $\SO_3$ is rigid over
$\BR$ because $\SO_3(\R)$ is compact, and not over $\BC$ because $\SO_{3,\C}$ is not a
torus. There is no conflict, because sources C10--C14 transfer rigidity only from
the complex fiber to the real one, never conversely.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Reduced coefficient algebras and dual numbers}\label{odg:cr:sub:reduced}

For a commutative $\Bbbk$-algebra $S$ let $\mathcal A_S(\Gamma)$ be the formal sums
$\sum_\gamma a_\gamma\omega^\gamma$ with $a_\gamma\in S$ and reverse-well-ordered support
in $\Gamma^{\ge0}$. The Hahn support lemma makes this a ring even when $S$ has zero
divisors, with maps $S\hookrightarrow\mathcal A_S(\Gamma)\xrightarrow{\ct}S$ whose
composite is the identity; for such $S$ no leading coefficient is divided by
\src{C11}.

\begin{lemma}[Coefficientwise separation \src{C11}]\label{odg:cr:lem:reducedinjective}
If $S$ is reduced, the coefficientwise maps
$\mathcal A_S(\Gamma)\to\mathcal A_{F_{\mathfrak p}}(\Gamma)$, with
$F_{\mathfrak p}=\Frac(S/\mathfrak p)$ for the primes $\mathfrak p$ of $S$, have kernels
with zero intersection.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For each prime $\mathfrak p$, the map $S\to F_{\mathfrak p}$ has kernel
$\mathfrak p$, so a series vanishes under that coefficientwise map exactly
when all its coefficients lie in $\mathfrak p$. A series in every kernel
therefore has each coefficient in the nilradical of $S$, which is zero.
No uniform finite support or finite collection of primes is required.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Reduced coefficient algebras \src{C11}]\label{odg:cr:thm:reduced}
Let $X$ be a separated $\Bbbk$-scheme with $X(\mathcal A_F(\Gamma))=X(F)$ for every field
extension $F/\Bbbk$. Then for every reduced commutative $\Bbbk$-algebra $S$ the inclusion
and $\ct$ are inverse bijections $X(S)\cong X(\mathcal A_S(\Gamma))$. This applies to
smooth proper varieties with globally generated cotangent sheaf, to semiabelian
varieties, to smooth geometrically integral affine curves not isomorphic to the
affine line, and to separated schemes of finite type with a quasi-finite map to one
of these.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Injectivity holds because $\ct$ is a retraction. For $\sigma\in X(\mathcal A_S(\Gamma))$
let $\sigma_0$ be the constant point of $\ct(\sigma)$. For each prime $\mathfrak p$ the
image of $\sigma$ over $\mathcal A_{F_{\mathfrak p}}(\Gamma)$ is constant by hypothesis,
and since all maps are coefficientwise its constant term is the image of
$\ct(\sigma)$; so $\sigma$ and $\sigma_0$ agree over every
$\mathcal A_{F_{\mathfrak p}}(\Gamma)$. To spell out the descent,
separatedness makes their equalizer a closed subscheme of
$\Spec\mathcal A_S(\Gamma)$, defined by an ideal $J$. Equality after each
coefficient map puts $J$ in every kernel. Their zero intersection, proved
in \cref{odg:cr:lem:reducedinjective}, gives $J=0$, so the equalizer is the
whole spectrum and $\sigma=\sigma_0$. If $S$ is the zero ring both spectra
are empty and the assertion is immediate. The listed classes satisfy the hypothesis over every
$F\supseteq\Bbbk$, which has characteristic zero: properness, smoothness and global
generation are stable under field extension
(\cref{odg:cr:cor:hahnproper,odg:cr:thm:semiabelian}), the affine-line condition is
geometric (\cref{odg:cr:thm:affine}), and \cref{odg:cr:thm:quasifinite} applies after
base change.
\end{proof}

The proof uses joint injectivity of the coefficient maps and the closed
diagonal, not a faithfully flat cover. Nor does it identify
$\mathcal A_S(\Gamma)$ with $S\otimes_\Bbbk\crA$ \src{C11}.
For example, take $S=\Bbbk[s]$ and $\Gamma=\Q$. The series
\[
 \sum_{n\ge1}s^n\omega^{1/n}\in\mathcal A_{\Bbbk[s]}(\Q)
\]
has reverse-well-ordered positive support. Its coefficients span an
infinite-dimensional $\Bbbk$-subspace of $S$. The coefficients of the
image of any tensor $\sum_{j=1}^r a_j\otimes f_j$ lie in the
finite-dimensional span of $a_1,\ldots,a_r$, so the natural tensor map
is not surjective in this example. This does not assert failure of
flatness. The principle uses all primes and needs no Jacobson or
finite-type condition on $S$; for $S=\Bbbk[s_1,\ldots,s_r]$ it says that a family of
points with Hahn coordinates and polynomial coefficient functions already comes from
a family over $S$ \src{C11}.

\begin{lemma}[Square-zero lifts \src{C11}]\label{odg:cr:lem:tangent}
Let $X$ be locally of finite type over $\Bbbk$, $S$ a $\Bbbk$-algebra and $p\in X(S)$.
Write $S[\epsilon]:=S[T]/(T^2)$, with $\epsilon$ the class of $T$.
With the constant lift as origin, the lifts of $p$ to $S[\epsilon]$
correspond to $\Hom_S(p^*\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk},S)$; if $p$ comes from a smooth point
$p_0\in X(\Bbbk)$, this is $T_{p_0}X\otimes_\Bbbk S$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The square-zero closed immersion $\Spec S\hookrightarrow\Spec S[\epsilon]$
is a homeomorphism on underlying spaces. Hence a lift has the same
underlying map as $p$, and can be described on the same affine charts.
Locally its ring map is uniquely $\varphi+\epsilon\delta$, with
$\delta$ additive and zero on $\Bbbk$. Multiplicativity is equivalent to
$\delta(ab)=\varphi(a)\delta(b)+\varphi(b)\delta(a)$, because
$\epsilon^2=0$. The universal property of K\"ahler differentials identifies
these derivations with the displayed Hom module. This construction
respects restrictions to smaller charts, so the local descriptions glue.
For a constant smooth $\Bbbk$-point $p_0$, its cotangent space is finite
dimensional, and taking its dual commutes with scalar extension to $S$.
This gives $T_{p_0}X\otimes_\Bbbk S$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The dual-number defect \src{C11}]\label{odg:cr:thm:dualdefect}
For an abelian variety $A$ over $\Bbbk$ there is a canonical split description
\[
 A\bigl(\crA[\epsilon]\bigr)\cong A(\Bbbk)\oplus\bigl(\Lie A\otimes_\Bbbk\crA\bigr),
\]
under which $A(\Bbbk[\epsilon])=A(\Bbbk)\oplus\Lie A$. Since
$\mathcal A_{\Bbbk[\epsilon]}(\Gamma)\cong\crA[\epsilon]$ coefficientwise,
\[
 A\bigl(\mathcal A_{\Bbbk[\epsilon]}(\Gamma)\bigr)\big/A(\Bbbk[\epsilon])
 \cong\Lie A\otimes_\Bbbk\crP .
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
First split every coefficient $a+\epsilon b$ into its two components.
Their supports are subsets of the original support; conversely the union
of two reverse-well-ordered supports is reverse well ordered.
This proves the natural ring isomorphism
$\mathcal A_{\Bbbk[\epsilon]}(\Gamma)\cong\crA[\epsilon]$ and its
compatibility with reduction and constant extraction.

Reduction modulo $\epsilon$ maps $A(\crA[\epsilon])$ onto $A(\crA)=A(\Bbbk)$
(\cref{odg:cr:thm:abelian}), split by constant lifts. By \cref{odg:cr:lem:tangent} the
kernel over the identity is $\Lie A\otimes_\Bbbk\crA$, and on square-zero tangent vectors
the group law is addition: the differential of multiplication at $(e,e)$
sends $(v,w)$ to $v+w$, and all products of the first-order errors vanish
modulo $\epsilon^2$. Commutativity makes the split extension a direct sum.
Applying the same natural construction over $\Bbbk$ identifies the
constant subgroup as $A(\Bbbk)\oplus(\Lie A\otimes_\Bbbk\Bbbk)$.
Finally the additive decomposition $\crA=\Bbbk\oplus\crP$ splits
$\Lie A\otimes_\Bbbk\crA$ into the ordinary tangent space and
$\Lie A\otimes_\Bbbk\crP$; quotienting by the former gives the assertion.
\end{proof}

If $\dim A>0$ and $\Gamma\ne0$, a nonzero $v\in\Lie A$ and $h=\omega^\gamma$ with
$\gamma>0$ give the new point $\epsilon hv$; so reducedness cannot be dropped in
\cref{odg:cr:thm:reduced}, even for elliptic curves. Concretely, on the smooth affine
curve $Y^2=X^3-X$ the pair $(0,\epsilon h)$ with $0\ne h\in\crP$ is a point over
$\mathcal A_{\Bbbk[\epsilon]}(\Gamma)$ not coming from $\Bbbk[\epsilon]$.
Here $\partial(Y^2-X^3+X)/\partial X=1$ at $(0,0)$, so the chosen point
is smooth, and substitution gives zero because $(\epsilon h)^2=0$.
Its $Y$-coordinate has a nonzero positive-growth coefficient and therefore
is not in $\Bbbk[\epsilon]$. No omnific ring
contains such a nilpotent; the example belongs to the coefficient-ring extension of
the theory only \src{C11}.

\subsection{Positive characteristic}\label{odg:cr:sub:charp}

\begin{proposition}[An elliptic point in characteristic two \src{C11, C14, C15}]\label{odg:cr:prop:char2}
Let $\Bbbk$ have characteristic two. In $\mathcal A_\Bbbk(\Q)$ put
\[
 x=\omega^{1/3},\qquad y=\sum_{n\ge1}\omega^{1/2^n}.
\]
Then $y^2+y=x^3$, and the projective closure $Y^2Z+YZ^2=X^3$ is a smooth cubic of
genus one with the rational point $[0:1:0]$. The point $(x,y)$ is nonconstant.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The support $\{2^{-n}:n\ge1\}$ is positive and reverse well ordered:
every nonempty subset has a largest exponent, indexed by its least $n$.
The Hahn product has only finitely many contributions at each exponent.
In characteristic two the off-diagonal terms pair as $(i,j)$ and $(j,i)$
and cancel, leaving precisely the diagonal terms. Consequently
\[
 y^2=\sum_{n\ge1}\omega^{2^{1-n}}
     =\omega+\sum_{n\ge1}\omega^{2^{-n}}=x^3+y.
\]
This is an identity of Hahn coefficients, without a convergence claim
for the sequence of partial sums. The partial
derivatives of $Y^2Z+YZ^2-X^3$ are $X^2$, $Z^2$ and $Y^2$ in characteristic two, with
no common projective zero; a smooth plane cubic has genus one.
\end{proof}

Source C14 states this for every field of characteristic two with $\Gamma=\Q$;
source C11 states it for $\Bbbk=\bar{\mathbb F}_2$ and $\Gamma=\Z[\tfrac12]$ with the
point $x=\omega$, $y=\sum_{n\ge1}\omega^{3/2^n}$, which is the same example with all
exponents multiplied by three. Source C15 states it for every field of characteristic two
with $\Gamma=\Z[\tfrac12]$ and the point $x=\sum_{n\ge1}\omega^{3/2^n}$, $y=\omega$ of
$x^2+x=y^3$, which is source C11's example with $x$ and $y$ interchanged. The partial sums $y_N$ satisfy
$y_N^2+y_N+x^3=\omega^{2^{-N}}$, and this residual monomial does not tend to zero in
the valuation topology; the proof is an exact coefficient identity, not a limit
\src{C11, C14}. Every additive map from a $2$-divisible group to a field of
characteristic two is zero, since $\lambda(\gamma)=2\lambda(\gamma/2)$; so the separating
Euler family disappears exactly where the characteristic-zero argument needs it. The
exponent group has rank one, so neither rank nor non-Archimedean complexity explains
the failure. The telescoping is the Artin--Schreier phenomenon in Hahn form and is not
claimed as new \src{C11}.

\begin{proposition}[Squarefree rigidity fails in characteristic $p$ \src{C13}]\label{odg:cr:prop:charp}
Over $\mathbb F_p$ with $\Gamma=\Q$, the elements $x=\omega$ and
$y=\omega-\omega^{1/p}$ of $\mathcal A_{\mathbb F_p}(\Q)$ satisfy $y^p=x^p-x$, although
$X^p-X$ is squarefree.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The Frobenius is additive in characteristic $p$, so
$y^p=\omega^p-\omega=x^p-x$; and $(X^p-X)'=-1$.
\end{proof}

The curve $y^p=x^p-x$ is itself an affine line in characteristic $p$: with
$w=y-x$ one has $w^p=-x$, so $x=-w^p$ and $y=w-w^p$ \mergetag. Thus this example
refutes the characteristic-$p$ analogue of \cref{odg:cr:thm:squarefree}, not of
\cref{odg:cr:thm:affine}; the genus-one example of \cref{odg:cr:prop:char2} refutes
the latter. Characteristic zero is used twice: to separate exponents by rational
linear functionals and to differentiate minimal polynomials of algebraic elements;
ordinary derivations in characteristic $p$ kill $p$-th powers, so the proofs give no
positive-characteristic classification \src{C10, C12, C13}.

\subsection{Discretely ordered rings}\label{odg:cr:sub:dor}

The rigidity singles out the omnific integers; it does not extend to every
discretely ordered subring of a surreal field. Let $\R((\omega^{-1}))$ be the ordered
field of Laurent series in the positive infinitesimal $\omega^{-1}$, a subfield of
$\No$, and put
\[
 x=\omega^2,\qquad y=\omega^3(1-\omega^{-4})^{1/2}
 =\omega^3-\tfrac12\omega^{-1}-\tfrac18\omega^{-5}-\tfrac1{16}\omega^{-9}-\cdots,
\]
so that $y^2=\omega^6-\omega^2=x^3-x$.

\begin{theorem}[A discretely ordered ring with no omnific copy \src{C11}]\label{odg:cr:thm:dor}
The ring $R_0=\Z[x,y]\subseteq\R((\omega^{-1}))$ is isomorphic to
$\Z[X,Y]/(Y^2-X^3+X)$ and is discretely ordered by the order of $\No$. It admits no
injective unital ring homomorphism into $\Oz$, although it is an ordered subring of
$\No$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Division by the monic polynomial $Y^2-X^3+X$ in $\Z[X][Y]$ gives a
unique remainder $f(X)+Yg(X)$ with $f,g\in\Z[X]$. Substitution is
well defined because the displayed binomial series squares to
$\omega^6-\omega^2$. If $f\ne0$, the leading exponent of $f(x)$ is
$2\deg f$, even; if $g\ne0$, that of $yg(x)$ is $3+2\deg g$, odd,
since the leading coefficient of $y$ is $1$. Thus the two leading terms
cannot cancel. A nonzero remainder has nonzero image, proving the
asserted ring isomorphism and excluding further polynomial relations.

If $g\ne0$ or $\deg f\ge1$, the leading exponent of that image is
positive and its absolute value exceeds every ordinary integer. Otherwise
the image is an ordinary integer. Hence any positive element is at least
$1$, proving discrete order. If $\psi:R_0\to\Oz$ were an injective unital homomorphism, then
$\psi(x),\psi(y)$ would solve the nonsingular equation $Y^2=X^3-X$, so both would be
integers by \cref{odg:cr:cor:weierstrass}. Put $n=\psi(x)\in\Z$.
A unital ring homomorphism fixes the ordinary integers, so
$\psi(x-n)=0$. But $x-n=\omega^2-n\ne0$, contradicting injectivity.
\end{proof}

The element $y$ has an infinite leading term and a nonzero infinitesimal tail, so it is
not omnific; discrete order says that no element lies strictly between $0$ and $1$, not
that elements lack infinitesimal terms \src{C11}. So the canonical integer part $\Oz$ of
$\No$ need not contain a copy of every discretely ordered ring inside $\No$. Van den
Dries studied which curves over $\Z$ have infinite points in \emph{some} discretely
ordered ring, in connection with open induction \cite{vdDries}; that quantifier over
rings is not the fixed-ring question here. Sources C11, C14 and C15 consulted only the
bibliographic record and the abstract of that paper, and no source uses it; the
abstract was confirmed during placement, but the paper itself was not read for this
report, and no comparison or priority statement is made. The theorem is consistent
with open induction in the nonnegative part of $\Oz$ (see
\cref{odg:rem:foursquares,odg:thm:induction}; the analogue for the definable
omnific ring is \replabel{dsn:cor:openinduction}), which concerns those rings, not
$R_0$.

\subsection{Coefficients, rings and the scope of the method}\label{odg:cr:sub:boundaries}

The rigidity assertions above assume constant coefficients, which the Euler
derivations kill. With varying coefficients this reasoning need not apply.
One may choose a nonconstant point first and build it into the
coefficients \src{C14}: $y^2=x^3+(1-\omega^3)$ has the omnific point $(\omega,1)$
\src{C10}, $y^2=x^3+x-u^2$ with $u$ positive infinite has $(u^2,u^3)$ although
$4+27u^4\ne0$ \src{C12}, and $y^2=x^3+x-\omega^2$ has $(\omega^2,\omega^3)$ \src{C13}.
No rigidity is claimed for equations whose coefficients vary at a surreal scale
(\cref{odg:cr:q:coefficients}).
Status (batch 33): in dimension zero source C17 proves such a theorem: a monic polynomial
over $\crA$ whose discriminant is a nonzero constant is a purely infinite translate of its
constant-term polynomial (\cref{odg:disc:thm:translation}); the examples above are curves,
where nothing of the kind is claimed.

The valuation ring is not rigid. For $\gamma>0$ and $\varepsilon=\omega^{-\gamma}\in\crm$,
the nonsingular cubic $y^2=x^3-x+1$ has the nonconstant $\crO$-point $x=\varepsilon$,
$y=(1-\varepsilon+\varepsilon^3)^{1/2}$ \src{C12}; over $\C$, at a point $(x_0,y_0)$ of
$y^2=x^3+ax+b$ with $y_0\ne0$, the formal implicit solution
$x=x_0+\varepsilon$, $y=y_0+\tfrac{3x_0^2+a}{2y_0}\varepsilon+\cdots$ is another
\src{C10}. For $\Gamma=\Z$, the curve $y^2=x^3+1$ has the $\crO$-point
$x=\omega^{-1}$,
$y=(1+\omega^{-3})^{1/2}$ \src{C15}. The two displayed square roots are formal binomial
series with constant term $1$, supported on nonpositive exponents. For example,
the powers of $-\varepsilon+\varepsilon^3$ have exponents tending downwards
in the cyclic subgroup generated by $\gamma$; each coefficient receives
only finitely many contributions. The resulting Hahn sum squares to
$1-\varepsilon+\varepsilon^3$. This requires no topological convergence
in the full value group. The discriminant factor of $X^3-X+1$ is
$4(-1)^3+27=23\ne0$, so that example is nonsingular.
Confusing the two rings would reverse the main conclusion. Together with
\cref{odg:cr:rem:fieldpoints}, this shows that nothing is claimed over $\No$, $\No[i]$
or $\Ofin$.

The exponent group may be arbitrary: no order unit, rank, cofinality, cardinality,
divisibility or finite-support condition is imposed, and well ordering with finite
coefficient convolution suffices. For $\Gamma=0$ the ring is $\Bbbk$ and everything is
trivially rigid. Nontriviality is needed for nonconstant boundary examples
and for the converse direction of
\cref{odg:cr:thm:affine,odg:cr:cor:allcurves} \src{C14}. Every field, module, scheme and
space of functionals is set-sized; only the final collection of points ranges over a
class \src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14}. The results classify which smooth curves acquire
nonordinary points; they give no enumeration, finiteness statement or height bound for
the ordinary points, and they decide no Diophantine problem over $\Z$ that was not
already decided \src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14}. What they leave open is recorded in
\cref{odg:cr:sub:settled}.

\subsection{Logarithmic symmetric differentials}\label{odg:log:sub:log}

This subsection is built from a sixth manuscript on the subject of the part,
\emph{Geometric Rigidity over Omnific Integer Rings}, tagged \src{C15} after its
file prefix \fn{15-logarithmic-rigidity-} (\cref{odg:sub:provenance}). Its pin
precedes the placement of sources C10--C14, and it saw none of them. About three
quarters of it proves results printed above; they are printed once, and source C15
is added to their credits: the two rings, the Euler derivations and their joint
constants (\cref{odg:cr:lem:rings,odg:cr:lem:euler,odg:cr:lem:separation,odg:cr:cor:algclosed}),
the squarefree theorem by the strict route, the cubic certificate and the
Weierstrass corollary (\cref{odg:cr:thm:squarefree,odg:cr:prop:cubic,odg:cr:cor:weierstrass}),
the singular families (\cref{odg:cr:prop:singular}), symmetric differentials and
ample cotangent bundles (\cref{odg:cr:thm:symmetric,odg:cr:cor:semiample}),
inheritance (\cref{odg:cr:prop:inherit}), the curve classification by one
logarithmic form over an algebraically closed field and its descent to every field
of characteristic zero
(\cref{odg:cr:lem:logdim,odg:cr:thm:logroute,odg:cr:rem:oneform,odg:cr:thm:affine,odg:cr:cor:allcurves}),
unimodular coordinates on curves of positive genus and the Fermat specialization
(\cref{odg:cr:cor:projectiveQ}), abelian varieties, tori, semiabelian varieties and
quasi-finite maps
(\cref{odg:cr:lem:invariant,odg:cr:thm:abelian,odg:cr:lem:torus,odg:cr:thm:semiabelian,odg:cr:thm:quasifinite}),
the characteristic-two point (\cref{odg:cr:prop:char2}), and the points over $\No$
and over the valuation ring (\cref{odg:cr:rem:fieldpoints,odg:cr:sub:boundaries}).
Its new material is printed here: logarithmic annihilation on a normal-crossings
compactification (\cref{odg:log:thm:annihilation}), the tangent-separation criterion
it gives (\cref{odg:log:cor:criterion}), the logarithmic hypothesis of its abstract
interface (\cref{odg:log:rem:interface}), descent over an arbitrary coefficient ring
(\cref{odg:log:thm:descent}) and closure under finite products
(\cref{odg:log:prop:products}). Labels of this subsection carry the sub-prefix
\fn{odg:log:}. Their manuscript proof review and source comparison are recorded in
\fn{RECONCILIATION.md}; Lean formalization remains pending.

\paragraph{Translation and letters.} Source C15 uses the $t$-convention. Its ring
$\mathcal A$ of series supported in $\Gamma_{\le0}$, its ideal $\mathcal I$ of series
supported in $\Gamma_{<0}$, its valuation ring $\mathcal V$ and its field
$\mathcal H=\Bbbk((t^\Gamma))$ are $\crA$, $\crP$, $\crO$ and $\crH$ with exponents
negated, and its ring $R_D=D\oplus\mathcal I$ is $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$
with $D$ renamed $\mathfrak o$ ($D$ is the Pell parameter here). Its derivation
$D_\ell$, for an additive map $\ell:\Gamma\to(\Bbbk,+)$, multiplies $t^\gamma$ by
$\ell(\gamma)$. In characteristic zero, $\ell$ extends uniquely
$\Q$-linearly to $\Gamma_\Q$; using this extension, it is
$\partial_{-\ell}$ in the notation of
\eqref{odg:cr:eq:euler}, so the two families coincide. Its symmetric power $q$ is
$r$ here, because $q$ is the valuation-ring point, as in
\cref{odg:cr:thm:annihilation}. We return to characteristic zero throughout
this subsection. Here $\overline X$ is a smooth
proper $\Bbbk$-scheme, $E\subseteq\overline X$ a reduced divisor with simple normal
crossings ($E=\varnothing$ is allowed), and $X=\overline X\setminus E$; the letter $E$
has no other meaning in this subsection. For a field $L\supseteq\Bbbk$ and $x\in X(L)$
a tangent vector is an $L$-linear map $\xi:x^*\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk}\to L$, and a
$\Bbbk$-derivation $\partial$ of $L$ gives the tangent vector $\partial x$ with
$(\partial x)(\dif f)=\partial\bigl(f(x)\bigr)$. For
$\sigma\in H^0\bigl(\overline X,\Sym^r\Omega^1_{\overline X/\Bbbk}(\log E)\bigr)$, whose
restriction to $X$ is a regular symmetric differential, $\sigma_x(\xi,\ldots,\xi)$ is the
value of $x^*\sigma$ on $\xi$. Near every point $z\in\overline X$ there are an affine
open $W\ni z$ and $u_1,\ldots,u_n\in\mathcal O_{\overline X}(W)$ such that
$\dif u_1,\ldots,\dif u_n$ is a basis of $\Omega^1_{W/\Bbbk}$, $E\cap W$ is the zero set
of $u_1\cdots u_s$ for some $s\le n$, and
\begin{equation}\label{odg:log:eq:basis}
 \Omega^1_{\overline X/\Bbbk}(\log E)\big|_W
 =\bigoplus_{i\le s}\mathcal O_W\,\frac{\dif u_i}{u_i}
 \oplus\bigoplus_{i>s}\mathcal O_W\,\dif u_i ,
\end{equation}
the local description of logarithmic poles along a simple normal-crossings
divisor \src{C15}. The simple normal-crossings hypothesis supplies the local
boundary parameters \cite[Tag 0BI9]{StacksSNC}; smoothness lets their
differentials be completed to a coordinate basis after shrinking $W$.
One can construct the displayed sheaf inside the regular differentials on
$X\cap W$: adjoin $\dif u_i/u_i$ for the boundary components to
$\Omega^1_{W/\Bbbk}$. Changing a component equation to $a_i u_i$, with
$a_i$ a unit, changes its logarithmic differential by the regular form
$\dif a_i/a_i$. Thus the modules agree on overlaps and glue. The displayed
generators are independent because they become a rescaling of the ordinary
coordinate basis on $X\cap W$. The construction for one smooth divisor is
\cite[Section 50.15]{StacksLog}; that section does not itself state the full
normal-crossings version used here.

\begin{lemma}[The valuation-ring side \src{C15}]\label{odg:log:lem:valuation}
Let $L/\Bbbk$ be a field extension, $V$ a valuation ring of $L$ containing $\Bbbk$, and
$\partial$ a $\Bbbk$-derivation of $L$ with $\partial(V)\subseteq V$ and
$\partial u/u\in V$ for every nonzero $u\in V$. For every $x\in X(L)$, every $r\ge1$ and
every $\sigma\in H^0\bigl(\overline X,\Sym^r\Omega^1_{\overline X/\Bbbk}(\log E)\bigr)$,
\[
 \sigma_x(\partial x,\ldots,\partial x)\in V .
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By the valuative criterion of properness for arbitrary valuation rings
\cite[Tag 0BX5]{StacksProper}, $x$, viewed in $\overline X(L)$, extends to
$q\in\overline X(V)$. Let $z$ be the image of the closed point of $\Spec V$ and choose
$W\ni z$ and $u_1,\ldots,u_n$ as in \eqref{odg:log:eq:basis}. Every point of $\Spec V$
is a generization of the closed point and open sets are stable under generization,
so $q$ factors through $W$. This uses that the point is closed in
$\Spec V$, not that its image $z$ is closed in $\overline X$.
Hence every regular function on $W$ takes a value in $V$ at
$q$, and that value is its value at $x$. Write $\sigma|_W$ as a finite sum
\[
 \sigma|_W=\sum_{|\alpha|=r}c_\alpha\prod_{i\le s}\Bigl(\frac{\dif u_i}{u_i}\Bigr)^{\alpha_i}
 \prod_{i>s}(\dif u_i)^{\alpha_i},\qquad c_\alpha\in\mathcal O_{\overline X}(W).
\]
For $i\le s$ the value $u_i(x)$ is nonzero, since $x$ lies in $X$, off the boundary;
so $\partial(u_i(x))/u_i(x)\in V$. For $i>s$, $\partial(u_i(x))\in V$, and
$c_\alpha(x)\in V$. The contraction
\[
 \sigma_x(\partial x,\ldots,\partial x)=\sum_{|\alpha|=r}c_\alpha(x)
 \prod_{i\le s}\Bigl(\frac{\partial(u_i(x))}{u_i(x)}\Bigr)^{\alpha_i}
 \prod_{i>s}\bigl(\partial(u_i(x))\bigr)^{\alpha_i}
\]
therefore lies in $V$. No discreteness, rank or Noetherian hypothesis on $V$ is used,
and the point $z$ may lie on $E$ or not \src{C15}.
\end{proof}

The logarithmic-derivative hypothesis cannot be replaced by stability of
$V$ alone. In $L=\Bbbk((t))$, the ordinary derivation $\dif/\dif t$
preserves $V=\Bbbk[[t]]$. But for $\overline X=\mathbb P^1$,
$E=\{0,\infty\}$ and $x=t\in\mathbb G_m(L)$, the global logarithmic
form $\dif z/z$ contracts to $1/t\notin V$. By contrast, an Euler
derivation has $\partial(t)/t\in\Bbbk\subseteq V$.

\begin{theorem}[Logarithmic annihilation \src{C15}]\label{odg:log:thm:annihilation}
Let $p\in X(\crA)$ and let $p_{\mathbb H}\in X(\crH)$ be its image. For every $\Q$-linear
$\lambda:\Gamma_\Q\to\Bbbk$, every $r\ge1$ and every
$\sigma\in H^0\bigl(\overline X,\Sym^r\Omega^1_{\overline X/\Bbbk}(\log E)\bigr)$,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:log:eq:annihilation}
 \sigma_{p_{\mathbb H}}\bigl(\partial_\lambda p_{\mathbb H},\ldots,\partial_\lambda p_{\mathbb H}\bigr)=0 .
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $\partial=\partial_\lambda$. The restriction $\sigma|_X$ is a regular symmetric
differential. Pull it back along $p:\Spec\crA\to X$ and apply the natural
map on differentials. The image is an element of
$\Sym^r_{\crA}\Omega^1_{\crA/\Bbbk}$, a finite sum
$\sum_ja_j\,\dif b_{j1}\cdots\dif b_{jr}$ with $a_j,b_{jh}\in\crA$; no chart around $p$
and no finite generation of $\crA$ is needed, because $\Spec\crA$ is affine. By
functoriality, as in the proof of \cref{odg:cr:thm:annihilation}, the left side of
\eqref{odg:log:eq:annihilation} is $\sum_ja_j\,\partial b_{j1}\cdots\partial b_{jr}$.
The contraction on products is symmetric, so it descends directly to the
symmetric power; a lift of a global symmetric section to a global tensor
section is not required. Each $\partial b_{jh}$ lies in $\crP$
(\cref{odg:cr:lem:euler}), and $\crP$ is an ideal of $\crA$
(\cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}), so the contraction lies in
$\crP^r\subseteq\crP$ because $r\ge1$. On the other hand
$\crO$ is a valuation ring of $\crH$ containing $\Bbbk$ (\cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}),
$\partial(\crO)\subseteq\crm\subseteq\crO$, and $\partial u/u\in\crO$ for every
$u\in\crH^\times$ (\cref{odg:cr:lem:euler}); so \cref{odg:log:lem:valuation} puts the
same element in $\crO$. The two constructions agree in $\crH$:
both send a local differential $\dif f$ to
$\partial(f(p_{\mathbb H}))$, and multiplication gives the same diagonal
contraction on symmetric powers. They may use different affine charts.
Since $\crP\cap\crO=\{0\}$ (\cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}), the contraction is zero.
\end{proof}

The proof uses the two inclusions into $\crH$, not a ring map between
$\crA$ and $\crO$ compatible with those inclusions. In fact, maps between
these rings do exist: constant extraction followed by inclusion gives
$\crA\to\Bbbk\hookrightarrow\crO$ and
$\crO\to\Bbbk\hookrightarrow\crA$. For $\Gamma\ne0$ they do not preserve
the common-field images of nonconstant elements. The two points instead
have a common image over $\crH$, where the scalars are compared. The derivation
$\partial_\lambda$ need not preserve the function field of the image of $p$; its
restriction there is a derivation into $\crH$, which is all that the universal property
of differentials requires \src{C15}. For $E=\varnothing$ the theorem is the diagonal
symmetric case of \cref{odg:cr:thm:annihilation} for the Hahn data. The logarithmic case
needs the support inclusion $\partial(\crA)\subseteq\crP$: a boundary factor contributes
$\partial u/u$, which lies in $\crO$ but in general not in $\crm$ (for $u=\omega^{-\gamma}$
it is the constant $\lambda(-\gamma)$), so the valuation-ring side gives only an element of
$\crO$, and $\crP\cap\crO=\{0\}$ must replace $\crA\cap\crm=\{0\}$. This is the mechanism
of \cref{odg:cr:thm:logroute}, now in every dimension.
Degree zero would not work: the global section $1$ contracts to $1$,
and $\crP^0=\crA$ supplies no annihilation.

\begin{definition}[Logarithmic tangent separation \src{C15}]\label{odg:log:def:separation}
The global logarithmic symmetric differentials of $(\overline X,E)$ \emph{separate
tangent directions} on $X$ if for every field extension $L/\Bbbk$, every $x\in X(L)$ and
every nonzero tangent vector $\xi$ of $X_L$ at $x$ there are $r\ge1$ and
$\sigma\in H^0\bigl(\overline X,\Sym^r\Omega^1_{\overline X/\Bbbk}(\log E)\bigr)$ with
$\sigma_x(\xi,\ldots,\xi)\ne0$.
\end{definition}

For $E=\varnothing$ this is detection of tangent directions (\cref{odg:cr:def:detect}).
As there, the quantification over extension fields is needed because the point
$p_{\mathbb H}$ is generally not classical.

\begin{corollary}[The logarithmic tangent criterion \src{C15}]\label{odg:log:cor:criterion}
If the global logarithmic symmetric differentials of $(\overline X,E)$ separate tangent
directions on $X$, then $X(\crA)=X(\Bbbk)$ for every $\Gamma$, and $\ct$ is inverse to the
inclusion of constant points. This holds in particular if, for every field extension
$L/\Bbbk$ and every $x\in X(L)$, the global logarithmic one-forms span the cotangent
space, that is,
$H^0\bigl(\overline X,\Omega^1_{\overline X/\Bbbk}(\log E)\bigr)\otimes_\Bbbk L\to
x^*\Omega^1_{X/\Bbbk}$ is surjective. For $\Bbbk=\R$ or $\C$ the same holds over $\BR$ or
$\BC$ in the class convention of \cref{odg:cr:sub:conventions}.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Let $p\in X(\crA)$ and let $\lambda$ be given. By \cref{odg:log:thm:annihilation} every
global logarithmic symmetric differential vanishes on the diagonal of the tangent vector
$\partial_\lambda p_{\mathbb H}$ at $p_{\mathbb H}\in X(\crH)$, so tangent separation with
$L=\crH$ gives $\partial_\lambda p_{\mathbb H}=0$; that is,
$\partial_\lambda\bigl(f(p_{\mathbb H})\bigr)=0$ for every regular function $f$ on an affine
open neighbourhood of $p_{\mathbb H}$, and for every $\lambda$. The scheme $X$ is open in
the proper, hence separated, scheme $\overline X$, so it is separated and of finite type,
and \cref{odg:cr:lem:constantpoint} applies to the separating data
$(\crH,\crA,\crO,\{\partial_\lambda\})$. Concretely, the coordinate map
of an affine neighbourhood of the image of $p_{\mathbb H}$ has values
in the joint constants $\Bbbk$. It defines a $\Bbbk$-point whose constant
$\crA$-extension agrees with $p$ over $\crH$; separatedness reflects
this equality back to $\crA$. The point $p$ need not lie in that one
affine chart before constancy is established. Applying $\ct$ identifies
the resulting point with $\ct(p)$ and proves injectivity of constant
inclusion. If the one-forms span, a nonzero tangent vector $\xi$ at $x$ is
nonzero on the image of some global logarithmic one-form $\eta$, and $r=1$, $\sigma=\eta$
separates it. A class point lies over some $\mathcal A_\R(\Gamma)$ or
$\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma)$.
\end{proof}

For $E=\varnothing$ the corollary is \cref{odg:cr:thm:symmetric}, which contains
\cref{odg:cr:cor:hahnproper,odg:cr:cor:semiample}. For curves a single form suffices
without the criterion, and this is how source C15 proves the classification of smooth
curves over an algebraically closed field: a nonconstant point has generic image, so the
function field $F$ embeds in $\crH$; unless the completion has genus zero and at most one
boundary point, there is a nonzero global logarithmic form $\eta$ (by
\cref{odg:cr:lem:logdim} for a nonempty boundary; without boundary the regular forms have
dimension $g$), which spans $\Omega^1_{F/\Bbbk}$; by
\cref{odg:log:thm:annihilation} its contraction with every $\partial_\lambda$ vanishes, so
the derivations kill $F$, contradicting $\trdeg_\Bbbk F=1$. This is
\cref{odg:cr:rem:oneform,odg:cr:thm:logroute}, with the computation at a local parameter
replaced by \cref{odg:log:lem:valuation}, which works wherever the centre of the
valuation-ring point lies; so this route needs no closed-centre step. For genus zero with
two or more punctures the form $\dif z/z$ replaces the unit argument of
\cref{odg:cr:lem:punctures}, as source C12 also notes. Source C15 proves semiabelian
rigidity by the extension argument of \cref{odg:cr:thm:semiabelian} and the quasi-finite
results by finite fibers, not through \cref{odg:log:cor:criterion}.

\begin{remark}[An abstract interface \src{C15}]\label{odg:log:rem:interface}
Let $\Bbbk\subseteq R\subseteq L$, with $L$ a field, let $R\to\Bbbk$ be a ring retraction
with kernel $J$, let $V$ be a valuation ring of $L$ containing $\Bbbk$, and let
$\mathcal D$ be a family of $\Bbbk$-derivations of $L$ with
\[
 \partial(R)\subseteq J,\qquad \partial(V)\subseteq V\quad(\partial\in\mathcal D),\qquad
 J\cap V=\{0\},\qquad \bigcap_{\partial\in\mathcal D}\ker\partial=\Bbbk .
\]
Then the proofs of \cref{odg:log:thm:annihilation,odg:log:cor:criterion} give
$X(R)=X(\Bbbk)$ for every smooth proper $X$ whose global symmetric differentials detect
tangent directions and, if in addition $\partial u/u\in V$ for every nonzero $u\in V$ and
every $\partial\in\mathcal D$, for every $X=\overline X\setminus E$ as in
\cref{odg:log:cor:criterion}. The constancy step uses only the joint constants and the
injectivity of $R\to L$, as in the proof of \cref{odg:cr:lem:constantpoint}:
annihilation puts each contraction in $J\cap V$, tangent separation makes
every $\partial p_L$ zero, and the coordinate map of an affine chart at
$p_L$ then has image in $\Bbbk$. The resulting constant point agrees
with $p$ over $R$ by the closed equalizer argument. No assertion that
these data satisfy the different ideal conditions of
\cref{odg:cr:def:data} is needed. This
interface is dual to \cref{odg:cr:def:data}: there the ring is only stable,
$\partial(R)\subseteq R$, the valuation ring is pushed into its maximal ideal and
$R\cap\mathfrak m_V=\{0\}$; here the ring is pushed into the augmentation ideal, the
valuation ring is only stable and $J\cap V=\{0\}$. The Hahn data $(\crH,\crA,\crP,\crO)$
satisfy both, and the logarithmic hypothesis by \cref{odg:cr:lem:euler}. Source C15 notes
that properness alone is not enough, nor is a family of derivations without control of
the augmentation ideal; these are what must be checked before the argument is used in
another generalized-series setting.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Descent over any coefficient ring \src{C15}]\label{odg:log:thm:descent}
Let $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$ be a subring, $\mathcal X$ a separated $\mathfrak o$-scheme of
finite type, $X=\mathcal X\times_{\mathfrak o}\Bbbk$ and
$R=\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$. If $X(\crA)=X(\Bbbk)$, then inclusion and $\ct$ are
inverse bijections $\mathcal X(\mathfrak o)\cong\mathcal X(R)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $p\in\mathcal X(R)$ and $p_0=\ct(p)\in\mathcal X(\mathfrak o)$. Composed with
$R\subseteq\crA$, $p$ gives a point of $\mathcal X(\crA)=X(\crA)$, which is constant by
hypothesis. Since $\ct:\crA\to\Bbbk$ restricts to $\ct:R\to\mathfrak o$, applying $\ct$
shows that this constant point is the image of $p_0$. So $p$ and the constant $R$-point
of $p_0$ agree after the injection $R\hookrightarrow\crA$, hence agree
(\cref{odg:cr:lem:separated}); faithful flatness is not needed. Injectivity of
$\mathcal X(\mathfrak o)\to\mathcal X(R)$ follows by applying $\ct$.
Explicitly, the equalizer of the two $R$-points is closed because
$\mathcal X$ is separated over $\mathfrak o$. Its defining ideal maps
to zero in $\crA$, hence is zero by injectivity. This explains why
neither flatness of $R\hookrightarrow\crA$ nor smoothness of the
arithmetic model is required.
\end{proof}

With $(\mathfrak o,\Bbbk)=(\Z,\C)$, the injection
$\Oz_\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal R_\Z(\C,\Gamma)$ and \cref{odg:cr:lem:separated} give the
conclusion of \cref{odg:cr:thm:descent}; with $(\mathfrak o,\Bbbk)=(\Z[i],\C)$ the ring
$R$ is the workspace form of $\Og$. For affine curves the theorem contains the rigid case
\cref{odg:cr:thm:arith}(i). No smoothness of $\mathcal X$ over $\mathfrak o$ is used: bad
reduction at primes of $\mathfrak o$ is allowed, since only the fiber over $\Bbbk$ is
assumed rigid \src{C15}.

\begin{corollary}[Omnific points on logarithmic complements \src{C15}]\label{odg:log:cor:omnific}
Let $(\mathfrak o,\Bbbk)=(\Z,\R)$ or $(\Z[i],\C)$, and let $\mathcal X$ be a separated
$\mathfrak o$-scheme of finite type whose fiber over $\Bbbk$ is
$X=\overline X\setminus E$, with $\overline X$ smooth and proper over $\Bbbk$, $E$ a
reduced divisor with simple normal crossings, and global logarithmic symmetric
differentials separating tangent directions on $X$. Then $\mathcal X(\Oz)=\mathcal X(\Z)$
in the real case and $\mathcal X(\Og)=\mathcal X(\Z[i])$ in the complex case, in the class
convention of \cref{odg:cr:sub:conventions}.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
In the class convention, one point is represented by finite presentation
data in a set-sized ring. Include its affine-chart data, the finitely many
denominators and unit-ideal witnesses defining their cover, and the
identities gluing them. The union of the supports of these finitely many
surreal numbers generates a set-sized ordered subgroup $\Gamma$.
All the data therefore lie in one workspace
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ with
$\Gamma\subseteq\No$ set-sized (\cref{odg:lem:workspace}). There
$X(\crA)=X(\Bbbk)$ by \cref{odg:log:cor:criterion}, and \cref{odg:log:thm:descent} makes
the point ordinary. The ordinary point is its constant term, so it does not depend on the
workspace.
\end{proof}

Source C15 states this corollary with two further cases, printed above: smooth
geometrically connected curves whose complexification is neither $\Aff^1$ nor
$\mathbb P^1$ (\cref{odg:cr:cor:omnific}, and \cref{odg:cr:thm:descent} with
\cref{odg:cr:thm:positivegenus} for proper curves), and schemes with a quasi-finite map to
a semiabelian variety (\cref{odg:cr:thm:descent,odg:cr:thm:semiabelian,odg:cr:thm:quasifinite}).
When $X$ is affine, \cref{odg:log:cor:omnific} adds a class of rigid affine varieties in
every dimension to the first part of \cref{odg:q:affine}. It is an exact equality of point
sets; it gives no enumeration, finiteness statement or height bound for the ordinary
points \src{C15}.

\begin{proposition}[Finite products \src{C15}]\label{odg:log:prop:products}
For fixed $\Bbbk$ and $\Gamma$, the class of separated $\Bbbk$-schemes $X$ of finite type
with $X(\crA)=X(\Bbbk)$ is closed under finite products. By
\cref{odg:cr:prop:inherit,odg:cr:thm:quasifinite} it is also closed under locally closed
subschemes and under quasi-finite morphisms into its members.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $\Bbbk$-schemes $X,Y$ and every $\Bbbk$-algebra $S$,
$(X\times_\Bbbk Y)(S)=X(S)\times Y(S)$ naturally in $S$, so the inclusion of constant
points of the product is the product of two bijections; the empty product $\Spec\Bbbk$ is
rigid trivially. Repeating the binary argument proves the assertion for
every finite number of factors. Finite products of separated finite-type
schemes remain separated and of finite type, so the construction stays
within the stated class. A locally closed immersion is quasi-finite;
the other closure assertions then follow from
\cref{odg:cr:thm:quasifinite} with their stated finiteness hypotheses.
\end{proof}

So products of curves of positive genus, locally closed subschemes of products of abelian
varieties and tori, and their finite covers are rigid; the defining systems may be
arbitrary finite systems, not only separated equations, and a finite cover may add
algebraic dependencies \src{C15}. Quasi-finiteness cannot be dropped: the projection
$\Aff^1\times G\to G$, with $G$ a semiabelian variety, has a rigid target.
For $\gamma>0$ the point $(\omega^\gamma,e)$ of its source is nonconstant
and maps to the identity $e$ of $G$ \src{C12, C15}.
Tangent separation is sufficient, not claimed necessary, and no
conclusion is drawn from general type: positivity of the canonical bundle alone need not
supply separating symmetric differentials \src{C15}. The criterion answers the first
question of \cref{odg:cr:q:higher} in sufficient form; what remains open is recorded in
\cref{odg:cr:sub:settled}.

\section{Equations with omnific coefficients}\label{odg:sec:coefficients}

For $P\in\Oz[X_1,\ldots,X_n]$ let $\overline P\in\Z[X_1,\ldots,X_n]$ apply
$\ct$ to every coefficient. Then \src{02, 05}
\[
 \ct(P(\mathbf x))=\overline P(\ct x_1,\ldots,\ct x_n),\qquad
 P(\mathbf x)=0\ \Longrightarrow\ \overline P(\ct\mathbf x)=0 .
\]
The reverse implication need not hold, because the coefficients are no longer
fixed by $\ct$.

\subsection{Quadratic equations}

\begin{theorem}[Square-discriminant criterion \src{02}]\label{odg:thm:quadraticformula}
For $b,c\in\Oz$, the equation $x^2+bx+c=0$ has a root in $\Oz$ if and only
if $\Delta=b^2-4c$ is a square in $\Oz$. If $y^2=\Delta$ with $y\in\Oz$, its
roots are $(-b\pm y)/2$, and both are omnific.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A root gives $(2x+b)^2=\Delta$. Conversely, suppose $y^2=\Delta$.
Specializing and reducing modulo $4$ gives $\ct(y)^2\equiv\ct(b)^2\pmod4$.
Integer squares are $0$ or $1$ modulo $4$, so $\ct(y)$ and $\ct(b)$ have the
same parity, and \cref{odg:thm:finitequotients} shows that $-b\pm y$ are
divisible by two in $\Oz$. The usual identity verifies the roots.
Conversely every root determines one of these two square roots by $y=2x+b$,
so the list is exhaustive. When $\Delta=0$ the two expressions coincide;
otherwise they are distinct.
\end{proof}

Monicity is essential to the criterion as stated. For a general quadratic
$ax^2+bx+c$ with $a\ne0$ in $\Oz$, a root is equivalent to an element
$y\in\Oz$ satisfying both $y^2=b^2-4ac$ and $y-b\in2a\Oz$.
Indeed a root gives $y=2ax+b$; conversely write $y-b=2ax$ and use
$y^2-(b^2-4ac)=4a(ax^2+bx+c)$, cancelling $4a\ne0$.
A square discriminant alone does not ensure this divisibility: the
polynomial $6x^2+x-1=(3x-1)(2x+1)$ has discriminant $25$, but its two field
roots $1/3$ and $-1/2$ are not omnific. For a monic quadratic, the parity
argument above supplies exactly the missing divisibility by two.

Testing whether a nonnegative surreal has an omnific square root is not the
same as testing whether it has a surreal square root: real closedness gives
the latter, while nonnegative support and an integral constant must still be
checked for the former.

\begin{example}[Residual and leading tests that do not lift \src{01, 02, 05}]\label{odg:ex:nonsquare}
The equation $x^2=\omega^2+1$ has no solution in $\Oz$
(\cref{odg:prop:notnormal}). Nevertheless its residual equation
$\ct(x)^2=1$ is solvable, and the leading terms allow degree $1$ and leading
coefficient $\pm1$. The positive root in $\No$ has the formal binomial
expansion
\[
 \sqrt{\omega^2+1}
 =\omega+\tfrac12\omega^{-1}-\tfrac18\omega^{-3}
   +\tfrac1{16}\omega^{-5}-\cdots,
\]
whose negative exponents exclude it from $\Oz$. This is a support-controlled
Hahn expansion, not a claim of convergence in the surreal order topology. In
contrast, $\omega^2+2\omega+1=(\omega+1)^2$ is an omnific square.
\end{example}

The sibling report \fn{docs/surreal/set-sized-quotients-of-omnific-integers}
prints a later manuscript's image-gap theorem at a fresh scale (its files
carry the prefix \fn{16-fresh-scale-}): if $f$ has degree at least two and
coefficients in the Hahn field $K_G$ with exponents in a set-sized group $G$,
and $t=\omega^b$ with $b>G$, then $f(\BR)\cap(f(t)+K_G)=\{f(t)\}$, and
likewise over $\BC$; for ordinary $f$ one may take $G=\{0\}$ and any $b>0$.
The unsolvability of $x^2=\omega^2+1$ in \cref{odg:ex:nonsquare} is the case
$f=X^2$, $t=\omega$, and the two-term equations of \cref{odg:prop:binomial}
are the cases $f=aY^n$, $t=\omega^{\gamma/n}$, after the substitution
$X=a^{1/n}Y$ in $\BR$. The short proofs here are kept; the general statement
is printed there.

\subsection{Initial forms}

\begin{proposition}[Initial forms of an omnific solution \src{01, 02}]\label{odg:prop:initial}
Let $P=\sum_\alpha a_\alpha\mathbf X^\alpha\in\Oz[X_1,\ldots,X_n]$ be nonzero,
let $\mathbf x\in\Oz^n$ have all coordinates nonzero, and write
$\gamma_i=\ddeg(x_i)$, $r_i=\lc(x_i)$. Discarding zero coefficients, put
\[
 W=\max_\alpha\Bigl(\ddeg(a_\alpha)+\sum_i\alpha_i\gamma_i\Bigr).
\]
If $P(\mathbf x)=0$, then
