\begin{equation}\label{odg:eq:initial}
 \sum_{\ddeg(a_\alpha)+\sum_i\alpha_i\gamma_i=W}
       \lc(a_\alpha)\prod_i r_i^{\alpha_i}=0,
\end{equation}
and in particular the maximum is attained by at least two monomials.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{odg:lem:degree}, each evaluated monomial has degree
$\ddeg(a_\alpha)+\sum_i\alpha_i\gamma_i$ and leading coefficient
$\lc(a_\alpha)\prod_ir_i^{\alpha_i}$. The coefficient of $P(\mathbf x)$ at
$\omega^W$ is exactly the sum in \eqref{odg:eq:initial}, and it must vanish; a
single maximizer would contribute a nonzero product.
\end{proof}

The maximum is taken over the finite nonempty polynomial support, so it
exists even when the surreal weights have arbitrary rank. The exponent
$W$ is a candidate degree before cancellation, not an assertion that
$\ddeg(P(\mathbf x))=W$. Several maximal terms can cancel; their leading
coefficients, including their signs, must actually sum to zero. Merely
having two terms of maximal weight is insufficient: for $P(X)=X^2+\omega^2$
and a candidate with $\ddeg(x)=1$, the initial equation is $r^2+1=0$,
which has no real solution.

Variables assigned zero can first be substituted out. If this makes the
polynomial identically zero, it imposes no initial-form condition; otherwise
apply the proposition to the remaining nonzero coordinates. For ordinary
coefficients this is a weighted initial polynomial over $\R$ with surreal
weights. The constant-coefficient equation and the initial equation constrain
opposite ends of a normal form; they are complementary necessary tests, not a
lifting criterion, as \cref{odg:ex:nonsquare} shows: the missing information
lies in the full tail of a potential root.

\subsection{Two-term roots and failed lifting}

\begin{proposition}[A two-term root obstruction \src{05}]\label{odg:prop:binomial}
Let $a\in\R_{>0}$, $\gamma>0$, $b\in\Z$, and let $n\ge2$ be ordinary. The
equation $X^n=a\omega^\gamma+b$ has a solution in $\Oz$ if and only if $b=0$.
When $b=0$ its positive solution is $a^{1/n}\omega^{\gamma/n}$, together with
its negative when $n$ is even.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
When $b=0$, put $u=a^{1/n}\omega^{\gamma/n}\in\Pi$. Then
$u^n=a\omega^\gamma$, and strict monotonicity of positive integer powers
on the positive cone gives the stated list of roots.

For $b\ne0$, the right side is still positive and infinite. Suppose an
omnific root exists, replacing it by its absolute value if $n$ is even;
the resulting root $x$ is positive. With the same positive $u$, factor
\[
 b=x^n-u^n=(x-u)S,\qquad
 S=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}x^{n-1-j}u^j.
\]
Both factors belong to $\BR$, so \cref{odg:lem:constantproduct} would make
$S$ a nonzero real constant. But every summand is positive and
$S\ge u^{n-1}$ is infinite, a contradiction. This finite-algebra proof
uses only the previously established unit computation.
\end{proof}

Source 05 instead proves the obstruction by displaying the positive field
root. Its formal binomial expansion is
\begin{equation}\label{odg:eq:binomial}
 a^{1/n}\omega^{\gamma/n}
 \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{1/n}{k}
 \Bigl(\frac ba\Bigr)^k\omega^{-k\gamma}.
\end{equation}
For every positive surreal $\gamma$, the exponents
$\gamma/n-k\gamma$ form a reverse-well-ordered set: the largest exponent
of any nonempty subset corresponds to its least natural index. Each
coefficient in an $n$-fold product involves finitely many index tuples
with a fixed sum. Hence the formal power-series binomial identity evaluates
coefficientwise to the $n$th-root identity in a set-sized Hahn workspace
containing $\gamma/n$. The leading coefficient is positive, so this is the
positive field root; the only other possible root is its negative for even
$n$.

If $b\ne0$, the $k=1$ term has nonzero coefficient
$(b/n)a^{1/n-1}$ at exponent $\gamma/n-\gamma<0$, which no other term has.
Thus the expansion identifies the forbidden term for both possible signs.
No convergence of partial sums in the full surreal order topology is used,
and no assumption that the multiples of $\gamma$ are cofinal among positive
surreals is needed.

\begin{example}[A simple residue root need not lift \src{05}]\label{odg:ex:hensel}
Let $f(T)=T^2-T-\omega$. Modulo $\Pi$ it becomes $T(T-1)$, and the residue
roots $0$ and $1$ are simple, with derivatives $-1$ and $1$, respectively,
both units in $\Z$. Nevertheless $f$ has no root in $\Oz$: its discriminant
$1+4\omega$ is not an omnific square by \cref{odg:prop:binomial}, so
\cref{odg:thm:quadraticformula} applies. Its roots in $\No$ are
\[
 \tfrac12\pm\bigl(\omega^{1/2}+\tfrac18\omega^{-1/2}
 -\tfrac1{128}\omega^{-3/2}+\cdots\bigr),
\]
with constant coefficient $\tfrac12$ and negative exponents. Hensel-style
intuition fails for $\Oz\to\Z$: the kernel $\Pi$ is not contained in the
Jacobson radical, since $-\omega\in\Pi$ but $1+\omega$ is not a unit, and
$\Pi^2=\Pi$, so there is no decreasing nilpotent-error filtration. This is a
failure of the simple-root lifting property itself.
\end{example}

This example concerns the constant-term map on the omnific ring. The
standard-part map on the valuation ring of finite surreals has a different
domain and an infinitesimal kernel. In particular $\omega$ is not finite,
so the displayed polynomial does not even have all its coefficients in
that valuation ring. Simple-residue-root lifting there, as used in the
trigonometry report (\replabel{trigonometry:lem:hensel}), makes no lifting
claim for $\Oz\to\Z$. The two uses of residue must be kept separate.

For general coefficients, a viable approach must inspect actual normal-form
supports. Matching constant terms is necessary, and occasionally useful, but
is not a sufficient solvability criterion (\cref{odg:q:roots}).

\subsection{Constant discriminants and \texorpdfstring{\'etale}{etale} units}\label{odg:disc:sub:disc}

This subsection is built from a fifteenth manuscript, \emph{Constant Discriminants and
\'Etale Unit Rigidity over Omnific Hahn Rings: Translation classification, arithmetic
descent, and normal spectral rigidity}, tagged \src{C17} after its file prefix
\fn{17-discriminant-rigidity-} (\cref{odg:sub:provenance}); the letter C is kept as for
sources C10--C16, because the sibling report has a manuscript 17 of its own. Source C17
works with equations whose coefficients are \emph{not} constant: monic polynomials in one
variable over the nonnegative-support ring. Its main theorem says that a nonzero
\emph{constant} discriminant forces such a polynomial to be a purely infinite translate of
its constant-term polynomial (\cref{odg:disc:thm:translation}). So a constant
nondegeneracy invariant recovers a rigidity theorem for coefficients that vary at a
surreal scale, in dimension zero. This is the first clause of
\cref{odg:cr:q:coefficients} for univariate equations, and for monic polynomials over $\Oz$
with discriminant in $\Z\setminus\{0\}$ it answers \cref{odg:q:roots}
(\cref{odg:disc:cor:roots}). The same mechanism shows that every unit of a finite
\'etale algebra over the ring is algebraic over the coefficient field
(\cref{odg:disc:thm:units}), which contains \cref{odg:dec:thm:etale} in characteristic
zero (\cref{odg:disc:rem:etalenorm}). Labels of this subsection carry the sub-prefix
\fn{odg:disc:}. It was appended at the end of \cref{odg:sec:coefficients}, so no existing
section, statement or equation number changed; its ten questions follow the last question
of \cref{odg:sec:questions}. The elementary lemmas, root-velocity certificate and
translation classification, its factor/Galois/resultant consequences and the finite
\'etale unit theorem through \cref{odg:disc:rem:etalenorm} have been reviewed;
monogenic descent, the later applications and the second proof remain outside that review, as recorded in
\fn{RECONCILIATION.md}.

\paragraph{Conventions and renamed symbols.} As in the curve part
(\cref{odg:cr:sub:conventions}), $\Bbbk$ is a field of characteristic zero, $\bar\Bbbk$
an algebraic closure, $\Gamma$ a set-sized ordered abelian group, not assumed divisible,
and $\Gamma_\Q$ its divisible hull. We use the $\omega$-convention: $\crA$ is the ring of
series with support in $\Gamma^{\ge0}$, $\crP$ its ideal of series with support in
$\Gamma^{>0}$, $\crO$ the valuation ring of series with support in $\Gamma^{\le0}$, and
$v=-\ddeg$. Source C17 uses the $t$-convention $t^\gamma=\omega^{-\gamma}$: its
$\mathcal A=k((t^{\Gamma_{\le0}}))$, $\mathcal I$, $\mathcal O_K$ and $K=k((t^\Gamma))$ are
$\crA$, $\crP$, $\crO$ and $\crH$ with exponents negated, and its algebraically closed
workspace $\widehat K=\bar k((t^{\Gamma_\Q}))$ with valuation ring $\widehat{\mathcal O}$
is here the Hahn field $\Hbar$ with coefficients in $\bar\Bbbk$ and exponents in
$\Gamma_\Q$, with valuation ring $\Obar$. Its coefficient subring $D\subseteq k$ is $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$ (here $D$ is the
Pell parameter), and its pullback $\mathcal A_D=D\oplus\mathcal I$ is
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}=\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)=\mathfrak o+\crP$. For
$P\in\crA[X]$, $\overline P=\ct P$ applies $\ct$ to every coefficient, as at the start
of \cref{odg:sec:coefficients} (the source writes $P_0$); the bar over a polynomial or a
matrix is this constant-term map, while $\bar\Bbbk$ is an algebraic closure. Its Euler
derivations $D_\chi$, $t^\gamma\mapsto\chi(\gamma)t^\gamma$ for
$\chi\in\Hom_\Q(\Gamma_\Q,\Q)$, are the $\partial_{-\chi}$ of \eqref{odg:cr:eq:euler}, the
same family; we write $\partial_\lambda$ with $\lambda\in\Hom_\Q(\Gamma_\Q,\Q)$, acting
coefficientwise on $\Hbar$ and on the coefficients of polynomials. They are not $d/dX$.
The source's finite \'etale algebra $B$, its constant subalgebra $C_B$ and its splitting
algebra $S$ are $\mathsf B$, $\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}$ and $\mathsf B_P$ (here $B$ is a
bilinear form, $\BR,\BC$ are rings and $S$ a target ring); the rank $N$ of $\mathsf B$ is
$r$ (here $N$ is a norm form, and $r$ is the dimension of the \'etale algebra $E$ of
\cref{odg:dec:thm:etale}, which is the rank in the constant case). The velocity
polynomial $Q$ is $\dot P$, the divided difference $S(X,Y)$ is $\mathsf W(X,Y)$, the root
velocities $u_i$ are $\dot r_i$, the certificate polynomials $F_{n,s}$ are
$\mathcal F_{n,s}$, the characteristic polynomial $H_y$ is $\operatorname{ch}_y$, the
spectral idempotents $E_j$ are $\mathsf P_j$ (here $E$ is an \'etale algebra), the target
translation $c$ of the critical-point theorem is $\beta$ (the letter of the sibling report,
\cref{odg:disc:rem:osq}), the number $r$ of polynomials in the resultant theorem is
$\ell$ and its graph $G$ is $\mathcal G$ ($G$ is the Gram matrix), the source's real
and complex workspaces $H$, $H[i]$ are $\mathbb H_\R(\Gamma_\Q)$ and
$\mathbb H_\C(\Gamma_\Q)$, and its $\mathcal A_{\C}$, $\mathcal I_{\C}$ are
$\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma)$, $\Pi_\C(\Gamma)$. The letters $h$ (the translation), $\Delta$
(a discriminant), $m=\binom n2$, $w_{ij}$, $e_s$, $F=\Frac\crA$ and $L_0$ are the
source's. \Cref{odg:tab:renames} lists these renamings and a few local ones (for
example the quadratic velocity coefficients $\alpha,\beta$ become $b_1,b_2$, and the
infinitesimal root $r$ of \cref{odg:disc:ex:twosided} becomes $\rho$).

Four readings must be kept apart. A \emph{constant} discriminant is one in $\Bbbk$, that
is, supported at the exponent $0$; it is not ``independent of $X$'', which every
discriminant is. The \emph{constant coefficient in $X$} of $P$ is $a_n$, whereas the
constant term of a series is its coefficient at $\omega^0$. A \emph{nonzero} constant
discriminant in $\mathfrak o$ need not be a \emph{unit} of $\mathfrak o$: the translation
theorem needs only the former, \'etaleness the latter. And $\crA$ is not the set of
series of nonpositive valuation (\cref{odg:sub:notation}): the theorems below are false
when the support condition is weakened to a condition on the leading exponent
(\cref{odg:disc:ex:twosided}).

Here a polynomial over $\mathfrak o$ is called \emph{squarefree} when it has no repeated
root over $\bar\Bbbk$, equivalently when it is squarefree over the field $\Bbbk$.
This is a field-theoretic condition, not a statement about square divisors in the possibly
nonnormal ring $\mathfrak o[X]$. The translation classification and its coefficient test
concern positive degree: for $P=1$ every translation gives the same polynomial, so there
is no unique $h$ when $\crP\ne0$.

\subsubsection{The splitting field and the Euler family}\label{odg:disc:sub:rings}

Source C17 reproves the ring facts it uses: $\crA=\Bbbk\oplus\crP$ with the retraction
$\ct$, $\crA\cap\crO=\Bbbk$, $\crP\cap\crO=0$, $\crA^\times=\Bbbk^\times$, and for the
pullback $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}\cap\Bbbk=\mathfrak o$ and
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}^\times=\mathfrak o^\times$ (its Lemma 2.1, here
\cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}); the Euler derivations with $\partial(\crA)\subseteq\crP$,
$\partial(\crP)\subseteq\crP$, $v(\partial x)\ge v(x)$, $\partial x/x\in\crO$ and joint
constants $\Bbbk$ (its Lemma 3.1, here \cref{odg:cr:lem:euler,odg:cr:lem:separation});
and that an element of $\crH$ algebraic over $\Bbbk$ lies in $\Bbbk$, so that every nonzero
element of $\crP$ is transcendental over $\Bbbk$ (its Lemma 6.3, here
\cref{odg:cr:cor:algclosed} and \cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}(d); over $\Oz$ this is
\cref{odg:prop:univariate}). These are printed once, with C17 named in their credits. Its
example $\sum_{m\ge1}\omega^{1/m}\in\crP$, whose support has no least element, is the one
after \cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}; no proof below assumes that $\crP$ is generated by
monomials. Both proofs of the source use these facts in the larger workspace $\Hbar$, and
the next lemma records what is used there.

\begin{lemma}[The Hahn splitting field \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:lem:hahn}
The field $\Hbar$ is algebraically closed, and it contains $\crH$ and $F=\Frac\crA$.
The coefficientwise Euler derivations $\partial_\lambda$,
$\lambda\in\Hom_\Q(\Gamma_\Q,\Q)$, are $\bar\Bbbk$-linear derivations of $\Hbar$ extending
those of $\crH$, with
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:hahn}
 \crA\cap\Obar=\Bbbk,\qquad \crP\cap\Obar=0,\qquad
 \frac{\partial_\lambda x}{x}\in\Obar\ (x\ne0),\qquad
 \bigcap_\lambda\ker\partial_\lambda=\bar\Bbbk .
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Algebraic closedness of a Hahn field with algebraically closed coefficient field of
characteristic zero and divisible exponent group is the standard criterion
\cite[Fact 2.1.1]{LM}. Extending coefficients along $\Bbbk\subseteq\bar\Bbbk$ and exponents
along $\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma_\Q$ preserves supports and coefficient convolutions, so
$\crH\subseteq\Hbar$, hence $F\subseteq\Hbar$; the field $F$ need not be $\crH$ (for
$\Gamma=\Z$, $\crA=\Bbbk[\omega]$ and $F=\Bbbk(\omega)$). The intersections compare supports,
as in \cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}(b). The Leibniz rule follows from additivity of $\lambda$
on each finite coefficient convolution, as in \cref{odg:cr:lem:euler}; linearity over
$\bar\Bbbk$ follows directly from the coefficient formula.
Coefficientwise differentiation can only remove support
terms, so $v(\partial_\lambda x)\ge v(x)$ and
$v(\partial_\lambda x/x)\ge0$ when the derivative is nonzero; the zero derivative also
lies in $\Obar$. Finally, for any nonzero $\gamma\in\Gamma_\Q$, extend $\gamma$ to a
$\Q$-basis and choose a rational linear functional with $\lambda(\gamma)=1$.
It detects any nonzero coefficient at $\gamma$. Thus a series killed by every
$\partial_\lambda$ is supported only at $0$, giving the joint kernel in
\eqref{odg:disc:eq:hahn}, with no restriction on the size of its support.
\end{proof}

These intersections do the work that a convergence or compactness argument might
otherwise be asked to do \src{C17}.

\begin{lemma}[Compatibility with algebraic embeddings \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:lem:extension}
Let $L/F$ be a finite separable extension and $\sigma:L\hookrightarrow\Hbar$ an
$F$-embedding. Each $\partial_\lambda$ on $F$ has a unique extension to a derivation of $L$,
and $\sigma$ intertwines it with the coefficientwise $\partial_\lambda$ on $\Hbar$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The derivation $\partial=\partial_\lambda$ preserves $F$, since it preserves $\crA$ and
obeys the quotient rule. For $z\in L$ let $R\in F[X]$ be its minimal polynomial.
Differentiating $\sigma(R)(\sigma z)=0$ in $\Hbar$ gives
\[
 \partial(\sigma z)
 =-\frac{\sigma(R^\partial(z))}{\sigma(R'(z))}
 =\sigma\!\left(-\frac{R^\partial(z)}{R'(z)}\right),
\]
where $R^\partial$ applies $\partial$ to the coefficients and $R'(z)\ne0$ by
separability. Hence the ambient derivation preserves $\sigma(L)$; pulling its restriction
back through $\sigma$ gives a derivation on $L$ extending the one on $F$ and proves the
required compatibility. Any extension must satisfy the same formula for every $z$, so
it is unique.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Constant coefficients remain independent \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:lem:independence}
Let $L/\Bbbk$ be a field extension. If $c_1,\ldots,c_k\in L$ are $\Bbbk$-linearly
independent, they are linearly independent over $\crH$, hence over every subfield of $\crH$,
in the Hahn field $\mathbb H_L(\Gamma_\Q)$ (for $L\subseteq\bar\Bbbk$, in $\Hbar$). The same holds
for vectors with entries in $L$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Expand a relation $\sum_jf_jc_j=0$ with $f_j\in\crH$ coefficientwise. At each exponent
$\gamma$ it gives the $\Bbbk$-linear relation $\sum_j(f_j)_\gamma c_j=0$, so every
coefficient of every $f_j$ vanishes. A finite union of supports is reverse well ordered,
so the comparison is legitimate. The vector case is the same.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{The finite certificate for root velocities}\label{odg:disc:sub:certificate}

If $r_1,\ldots,r_n$ are distinct roots of a monic polynomial and $\partial$ is a
derivation, put $w_{ij}=(\partial r_i-\partial r_j)/(r_i-r_j)$ for $i<j$. Differentiating
the discriminant gives only
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:sumvelocity}
 \frac{\partial\Disc(P)}{\Disc(P)}=2\sum_{i<j}w_{ij},
\end{equation}
and a vanishing sum does not force the individual $w_{ij}$ to vanish. The missing
information is supplied by \emph{every} elementary symmetric function of the $w_{ij}$
\src{C17}. We use $\Disc(P)=\prod_{i<j}(r_i-r_j)^2=(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}\Res(P,P')$ for monic
$P$, and $\Disc(P)=1$ when $n=1$; the sign disappears under logarithmic differentiation,
so \eqref{odg:disc:eq:sumvelocity} has no extra sign.

\begin{lemma}[Universal denominator certificate \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:lem:certificate}
Let $P=X^n+a_1X^{n-1}+\cdots+a_n$ be monic and separable over a field, with roots
$r_1,\ldots,r_n$, let $\dot P=b_1X^{n-1}+\cdots+b_n$, and put
\[
 \dot r_i=-\dot P(r_i)/P'(r_i),\qquad w_{ij}=(\dot r_i-\dot r_j)/(r_i-r_j),\qquad
 m=\tbinom n2,\qquad \Delta=\Disc(P).
\]
For $1\le s\le m$ there is a universal polynomial
$\mathcal F_{n,s}\in\Z[a_1,\ldots,a_n,b_1,\ldots,b_n]$, homogeneous of degree $s$ in
$b_1,\ldots,b_n$, with
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:certificate}
 \Delta^{2s}\,e_s\bigl((w_{ij})_{i<j}\bigr)=\mathcal F_{n,s}(a_1,\ldots,a_n,b_1,\ldots,b_n),
\end{equation}
where $e_s$ is the $s$th elementary symmetric polynomial in the $m$ edge variables. No
optimality of the exponent $2s$ is asserted.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The numerator of
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:W}
 \mathsf W(X,Y)=\frac{\dot P(Y)P'(X)-\dot P(X)P'(Y)}{X-Y}
\end{equation}
vanishes at $X=Y$, so $\mathsf W$ is a polynomial over $\Z[a,b]$, symmetric in $X,Y$ and
linear in $b$. A direct calculation gives
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:wformula}
 w_{ij}=\frac{\mathsf W(r_i,r_j)}{P'(r_i)P'(r_j)}.
\end{equation}
By the root product identity $\prod_{i=1}^nP'(r_i)=(-1)^m\Delta$ we have
$\Delta/P'(r_i)=(-1)^m\prod_{u\ne i}P'(r_u)$. In particular the two signs cancel in
\[
 \Delta^2w_{ij}=\mathsf W(r_i,r_j)
       \prod_{u\ne i}P'(r_u)\prod_{v\ne j}P'(r_v).
\]
This is a polynomial in the roots and $b$, linear in $b$. Its $s$th elementary symmetric
function is $\Delta^{2s}e_s(w)$, a polynomial homogeneous of degree $s$ in $b$.
Permuting the roots permutes the unordered edges,
and both $w_{ij}$ and the polynomial just built respect this action, so the latter is
symmetric in the roots. Treat the roots as indeterminates, with $a_j=(-1)^je_j(r)$ and $b$
independent. The fundamental theorem on symmetric polynomials over $\Z[b]$ expresses the
polynomial in the $e_j(r)$; substituting $e_j(r)=(-1)^ja_j$ gives $\mathcal F_{n,s}$ over
$\Z$. The elimination can be done in each $b$-homogeneous component separately, so the
homogeneity is kept.
\end{proof}

The lemma is an identity in universal polynomial rings; it does not assert that integral
elements of $\Frac\crA$ lie in $\crA$, and no integral closedness of $\crA$ is used
\src{C17}; $\crA$ is not integrally closed in general (\cref{odg:cr:sub:singular}).

\begin{remark}[The quadratic certificate \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:rem:quadratic}
For $P=X^2+aX+b$ and $\dot P=b_1X+b_2$ there is one relative velocity, and
\eqref{odg:disc:eq:sumvelocity} gives $w=(ab_1-2b_2)/\Delta$ with $\Delta=a^2-4b$.
So the general certificate is $\mathcal F_{2,1}=\Delta(ab_1-2b_2)$, although the
exponent $1$ already suffices. In the application $b_1,b_2\in\crP$ and
$\Delta\in\Bbbk^\times$, so $w\in\crP$, while $w\in\Obar$; hence $w=0$. This is completing
the square, and the lemma explains why the same method works in every degree.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[All relative Euler velocities vanish \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:prop:velocities}
Let $P\in\crA[X]$ be monic with $\Delta=\Disc(P)\in\Bbbk^\times$. For every Euler
derivation $\partial=\partial_\lambda$ of $\Hbar$ and any two roots $r_i,r_j\in\Hbar$ of
$P$, $\partial(r_i-r_j)=0$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $n=1$ there is nothing to prove. Otherwise differentiating $P(r_i)=0$ gives
$\partial r_i=-\dot P(r_i)/P'(r_i)$, where $\dot P=P^{\partial}$ has the coefficients
$b_j=\partial a_j\in\crP$ (\cref{odg:cr:lem:euler}). By \eqref{odg:disc:eq:hahn} each
$w_{ij}=\partial(r_i-r_j)/(r_i-r_j)$ lies in $\Obar$, so every $e_s(w)$ lies in $\Obar$.
On the other hand $\mathcal F_{n,s}$ has positive degree in $b$ and coefficients in
$\Z[a]\subseteq\crA$, so $\mathcal F_{n,s}(a,b)\in\crP$, and with $\Delta\in\Bbbk^\times$,
\eqref{odg:disc:eq:certificate} gives $e_s(w)\in\crP$. By \eqref{odg:disc:eq:hahn},
$e_s(w)=0$ for $1\le s\le m$. Thus $\prod_{i<j}(T-w_{ij})=T^m$; evaluating at an edge
velocity gives $w_{ij}^m=0$. Since $m\ge1$ and $\Hbar$ is a field, every $w_{ij}$ is zero.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Translation classification}\label{odg:disc:sub:translation}

\begin{theorem}[Translation classification \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:thm:translation}
Let $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$ be a unital subring and
$P=X^n+a_1X^{n-1}+\cdots+a_n\in\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}[X]$, $n\ge1$. The following are
equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=*]
\item $\Disc(P)$ is a nonzero constant, that is, $\Disc(P)\in\mathfrak o\setminus\{0\}$;
\item $\overline P$ is squarefree and there is $h\in\crP$ with
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:translation}
 P(X)=\overline P(X-h).
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
The translation is unique and equals
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:h}
 h=-\frac{a_1-\ct a_1}{n}.
\end{equation}
The discriminant need not be a unit of $\mathfrak o$, and $1/n$ need not lie in
$\mathfrak o$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
First let $\mathfrak o=\Bbbk$. A nonzero discriminant gives distinct roots
$r_1,\ldots,r_n$ in $\Hbar$. \Cref{odg:disc:prop:velocities} for every $\lambda$ and
\eqref{odg:disc:eq:hahn} give $r_i-r_j\in\bar\Bbbk$ for all $i,j$. Let $\mu=-a_1/n$ be the
mean of the roots. Every $r_i-\mu=n^{-1}\sum_j(r_i-r_j)$ lies in $\bar\Bbbk$, so
$R(X)=P(X+\mu)$ has its coefficients in $\bar\Bbbk$. They also lie in $\crA$, because
$\mu\in\crA$, hence in $\crA\cap\bar\Bbbk=\Bbbk$. Write $\mu=\mu_0+h$ with $\mu_0=\ct\mu$
and $h\in\crP$. Then $P(X)=R(X-\mu_0-h)$, and applying the ring homomorphism $\ct$ to the
coefficients gives $\overline P(X)=R(X-\mu_0)$. So $P(X)=\overline P(X-h)$ and
$h=-(a_1-\ct a_1)/n$. Translation preserves root differences, so
$\Disc(\overline P)=\Disc(P)\ne0$; hence $\overline P$ is squarefree over $\Bbbk$.

For general $\mathfrak o$, regard $P$ as a polynomial over $\crA$. A nonzero constant
discriminant lies in $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}\cap\Bbbk=\mathfrak o$ and is a unit of
$\Bbbk$, so the first case applies. Since $a_1-\ct a_1\in\crP$ and $\crP$ is a
$\Bbbk$-vector space, $h\in\crP\subseteq\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}$ even when
$1/n\notin\mathfrak o$, and $\overline P\in\mathfrak o[X]$. Conversely a translation does
not change root differences, so $\overline P(X-h)$ has the discriminant of the squarefree
$\overline P$. Uniqueness follows from the coefficient of $X^{n-1}$; for $n=1$ the formula
is immediate.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Complete parameterization \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:cor:param}
For $n\ge1$ the map $(\overline P,h)\mapsto\overline P(X-h)$ is a bijection from pairs of
a squarefree monic $\overline P\in\mathfrak o[X]$ of degree $n$ and $h\in\crP$ onto the
monic polynomials of degree $n$ in $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}[X]$ with nonzero constant
discriminant. Every fiber of $P\mapsto\overline P$ on the latter set is a free transitive
orbit of the additive group $\crP$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
\Cref{odg:disc:thm:translation} gives existence and uniqueness. Translation by an element
of $\crP$ preserves $\overline P$, and a nonzero translation changes the coefficient of
$X^{n-1}$ in characteristic zero.
\end{proof}

An equivalent finite coefficient test avoids computing a large discriminant \src{C17}:
given monic $P$ of positive degree, compute $h$ by \eqref{odg:disc:eq:h} and expand
$P(X+h)$; then $P$ has a
nonzero constant discriminant if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:test}
 P(X+h)=\overline P(X)\qquad\text{and}\qquad \Disc(\overline P)\ne0 .
\end{equation}
This is an algebraic criterion, not an algorithm for arbitrary presentations of infinite
Hahn series; it becomes effective only in a representation with exact arithmetic,
constant extraction and equality \src{C17}.

\begin{example}[An infinite-support family \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:ex:infinite}
Let $h=\sum_{m\ge1}\omega^{1/m}\in\Pi$, whose exponents decrease to $0$. The cubic
\[
 P(X)=(X-h)^3-2(X-h)+1=X^3-3hX^2+(3h^2-2)X-h^3+2h+1
\]
lies in $\Oz[X]$ and has discriminant $-4(-2)^3-27=5$. Its roots are $h+c_i$, where
$c_1=1$ and $c_{2,3}=(-1\pm\sqrt5)/2$ are the roots of $X^3-2X+1$. The translation has
infinite support accumulating at the exponent $0$; the theorem is not confined to
finitely many monomials.
\end{example}

\begin{corollary}[Omnific and Gaussian omnific form \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:cor:omnific}
Let $P\in\Oz[X]$ be monic of positive degree. Then $\Disc(P)\in\Z\setminus\{0\}$ if and only if
$P=\overline P(X-h)$ with $\overline P\in\Z[X]$ squarefree and $h\in\Pi$, and $h$ is
unique. Its roots in $\No[i]$ are $h+c_i$ with $c_i$ the complex roots of $\overline P$.
The same holds over $\Og$, with $\overline P\in\Z[i][X]$ and $h\in\Pi_\C$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The finitely many coefficients lie in a workspace $\mathcal R_\Z(\R,\Gamma)$ or
$\mathcal R_{\Z[i]}(\C,\Gamma)$ over a set-sized divisible group $\Gamma\subseteq\No$
(\cref{odg:lem:workspace}). Apply \cref{odg:disc:thm:translation} with
$(\mathfrak o,\Bbbk)=(\Z,\R)$ or $(\Z[i],\C)$, and read the identity in the full class.
The roots of $\overline P$ are algebraic over $\Q$ or $\Q(i)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Omnific roots at a constant discriminant \mergetag]\label{odg:disc:cor:roots}
Let $P\in\Oz[X]$ be monic of positive degree with $\Disc(P)\in\Z\setminus\{0\}$, and let $h$ be given by
\eqref{odg:disc:eq:h}. The roots of $P$ in $\Oz$ are exactly the elements $h+c$ with $c$
an integer root of $\overline P$; in particular $P$ has a root in $\Oz$ if and only if
$\overline P$ has one in $\Z$, and then every integer root of $\overline P$ lifts to
exactly one omnific root. The same holds over $\Og$ with $\Z[i]$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{odg:disc:cor:omnific} every root in $\No[i]$ is $h+c$ with $c$ a root of
$\overline P$. If $h+c\in\Oz$, then $c=(h+c)-h\in\Oz\cap\C=\Z$
(\cref{odg:prop:units}). Conversely $h+c\in\Oz$ for $c\in\Z$. The Gaussian case is the
same with $\Og\cap\C=\Z[i]$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Comparison with the quadratic and lifting results \mergetag]\label{odg:disc:rem:comparison}
For a monic quadratic $x^2+bx+c$ over $\Oz$ with $\Delta=b^2-4c\in\Z\setminus\{0\}$,
\cref{odg:thm:quadraticformula} asks whether $\Delta$ is a square in $\Oz$, and by
\cref{odg:prop:univariate} a nonzero integer is an omnific square only if it is an integer
square; the roots $(-b\pm y)/2$ with $y\in\Z$ are then $h+c$ for the integer roots $c$ of
$\overline P$, as \cref{odg:disc:cor:roots} says. For such polynomials the residue roots do
lift: every root of $\overline P$ is simple, and each integer one lifts to exactly one
omnific root. The polynomial $T^2-T-\omega$ of \cref{odg:ex:hensel}, whose simple residue
roots $0$ and $1$ do not lift, has the nonconstant discriminant $1+4\omega$; its
translation $h$ from \eqref{odg:disc:eq:h} is $0$, and $\overline P(X-h)=X^2-X\ne P$. So
the constant discriminant is exactly the hypothesis that separates the two behaviours in
these examples. Monicity cannot simply be dropped: $P(X)=\omega X^2+X$ has discriminant
$1$ and $\overline P=X$, but no translate of $\overline P$ has degree two. In contrast,
the ordinary polynomial $6x^2+x-1$ after \cref{odg:thm:quadraticformula} illustrates a
different obstruction: its discriminant is a square but its roots are not omnific
integers, because the denominator in the nonmonic quadratic formula is not harmless.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{The polynomial-parameter case \src{C17}.} For $\Gamma=\Z$ a reverse-well-ordered
subset of $\Z^{\ge0}$ is finite and $\crA=\Bbbk[\omega]$. Writing $Y=\omega$, the theorem
says that a monic $P(X,Y)\in\Bbbk[Y][X]$ with nonzero constant discriminant satisfies
\[
 P(X,Y)=P\bigl(X-h(Y),0\bigr),\qquad h(Y)=-\frac{a_1(Y)-a_1(0)}n .
\]
This is a uniform answer in all positive degrees and characteristic zero to a question posed on
Mathematics Stack Exchange by the user Oblomov \cite{Oblomov}, and it shows why the
familiar quadratic and cubic computations are not isolated. For several parameters,
embed $\Bbbk[Y_1,\ldots,Y_k]$ in a Hahn ring with $Y_i=\omega^{\varepsilon_i}$ for the
standard basis $\varepsilon_i$ of lexicographically ordered $\Z^k$: every nonconstant
monomial has positive exponent, the embedding is injective, and $\ct$ is evaluation at the
origin, so a monic polynomial over $\Bbbk[Y_1,\ldots,Y_k]$ with nonzero constant
discriminant is a translate of its value at the origin. Source C17 does not present these
finite-support cases as new or as a deep theorem about the affine line; its proposed
contribution is the arbitrary-rank, arbitrary-support theorem and its consequences.

\subsubsection{Factors, Galois groups, marks and resultants}\label{odg:disc:sub:consequences}

\begin{proposition}[Monic factors are translated constant factors \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:prop:factors}
Let $P=\overline P(X-h)$ be as in \cref{odg:disc:thm:translation}. Translation by $h$ is a
bijection between the monic divisors of $\overline P$ in $\mathfrak o[X]$ and the monic
divisors of $P$ in $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}[X]$; in particular their monic factorization
patterns agree.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Substitution $X\mapsto X-h$ carries a monic factorization of $\overline P$ over
$\mathfrak o$ to one of $P$ over $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}$. Conversely write $P=QT$
with $Q$ monic. Since $P$ is monic and the coefficient ring is a domain, $T$ is monic
too. Translating gives $\overline P=Q(X+h)T(X+h)$. In $\Hbar[X]$ the roots of each
factor are among the roots of $\overline P$, hence lie in $\bar\Bbbk$. Monicity then
puts every coefficient of both factors in $\bar\Bbbk$, including when one factor is
$1$. These coefficients also lie in $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}$, so they belong to
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}\cap\bar\Bbbk=\mathfrak o$. Thus the translated divisor and
its quotient both descend. The two substitutions are inverse, giving the bijection.
\end{proof}

The word ``monic'' matters over a ring $\mathfrak o$ that is not a field: an assertion
about arbitrary factorizations must also account for units and leading coefficients
\src{C17}.

\begin{theorem}[Galois groups are unchanged \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:thm:galois}
Let $P\in\crA[X]$ be monic of positive degree with $\Disc(P)\in\Bbbk^\times$, let $F=\Frac\crA$, and let
$L_0\subseteq\bar\Bbbk$ be the splitting field of $\overline P$ over $\Bbbk$. The splitting
field of $P$ over $F$ in $\Hbar$ is the compositum $FL_0$, and restriction is an
isomorphism $\Gal(FL_0/F)\cong\Gal(L_0/\Bbbk)$. An irreducible factor of $\overline P$ over
$\Bbbk$ stays irreducible over $F$, and its translate has the same degree.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{odg:disc:thm:translation} the roots of $P$ are $h+c_i$ with $h\in\crP\subset F$
and $c_i$ the roots of $\overline P$. Thus adjoining them to $F$ adjoins the $c_i$.
By \cref{odg:disc:lem:independence} a $\Bbbk$-basis of $L_0$ remains $F$-linearly
independent. Hence the multiplication map $F\otimes_\Bbbk L_0\to\Hbar$ is injective.
Its image is a finite-dimensional domain over $F$, therefore a field, and is precisely
$FL_0$, of degree $[L_0:\Bbbk]$ over $F$.

Every $\Bbbk$-automorphism of $L_0$ extends by $\mathrm{id}_F\otimes\sigma$ through
this isomorphism. Conversely an $F$-automorphism of $FL_0$ permutes the roots of
$\overline P\in\Bbbk[X]$, so preserves the field $L_0$ they generate over $\Bbbk$.
Its restriction determines it because $F$ and $L_0$ generate the compositum. These
operations are inverse. For a root $c_i$, the same basis argument for $\Bbbk(c_i)$
gives $[F(c_i):F]=[\Bbbk(c_i):\Bbbk]$, proving that its minimal polynomial remains
irreducible over $F$. Translation is an automorphism of $F[X]$ preserving degree,
which proves the last assertion.
\end{proof}

For omnific polynomials the coefficient theorem descends to $\Z$, but the field of this
Galois statement must be specified: with $\Bbbk=\R$ the finer rational Galois group of
$\overline P$ is lost. To keep it one uses a Hahn workspace with coefficient field $\Q$
when the coefficients admit such a presentation, or applies ordinary splitting theory over
$\Q$ to $\overline P$ separately. There is no claim that the rational Galois group
survives after extending constants to $\R$: for example $X^2-2$ already splits over
$\R$, although its splitting field over $\Q$ has degree two \src{C17}.

\begin{corollary}[Marked-value rigidity \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:cor:marks}
Let $P=\overline P(X-h)$ be as in \cref{odg:disc:thm:translation}, with $h\in\crP$. If
$a\in\crA$ and $P(a)\in\Bbbk$, then $a-h\in\Bbbk$, that is, $a=h+\ct a$. Consequently an
ordinary coefficient of $X^{n-1}$, or an ordinary value $P(c)$ at one $c\in\Bbbk$, forces
$h=0$ and $P=\overline P$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
$\overline P(a-h)=P(a)\in\Bbbk$ makes $a-h\in\crA$ a root of
$\overline P(T)-P(a)\in\Bbbk[T]$. This polynomial is nonzero because $\overline P$ has
positive degree. Thus $a-h\in\Bbbk$ by \cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}(d), and
$\ct(a-h)=\ct a$ identifies this constant, since $\ct h=0$.
For $a=c\in\Bbbk$ this gives $h\in\Bbbk\cap\crP=0$; the coefficient case is
\eqref{odg:disc:eq:h}.
\end{proof}

The marked value may be zero: a monic polynomial with nonzero constant discriminant whose
coefficient $a_n$ (its constant coefficient in $X$) is constant is entirely constant. The
constant coefficient in $X$ must not be confused with the constant term of a series
\src{C17}.

\begin{theorem}[Coherent translations \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:thm:resultants}
Let $\ell\ge1$ and let $P_1,\ldots,P_\ell\in\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}[X]$ be monic of positive degree, each with
nonzero constant discriminant, and let $\mathcal G$ be a connected graph on
$\{1,\ldots,\ell\}$ such that $\Res(P_i,P_j)$ is a nonzero constant for every edge $ij$.
Then there is a unique $h\in\crP$ with $P_i(X)=\overline P_i(X-h)$ for all $i$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $P_i=\overline P_i(X-h_i)$. For an edge $ij$ let $c_1,\ldots,c_a$ and
$d_1,\ldots,d_b$ be the roots of $\overline P_i$ and $\overline P_j$. Then
\[
 \Res(P_i,P_j)=\prod_{u=1}^a\prod_{v=1}^b(h_i-h_j+c_u-d_v),
\]
the value at $h_i-h_j$ of
$G_{ij}(T)=\Res_X(\overline P_i(X-T),\overline P_j(X))\in\Bbbk[T]$.
The displayed product shows that $G_{ij}$ is monic of degree $ab\ge1$.
If the resultant equals $c\in\Bbbk$, then $h_i-h_j$ is a root of the nonzero
polynomial $G_{ij}(T)-c$. If $h_i\ne h_j$, then $h_i-h_j$ is a
nonzero element of $\crP$, transcendental over $\Bbbk$ (\cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}(d)), which
contradicts a constant resultant. So $h_i=h_j$ along every edge, and connectedness gives a
common $h$, unique by any one $P_i$. When $\ell=1$ this is the original translation
theorem, with no edge condition. Nonemptiness is needed for uniqueness: an empty family
places no restriction on $h$.
\end{proof}

Only the edges of a spanning tree are needed. The nonzero condition says that the marked
configurations do not meet along the chosen edges; the proof of equal translations would
also work with a zero constant resultant. For the complete graph with nonzero resultants,
$\Disc(\prod_iP_i)=\prod_i\Disc(P_i)\prod_{i<j}\Res(P_i,P_j)^2$ gives a second proof by
applying \cref{odg:disc:thm:translation} to the product; the connected-graph version
records the weaker hypothesis \src{C17}.

\subsubsection{Finite \texorpdfstring{\'etale}{etale} algebras and their units}\label{odg:disc:sub:etale}

Throughout, $\mathsf B$ is a nonzero finite \'etale $\crA$-algebra, with the usual
commutative-algebra definition of \'etaleness \cite{StacksEtale}. Finiteness,
flatness and finite presentation give a finite locally free, hence finite projective,
$\crA$-module \cite{StacksFiniteProjective}. Its rank is constant, say $r\ge1$, because
$\crA$ is a domain and so
$\Spec\crA$ is connected. The standard inputs are preservation under base change,
formal \'etaleness \cite{StacksFormal}, and the description of a finite \'etale algebra over
a field as a finite product of finite separable field extensions
\cite{StacksEtaleField}. The generic algebra $\mathsf B_F=F\otimes_{\crA}\mathsf B$ is such
a product. A projective module over a domain is a direct summand of a free module, hence
torsion free, so $\mathsf B\hookrightarrow\mathsf B_F$ and $\mathsf B$ is reduced.
The rank cannot be zero: local freeness would then give $\mathsf B=0$.
These statements do not require $\mathsf B$ to be a domain; its generic algebra may
have several field factors. For
$y\in\mathsf B$, multiplication by $y$ has a characteristic polynomial
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:charB}
 \operatorname{ch}_y(T)=T^r+c_1(y)T^{r-1}+\cdots+c_r(y)\in\crA[T],
\end{equation}
defined in local bases of the projective module; the local coefficients glue by invariance
under change of basis, commute with base change and satisfy Cayley--Hamilton, both being
local matrix identities. In a splitting field of $\mathsf B_F$ it is the product of
$T-\sigma(y)$ over the $r$ generic embeddings $\sigma$ of all the field factors. No global
basis of $\mathsf B$ is assumed \src{C17}.

\begin{lemma}[Characteristic polynomials after reduction \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:lem:reduction}
Let $M$ be a finite projective module of constant rank $r$ over a commutative ring $R$,
let $J\subseteq R$ be an ideal, and let $\tau\in\End_R(M)$ have image in $JM$. Then the
nonleading coefficients of the characteristic polynomial of $\tau$ lie in $J$, and
Cayley--Hamilton holds for $\tau$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
On a localization where $M$ is free, the reduction of the matrix of $\tau$ modulo $J$ is
zero, so the characteristic polynomial reduces to $X^r$. The images of the coefficients
in $R/J$ therefore vanish after localization at every prime, hence vanish. Substituting
$\tau$ into its characteristic polynomial gives an endomorphism that is zero on every free
localization, hence zero.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The extended derivation vanishes in the augmentation fiber \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:lem:etaleD}
Each $\partial=\partial_\lambda$ extends uniquely to a derivation $\widetilde\partial$ of
$\mathsf B$, and
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:DBideal}
 \widetilde\partial(\mathsf B)\subseteq\crP\mathsf B .
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Give $\mathsf B[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^2)$ the $\crA$-algebra structure
$a\mapsto a+\partial(a)\epsilon$. The identity of its quotient $\mathsf B$ lifts uniquely
along the square-zero ideal $(\epsilon)$ by formal \'etaleness \cite{StacksFormal}; the lift
has the form $b\mapsto b+\widetilde\partial(b)\epsilon$, and its multiplicativity is the
Leibniz rule. Since $\partial(\crP)\subseteq\crP$, $\widetilde\partial$ preserves
$\crP\mathsf B$, and its reduction is a derivation of $\mathsf B_0=\mathsf B/\crP\mathsf B$
that vanishes on $\crA/\crP=\Bbbk$. But $\mathsf B_0$ is finite \'etale over $\Bbbk$, a
product of finite separable extensions. A derivation $D$ kills every idempotent $e$:
$D(e)=2eD(e)$, and $1-2e$ is a unit with square $1$. It therefore preserves each field
factor. In a separable field factor, differentiating a minimal polynomial $g$ over
$\Bbbk$ gives $g'(z)D(z)=0$, hence $D(z)=0$. This proves
\eqref{odg:disc:eq:DBideal}.
\end{proof}

The quotient rule also extends $\widetilde\partial$ to $\mathsf B_F$. The same
idempotent calculation shows that it preserves each generic field factor, and
\cref{odg:disc:lem:extension} makes every embedding of that factor into $\Hbar$
compatible with the ambient Euler derivation. Thus the generic images used below
are compatible with differentiation even when $\mathsf B$ is disconnected.

\begin{proposition}[No nonconstant logarithmic derivative of a unit \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:prop:unitD}
For $u\in\mathsf B^\times$ and every $\lambda$, $\widetilde\partial_\lambda u=0$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Put $y=u^{-1}\widetilde\partial u$, which lies in $\crP\mathsf B$ by
\cref{odg:disc:lem:etaleD}. Multiplication by $y$ has image in $\crP\mathsf B$, so
$c_j(y)\in\crP$ for $j\ge1$ (\cref{odg:disc:lem:reduction}). Realize every generic
embedding $\sigma$ of every field factor of $\mathsf B_F$ in $\Hbar$. By
\cref{odg:disc:lem:extension}, $\sigma(y)=\partial_\lambda\sigma(u)/\sigma(u)$, with nonzero
denominator because $u$ is a unit, so $\sigma(y)\in\Obar$ by \eqref{odg:disc:eq:hahn}. The
$c_j(y)$ are up to sign elementary symmetric functions of these $r$ values, so
$c_j(y)\in\crP\cap\Obar=0$. Cayley--Hamilton gives $m_y^r=0$ for multiplication
$m_y$ by $y$; evaluating this endomorphism at $1$ gives $y^r=0$. As $\mathsf B$ is reduced,
$y=0$, and so $\widetilde\partial u=uy=0$.
\end{proof}

This is the finite-projective analogue of the root-velocity argument: the ideal
information comes from the reduction of a multiplication operator instead of a universal
discriminant denominator, and disconnected algebras are allowed \src{C17}.

\begin{proposition}[Constants, their dimension and their fiber \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:prop:constants}
The set $\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}$ of elements of $\mathsf B$ algebraic over $\Bbbk$ is a
finite \'etale $\Bbbk$-subalgebra, and
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:constantkernel}
 \mathsf C_{\mathsf B}=\bigcap_\lambda\ker\widetilde\partial_\lambda .
\end{equation}
The map $\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}\to\mathsf B/\crP\mathsf B$ is injective, so
$\dim_\Bbbk\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}\le r$, and the natural map
$\crA\otimes_\Bbbk\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}\to\mathsf B$ is injective.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Elements algebraic over a field form a subalgebra, since finitely many of them generate a
finite-dimensional algebra. For an algebraic $b$, choose an annihilating polynomial
and let $g\in\Bbbk[T]$ be its squarefree part. Some power of $g$ is divisible by the
original polynomial, so $g(b)$ is nilpotent and hence zero in the reduced algebra
$\mathsf B$. In characteristic zero, $\gcd(g,g')=1$; a B\'ezout identity evaluated at
$b$ makes $g'(b)$ invertible in $\Bbbk[b]$. Since $\widetilde\partial$ kills $\Bbbk$,
$0=\widetilde\partial g(b)=g'(b)\widetilde\partial b$ gives
$\widetilde\partial b=0$. Conversely, if every
$\widetilde\partial_\lambda$ kills $b$, then each of its finitely many generic images is
killed by every $\partial_\lambda$ and lies in $\bar\Bbbk$ by \eqref{odg:disc:eq:hahn};
the product of their minimal polynomials over $\Bbbk$ vanishes in every generic
embedding. Those embeddings jointly inject $\mathsf B_F$ into $\Hbar^{\,r}$, so this
polynomial annihilates $b$ in $\mathsf B_F$, hence in $\mathsf B$.

For $b\in\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}$ all generic images lie in $\bar\Bbbk$, so the coefficients
of $\operatorname{ch}_b$ lie in $\crA\cap\bar\Bbbk=\Bbbk$. If moreover $b\in\crP\mathsf B$,
these constant nonleading coefficients lie in $\crP$ (\cref{odg:disc:lem:reduction}), so
$\operatorname{ch}_b=T^r$, and Cayley--Hamilton and reducedness give $b=0$. Hence
$\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}$ embeds in the $r$-dimensional fiber $\mathsf B/\crP\mathsf B$. A
finite-dimensional reduced algebra over a field of characteristic zero is a product of
finite separable extensions, hence finite \'etale. Finally embed $\mathsf B$ in the product
of the generic splitting fields, and then in $\Hbar^{\,r}$. The images of a $\Bbbk$-basis of
$\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}$ are $\Bbbk$-linearly independent vectors with entries in
$\bar\Bbbk$, so they are $F$-linearly independent by the vector form of
\cref{odg:disc:lem:independence}, hence $\crA$-linearly independent. Since these basis
elements also form an $\crA$-basis of $\crA\otimes_\Bbbk\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}$, this is
exactly the asserted injectivity; it does not assert surjectivity.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Units of finite \'etale algebras \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:thm:units}
Let $\mathsf B$ be a nonzero finite \'etale $\crA$-algebra of rank $r$. Then
$\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}$ is finite \'etale over $\Bbbk$ with
$\dim_\Bbbk\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}\le r$, and
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:units}
 \mathsf B^\times=\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}^\times .
\end{equation}
The map $\crA\otimes_\Bbbk\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}\to\mathsf B$ is injective, and it is an
isomorphism if and only if $\mathsf B$ is generated as an $\crA$-algebra by its units.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\Cref{odg:disc:prop:unitD} and \eqref{odg:disc:eq:constantkernel} give
$\mathsf B^\times\subseteq\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}$, and the inverse of a unit is killed by the
same derivations; so $\mathsf B^\times=\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}^\times$. The other structural
assertions are \cref{odg:disc:prop:constants}. If $\mathsf B$ is generated by its units, it
is generated by elements of $\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}$, so the injective map is onto.
Conversely a finite \'etale $\Bbbk$-algebra $C=\prod_jL_j$ is spanned by its units: for
$c\in C$ choose $0\ne\lambda_0\in\Bbbk$ different from every component $c_j$ that lies in
$\Bbbk$, which is possible because $\Bbbk$ is infinite; then $\lambda_0$ and $c-\lambda_0$
are units with sum $c$. So $\crA\otimes_\Bbbk\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}$, when it equals
$\mathsf B$, is generated by units.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Geometrically constant coefficients \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:cor:algclosed}
If $\Bbbk$ is algebraically closed, then $\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}\cong\Bbbk^j$ for some
$1\le j\le r$ and $\mathsf B^\times\cong(\Bbbk^\times)^j$; if moreover $\mathsf B$ is
connected, $\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}=\Bbbk$ and $\mathsf B^\times=\Bbbk^\times$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A finite \'etale algebra over an algebraically closed field is a product of copies of the
field, and two or more factors give nontrivial idempotents of $\mathsf B$. More generally,
every idempotent of $\mathsf B$ already belongs to $\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}$, because it
satisfies $T^2-T=0$. Thus the constant subalgebra records all idempotents, even before
assuming that $\Bbbk$ is algebraically closed.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Why this is not yet a covering classification \src{C17}.} A finite \'etale
algebra might contain elements that are not combinations of its units, and
\cref{odg:disc:thm:units} does not exclude them. The generic primitive element theorem does
not give a global monogenic presentation over $\crA$: a generic generator may need
localization, and its discriminant need not be a unit before localization. The injection
$\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}\to\mathsf B/\crP\mathsf B$ isolates the issue: every algebraic
constant is visible in the augmentation fiber, but it is not shown that every element of
the fiber lifts to one. Such a surjectivity, with a global descent argument, would be a
substantive further step, and it is not used below (\cref{odg:disc:q:etale}).

\begin{remark}[The \'etale norm theorem as a constant case \mergetag]\label{odg:disc:rem:etalenorm}
\Cref{odg:disc:thm:units} contains \cref{odg:dec:thm:etale} when $\Bbbk$ has
characteristic zero. The coordinate conclusion is vacuous when $E=0$, so assume
$E\ne0$. With $K\subseteq\Bbbk$, $E$ and $e_1,\ldots,e_r$ as there, the algebra
$\mathsf B=\crA\otimes_KE$ is finite \'etale over $\crA$ of rank $r$ by base change, and
$z=\sum_je_jx_j$ with $x_j\in A\subseteq\crA$ has norm $N(\mathbf x)=a\in K^\times$,
so multiplication by $z$ has invertible determinant in the induced $\crA$-basis.
It is therefore an invertible endomorphism; applying its inverse to $1$ gives the
inverse of $z$. Thus $z\in\mathsf B^\times\subseteq\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}$. Each of the $r$
maps $\sigma_\ell:E\to\bar\Bbbk$ extends to $\mathsf B\to\mathcal A_{\bar\Bbbk}(\Gamma)$,
and $\sigma_\ell(z)=\sum_j\sigma_\ell(e_j)x_j$ is algebraic over $\Bbbk$, hence
constant (\cref{odg:cr:cor:algclosed} over $\bar\Bbbk$). For every $\gamma\ne0$ the
coefficient vector $\mathbf x_\gamma$ therefore satisfies $M\mathbf x_\gamma=0$ with the
invertible matrix $M=(\sigma_\ell(e_j))$, so $x_j\in\Bbbk\cap A=\mathfrak o$. The last step
is the one of the printed proof; the unit theorem replaces its factorization of the norm.
It does not replace the printed proof, which works in every characteristic (paragraph
\emph{Boundary examples and characteristic} after \cref{odg:dec:thm:etale}), whereas
source C17 uses characteristic zero throughout. \Cref{odg:disc:thm:units} goes further:
$\mathsf B$ need not be a base change, and all its units are algebraic. The dual-number
example after \cref{odg:dec:thm:etale}, a unit with a nonconstant nilpotent coordinate,
and the finite free reduced algebra of \cref{odg:disc:ex:nonetale}, a nonconstant unit
without nilpotents, are the two ways the \'etale hypothesis can fail.
\end{remark}

\subsubsection{Monogenic and arithmetic descent}\label{odg:disc:sub:monogenic}

\begin{theorem}[Monogenic finite \'etale algebras are constant \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:thm:monogenic}
If a finite \'etale $\crA$-algebra $\mathsf B$ is generated by one element $b$, then
$\mathsf B\cong\crA\otimes_\Bbbk C$ for a finite \'etale $\Bbbk$-algebra $C$. More
precisely, if $\mathsf B$ has rank $r$ and $P=\operatorname{ch}_b$, then
\[
 \mathsf B\cong\crA[X]/(P)\cong\crA[X]/(\overline P),
\]
the second isomorphism being induced by a translation by an element of $\crP$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Cayley--Hamilton gives a surjection $\crA[X]/(P)\to\mathsf B$ from a free module of rank
$r$ onto a projective module of rank $r$; locally it is a surjection between free modules
of the same finite rank, hence an isomorphism, and so it is one globally. In this monic
presentation \'etaleness makes $\Disc(P)$ a unit of $\crA$ by the discriminant criterion
\cite{StacksDisc}, hence an element of $\Bbbk^\times$ (\cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}(c)).
\Cref{odg:disc:thm:translation} gives $P=\overline P(X-h)$ with $h\in\crP$, and $Y=X-h$
gives the second isomorphism; $\Bbbk[Y]/(\overline P)$ is finite \'etale because
$\overline P$ is squarefree.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The split complex case \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:cor:split}
Every monogenic finite \'etale $\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma)$-algebra is a finite product of copies
of $\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Over $\C$ the squarefree $\overline P$ splits into distinct linear factors whose pairwise
differences are units of $\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma)$, so the Chinese remainder theorem applies
over $\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Arithmetic monic descent \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:thm:arithmetic}
Let $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$ be a unital subring and $P\in\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}[X]$
monic with $\Disc(P)\in\mathfrak o\setminus\{0\}$. Then there is an
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}$-algebra isomorphism
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:arithmetic}
 \mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}[X]/(P)\cong\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}\otimes_{\mathfrak o}
 \mathfrak o[X]/(\overline P).
\end{equation}
If $\Disc(P)\in\mathfrak o^\times$, both sides are finite \'etale over
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}$ and $\mathfrak o[X]/(\overline P)$ is finite \'etale over
$\mathfrak o$. Every monogenic finite \'etale $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}$-algebra descends in
this way.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Translation by $h\in\crP\subseteq\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}$ is an automorphism of the
polynomial ring, so \cref{odg:disc:thm:translation} gives \eqref{odg:disc:eq:arithmetic}.
For a unit discriminant the finite free trace pairing is nondegenerate, and the
discriminant criterion gives \'etaleness over each coefficient ring. For a monogenic
finite \'etale algebra the characteristic-polynomial argument of
\cref{odg:disc:thm:monogenic} works over the domain $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}$, and its
discriminant is a unit of $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}$, hence of $\mathfrak o$
(\cref{odg:cr:lem:rings}(c)).
\end{proof}

For example $P=(X-h)^2-2\in\Oz[X]$, $h\in\Pi$, has discriminant $8$ and is a translate of
an ordinary polynomial, although its algebra is not \'etale at the prime $2$. The theorem
classifies the polynomial when the stronger \'etale hypothesis fails; confusing ``nonzero
integer'' with ``unit integer'' would erase the distinction \src{C17}.

\begin{remark}[The arithmetic scope \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:rem:scope}
The descent for $\Oz$ and $\Og$ concerns finite monic presentations, read in set-sized
workspaces as before. \Cref{odg:disc:thm:units} is proved for $\crA=\Bbbk\oplus\crP$, not
for $\mathfrak o+\crP$ by replacing $\Bbbk$ with $\mathfrak o$: finite \'etale algebras
over $\mathfrak o$ may have a richer arithmetic unit group, and reduction modulo $\crP$
need not be a product of fields. No extension of the unit theorem to that case is made
(\cref{odg:disc:q:arithmetic}).
\end{remark}

\subsubsection{Critical-point configurations}\label{odg:disc:sub:critical}

A translation theorem for the derivative gives a consequence about polynomials as
algebraic maps, not about iteration or analytic continuation \src{C17}.

\begin{theorem}[Two-translation classification \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:thm:critical}
Let $f=X^d+a_1X^{d-1}+\cdots+a_d\in\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}[X]$ with $d\ge2$, and suppose
that the monic critical polynomial $f'/d\in\crA[X]$ has nonzero constant discriminant.
Then there are unique $h,\beta\in\crP$ with
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:critical}
 f(X)=\overline f(X-h)+\beta,\qquad
 h=-\frac{a_1-\ct a_1}{d},\qquad \beta=f(h)-\overline f(0).
\end{equation}
Conversely, a polynomial of the form \eqref{odg:disc:eq:critical} has nonzero constant
critical discriminant exactly when $\overline f'/d$ is squarefree.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The coefficient of $X^{d-2}$ in $f'/d$ is $(d-1)a_1/d$, so
\cref{odg:disc:thm:translation} over $\crA$ gives $f'(X)=\overline f'(X-h)$ with $h$ as
stated. Then $f(X)-\overline f(X-h)$ has zero derivative, so it is a constant $\beta$ in
characteristic zero; it lies in $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}$, and $\ct\beta=0$, so
$\beta\in\crP$. Evaluation at $X=h$ gives the formula. Uniqueness of $h$ follows from the
coefficient of $X^{d-1}$, and that of $\beta$ is then immediate. The converse follows by
differentiating, since translation does not change the discriminant.
\end{proof}

If $\xi_1,\ldots,\xi_{d-1}$ are the critical points of $\overline f$, those of $f$ are
$h+\xi_j$, with critical values $\beta+\overline f(\xi_j)$; all gaps between critical
points and between critical values are ordinary constants, and distinct critical values of
$\overline f$ remain distinct. This is a classification up to independent source and
target translations. It is not in general a conjugacy $f=T_h\circ\overline f\circ T_{-h}$,
which would need $\beta=h$ \src{C17}.

\begin{example}[A repeated critical value \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:ex:critical}
For $s,t\in\crP$, $f(X)=(X-s)^4-2(X-s)^2+t$ has critical polynomial $(X-s)^3-(X-s)$, of
discriminant $4$, critical points $s-1,s,s+1$ and critical values $t-1,t,t-1$, and
$\overline f=X^4-2X^2$. The theorem allows the repeated critical value; no Morse or
distinct-critical-value hypothesis is imposed.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[The same normal forms in the sibling report \mergetag]\label{odg:disc:rem:osq}
The sibling report \fn{docs/surreal/set-sized-quotients-of-omnific-integers} reaches the
same normal forms from a different hypothesis. For an integer-valued polynomial $f$ over
$\Oz$ or $\Og$ with more than $d=\deg f$ inputs of ordinary output, its canonical
translation (\replabel{osq:or:thm:canonical}, \replabel{osq:or:thm:eta}) gives
$f=\ct_*(f)(X-\eta)$ with $\eta=-\mathring a_{d-1}/(da_d)$, where $\ct_*(f)=\overline f$ and
$\mathring a=a-\ct a$; for monic $f$ this is \eqref{odg:disc:eq:h}. Its rich-target theorem
(\replabel{osq:or:thm:targets}) gives $f=\overline f(X-\eta)+\beta$ with
$\beta=f(\eta)-\ct f(\eta)$, the form \eqref{odg:disc:eq:critical}. For monic $P\in\Oz[X]$
with $\Disc(P)\in\Z\setminus\{0\}$, \cref{odg:disc:cor:omnific} gives
$P(h+c)=\overline P(c)\in\Z$ for every $c\in\Z$, so the sibling hypothesis holds and the two
translations coincide; likewise \cref{odg:disc:thm:critical} makes $\beta+\Z$ a rich target.
The converse fails: $(X-h)^2$ with $0\ne h\in\Pi$ has ordinary outputs on all of $h+\Z$ and
discriminant zero. The sibling hypothesis needs the discrete order of $\Oz$, whereas
\cref{odg:disc:thm:translation} holds over every $\crA$ and $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}$.
Source C17 does not cite the sibling report, of which it read only the guide.
\end{remark}

\subsubsection{Normal and Hermitian matrices}\label{odg:disc:sub:matrix}

Work in $\mathbb H_\R(\Gamma_\Q)$, ordered by the sign of the coefficient of the dominant
monomial (the one of largest exponent), and in $\mathbb H_\C(\Gamma_\Q)$, with conjugation on
coefficients. For a vector $v$ the form $\langle v,v\rangle=\sum_jv_j\overline{v_j}$ is
positive for $v\ne0$; the arguments are finite algebra over this ordered field, not
Hilbert-space arguments \src{C17}. For a matrix, $M^*$ is the conjugate transpose and
$\ct M$ applies $\ct$ entrywise.

\begin{lemma}[A normal idempotent is an orthogonal projection \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:lem:projection}
Let $\mathsf P\in M_n(\mathbb H_\C(\Gamma_\Q))$ satisfy $\mathsf P^2=\mathsf P$ and
$\mathsf P\mathsf P^*=\mathsf P^*\mathsf P$. Then $\mathsf P=\mathsf P^*$,
$0\le\mathsf P_{jj}\le1$ and $|\mathsf P_{jk}|^2\le1$. If its entries lie in
$\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma)$, they lie in $\C$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Normality gives $\langle\mathsf Pv,\mathsf Pv\rangle=\langle\mathsf P^*v,\mathsf P^*v\rangle$,
so $\ker\mathsf P=\ker\mathsf P^*$. With $(\operatorname{im}\mathsf P)^\perp=\ker\mathsf P^*$,
the decomposition $\operatorname{im}\mathsf P\oplus\ker\mathsf P$ is orthogonal, and
$\mathsf P$ acts on its summands by $1$ and $0$; so $\mathsf P=\mathsf P^*$. This uses only
positive definiteness and the dimension formula for orthogonal complements. The $jj$ entry of
$\mathsf P=\mathsf P\mathsf P^*$ is $\mathsf P_{jj}=\sum_k\mathsf P_{jk}\overline{\mathsf
P_{jk}}$, so $\mathsf P_{jj}\ge0$ and $\mathsf P_{jj}\ge\mathsf P_{jj}^2$, whence
$\mathsf P_{jj}\le1$, and every summand is at most $1$. An element of positive degree has
squared modulus of positive degree with positive dominant coefficient, so it is infinite and
exceeds $1$. Hence every entry lies in $\mathcal O_\C(\Gamma_\Q)$, and an entry in
$\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma)$ lies in $\C$ by \eqref{odg:disc:eq:hahn}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Normal matrices \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:thm:matrix}
Let $M\in M_n(\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma))$ satisfy $MM^*=M^*M$, and suppose that its
characteristic polynomial has nonzero constant discriminant. Then
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:matrix}
 M=\ct M+hI_n,\qquad h=\frac{\tr M-\ct(\tr M)}{n}\in\Pi_\C(\Gamma).
\end{equation}
For Hermitian $M$, $h$ has real coefficients. In particular this applies to normal
matrices over $\Og$ and real symmetric matrices over $\Oz$, read in a workspace.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{odg:disc:thm:translation} applied to the characteristic polynomial, whose constant
term is the characteristic polynomial of $\ct M$, the eigenvalues of $M$ are the distinct
elements $h+c_j$, where $c_1,\ldots,c_n\in\C$ are the eigenvalues of $\ct M$ and $h$ is as
stated. The Lagrange spectral idempotents
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:projectors}
 \mathsf P_j=\prod_{u\ne j}\frac{M-(h+c_u)I_n}{c_j-c_u}
\end{equation}
have entries in $\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma)$, since the denominators are nonzero complex
numbers. Interpolation modulo the squarefree characteristic polynomial and Cayley--Hamilton
give $\mathsf P_j^2=\mathsf P_j$, $\mathsf P_j\mathsf P_u=0$ for $j\ne u$,
$\sum_j\mathsf P_j=I_n$ and $M=\sum_j(h+c_j)\mathsf P_j$. A polynomial in the normal
matrix $M$ commutes with the adjoint of any polynomial in $M$, so every $\mathsf P_j$ is
normal, and \cref{odg:disc:lem:projection} gives $\mathsf P_j\in M_n(\C)$. Thus
$M=hI_n+\sum_jc_j\mathsf P_j$, and taking constant terms identifies the sum as $\ct M$.
For Hermitian $M$ the trace is real, hence so is $h$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Traceless rigidity and fixed eigenspaces \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:cor:traceless}
Under the hypotheses of \cref{odg:disc:thm:matrix},
$M-\frac{\tr M}nI_n=\ct M-\frac{\tr(\ct M)}nI_n$. An ordinary trace forces $M$ to be an
ordinary matrix, all spectral projections are ordinary, and an ordinary unitary change of
basis diagonalizes $\ct M$ and $M$ simultaneously.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Subtract the traces in \eqref{odg:disc:eq:matrix}. The proof of the theorem makes every
spectral projection ordinary; alternatively, diagonalize the ordinary normal matrix
$\ct M$, whose eigenspaces are not changed by adding $hI_n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What positivity contributes \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:rem:positivity}
The characteristic-polynomial theorem controls eigenvalues but not entries or eigenspaces.
Positivity turns normal idempotents into bounded projections, and one-sided support turns
bounded entries into constants; without this step the matrix conclusion is false
(\cref{odg:disc:ex:nonnormal}). No physical prediction about infinitesimal observables
follows from the algebraic statement.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Symmetric omnific matrices \mergetag]\label{odg:disc:rem:symmetric}
For real symmetric matrices over $\Oz$ there is a second route through
\cref{odg:cor:symmetric}. If the characteristic polynomial of $M$ has discriminant in
$\Z\setminus\{0\}$, \cref{odg:disc:cor:omnific} writes it as $\overline P(X-h)$ with
$h=(\tr M-\ct\tr M)/n\in\Pi$. Then $M-hI_n$ is a symmetric matrix over $\Oz$ with the integer
characteristic polynomial $\overline P$, so all its entries are ordinary integers by
\cref{odg:cor:symmetric}, and $M-hI_n=\ct M$. That corollary needs no discriminant
hypothesis when the characteristic polynomial is already constant, while
\cref{odg:disc:thm:matrix} allows a purely infinite trace and complex normal matrices. The
nilpotent example after \cref{odg:cor:symmetric} and \cref{odg:disc:ex:nonnormal} show the
same failure without symmetry or normality.
\end{remark}

\subsubsection{Counterexamples and the boundary}\label{odg:disc:sub:boundaries}

\begin{example}[Two-sided coefficients \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:ex:twosided}
In a real closed Hahn field, or in $\No$, let $x=\omega$ and
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:twosided}
 \rho=\frac{x-\sqrt{x^2-4/x}}2 .
\end{equation}
The square root exists since $x^2-4/x>0$; $\rho$ and $x-\rho$ are the roots of
$Y^2-xY+1/x$, so $\rho(x-\rho)=x^{-1}$, $0<\rho<x$, and $\rho$ is positive
infinitesimal with dominant term $x^{-2}$. The monic cubic $P=X(X-x)(X-\rho)$ has
distinct roots and $\Disc(P)=x^2\rho^2(x-\rho)^2=1$, but its root gap $x$ is infinite, so
it is not a translate of a constant polynomial. Its coefficient $x\rho$ is a nonzero
infinitesimal, so $P$ does not have coefficients in $\BR$. The real diagonal matrix
$\operatorname{diag}(0,x,\rho)$ is normal with the same discriminant and violates
\cref{odg:disc:thm:matrix} once the support hypothesis is dropped. So ``over surreal
numbers'' cannot replace ``with coefficients in the one-sided ring''. (Here $\rho$ replaces
the source's $r$.)
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Characteristic $p$ \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:ex:charp}
Let $\Bbbk$ have characteristic $p>0$, $\gamma>0$ and $u=\omega^\gamma$ in the
nonnegative-support ring over $\Bbbk$. The polynomial
\begin{equation}\label{odg:disc:eq:charp}
 P(X)=X^{p^2}+uX^p+X
\end{equation}
has derivative $1$, so it is separable with the constant discriminant
$(-1)^{p^2(p^2-1)/2}$, and $\overline P=X^{p^2}+X$. But
$\overline P(X-h)=X^{p^2}+X-h^{p^2}-h$ has no term in $X^p$ for any $h$, so
$P\ne\overline P(X-h)$. This works in every positive characteristic and avoids an
Artin--Schreier example whose right side could be absorbed by an infinite translation.
It is consistent with the failure of squarefree rigidity in characteristic $p$
(\cref{odg:cr:sub:charp}).
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Zero discriminant \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:ex:zero}
For $0\ne s\in\crP$, $X^2(X-s)$ has discriminant zero and $\overline P=X^3$, but it is
not $(X-h)^3$, which has one distinct root. Repeated roots allow relative motion that the
logarithmic-gap argument cannot constrain, because its denominators vanish.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Nonnormal matrices \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:ex:nonnormal}
For distinct $c_1,c_2\in\C$ and $0\ne s\in\Pi_\C(\Gamma)$, the matrix
$M=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}c_1&s\\0&c_2\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$ has constant
characteristic polynomial $(X-c_1)(X-c_2)$ with nonzero constant discriminant, but it is
not $\ct M+hI_2$; its corner entry moves an eigenspace without moving an eigenvalue. For
$c_1=0$, $c_2=1$ and real $s$, $MM^*-M^*M$ has the diagonal entry $s^2\ne0$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Finite free and reduced is not enough \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:ex:nonetale}
Take $\Gamma=\Z$, so $\crA=\Bbbk[\omega]$, and
$\mathsf B=\crA[Y]/(Y^2-\omega^2-1)$. It is finite free of rank two and reduced, since its
generic polynomial is separable and it injects into its generic fiber. It has the unit
$u=\omega+Y$ with inverse $Y-\omega$, and $u$ is not algebraic over $\Bbbk$: otherwise
$u^{-1}$ would be algebraic too, and so would $2\omega=u-u^{-1}$. Its discriminant
$4(\omega^2+1)$ is not a unit of $\crA$, so $\mathsf B$ is not \'etale, and replacing
``finite \'etale'' by ``finite free and reduced'' makes \cref{odg:disc:thm:units} false.
In $\crH$ the generic image of $Y$ is $\pm\sqrt{\omega^2+1}$, the element of
\cref{odg:ex:nonsquare} and \cref{odg:prop:notnormal}; the unit $u$ becomes
$\omega+\sqrt{\omega^2+1}$, infinite, with inverse
$\sqrt{\omega^2+1}-\omega=\tfrac12\omega^{-1}-\cdots$ \mergetag.
\end{example}

Source C17's theorems do not classify the prime elements of $\Oz$, prove a unique
factorization or refinement conjecture, determine birthdays of products, or construct a
preferred derivation of the surreal field. No set-theoretic strength beyond set-sized Hahn
and vector-space constructions is used; a request for a broad research direction was
answered by a focused algebraic theorem package, not by a claim about large cardinals
\src{C17}.

\subsubsection{A second proof and the common mechanism}\label{odg:disc:sub:second}

The unit theorem gives a second route to \cref{odg:disc:thm:translation} that does not use
the universal certificate \src{C17}.

\begin{lemma}[Finite \'etale splitting algebra \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:lem:splitting}
If $R$ is a commutative ring and $P\in R[X]$ is monic of degree $n$ with unit discriminant,
there is a finite free \'etale $R$-algebra $\mathsf B_P$ of rank $n!$ in which
$P=\prod_{j=1}^n(X-r_j)$ and every difference $r_i-r_j$, $i\ne j$, is a unit.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Adjoin a root $r_1$ by $R_1=R[X]/(P)$, finite free of rank $n$ and \'etale by the
discriminant criterion. Divide $P$ by $X-r_1$ to get a monic $Q_1\in R_1[X]$ of degree
$n-1$. The identity $\Disc(P)=\Disc(Q_1)Q_1(r_1)^2$ shows that $\Disc(Q_1)$ is a unit,
since factors of a unit are units. Repeat with $Q_1$. The ranks multiply to $n!$, and
compositions of finite \'etale maps are finite \'etale. In the result the product of the
squared differences is $\Disc(P)$, so each difference is a unit. This also covers $n=1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Second proof of \cref{odg:disc:thm:translation} \src{C17}]
Over $\crA$, apply \cref{odg:disc:lem:splitting} and \cref{odg:disc:thm:units}: the root
differences in $\mathsf B_P$ are units, hence algebraic over $\Bbbk$. Map a field factor of
the generic algebra of $\mathsf B_P$ to $\Hbar$. The images of the roots split $P$, and
every gap is algebraic over $\Bbbk$, so it lies in $\bar\Bbbk$ (by \cref{odg:cr:cor:algclosed}
for the coefficient field $\bar\Bbbk$).
Subtracting the mean and taking constant terms proves $P=\overline P(X-h)$ as in the first
proof, and the arithmetic pullback follows as there.
\end{proof}

There is no circularity: the proof of \cref{odg:disc:thm:units} uses the Euler derivations,
formal \'etale lifting, characteristic polynomials and opposite supports, not
\cref{odg:disc:thm:translation}. The first proof remains useful because its certificate is
finite and uniform in the degree and avoids the splitting algebra of rank $n!$ \src{C17}.

Both proofs follow one pattern. A derivation sends ordinary coefficients into a strict
ideal; logarithmic differentiation produces quantities in the opposite valuation ring; a
symmetric or characteristic polynomial puts all their invariants into the strict ideal as
well; the intersection is zero, so the polynomial is a pure power and reducedness kills the
quantities; a separating family of derivations then identifies the residual constants. The
point is not merely that ``infinite and finite cannot agree'': individual roots or elements
of a finite algebra need not lie in the ring, and the invariant polynomial carries the
information back to the ring, where the intersection argument is legitimate. So the trace
of a logarithmic derivative alone, or the product of the root gaps alone, does not suffice
\src{C17}. This is the two-ring mechanism of \cref{odg:cr:sub:tworing}, with the pair
$(\crA,\crP)$ and the valuation ring, applied to roots and to finite algebras instead of
points of varieties \mergetag.

\subsubsection{Source C17's verification and formalization notes}\label{odg:disc:sub:audit}

Source C17's script (\fn{code/17-discriminant-rigidity-verify.py}) performs exact finite
computations over rational polynomial rings and finite fields: translation formulas,
discriminants, resultants, the quadratic certificate, root-velocity identities, critical
configurations, projection examples and the counterexamples. Its recorded run
(\fn{data/17-discriminant-rigidity-verification.json}) passed 1,126 assertions in 13 groups
(\cref{odg:app:verification}). Such checks can catch sign errors, wrong normalizations,
false examples and failures of a finite identity; they cannot certify the theorem for
arbitrary supports, the formal \'etale argument, or the existence of the certificate in
every degree. No Lean implementation accompanies the source, and the Lean coverage of the
repository does not by itself cover these statements \src{C17}.

\begin{center}
\small
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.15}
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.25\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.65\textwidth}}
\toprule
Claim & Decisive dependencies and exclusions \src{C17}\\
\midrule
\Cref{odg:disc:thm:translation} & Hahn splitting field; separating Euler family; universal
symmetric certificate; $\crP\cap\Obar=0$. No rank-one norm and no normality of $\crA$.\\
\Cref{odg:disc:thm:units} & Formal \'etale extension; reduction modulo $\crP$;
characteristic polynomials on a finite projective module; reduced generic fiber. No
global primitive element.\\
\Cref{odg:disc:thm:arithmetic} & The translation lies in $\crP$, so division by the degree
does not need the degree to be a unit of $\mathfrak o$.\\
\Cref{odg:disc:thm:matrix} & Polynomial projectors; normal idempotents; positive Hermitian
form; bounded one-sided entries are constants.\\
Full surreal cases & Finite input supports lie in a set-sized divisible exponent group; no
proper-class summation, no large-cardinal axiom.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

A formalization should keep these divisions: in particular it should not replace the strict
ideal $\crP$ by an ideal of support bounded away from $0$, nor the separating set of Euler
functionals by one alleged faithful real-valued functional. Source C17 proposes an order:
first a generic interface (a ring $A$ and an ideal $I$ inside a valued field, derivations
carrying $A$ into $I$, and $I\cap\mathcal O=0$); then the certificate, organized through the
permutation action on unordered pairs, and the vanishing lemma for characteristic
polynomials, first for free algebras and then descended from local bases; then the
instantiation with Hahn series, whose joint-constants lemma needs coefficient
extensionality and separation of exponents by linear functionals; the surreal
specialization through the repository's normal-form and omnific-ring identifications,
with an exact theorem mapping rather than a claim that an abstract theorem has been proved
for the constructed surreals; and, largely independently, the projectors and the
normal-idempotent lemma. This is a proposal; no stage is claimed done
(\cref{odg:disc:q:lean}). The interface is the abstract one of \cref{odg:log:rem:interface}
with the roles of the ring and the valuation ring as in \cref{odg:cr:sub:tworing}.

\subsubsection{What source C17 does not claim}\label{odg:disc:sub:nonclaims}

The limitations stated by source C17 are listed in \cref{odg:app:nonclaims}. In short: it
does not show that every finite \'etale $\crA$-algebra is constant; it does not extend the
unit theorem to $\mathfrak o+\crP$; it proves no factorization, prime or birthday result and
uses no large cardinal; it constructs no preferred surreal derivation; it does not claim the
exponent $2s$ optimal; its Galois statement depends on the chosen coefficient field; its
critical-point theorem is not a conjugacy; its main theorems fail without one-sided support,
characteristic zero or a nonzero discriminant, and the matrix theorem without normality; the
polynomial-parameter case is not claimed new; its finite checks do not prove the
arbitrary-support theorems; it has no Lean formalization and certifies no novelty.

\section{Fixed Hahn workspaces and a formalization route}\label{odg:sec:formal}

\subsection{Which results are local in the exponent group?}

For a set-sized ordered abelian group $\Gamma$, work in a Hahn field whose
monomials are written $\omega^\gamma$ with reverse-well-ordered supports, and
define \src{01, 02}
\[
 \Oz_\Gamma=\Z+\Bigl\{\sum_{\gamma>0}r_\gamma\omega^\gamma\Bigr\},
 \qquad
 \mathcal B_\Gamma=\R+\Bigl\{\sum_{\gamma>0}r_\gamma\omega^\gamma\Bigr\}.
\]
The displayed sums are elements of the ambient Hahn field with the specified
support restrictions; this is a convention, not an identification of every
abstract group element with a surreal exponent. When an ordered embedding of
$\Gamma$ into $\No$ is fixed, it gives the corresponding surreal workspace.

\paragraph{Local results.} The ring decompositions, the unit lemma, the
retraction, finite quotients, equational transfer, the Smith criterion,
constant-product and decomposable rigidity, binary, Pell and norm rigidity,
the quadratic constructions, the quartic guards, and all explicit
polynomial identities apply to $\Oz_\Gamma$ \src{01, 02}. Their proofs use finite algebra,
leading exponents and real coefficients, not the availability of a new
exponent outside $\Gamma$. The derivation arguments of
\cref{odg:sec:derivations} take place in the workspace of the rational span of
the relevant supports. A functional on that rational span restricts to
$\Gamma$, and its Euler derivation changes coefficients without changing
exponents. Thus it preserves the original workspace; passing to the
divisible hull to choose the functional does not require the point or its
derivatives to acquire new exponents \src{05}. If $\Gamma\ne0$, its positive cone
provides nonconstant parameters for the infinite-family statements; the case
$\Gamma=0$ is $\Oz_\Gamma=\Z$. The Lorentz family uses only $t$, $t^2$ and
real scalars, so one positive monomial already produces examples. If the
coefficient field is restricted below $\R$, the general indefinite lifting
additionally needs the chosen real vectors $e,u$ to be available, or an
explicit scalar extension \src{02}. The definability results of sources 06
and 07 (\cref{odg:def:sec:arithmetic,odg:def:sec:ct}) and the multiplier
identity are proved for an arbitrary ordered exponent group, so they apply to
$\Oz_\Gamma=\mathcal R_\Z(\R,\Gamma)$ as stated; the multiplier identity needs
$\Gamma\ne0$. Only $\Frac(\Oz)=\No$ and the reconstruction of $\No$
(\cref{odg:def:thm:realrecovery}) use the full class, and the fraction field
of $\Oz_\Gamma$ need not be the whole Hahn field \src{06}.

\paragraph{Full-class results.} The common-divisor theorem for an arbitrary
set of purely infinite elements, the monomial clearing theorem, $\Pi^2=\Pi$,
the non-generation of $\Pi$ by a set, $\Frac(\Oz)=\No$, the general pair
without a gcd (\cref{odg:thm:nogcd}) and the set-sized quotient theorem
(\cref{odg:fact:settarget}) require bounds that may not exist in $\Gamma$.
For instance, in $\Gamma=\R$ the set $\{1/k:k\geq1\}$ has no strictly
positive real lower bound, whereas in $\No$ it has one. A support in $\Gamma$
may also be unbounded below in $\Gamma$. These conclusions are not asserted
for $\Oz_\Gamma$; in particular one must not infer $\Pi_\Gamma^2=\Pi_\Gamma$ by
copying the proof for $\No$ \src{01, 02, 05}. Proofs that stay inside a
workspace must be distinguished from proofs that enlarge it. The conclusion of
\cref{odg:thm:nogcd}, though not the proof printed with it, does hold in every
$\Oz_\Gamma$ for each $0\ne t\in\Pi_\Gamma$ and irrational real $r$,
by the halving route of sources 08 and 09
(\cref{odg:frac:rem:halving}). Nontriviality of $\Gamma$ is needed for
such a pair to exist; when $\Gamma=0$ the ring is $\Z$, a GCD domain.
For a concrete failure of another full-class conclusion, take $\Gamma=\Z$.
A reverse-well-ordered subset of the positive integers is finite, so
$\Pi_\Gamma=\omega\R[\omega]$ and
$\Pi_\Gamma^2=\omega^2\R[\omega]\ne\Pi_\Gamma$. In particular,
$\omega$ is not a sum of products of two purely infinite elements in
that workspace.

\paragraph{Fractions.} In \cref{odg:frac:sec:fractions} the multiplier theorem
is ring-theoretic: it uses a domain containing $\R$, a retraction to
$\R$, its integer pullback, and a unimodular tuple in that domain
(\cref{odg:frac:thm:pullback}). These hypotheses, especially unimodularity,
are retained; an arbitrary ring map alone does not give the conclusion.
The polynomial models, the scale defect and the
$\Tor_1$ computation concern set-sized rings; and the classification of
