representations, primitivity and least denominators uses only B\'ezout
identities and constant coefficients \src{08}. The non-generation of the
denominator ideals $q(t)\Pi$, the absence of a set of denominators for all
surreals, the orbit density and the focusing theorem use the full class
\src{08, 09}. Sources 08 and 09 show in one workspace that the fraction field
of its omnific ring is smaller than the Hahn field (\cref{odg:frac:ex:workspace}).

\paragraph{Curve and differential rigidity.} The characteristic-zero
rigidity criteria in
\cref{odg:cr:sec:principle,odg:cr:sec:curves,odg:cr:sec:groups}
are first proved for set-sized Hahn rings. No divisibility or rank
restriction on the ordered exponent group is imposed. Their own hypotheses
on the coefficient field, geometry and coefficient ring are still needed:
a theorem about $\crA$-points is not automatically a theorem about
$\Oz_\Gamma$-points. The arithmetic conclusions use the descent statements
\cref{odg:cr:thm:arith,odg:cr:thm:descent,odg:log:thm:descent}.
The positive-characteristic counterexamples and reduced- or dual-number
coefficient extensions have their separately stated domains.

For the actual rings $\BR$, $\BC$, $\Oz$ and $\Og$, each point and its
finite presentation witnesses lie in one set-sized workspace
\src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14, C15, C16}. This proves the asserted
class-level equalities one point at a time. Conversely, nonconstant points
on an affine line require $\Gamma\ne0$; at $\Gamma=0$ every point is
constant, regardless of the target. In the affine-line case the arithmetic
constant-term fibers are copies of $\Pi_\R(\Gamma)$, whereas the rigid
curve cases have singleton fibers over their ordinary points. For singular
curves, the arithmetic converse needs the normalization-preimage condition
of \cref{odg:sg:prop:arithfamily}; it must not be replaced by the smooth
fiber formula.

The full-class projective clearing in
\cref{odg:cor:projectiveclear}, quoted in
\cref{odg:cr:sub:projective}, is a separate step. So is the statement that
nonempty affine-line fibers are proper classes
(\cref{odg:cr:cor:omnific}): fibers inside a fixed workspace are sets.

\paragraph{Constant discriminants.} Every result of \cref{odg:disc:sub:disc} is proved over
$\crA$ or $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ for an arbitrary set-sized ordered group
$\Gamma$, with the splitting field $\Hbar$ over its divisible hull, and the statements about
$\Oz$, $\Og$ and omnific matrices are read in a workspace containing the finitely many
coefficients \src{C17}. They therefore hold for $\Oz_\Gamma$ as stated, and no statement of
that subsection uses the full class.

\subsection{A formalization route}

The implementation ledger \fn{docs/FORMALIZATION.md} distinguishes proved
Lean declarations from manuscript proofs and proposed modules. At commit
\texttt{38425db}, the implemented prerequisites concern the actual surreal
and surcomplex carriers, using their proved normal-form equivalence. They
are not merely assertions about one fixed Hahn field. The dependency order
is as follows.

First come the real and complex nonnegative-support rings, constant-term
retractions, purely infinite ideals and omnific integer ring
(\cref{odg:prop:ring}); then degree, units, discreteness and the exact floor
(\cref{odg:lem:degree,odg:prop:units,odg:thm:floor}). The floor includes the
subtraction of one at an integer constant coefficient with a negative
infinitesimal tail. The files are \fn{OmnificIntegers.lean},
\fn{OmnificDegree.lean}, \fn{OmnificUnits.lean} and \fn{OmnificFloor.lean}
under \fn{Surreal/Foundations/}, with the complex support-ring and unit
counterparts under \fn{Surreal/Surcomplex/}.

Next, ordinary polynomial-root rigidity, residue arithmetic, integer
divisors, positive-characteristic ideals and the canonical retraction are
proved. The separate support-bound arguments prove common monomial
clearing, $\Frac(\Oz)=\No$, idempotence of $\Pi$, and the stated failure of
GCD, Noetherian and integral-closure properties. These full-class arguments
use the normal-form bridge's explicit smallness hypotheses; they are not
consequences of the elementary fixed-workspace ring package alone.

The equation-retraction theorem and positive-existential transfer are
proved in Lean, while the separate undecidability claims remain pending.
For linear systems, \cref{odg:thm:smith} is implemented relative to a chosen
integer Smith reduction; \cref{odg:thm:linear} has its full ordinary-plus-
purely-infinite decomposition and unique rational-kernel parameters in
\fn{Surreal/Foundations/OmnificLinearSystems.lean}. A zero transformed row
requires its whole right-hand entry to vanish, not just its constant term.

Finally, \cref{odg:lem:constantproduct,odg:thm:decomposable} are implemented
in the \fn{DecomposableFibers.lean} modules and the omnific specializations
under \fn{Surreal/Surcomplex/}. The real kernel of complex forms on real
omnific variables is kept distinct from full complex column rank for
complex support-ring variables. The coefficientwise statement uses actual
Conway coefficients; no finite-support restriction is introduced. The
Gaussian fiber theorem and its coefficientwise complex-kernel description
are also proved in commit \texttt{4037368}; commit \texttt{ae0210c} proves
the converse existence criterion, using a nonzero purely infinite monomial.
The files \fn{GaussianOmnificIntegers.lean},
\fn{GaussianDecomposableFibers.lean} and
\fn{DecomposableKernelCriterion.lean} under \fn{Surreal/Surcomplex/}
retain the ordinary-point and nonzero-level hypotheses. Differential and
geometric rigidity and the remaining unmapped Diophantine results are
pending. Exact theorem names and any newer coverage belong in the ledger.

The sources proposed overlapping routes; the following merges them. The
module names in the table are proposals, not the names of the implemented files.

% This uncaptioned roadmap must not renumber the later labelled table.
\addtocounter{table}{-1}
\begingroup
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{longtable}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.3\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.6\textwidth}}
\toprule
Proposed layer & Main obligations\\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
Proposed layer & Main obligations\\
\midrule
\endhead
\bottomrule
\endfoot
\fn{Omnific.Basic} \src{01, 02, 05} & In a real Hahn field over a
set-sized ordered divisible group: the nonnegative-support subring, the
integer-constant subring, the retraction $\ct$ from the zero-exponent
convolution formula, and the finite-element criterion.\\
\fn{Omnific.Residue} \src{01, 02} & $n\Oz=\Pi+n\Z$, finite quotients,
preservation of $\Pi$; source 02 suggests proving these once for any ring
retraction onto $\Z$ with an integer-divisible kernel.\\
\fn{Omnific.Rigidity} \src{01, 02, 05} & Additive leading degree, units of
the real and complex support rings, the exact decomposable fibers (finite
linear algebra plus coefficient extensionality), binary and norm rigidity.\\
\fn{Omnific.Derivation} \src{05} & Euler derivations from a $\Q$-linear
functional (Leibniz from finite convolution fibers), the quotient step
for separated powers, comaximality of powers, and Wronskian degree bounds.\\
\fn{Omnific.Quadratic} \src{01, 02} & The finite bilinear identities for
$U_t$ over any field of characteristic zero, the isotropic-vector choices,
and the level classification.\\
\fn{Omnific.IntegralityGuard} \src{01, 05} & Pell rigidity with
\fn{Nat.sum_four_squares} (source 05's six-auxiliary form); source 01's
five-auxiliary form needs the three-square input separately. The
manuscript's imported number theory is not supplied by the ring package.\\
\fn{Omnific.Definability} \src{06, 07} & Units of $\mathcal A_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$
on the large-monomial side (a small-monomial Hahn implementation must reverse
the exponent sign), Pell rigidity by coefficient extension, the finite
Pell-divisibility argument, intersectivity of $\Lambda$ with every prime power
including $2$, $13$ and $17$ (a finite modular computation is no substitute),
the order-free system, the augmentation root detector (a statement about
augmented commutative algebras, independent of surreals), the multiplier
identity, and then the transport to $\Oz$ and $\Og$.\\
\fn{Omnific.Global} \src{01, 02, 05} & The small-cut theorem for support
bounds, common monomial divisibility and global clearing, kept separate and
with explicit smallness (universe) hypotheses; the upper bound for clearing
and the positive lower bound for idempotence are distinct lemmas.\\
\fn{Omnific.Fractions} \src{09} & Pullback algebra for a domain with a split
map to a field (the multiplier theorem and the B\'ezout lift); ordered
denominator ideals in a discretely ordered domain with integer-part division
(principal ideals and least members, covariance); then the normal-form
instantiation; last, the matrices $\mathsf F_{x,b}$ and focusing, with an
explicit smallness hypothesis on the input family.\\
\fn{Omnific.CurveRigidity} \src{C12, C13; also C10, C15} & First the elementary chain in a
set-sized Hahn field: the one-sided rings, the image condition
$\partial(\crA)\subseteq\crP$, the certificate \eqref{odg:cr:eq:universal} and the
ordered-group degree contradiction; the cubic certificate is a ring-normalization
identity. Then, separately, the geometric chain in three layers: a differential
support algebra with $\crP\cap\crO=0$; a one-variable function-field statement with a
logarithmic differential and a valuation centre; and the compactification and
Riemann--Roch input producing that differential. No proper-class scheme; the surreal
instances last.\\
\end{longtable}
\endgroup

Source 05 points to the normal-form bridge (\fn{docs/NORMAL_FORM_BRIDGE.md})
and the modules \fn{SmallNormalFormFieldEquiv.lean},
\fn{SmallNormalFormHahnEmbedding.lean},
\fn{SmallNormalFormWorkspace.lean} and \fn{SignSequenceWorkspace.lean}
under \fn{Surreal/Foundations/} as the transfer route to the actual
surreal carrier; all four exist at the placement commit (with
\fn{SignSequenceWorkspaceExponents.lean}), and their signatures must be
checked before use. The imported logical theorems (Tarski's quantifier
elimination, MRDP) remain separate dependencies; formalizing the retraction
does not formalize them. The transvection identity is finite bilinear
algebra with no dependence on birthday recursion, and the only hard
number-theoretic input to the guards is the three- or four-square theorem.
The order-free system needs only elementary local arithmetic of $\Lambda$ and
the finite Pell argument; the number-field extension also needs Dirichlet's
theorem and quadratic reciprocity, and the reconstruction of $\No$ the real
closedness of $\No$. Sources 06 and 07 propose the order followed in the row
\fn{Omnific.Definability}, keeping the class-sized instantiation separate from
the set-sized Hahn results; this is a proposal, not a report of completed
formalization. Sources C12 and C13 propose the order of the row
\fn{Omnific.CurveRigidity}, and source C10 notes that finite coordinate certificates
for arbitrary smooth curves would make the geometric chain checkable in the same way
as the cubic certificate (\cref{odg:cr:q:coefficients}); these too are proposals.
Source C15 proposes the same two stages, with the local description of logarithmic
forms in the geometric one, and notes that a conditional Lean theorem would not by
itself verify the instantiations. Source C16 proposes, for its own results, finite differential algebra first,
then the abstract valuation contradiction, then normalization of curves and
Riemann--Roch, and the proper-class application last (\cref{odg:sg:q:lean}); this too is a
proposal. Source C17 proposes a generic interface of a ring, a strict ideal and derivations
with $I\cap\mathcal O=0$, then its certificate and the characteristic-polynomial lemma as
finite algebra, then the Hahn instantiation and the surreal specialization
(\cref{odg:disc:sub:audit,odg:disc:q:lean}); this is a proposal as well.

\section{Questions and synthesis}\label{odg:sec:questions}

The sources list continuation problems that their proofs do not decide. None
of them claims that these problems are open in the literature, or that the
list is exhaustive. Two were settled inside the batch of manuscripts from
which sources 01, 02 and 05 come. In the merge of sources 06 and 07 one more
was answered and two were answered in part. Source 08 answers
\cref{odg:q:homogeneous} in part, and sources 08 and 09 add five questions
(\cref{odg:frac:sub:settled}). Sources C10--C14 answer \cref{odg:q:affine} for
smooth curves, answer \cref{odg:q:search,odg:q:homogeneous} in part, and add six
questions (\cref{odg:cr:sub:settled}); the three questions stay open, re-scoped.
Source C15 answers the first question of \cref{odg:cr:q:higher} in sufficient form and
adds a class of rigid varieties to \cref{odg:q:affine}; both stay open, re-scoped.
Source C16 answers the first two clauses of \cref{odg:cr:q:singular} and the curve part of
\cref{odg:q:affine}, answers the existence part of \cref{odg:q:search} for singular curves
over $\Og$ and in part over $\Oz$, and adds eight questions; the questions stay open,
re-scoped.
Source C17 answers \cref{odg:q:roots} for monic polynomials over $\Oz$ and $\Og$ with a
nonzero constant discriminant, answers the first clause of \cref{odg:cr:q:coefficients} in
dimension zero, and adds ten questions; both questions stay open, re-scoped.

\subsection{Questions settled by source 05}\label{odg:sub:settled}

\paragraph{Coprime exponents and $Y^2=X^3+1$ (source 01).} Source 01 asked
which curves such as $Y^2=X^3+1$ have genuinely infinite omnific points, and
proved rigidity of $x^m-y^n=c\ne0$ for positive $m,n$ with $\gcd(m,n)>1$
(\cref{odg:rem:powers}). \Cref{odg:thm:separated} of source 05 answers this
for every separated equation $ax^m+by^n=c$ with $abc\ne0$ and $m,n\ge2$; in
particular $Y^2=X^3+1$ and $x^2-y^3=1$ have only ordinary omnific points
(\cref{odg:cor:mordell}). The general part of source 01's question, a
criterion for arbitrary affine curves, was left open as \cref{odg:q:affine};
sources C10--C14 answer it for smooth curves (\cref{odg:cr:sub:settled}), and it
remains open for singular curves and in higher dimension. Status (batch 31): source
C16 settles the geometrically integral singular curves (\cref{odg:cr:sub:settled}); higher
dimension remains open.

\paragraph{Unimodular Fermat (sources 01 and 02).} Source 01 asked for the
classification of primitive or unimodular omnific solutions of
$X^n+Y^n=Z^n$ with nonzero coordinates, $n\ge3$; source 02 asked which
homogeneous systems admit nonzero omnific solutions generating $\Oz$.
\Cref{odg:thm:unimodularfermat,odg:cor:flt} of source 05 settle the
unimodular Fermat case: there are none. The primitive case (no common nonunit
divisor) remains open as \cref{odg:q:primitivefermat}, and source 02's
question for other homogeneous systems remains open as
\cref{odg:q:homogeneous}.

\subsection{Questions answered in the merge of sources 06 and 07}\label{odg:def:sub:settled}

\paragraph{An existential definition of $\Pi$ (source 01; source 07).} Source
01 asked for an existential definition of $\Pi$ over $\Oz$
(\cref{odg:q:existentialPi}), and source 07 closed by asking whether the purely
infinite ideal, or the graph of $\ct$, has a positive-existential definition.
Source 06 answers both: $\exists y\,(x^2=2y^2)$ defines $\Pi$, with one witness
and no parameters, in $\Oz$, in $\Og$ and in every ring
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ with $\sqrt2\in\Bbbk$ and
$\sqrt2\notin\Frac(\mathfrak o)$ (\cref{odg:def:thm:ideal}), and the graph of
$\ct$ is Diophantine (\cref{odg:def:thm:ctgraph}). Source 07's detector makes
the complement $\{\ct\ne0\}$ Diophantine too (\cref{odg:def:thm:detector}). The
second part of \cref{odg:q:existentialPi}, which fibers or subideals of $\Pi$
are Diophantine, remains open.

\paragraph{The least degree of a definition of $\Z$ (source 06).} Source 06
asked for the least total degree, and the best trade-off between degree and
witnesses, of a single integer polynomial defining $\Z$ in $\Oz$, offering a
quintic with seven witnesses. The guards of sources 01 and 05
(\cref{odg:thm:guard,odg:thm:standarddef}), which source 06 did not see, reach
degree four with five and six witnesses. This answers the question in part;
the least degree and the trade-off remain open and are merged into
\cref{odg:q:guard}, together with source 06's question on a single Gaussian
polynomial and source 07's on better quantifier and degree bounds.

\subsection{Questions in the merge of sources 08 and 09}\label{odg:frac:sub:settled}

\paragraph{Primitive homogeneous solutions (source 02).} Source 02 asked which
homogeneous systems admit a nonzero omnific solution whose coordinates
generate $\Oz$ (\cref{odg:q:homogeneous}). Source 08's rational-curve criterion
answers it in part (\cref{odg:frac:cor:curves,odg:frac:cor:homogeneous}). If
the projective zero locus contains a curve $[p_0:\cdots:p_n]$ given by real
polynomials with gcd one and rational specialization at $T=0$, then after a real
normalization every $0\ne t\in\Pi$ gives a unimodular omnific solution
$(p_0(t),\ldots,p_n(t))$. Along such a curve, the points with primitive
coordinates are exactly those of rational specialization. Points off rational
curves and the dependence on the integral model remain open, so the question
stays open, re-scoped. Neither source addresses
\cref{odg:q:primitivefermat}: for $n\ge3$ the Fermat curve has positive genus
and contains no nonconstant rational curve, so the criterion does not apply to
it.

\paragraph{New questions (sources 08 and 09).} Sources 08 and 09 end with
questions about denominator ideals beyond one parameter, the extent of the
unimodular region, orbits outside it, several parameters and other scale
extensions. They are
\cref{odg:frac:q:ideals,odg:frac:q:region,odg:frac:q:orbits,odg:frac:q:parameters,odg:frac:q:scales}
below; source 08's first question and source 09's second are merged into
\cref{odg:frac:q:ideals}. The sources present them as questions of their
articles, not as certified open problems.

\subsection{Questions in the merge of sources C10--C15}\label{odg:cr:sub:settled}

\paragraph{Rigid affine varieties and curves (sources 01, 02, 05).} All five
sources answer the second part of \cref{odg:q:affine} for smooth geometrically
integral curves: over any field of characteristic zero and any nontrivial
ordered exponent group, a smooth affine curve has nonconstant points over
the one-sided Hahn ring exactly when
it is the affine line (\cref{odg:cr:thm:affine}). The trivial exponent
group gives only the coefficient field and is rigid for every target.
For an affine integral model with smooth geometrically integral real fiber, $\mathcal C(\Oz)=\mathcal C(\Z)$ unless that real fiber is an
affine line, in which case every fiber of $\ct$ over an integer point is a copy of
$\Pi$ (\cref{odg:cr:cor:omnific}). The named test $Y^2=X^3+aX+b$ is settled
completely: only ordinary solutions when $4a^3+27b^2\ne0$
(\cref{odg:cr:cor:weierstrass}, also for general Weierstrass equations,
\cref{odg:cr:cor:generalweierstrass}) and injective polynomial families otherwise
(\cref{odg:cr:prop:singular}). For the first part, \cref{odg:cr:thm:descent}
reduces the question for every separated model to the rigidity of its complex fiber
over the rings $\mathcal A_\C(\Gamma)$, and the part supplies rigid affine varieties
in every dimension: locally closed subschemes and quasi-finite covers of abelian and
semiabelian varieties (\cref{odg:cr:thm:abelian,odg:cr:thm:quasifinite}). Source C15
adds descent over every coefficient ring (\cref{odg:log:thm:descent}) and the
complements of simple normal-crossings divisors whose logarithmic symmetric differentials
separate tangent directions (\cref{odg:log:cor:criterion,odg:log:cor:omnific}). The
question stays open for singular curves and for general affine varieties of higher
dimension; its refined forms are \cref{odg:cr:q:singular,odg:cr:q:higher}. Status
(batch 31): source C16 settles the curve part; see the paragraph on singular curves
below.

\paragraph{Effective finite-support discovery (source 05).} For smooth affine curves
the existence part of \cref{odg:q:search} is answered: a nonordinary omnific point
exists if and only if one with finite supports exists, and then one with the same
constant term (\cref{odg:cr:cor:polywitness}). In the affine-line case integer
polynomial arcs through every integer point and a finite congruence test describe
the points once an affine-line isomorphism and its inverse are supplied
(\cref{odg:cr:lem:arcs,odg:cr:prop:congruence}). The question stays open beyond
smooth curves, and \cref{odg:cr:cor:polywitness} is not an algorithm for the ordinary
points. Status (batch 31): source C16 extends the answer to singular curves in part
(paragraph on singular curves below).

\paragraph{Primitive homogeneous solutions (source 02).} For projective varieties
whose complex fiber is rigid, in particular smooth projective curves of positive
genus and abelian varieties, every unimodular omnific solution is an ordinary integer
tuple, and a coordinate ideal is invertible exactly when it is principal, exactly
when the point is rational (\cref{odg:cr:thm:unimodular,odg:cr:thm:ozideal,odg:cr:cor:projectiveQ}).
For these targets the answer does not depend on the integral model beyond the fixed
projective embedding: the unimodular points are the rational points. With
\cref{odg:frac:cor:homogeneous} for rational curves, this answers
\cref{odg:q:homogeneous} for two opposite classes of varieties; other varieties, and
primitive tuples that are not unimodular, remain open
(\cref{odg:q:primitivefermat,odg:cr:q:primitive}).

\paragraph{Logarithmic forms in higher dimension (source C12; source C15).} Source C12
asked which hypotheses on logarithmic one-forms force all $\crA$-points of a smooth
affine variety with a smooth normal-crossings compactification to be constant, and noted
that in higher dimension one form need not span the cotangent space and that the centre
of an arbitrary-rank valuation must be handled. Source C15, which did not see source
C12, answers this in sufficient form in every dimension, without assuming the variety
affine: if the global logarithmic symmetric differentials separate tangent directions
after every field extension, in particular if the global logarithmic one-forms span the
cotangent space at every point, then $X(\crA)=X(\Bbbk)$
(\cref{odg:log:thm:annihilation,odg:log:cor:criterion}); its local computation handles
the centre of an arbitrary valuation ring and the boundary parameters
(\cref{odg:log:lem:valuation}). Which hypotheses are necessary, a classification of the
rigid complements, the converse from the absence of polynomial curves and the question of
source C10 on surviving tangent directions remain open, so \cref{odg:cr:q:higher} stays
open, re-scoped.

\paragraph{New questions (sources C10--C14).} The sources' continuation questions
are merged into six. Singular curves (all five sources) are
\cref{odg:cr:q:singular}; rigidity beyond cotangent generation (C11, C13, C14) is
\cref{odg:cr:q:beyond}; higher-dimensional varieties (C10's discussion, C12's
logarithmic question and C13's polynomial-arc question) are \cref{odg:cr:q:higher};
primitive versus unimodular tuples (C12, C13, C14, and the corresponding remarks of
C10 and C11) are \cref{odg:cr:q:primitive}; positive characteristic (C11, and C10's
suggestion of higher derivations) is \cref{odg:cr:q:charp}; omnific coefficients,
effective certificates and other coefficient systems (C12, and C10's remarks) are
\cref{odg:cr:q:coefficients}. Source C15's four closing questions fall under these:
singular targets under \cref{odg:cr:q:singular}; rigidity beyond differential positivity,
and whether the absence of nonconstant maps from $\Aff^1$ implies rigidity, under
\cref{odg:cr:q:higher} (for proper varieties see also \cref{odg:cr:q:beyond}); positive
characteristic under \cref{odg:cr:q:charp}; and minimal certificates for curves given by
several equations under \cref{odg:cr:q:coefficients}. None of the sources presents these
as known open problems of the literature.

\paragraph{Singular curves (source C16).} Source C16 answers the first two clauses of
\cref{odg:cr:q:singular}. Uniformly in $\Gamma\ne0$ and over every field of characteristic
zero, a geometrically integral affine curve has only constant $\crA$-points unless its
normalization is $\Aff^1$ (\cref{odg:sg:thm:main}); the answer is expressed through the
normalization and its points at infinity (genus zero and exactly one boundary point), and
the conductor supplies finite certificates (\cref{odg:sg:thm:conductor,odg:sg:cor:jetbound}).
Over an algebraically closed field this extends to all affine schemes of dimension at
most one, reducible or not (\cref{odg:sg:thm:allcurves}). The third clause, which finite
normalization maps admit nonconstant points without a lift, stays open as
\cref{odg:sg:q:lifting}, so \cref{odg:cr:q:singular} stays open, re-scoped. For
\cref{odg:q:affine} this settles the curve part: every omnific point of a model whose real
fiber is geometrically integral is ordinary unless the normalization of that fiber is
$\Aff^1_\R$, and every Gaussian omnific point of a model whose complex fiber is
geometrically integral is ordinary unless that normalization is $\Aff^1_\C$
(\cref{odg:sg:thm:omnific}); conversely nonordinary Gaussian points exist exactly when in
addition there is a Gaussian integer point (\cref{odg:sg:cor:gaussian}), and nonordinary
omnific points exist when some integer point has a real preimage under the normalization
(\cref{odg:sg:prop:arithfamily}), in particular when it is nonsingular. For
\cref{odg:q:search} it answers the existence part for singular curves over $\Og$, and
over $\Oz$ at integer points with a real normalization preimage; the case of integer
points whose preimages are all nonreal, such as an isolated real node, stays open
(\cref{odg:sg:q:real}). For \cref{odg:cr:q:charp} it adds a necessary condition in every
characteristic, one boundary point (\cref{odg:sg:prop:oneplace}); for
\cref{odg:cr:q:coefficients} it gives finite derivative-order certificates for every
rigid curve, without optimality or an algorithm (\cref{odg:sg:q:order,odg:sg:q:engine}).

\paragraph{New questions (source C16).} Its eight questions are printed as stated:
lifting through the normalization (\cref{odg:sg:q:lifting}, the third clause of
\cref{odg:cr:q:singular}), real singular fibers (\cref{odg:sg:q:real}), optimal
certificates and a certificate-producing procedure (\cref{odg:sg:q:order,odg:sg:q:engine},
refining \cref{odg:cr:q:coefficients}), positive characteristic (\cref{odg:sg:q:charp},
refining \cref{odg:cr:q:charp}), normalization invariance in higher dimension
(\cref{odg:sg:q:birational}), images of the constant term (\cref{odg:sg:q:images}) and
formalization (\cref{odg:sg:q:lean}). Source C16 does not present them as known open
problems of the literature.

\paragraph{Roots and coefficients (source C17).} For a monic $P\in\Oz[T]$ with
$\Disc(P)\in\Z\setminus\{0\}$, source C17 answers \cref{odg:q:roots}: $P=\overline P(T-h)$
with $h=-(a_1-\ct a_1)/n\in\Pi$, so the omnific roots of $P$ are $h+c$ for the integer roots
$c$ of $\overline P$, and the finite coefficient test \eqref{odg:disc:eq:test} decides
whether the hypothesis holds, relative to exact coefficient arithmetic
(\cref{odg:disc:thm:translation,odg:disc:cor:roots}); the same holds over $\Og$. This
complements \cref{odg:thm:quadraticformula} and explains \cref{odg:ex:hensel}, whose
discriminant $1+4\omega$ is not constant (\cref{odg:disc:rem:comparison}). Nonconstant
discriminants, zero discriminants and nonmonic polynomials stay open, and so does the
decidability clause for general support classes. For the first clause of
\cref{odg:cr:q:coefficients}, a nonzero constant discriminant is a nondegeneracy condition
that recovers rigidity for coefficients varying at a surreal scale in dimension zero, over
every $\crA$ and $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$; units of finite \'etale algebras
and normal matrices follow the same pattern
(\cref{odg:disc:thm:units,odg:disc:thm:matrix}). Curves and higher-dimensional varieties
with nonconstant coefficients stay open (\cref{odg:cr:sub:boundaries}).

\paragraph{New questions (source C17).} Its ten questions are printed as stated: full and
arithmetic \'etale descent (\cref{odg:disc:q:etale,odg:disc:q:arithmetic}), projective modules
and nonnormal spectral data (\cref{odg:disc:q:picard}), sharp certificates
(\cref{odg:disc:q:certificate}), nonconstant discriminants (\cref{odg:disc:q:defect},
refining \cref{odg:q:roots}), positive characteristic (\cref{odg:disc:q:charp}), restricted
support rings (\cref{odg:disc:q:restricted}), several configurations
(\cref{odg:disc:q:several}), polynomial dynamics (\cref{odg:disc:q:dynamics}) and
formalization (\cref{odg:disc:q:lean}). Source C17 does not present them as known open
problems of the literature.

\subsection{Open questions}

\begin{question}[Rigid affine varieties and curves \src{01, 02, 05}; smooth curves answered by \src{C10--C14}]\label{odg:q:affine}
For which affine varieties $X$ over $\Z$ does $X(\Oz)=X(\Z)$ hold? For which
affine curves does every omnific point have ordinary coordinates? A natural
next test is $Y^2=X^3+aX+b$ with $a\ne0$, outside
\cref{odg:thm:separated}.
\end{question}
\Cref{odg:thm:decomposable,odg:thm:binary,odg:thm:separated} answer this for
nonzero decomposable levels with trivial common kernel and for separated
equations, \cref{odg:thm:bounded,odg:thm:infinity} for bounded loci and
systems with no real point at infinity, and \cref{odg:thm:quadclassification}
and polynomial arcs give large opposing classes. A satisfactory criterion
should separate polynomial parametrization, torus rigidity and genuinely
algebraic branches \src{01}; it needs either a new degree inequality or a
structural analysis of possible supports \src{05}. No claim is made that all
other fibers are rigid, or that all nontrivial fibers come from real
polynomial curves \src{02}. The report
\fn{docs/surreal/omnific-groups-and-lattices} answers the question for closed
subgroup schemes of $\GL_N$ over $\Z$ (and over $\Z[i]$ for $\Og$). Sources
C10--C14 answer the second part for smooth geometrically integral curves and
settle the test equation (\cref{odg:cr:sub:settled}). An affine model
with smooth geometrically integral real fiber can have nonordinary omnific
points only when that real fiber is the affine line; in that case such
points exist precisely when the model has an ordinary integer point.
This is a condition on the real fiber, not an assertion that the model is
$\Aff^1_\Z$. Nonsingular Weierstrass equations have only ordinary
solutions, while the singular cases have the displayed polynomial families. For smooth curves the criterion is exactly the separation into polynomial
parametrization, torus rigidity and genuinely algebraic branches asked for above,
and source 02's caution is resolved: every nontrivial fiber of a smooth affine curve
comes from a real polynomial curve (\cref{odg:cr:cor:omnific,odg:cr:cor:polywitness}).
Source C15 adds a class in every dimension: complements of normal-crossings divisors
whose logarithmic symmetric differentials separate tangent directions
(\cref{odg:log:cor:omnific}). Singular curves and general affine varieties of higher
dimension remain open (\cref{odg:cr:q:singular,odg:cr:q:higher}). Status (batch 31):
source C16 settles the curve part for geometrically integral curves, and over the Gaussian
ring completely (\cref{odg:cr:sub:settled}); over $\Oz$ the case of an exceptional curve
whose integer points all lack a real normalization preimage remains (\cref{odg:sg:q:real}).
General affine varieties of higher dimension remain open.

\begin{question}[Primitive Fermat triples \src{01, 05}]\label{odg:q:primitivefermat}
For $n\ge3$, can the unit-ideal hypothesis of
\cref{odg:thm:unimodularfermat} be weakened to ``no common nonunit
divisor''? That is, are there primitive, non-unimodular omnific solutions of
$X^n+Y^n=Z^n$ with nonzero coordinates?
\end{question}
The answer cannot be read off from ordinary integer intuition; it needs an
explicit relation between common divisors, ideal generation and the
applicable factorization theory. \Cref{odg:thm:unimodularfermat} deliberately
avoids any such relation, and the binary nonzero-fiber theorem does not
directly answer a three-variable homogeneous question \src{01}.

\begin{question}[Primitive homogeneous solutions \src{02}]\label{odg:q:homogeneous}
Which homogeneous systems admit a nonzero omnific solution whose coordinates
generate $\Oz$, and how does this depend on the chosen integral model of the
projective variety?
\end{question}
The real-solvability criterion \cref{odg:thm:cone} omits unimodularity, and
\cref{odg:thm:realray} exhibits the gap. A gcd normalization cannot be
assumed, by \cref{odg:thm:nogcd}. Fermat equations of exponent at least
three have none with all coordinates nonzero (\cref{odg:cor:flt}), although
$(1,0,1)$ is a unimodular solution with a zero coordinate; the exponent-two Pythagorean and
indefinite quadratic cases have many (\cref{odg:prop:pythagoras},
\eqref{odg:eq:hyperunimodular}). Source 08 answers the question in part
(\cref{odg:frac:sub:settled}): a rational curve of real polynomials with gcd one
in the zero locus gives unimodular omnific solutions exactly when its
specialization at $T=0$ is rational (\cref{odg:frac:cor:homogeneous}). Sources
C10--C14 answer it in part at the opposite extreme (\cref{odg:cr:sub:settled}): on a
projective variety whose complex fiber is rigid, such as a smooth curve of positive
genus or an abelian variety, the unimodular omnific solutions are exactly the
ordinary unimodular ones, and in a fixed embedding the points with such solutions are
the rational points (\cref{odg:cr:thm:unimodular,odg:cr:cor:projectiveQ}). Other
varieties, and the dependence on the integral model in general, remain open.

\begin{question}[An existential definition of $\Pi$ \src{01}; first part answered by \src{06}]\label{odg:q:existentialPi}
Is there an existential definition of $\Pi$ over $\Oz$ with ordinary integer
parameters? More generally, which fibers or subideals of $\Pi$ are
Diophantine? Retraction stability is necessary but far from sufficient.
\end{question}
The first question is answered: $\exists y\,(x^2=2y^2)$ defines $\Pi$
(\cref{odg:def:thm:ideal}, \cref{odg:def:sub:settled}). The second remains
open. Retraction stability is still necessary
(\cref{odg:def:thm:collapse}): a Diophantine set containing a nonzero element
of $\Pi$ contains $0$.

\begin{question}[Complexity of the integrality guard \src{01, 06, 07}]\label{odg:q:guard}
Can the number of auxiliary variables be reduced below five, with a single
quartic equation, or can a meaningful lower bound be proved? What is the least
total degree of a single integer polynomial defining $\Z$ in $\Oz$, and what
is the best trade-off between degree and witnesses \src{06}? Can the Gaussian
three-equation system of \cref{odg:def:thm:constants} be replaced by a single
polynomial of controlled degree \src{06}, and can its quantifier and degree
bounds be improved without an order \src{07}? The present constructions give
explicit upper bounds only (five in \cref{odg:thm:guard}, six in
\cref{odg:thm:standarddef}, seven in the quintic
\eqref{odg:def:eq:quintic}); other rigid arithmetic families might serve as
finite bounds.
\end{question}
Any Gaussian sum-of-squares replacement must address isotropy
($1^2+i^2=0$) rather than assume it away \src{06}. The quantifier-free
obstruction \cref{odg:def:prop:notqf} is only a first lower bound on logical
complexity; it does not make any of the present counts optimal.

\begin{question}[Roots with omnific coefficients \src{01, 02, 05}]\label{odg:q:roots}
For polynomials with finitely represented omnific coefficients, which root
questions in a chosen support class are decidable? Can one give tractable
necessary and sufficient support criteria for a root of a polynomial in
$\Oz[T]$ to be omnific?
\end{question}
\Cref{odg:thm:quadraticformula} reduces the monic quadratic case to square
membership, \cref{odg:prop:binomial} settles two-term binomials, and
\cref{odg:ex:hensel} rules out a naive lifting theorem; multiterm
cancellation needs separate analysis. Any effective claim must specify the
coefficients, the exponent representation, and whether workspace enlargement
is permitted. An unrestricted algorithm for ordinary-coefficient affine
existence would contradict \cref{odg:cor:H10}. Status (batch 33): for monic
polynomials with a discriminant in $\Z\setminus\{0\}$ (in $\Z[i]\setminus\{0\}$ over $\Og$)
source C17 gives a complete answer: the omnific roots are $h+c$ for the integer roots $c$
of $\overline P$, with $h$ read off the second coefficient
(\cref{odg:disc:cor:roots,odg:disc:eq:test}). The question stays open for other
polynomials (\cref{odg:cr:sub:settled}).

\begin{question}[The exact size boundary \src{05}]\label{odg:q:size}
Which set-sized exponent groups or universe-relative constructions admit
analogues of the common-monomial and set-sized quotient theorems?
\end{question}
The sibling report \fn{docs/surreal/set-sized-quotients-of-omnific-integers}
contains cardinal-threshold results on this question for specific set-sized
integer parts.

\begin{question}[Effective finite-support discovery \src{05}]\label{odg:q:search}
How far can a search for polynomial arcs and exact B\'ezout certificates,
suggested by \cref{odg:thm:specialization}, go before transfinite support
constructions are needed? It can find witnesses and expose obstructions, but
it cannot become a complete decision procedure for integer-coefficient
systems, by \cref{odg:cor:H10}.
\end{question}
For smooth affine curves the existence part is answered by sources C12 and C13: a
nonordinary omnific point exists exactly when one of finite support with the same
constant term exists (\cref{odg:cr:cor:polywitness}), and source C14 makes the
affine-line case constructive once a parametrization is supplied
(\cref{odg:cr:lem:arcs,odg:cr:prop:congruence}). Beyond smooth curves the question
stays open (\cref{odg:cr:sub:settled}). Status (batch 31): source C16 answers the existence part
for geometrically integral singular curves over $\Og$
(\cref{odg:sg:cor:gaussian}) and over $\Oz$ at integer points with a real normalization
preimage (\cref{odg:sg:prop:arithfamily}); the remaining real case is \cref{odg:sg:q:real},
and higher dimension stays open.

\begin{question}[Coefficient recovery of lower complexity \src{06}]\label{odg:def:q:coeffpe}
Can the canonical $\R$ or $\C$ in the interpreted fraction field be recovered
with weaker quantifier complexity than the multiplier formula of
\cref{odg:def:cor:internal}, keeping the integer-part ring as the only named
structure?
\end{question}
The pure field and the field with its integer-part predicate must be
distinguished in formulating this. The present formulas are first order in
the interpreted ring structure; they give no Diophantine definition of $\Z$ in
$\Q$ and no solution of Hilbert's tenth problem over $\Q$ \src{06}.

\begin{question}[Which real structure must be added? \src{06}]\label{odg:def:q:realform}
What additional natural structure on $\Og$ rules out the coefficient-fixing
twists of \cref{odg:def:lem:twist} and determines the real omnific subring,
without simply naming conjugation or $\Oz$?
\end{question}
Naming all complex constants does not suffice
(\cref{odg:def:thm:norealaxis}); naming a suitable involution does, but
gives the real structure explicitly rather than reconstructing it \src{06}.
Status (batch 32): further negative information, not an answer. By
\replabel{saut:fs:thm:main-count} of
\fn{docs/surcomplex/surcomplex-field-automorphisms}, the involutions of $\No[i]$
that preserve the valuation, Hahn summation and $\Og$ fall into
$2^{2^{\aleph_0}}$ conjugacy types, with representatives whose fixed fields are
pairwise nonisomorphic and whose fixed rings are integer parts; so these
structures alone do not single out the real omnific subring, even up to
isomorphism.
Status (batch 34): ordinary arithmetic does not separate these rings either.
Each integer-part ring $R_F$ of that report, for a set-sized real closed
field $F$ (with $\Oz=R_{\R}$), has $R_F/nR_F\cong\Z/n\Z$ for every positive ordinary
integer $n$, with every finite
quotient factoring through $\ct$, and a system of integer polynomial
equations is solvable in $R_F$ exactly when it is solvable in $\Z$
(\replabel{saut:gr:prop:finite}, \replabel{saut:gr:prop:dio}). This is
further negative information, not an answer, and not elementary equivalence.

\begin{question}[Infinite algebraic extensions \src{07}]\label{odg:def:q:algebraic}
Which coefficient subrings of algebraic extensions of $\Q$ of infinite degree
admit a root-free intersective polynomial, or another positive-existential
certificate of a nonzero constant, so that
\cref{odg:def:thm:numberfield} extends to them?
\end{question}
No polynomial of the shape used in \cref{odg:def:lem:tailored} is root-free in
a field containing all algebraic numbers, so a different certificate would be
needed there \src{07}.

\begin{question}[Denominator ideals beyond one parameter \src{08, 09}]\label{odg:frac:q:ideals}
Which surreal tuples have denominator ideals generated by a set? Must such an
ideal be principal, or can a finitely or set-generated nonprincipal denominator
ideal occur \src{08}? For arbitrary $x\in\No$, does a principal $\Den(x)$ force a
unimodular presentation; which nonprincipal denominator ideals occur outside
$\Vreg$; and can a primitive presentation exist there without a B\'ezout
identity \src{09}?
\end{question}
\Cref{odg:frac:thm:main,odg:frac:thm:dichotomy} answer this for tuples in
$\R(t)$ and on $\Vreg$ only; they do not classify $\Den(x)$ for all $x\in\No$.

\begin{question}[The unimodular region \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:q:region}
Which points of $\mathbb P^1(\No)$ lie in $\Vreg$? Give a support-theoretic
criterion for a fraction to admit a unimodular presentation over $\BR$, or an
explicit obstruction.
\end{question}
Every rational function of one parameter lies in $\Vreg$, even when its normal
form has infinitely many terms. The dense fibers do not imply
$\Vreg=\mathbb P^1(\No)$ \src{09}.

\begin{question}[Orbits outside the region \src{09}]\label{odg:frac:q:orbits}
By \cref{odg:frac:cor:dense}, every $\Kcong$-orbit is dense, including any orbit
outside $\Vreg$. What complete invariants distinguish such orbits, if they exist?
\end{question}
Within $\Vreg$ the invariant is exactly one real projective point
(\cref{odg:frac:thm:orbits}).

\begin{question}[Several parameters \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:q:parameters}
Is there a similarly explicit classification for fractions in
$\R(t_1,\ldots,t_k)$ with suitably independent purely infinite parameters? What
local or projective data replace the single specialization at zero?
\end{question}
The proof does not repeat directly: a multivariate gcd of one does not give a
B\'ezout identity, as $T_1,T_2$ in $\R[T_1,T_2]$ show, and
\cref{odg:frac:thm:pullback} needs unimodularity \src{08}.

\begin{question}[Other scale extensions \src{08}]\label{odg:frac:q:scales}
For inclusions of more general set-sized omnific Hahn models, which extensions
commute with denominator ideals, and can their relation defects be expressed in
terms of the newly admitted support scales?
\end{question}
\Cref{odg:frac:thm:scale,odg:frac:prop:tor} settle one explicit case; they give
no general flatness or normalization theorem for Hahn integer parts \src{08}.

\begin{question}[Singular curves \src{C10, C11, C12, C13, C14, C15}]\label{odg:cr:q:singular}
Which integral singular affine curves over $\Bbbk$ have only constant
$\crA$-points, uniformly in $\Gamma$? Can the answer be expressed through the
normalization, the conductor and the points at infinity, and which finite
normalization maps admit nonconstant points downstairs without a lift over the same
ring?
\end{question}
The node and the cusp show that ``only the affine line'' is no longer the right
statement. A normalization lift cannot simply be assumed from integrality,
and $\crA$ need not be normal in its own fraction field (\cref{odg:cr:sub:singular}) \src{C10, C11, C13, C14}. Finite or
quasi-finite maps to rigid targets are covered (\cref{odg:cr:thm:quasifinite}), but
not every singular curve has one \src{C14}. Source C12 proposes a route through
logarithmic differentials on the normalization whose pullbacks are regular on the
singular curve, with the conductor controlling the descent; this is a proposal, not a
proved criterion. Source C15 asks the same question for every exponent group, naming the
identifications of branches among the data; the quasi-finite criterion settles some
singular targets but gives no classification \src{C15}. Status (batch 31): source C16
answers the first two clauses for geometrically integral curves: uniformly in
$\Gamma\ne0$, only constant points exist unless the normalization is $\Aff^1$, a condition
on the genus and the points at infinity of the normalization, and a conductor gives finite
certificates (\cref{odg:sg:thm:main,odg:sg:thm:conductor}). The third clause stays open as
\cref{odg:sg:q:lifting}; the question stays open, re-scoped.

\begin{question}[Beyond cotangent generation \src{C11, C13, C14}]\label{odg:cr:q:beyond}
For which smooth proper varieties does $X(\crA)=X(\Bbbk)$ hold for every $\Gamma$?
Must a smooth proper complex variety containing no rational curves satisfy
$X(\BC)=X(\C)$ \src{C11, C13}? Which positivity conditions on the cotangent bundle
suffice, and what obstruction is encoded by the locus where global forms fail to
generate \src{C11, C14}?
\end{question}
Global generation of $\Omega^1$, or tangent detection by symmetric differentials, is
sufficient (\cref{odg:cr:thm:proper,odg:cr:thm:symmetric}) and not claimed necessary;
no implication from the absence of rational curves, from nefness, bigness or general
type is proved, and varieties without global one-forms are not classified
\src{C11, C13, C14}.

\begin{question}[Affine varieties of higher dimension \src{C10, C12, C13, C15}; logarithmic sufficient condition \src{C15}]\label{odg:cr:q:higher}
For a smooth affine variety with a smooth normal-crossings compactification, which
hypotheses on logarithmic one-forms force all $\crA$-points to be constant
\src{C12}? For an affine complex variety $X$, does every nonconstant $\BC$-point force
a nonconstant polynomial arc $\Aff^1\to X$ \src{C13}? Which of the Euler tangent
directions allowed by the global forms, and by the fibers of an Albanese map, arise
from Hahn points \src{C10}? For which smooth varieties does the absence of nonconstant
morphisms $\Aff^1\to X$ imply $X(\crA)=X(\Bbbk)$, and which additional hypothesis bridges
that condition and tangent separation by logarithmic symmetric differentials \src{C15}?
\end{question}
For smooth curves the first two questions have affirmative answers
(\cref{odg:cr:thm:logroute,odg:cr:thm:affine}). In every dimension source C15 answers the
first question in sufficient form: tangent separation by global logarithmic symmetric
differentials, in particular spanning by global logarithmic one-forms, forces every
$\crA$-point to be constant (\cref{odg:log:cor:criterion}); necessary conditions and a
classification remain open (\cref{odg:cr:sub:settled}).
In higher dimension one nonzero logarithmic form need not span the cotangent space,
the centre of an arbitrary-rank valuation must be handled, and no equivalence with
uniruledness, rational connectedness or the existence of polynomial curves is
asserted \src{C10, C12}. Source C15's local computation handles the centre of an
arbitrary valuation ring (\cref{odg:log:lem:valuation}); it asserts no equivalence
between rigidity and the absence of nonconstant maps from $\Aff^1$, and such an
equivalence may not be inferred from the classification of curves \src{C15}. If $\Gamma\ne0$ and $X(\Bbbk)\ne\varnothing$, then
$X\times\Aff^1$ is not rigid: for $x_0\in X(\Bbbk)$ and $\gamma>0$,
$(x_0,\omega^\gamma)$ is a nonconstant point \src{C12}.
The point hypothesis matters: constant extraction gives
$X(\crA)\to X(\Bbbk)$, so if the latter is empty, both the source
and the product's point set are empty and the equality holds vacuously.

\begin{question}[Primitive versus unimodular \src{C12, C13, C14; also C10, C11}]\label{odg:cr:q:primitive}
For positive-genus projective curves over $\Z$, can the unit-ideal hypothesis of
\cref{odg:cr:cor:projectiveQ} be weakened to the absence of a common nonunit divisor in
$\Oz$? Can a nonrational point of such a curve be represented by a primitive omnific
tuple whose coordinate ideal is not invertible \src{C12}?
\end{question}
This contains \cref{odg:q:primitivefermat}. \Cref{odg:cr:thm:ozideal} decides
invertibility and principality, not the existence of a common nonunit divisor in a
noninvertible ideal; the tuple of \cref{odg:cr:ex:denominator} has one and does not
answer the question \src{C12}. No gcd or B\'ezout property of $\Oz$ may be substituted
for an explicit unit-ideal identity \src{C10, C11, C13, C14}.

\begin{question}[Positive characteristic \src{C11; also C10, C15}]\label{odg:cr:q:charp}
For which ordered groups $\Gamma$, fields $\Bbbk$ of positive characteristic and smooth
curves do nonconstant $\crA$-points exist? Can the Artin--Schreier mechanism of
\cref{odg:cr:prop:char2} be characterized by ramification and the $p$-divisible part
of $\Gamma$ \src{C11}, and can iterative higher derivations replace the Euler
derivations \src{C10}?
\end{question}
\Cref{odg:cr:prop:char2,odg:cr:prop:charp} show that both the curve classification and
the squarefree theorem fail as stated in positive characteristic
(\cref{odg:cr:sub:charp}): a characteristic-free version of the theorems is false, not
merely unproved. Source C15 asks the same question, how the $p$-divisibility of
$\Gamma$ and the inseparable geometry of the target control the points, and whether
higher or iterative differential operators can replace the Euler family \src{C15}. Status (batch 31): source C16 proves in every characteristic that a curve with a
nonconstant point has exactly one boundary point in its normalization
(\cref{odg:sg:prop:oneplace}); its refinement is \cref{odg:sg:q:charp}.

\begin{question}[Omnific coefficients and effective certificates \src{C12; also C10, C15}]\label{odg:cr:q:coefficients}
For equations with genuinely omnific coefficients, which support and nondegeneracy
conditions recover a rigidity theorem \src{C12}? For an arbitrary smooth curve, can
the global-form argument be turned into a finite coordinate certificate like
\cref{odg:cr:prop:cubic} with useful complexity bounds \src{C10}, and can the support
obstruction be combined with effective methods for integral points without losing
explicit bounds \src{C12}? Which other pairs of a ring and a valuation ring in one
field satisfy \eqref{odg:cr:eq:data} \src{C10}?
\end{question}
With nonconstant coefficients even nonsingular cubics have nonordinary omnific points
(\cref{odg:cr:sub:boundaries}); these are separate from the proved constant-coefficient
statements \src{C12}. A finite certificate would formalize the mechanism; it would not
by itself enumerate integer points \src{C10}. Source C15 asks for such a certificate of the
logarithmic obstruction, extracted effectively, for a smooth affine curve presented by
several equations, so that a checker need not reconstruct the geometry \src{C15}. Status
(batch 31): source C16 gives finite certificates for every rigid curve, smooth or singular,
with an explicit derivative-order bound (\cref{odg:sg:thm:conductor,odg:sg:cor:jetbound,odg:sg:thm:example});
optimal bounds and a certificate-producing procedure are \cref{odg:sg:q:order,odg:sg:q:engine}.
Status (batch 33): for the first clause source C17 gives a rigidity theorem in dimension zero:
a monic univariate polynomial over $\crA$ or $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ whose
discriminant is a nonzero constant is a purely infinite translate of its constant-term
polynomial (\cref{odg:disc:thm:translation}), with a universal finite certificate
(\cref{odg:disc:lem:certificate}); the batch-31 note above concerns only the certificate
clause. Curves and varieties with nonconstant coefficients stay open.

The next eight questions are those of source C16, printed as it states them; it presents
them as continuations of its article, not as recognized open problems of the literature.

\begin{question}[Exact normalization lifting \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:q:lifting}
If $C$ has normalization $\Aff^1_\Bbbk$, does every nonconstant $\crA$-point of $C$ lift to an
$\crA$-point of the normalization? If not, what are the smallest explicit singular curve and
ordered group exhibiting the failure?
\end{question}
This is the third clause of \cref{odg:cr:q:singular}, which \cref{odg:sg:thm:main} leaves
open. The conductor proof controls powers only after multiplication. A first test is a
rational curve with two distinct parameter values identified at a node; a positive answer
must deal with the non-normality of \cref{odg:cr:sub:singular} rather than assume it away
\src{C16}.

\begin{question}[Real singular arithmetic fibers \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:q:real}
For a real curve with normalization $\Aff^1_\R$, which ordinary integer points are constant
terms of nonordinary omnific points? Is a real preimage under the normalization necessary,
as well as sufficient?
\end{question}
This is the remaining real--Gaussian asymmetry of \cref{odg:sg:cor:gaussian}. A concrete
test is the isolated real node of $Y^2=X^2(X-1)$ at $(0,0)$: the normalization is
$X=T^2+1$, $Y=T(T^2+1)$, the preimages of the origin are $T=\pm i$, and the real
polynomial families of \cref{odg:sg:prop:arithfamily} do not reach that fiber, while the
complex ones do \src{C16}.

\begin{question}[Optimal certificate complexity \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:q:order}
How small can the derivative order of \eqref{odg:sg:eq:jetbound} be made by optimizing the
conductor, the presentation of $\alpha$ and the regular differential? Can the least order be
bounded by the genus, the conductor length and the pole semigroup at infinity alone?
\end{question}
The seventh-order certificate of \cref{odg:sg:thm:example} improves the direct bound eleven;
no optimality is claimed. The identity is an effective target for symbolic algebra and
formal verification \src{C16}. This refines the certificate part of
\cref{odg:cr:q:coefficients}.

\begin{question}[A constructive curve-rigidity procedure \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:q:engine}
Can normalization, conductor computation and the construction of a regular differential be
combined into a certificate-producing procedure for arbitrary affine curve presentations
over effective fields of characteristic zero, with proofs checked independently of the
computer algebra system?
\end{question}
For superelliptic input the multiplicities already decide (\cref{odg:sg:thm:super}). For
general input a certificate should specify a normalization presentation, conductor
witnesses, a differential presentation and the finite derivative identity, not only a
yes-or-no answer \src{C16}. This refines the effective-certificate part of
\cref{odg:cr:q:coefficients}.

\begin{question}[Positive-characteristic replacement \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:q:charp}
Among one-place affine curves in characteristic $p$, which admit nonconstant $\crA$-points?
Can support-preserving Hasse--Schmidt operators separate subclasses according to the
$p$-divisibility of $\Gamma$?
\end{question}
The first step is to separate the failure of a derivation to detect a scale from the genuine
existence of a Hahn point; Seidenberg's higher-derivation theorem is relevant but not
sufficient by itself \src{C16}. \Cref{odg:sg:prop:oneplace} gives the one-place condition
in every characteristic; the question refines \cref{odg:cr:q:charp}.

\begin{question}[Higher-dimensional normalization invariance \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:q:birational}
For which affine varieties is the property $X(\crA)=X(\Bbbk)$ invariant under finite
birational morphisms? Can conductor certificates be combined with enough differential
tensors to remove singularity assumptions from the higher-dimensional criteria?
\end{question}
A promising restricted setting has a controlled rank-one valuation at the centre and
differential tensors detecting every relevant tangent direction; the curve theorem is the
one-dimensional model, not a proof there \src{C16}. The criteria in question are
\cref{odg:cr:thm:symmetric,odg:log:cor:criterion} (see \cref{odg:cr:q:higher}).

\begin{question}[Images, not just existence \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:q:images}
For an exceptional curve, describe the image of $C(\crA)$ under $\ct$, the possible support
order types in each fiber, and their behaviour under embeddings of ordered groups. How much
of this is encoded by the conductor?
\end{question}
\Cref{odg:sg:cor:scale} concerns existence only; fibers may keep rank and support
information that the dichotomy forgets \src{C16}. For smooth curves,
\cref{odg:cr:thm:arith} gives singleton fibers in the rigid case and
copies of $\crP$ in the affine-line case, over each ordinary point.
It does not give the affine-line fiber formula for singular curves.

\begin{question}[Formalization across the normal-form bridge \src{C16}]\label{odg:sg:q:lean}
Can the finite power identity, the conductor certificate and the one-variable valuation
argument be formalized in Lean and transported to the repository's surreal normal-form
interface?
\end{question}
Source C16 proposes the order: finite differential algebra, then the abstract valuation
contradiction, then normalization of curves and Riemann--Roch, and the proper-class
application last; none of these stages is claimed done \src{C16}.

The next ten questions are those of source C17 (\cref{odg:disc:sub:disc}), printed as it
states them, in the notation of \cref{odg:disc:sub:disc}. Source C17 calls them questions
left open by its article, not a catalogue certified by a literature search, and names the
first two as the most substantial.

\begin{question}[Full finite \'etale descent \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:q:etale}
Is base change an equivalence from finite \'etale $\Bbbk$-algebras to finite \'etale
$\crA$-algebras, for every field $\Bbbk$ of characteristic zero and every ordered abelian
group $\Gamma$?
\end{question}
The monogenic and unit-generated cases are settled
(\cref{odg:disc:thm:monogenic,odg:disc:thm:units}). A first objective is to prove or
disprove that the injection $\mathsf C_{\mathsf B}\to\mathsf B/\crP\mathsf B$ is always
surjective. A proof must bridge a global issue, which generic primitive elements and local
monogenic charts do not settle; a counterexample would have to hide its nonconstant
structure outside its unit group \src{C17}.

\begin{question}[Arithmetic \'etale descent \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:q:arithmetic}
For a number ring $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$, does every finite \'etale
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}$-algebra descend from $\mathfrak o$? Which additional hypotheses
on $\Bbbk$ are necessary?
\end{question}
\Cref{odg:disc:thm:arithmetic} handles monogenic presentations. A next step compares base
change to $\crA$ with reduction to $\mathfrak o$, keeping track of flatness and arithmetic
fibers; the field-coefficient unit theorem must not be applied over $\mathfrak o$
(\cref{odg:disc:rem:scope}). The cases $\mathfrak o=\Z,\Z[i]$ concern $\Oz$ and $\Og$
\src{C17}.

\begin{question}[Projective modules and nonnormal spectral data \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:q:picard}
What is $\operatorname{Pic}(\crA)$, and which projective modules occur as eigenmodules of a
matrix with nonzero constant characteristic discriminant?
\end{question}
The translation theorem fixes eigenvalues; the rest of the matrix is a system of projective
summands attached to the spectral idempotents. Normality makes them constant by positivity,
while \cref{odg:disc:ex:nonnormal} shows freedom without it. A classification up to
similarity should separate changes of basis from nontrivial projective summands \src{C17}.

\begin{question}[Sharp denominator certificates \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:q:certificate}
What is the least exponent $d(n,s)$ for which $\Delta^{d(n,s)}e_s(w)$ is a universal
polynomial in the coefficients and their derivatives? Can the certificates be generated
with practical size?
\end{question}
\Cref{odg:disc:lem:certificate} gives $d(n,s)\le2s$, and for $n=2$, $s=1$ one power of
$\Delta$ suffices (\cref{odg:disc:rem:quadratic}). The problem is finite algebra,
independent of surreal foundations \src{C17}.

\begin{question}[Nonconstant discriminants and quantitative defects \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:q:defect}
Can the support or valuation of $\Disc(P)$ bound the possible nonconstant motion of the root
gaps when the discriminant is not constant?
\end{question}
The identities \eqref{odg:disc:eq:certificate} remain valid, and their valuations give
inequalities between derivative coefficients and powers of $\Delta$, but the
zero-intersection argument no longer closes. A Newton-polygon theory of the velocity
polynomial could measure how far a polynomial is from a constant configuration plus a
translation \src{C17}. The example $T^2-T-\omega$ of \cref{odg:ex:hensel}, with
discriminant $1+4\omega$, is a test case.

\begin{question}[Positive characteristic \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:q:charp}
Which restrictions on degree, ramification or additive-polynomial structure restore a
useful translation theorem in characteristic $p$?
\end{question}
\Cref{odg:disc:ex:charp} shows that separability and a unit discriminant do not suffice. A
substitute must account for Frobenius and for derivations that do not see $p$-divisible
exponents; degrees below $p$, then tame splitting extensions, are natural first cases,
neither asserted sufficient \src{C17}. Compare \cref{odg:cr:q:charp,odg:sg:q:charp}.

\begin{question}[Restricted support rings \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:q:restricted}
For which proper subrings of a Hahn ring does the discriminant theorem hold, with the
translation in the subring?
\end{question}
A candidate should be closed under the Euler derivations, contain the purely nonconstant part
divided by the degree, and keep the opposite-support intersection; effective left-finite,
bounded-denominator and definability-restricted representations are natural tests
\src{C17}.

\begin{question}[Several polynomial configurations \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:q:several}
What are the minimal invariant conditions forcing a family of finite root configurations to
share one translation, or one affine transformation?
\end{question}
Constant discriminants and a spanning tree of constant resultants suffice for a translation
(\cref{odg:disc:thm:resultants}). A controlled common scaling would need a normalization of
the weights of discriminants and resultants, distinguishing labelled configurations from
their symmetric quotients and handling arithmetic descent \src{C17}.

\begin{question}[Polynomial dynamics \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:q:dynamics}
Under which extra conditions does $f=\overline f(X-h)+\beta$ become conjugate, rather than
left-right equivalent, to an ordinary polynomial?
\end{question}
\Cref{odg:disc:thm:critical} reduces this to a relation between $h$, $\beta$ and the chosen
dynamical normalization; fixed points, critical orbits or an ordinary marked value are
possible extra data, and iterates must be kept separate from the fixed-degree theorem
\src{C17}.

\begin{question}[Formal verification and constructive extraction \src{C17}]\label{odg:disc:q:lean}
Can the universal certificate and the finite \'etale unit theorem be formalized for the
repository's actual Hahn and surreal constructions, with an executable translation
extractor for supported notation systems?
\end{question}
The extractor is \eqref{odg:disc:eq:h}; the difficulty is certified equality of the
resulting infinite coefficients and a proof that the input denotes a legal Hahn series. A
verified finite-support implementation, then the generic support interface, is proposed as a
first milestone (\cref{odg:disc:sub:audit}) \src{C17}.

\subsection{Synthesis}

The arithmetic of $\Oz$ is governed by three simultaneous facts:
\[
 \Oz/\Pi=\Z,\qquad \Oz^\times=\{\pm1\},\qquad\Frac(\Oz)=\No.
\]
The first makes ordinary congruences and equation-existence questions look
classical. The second imposes powerful rigidity on nonzero product fibers.
The third makes projective representation flexible while saying little about
primitive representatives. Growth degree, support and chosen Euler
derivations then govern whether individual solutions escape the ordinary
integers \src{05}.

For Diophantine equations the key divide is not only between finite and
infinite numbers. It is between \emph{equation existence}, which the
retraction controls; \emph{multiplicative, differential or bounded
rigidity}, which can force every coordinate to be ordinary; and
\emph{polynomial flexibility}, which creates infinite points without any
infinitesimal tail \src{02}. The difference between a Pell conic and a
three-variable indefinite quadric is structural: binary factorization forces
constants, while an additional indefinite dimension supports finite
polynomial isometries. The guards use the rigid side of this dichotomy to
recover the ordinary integers inside $\Oz$. Omnific integers preserve much
more of ordinary equational existence than their size suggests, and much less
of ordinary quantified arithmetic than their discreteness suggests
\src{05}. None of this requires unique factorization, a Euclidean termination
principle, or Conway's refinement property.

Sources 06 and 07 show that the decomposition itself is visible from inside
the ring. The ideal $\Pi$ is defined by $\exists y\,(x^2=2y^2)$, its complement is
the set of divisors of values of $\Lambda$, and $\Z$ is cut out by a Pell
equation, a divisibility and an intersective certificate; so the retraction
$\ct$, although not a symbol of the language, has a Diophantine graph. The
multiplier ring of $\Pi$ then recovers the real coefficients, and with them the
valued field $\No$, from the pure ring $\Oz$, while the Gaussian ring $\Og$
recovers $\C$ but not its real form. Arithmetic recovery and recovery of a real
form are different tasks \src{06}.

Sources 08 and 09 say how much the third fact leaves of ordinary fractions.
Wherever a point has a unimodular presentation over $\BR$, and in particular
for all rational functions of one purely infinite parameter, the retraction
decides lowest terms: a rational constant vector gives a principal denominator
ideal and a primitive presentation unique up to an overall sign, and an
irrational one gives a
scalar copy of $\Pi$ and no gcd. This arithmetic is invisible to the order:
both kinds of point occur in every interval, and the specialization is
continuous nowhere.

Sources C10--C14 turn the differential side of the second divide into geometry.
Euler derivations preserve the one-sided ring and push the opposite valuation ring
into its maximal ideal. Properness lets the same field point be seen from
both rings, so each positive-degree global symmetric differential has zero
contraction against the induced Euler tangent. The differential itself need
not vanish at that point. Tangent separation then forces constancy. For
smooth curves the three mechanisms are then exhaustive: positive genus gives
differential rigidity, two or more punctures of a rational curve give unit rigidity,
and the single remaining case, the affine line, gives polynomial flexibility with
every fiber a copy of $\Pi$. The elliptic test equation of \cref{odg:q:affine} is the
first case with a one-line certificate, and its singular members are the polynomial
case. Source C15 carries the differential side to logarithmic forms: a form with
logarithmic poles along the boundary still contracts into the valuation ring, so with the
image condition $\partial(\crA)\subseteq\crP$ its Euler contraction
vanishes as well, and complements whose
logarithmic forms separate tangent directions are rigid in every dimension.

Source C16 removes smoothness from the curve case without assuming that points
lift to the normalization. One conductor element of the curve clears every power of a contracted
regular differential after a fixed multiplication, and at the one boundary place selected
by a nonconstant point the valuation is discrete; so a regular differential forces a
contradiction after finitely many derivatives. The only escape is normalization by the
affine line, and larger scales never make a rigid constant curve flexible.

\subsection{Relations to neighbouring reports}\label{odg:sub:neighbours}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
\item \fn{docs/foundations-and-computation/foundations}: defines
$\Oz=\Pi\oplus\Z$ (\replabel{found:eq:omnific}, subsection
\replabel{found:sub:omnific}) with the same letter $\Pi$; this report develops
the arithmetic of that ring.
\item \fn{docs/surcomplex/trigonometry}: writes the purely infinite part
with $t$-exponents of the opposite sign (\replabel{trigonometry:eq:split};
translation in \cref{odg:sub:notation}); its integer-part construction agrees
with \cref{odg:thm:floor}, and its identity
$2\pi\Oz=\Pi+2\pi\Z$ (\replabel{trigonometry:eq:periodclass}) rests on the same
fact $r\Pi=\Pi$ for real $r\ne0$ as \cref{odg:thm:finitequotients}.
\item \fn{docs/surcomplex/gamma-and-zeta-functions}: the parity statements
of \replabel{gz:cor:canonical-zeros} ($-\omega/2\in\Oz$, $-\omega-1\notin2\Oz$)
are instances of \cref{odg:thm:finitequotients}; its statement that the
internal integers of an ultrapower are not $\Oz$ is consistent with
\cref{odg:thm:induction,odg:rem:ringinduction}.
\item \fn{docs/surreal/transcendence-over-bounded-support}: its
hypothesis \replabel{bst:hyp:gcd} concerns the rank-one bounded-support
ring over an algebraically closed coefficient field, whose units are the
nonzero monomials. Its support is bounded in both directions and may
contain either sign of exponent. By contrast, \cref{odg:thm:nogcd}
concerns $\Oz$, whose exponents are all nonnegative and whose units are
$\pm1$. The conditional GCD assertion and the no-GCD theorem apply to
different rings and do not imply or contradict each other.
\item \fn{docs/surreal/set-sized-quotients-of-omnific-integers}: prints the
universal set-sized quotient theorem (\cref{odg:fact:settarget}) with source
05's proof and further threshold results; its preliminaries overlap
\cref{odg:sec:basic,odg:sec:residues,odg:sec:class}. Its manuscript 13
proves the Gaussian fiber theorem, the kernel converse and \'etale norm
rigidity printed here once in \cref{odg:sec:rigidity}; its manuscript 16
proves the fresh-scale image-gap theorem of which the equations of
\cref{odg:ex:nonsquare,odg:prop:binomial} are special cases. Its manuscript
14 proves the constant-direction criterion over $\Z$ and $\Z[i]$ printed here
as \cref{odg:frac:cor:gaussiandirections}; its coefficient-lattice results
contain the rank-two case of \cref{odg:frac:thm:realconstants}
(\replabel{osq:tn:thm:duals}), the intersection formula of
\cref{odg:frac:ex:nolcm} for monomial $t$ (\replabel{osq:tn:thm:syzygy}), and
the scalar step of \cref{odg:frac:thm:pullback} (\replabel{osq:tn:thm:hom}).
Localization at the positive monomials gives $\No$
(\replabel{osq:prop:fractions}), and the residue field $\Q$ of $\Oz_\Pi$ agrees
with its list of small primes (\replabel{osq:cor:smallprimes}). Its class-valued
derivations $\Oz\to\Pi$ (\replabel{osq:prop:classder}) contain the support inclusion
$\partial(\crA)\subseteq\crP$ used in \cref{odg:cr:sec:principle}; its theorem that
derivations of the class ring into set-sized modules vanish
(\replabel{osq:thm:derivations}) is no conflict, since the Euler derivations here act
on set-sized workspaces and take values in them. A derivation on a
workspace is not a derivation from the full class $\Oz$ into a set-sized
$\Oz$-module; the latter module structure is an additional hypothesis. Its manuscript 18's canonical translation
and rich-target theorems (\replabel{osq:or:thm:canonical}, \replabel{osq:or:thm:targets})
give the same translation normal forms as
\cref{odg:disc:thm:translation,odg:disc:thm:critical}, for integer-valued
polynomials with more inputs of ordinary output, or of output in one
ordinary coset, than their degree. These are different hypotheses from a
constant discriminant (\cref{odg:disc:rem:osq}); discreteness alone does not
force a polynomial into translation form.
\item \fn{docs/surreal/omnific-preserving-automorphisms}: its support-preserving
Euler derivations map $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(k,\Gamma)$ into $\Pi_k(\Gamma)$
(\replabel{opa:par:prop:Euler}), the support inclusion of \cref{odg:cr:lem:euler} on
the arithmetic rings; sources C10--C14 did not cite it.
\item \fn{docs/surcomplex/hahn-tate-uniformization}: its Tate points concern field
points of curves with nonconstant $j$-invariant, outside the constant-coefficient
hypothesis of \cref{odg:cr:sec:curves}; like
\cref{odg:cr:rem:fieldpoints,odg:cr:sub:boundaries}, they show that field points and
points with varying coefficients are not constrained by the ring rigidity here.
\item \fn{docs/surreal/omnific-groups-and-lattices}: its non-elementary
unipotents and cusp-residue obstruction
(\replabel{ogl:el:thm:nonel}, \replabel{ogl:el:prop:cuspresidue}) show that the
focusing matrices of \cref{odg:frac:lem:focusmatrix} are not products of
elementary matrices at irrational centres, and that the density of
\cref{odg:frac:cor:dense} needs such matrices (\cref{odg:frac:rem:elementary}).
That report also cites \cref{odg:thm:nogcd,odg:thm:realray}, and it answers
\cref{odg:q:affine} for closed subgroup schemes of $\GL_N$ over $\Z$ (and over
$\Z[i]$ for $\Og$), not for general affine varieties or curves; its updated remark
\replabel{ogl:alg:rem:affine} now records the smooth classification and the
normalization criterion for geometrically integral singular curves. The
latter is a geometric criterion for nonconstant $\crA$-points, not a complete
arithmetic existence test over $\Oz$: the case of an affine-line
normalization with integer points but no real normalization preimage of
any of them remains open here (\cref{odg:sg:q:real}). Over $\Og$, an ordinary
Gaussian point and an affine-line normalization do suffice
(\cref{odg:sg:cor:gaussian}). The two answers agree where
they overlap (\cref{odg:cr:rem:ogl}): $\Ga$ is nonrigid and the one-dimensional tori
are rigid; the vector-group kernel of \cref{odg:cr:thm:groupkernel} is its unipotent
kernel \replabel{ogl:alg:prop:bch} for commutative groups; semiabelian rigidity
extends its torus freezing. For a smooth geometrically integral real affine
curve, being the affine line is equivalent to its complexification being
the affine line. Thus rigidity over the real and complex support rings
coincides; arithmetic existence still requires the relevant ordinary
point. Such geometric rigidity does not coincide for all groups ($\SO_3$).
\Cref{odg:def:thm:homct} concerns homomorphisms into class-sized Hahn rings as
well and is consistent with the quotient theorem there.
\item \fn{docs/surcomplex/surcomplex-field-automorphisms}: constructs the
coefficient-fixing phase twists and the dilations $\mathsf S_a$; source 06
credits it and adds that the twist of \cref{odg:def:lem:twist} preserves $\Og$,
whence \cref{odg:def:thm:norealaxis}.
\item \fn{docs/surcomplex/single-dilation-hahn-support}: shows that the single
exponent dilation $S_2$ of the full Hahn field $k((t^\Gamma))$, with
$k$ of characteristic zero and $\Gamma$ nonzero and $2$-divisible, defines
its coefficient field, constant coefficient and valuation ring in the
language of fields with one named automorphism; \cref{odg:def:thm:multiplier,odg:def:thm:realrecovery} recover the
coefficient field, order and valuation ring from the pure ring $\Oz$ and its
Diophantine ideal instead.
\item \fn{docs/surreal/euclidean-three-space}: its theorem that class
homomorphisms from $\SO(3,\No)$ to set-sized groups factor through the
standard part (\replabel{e3:cut:thm:smallquotient}) is an analogous size
phenomenon for groups, not the same theorem; by \cref{odg:cor:orthogonal},
$\SO(3,\Oz)$ is finite.
\end{itemize}

batch item numbers; they are dated September 22, 2026, and the repository

eight placed in commit \texttt{cf350b1}, numbered here after the earlier
sources, as their shipped file prefixes are; they are dated September 23,
2026, and the commit they pin is
\nolinkurl{71e9606d297c9b98c732070dc462682cc6948981} (``Prove Prony's main
theorem, holonomic ODE rigidity and eight more results''), which precedes
\texttt{be06fc8}. None of the five was aware of the others. Item 03 of the
second batch is manuscript 13 of the sibling report; its Gaussian and \'etale
fiber results (its Sections 8 and 9) are printed here, tagged \src{13}, and
its remaining results there. The manuscripts themselves are not shipped.
Their code and data are shipped under the prefixes
\fn{01-diophantine-geometry-}, \fn{02-diophantine-},
\fn{05-diophantine-rigidity-}, \fn{06-definability-reconstruction-} and

Sources 08 and 09 are items 01 and 02 of a batch of nine placed in commit

\nolinkurl{9a385d3957bdfe3d9ea79f9a524751c90bd2c894} (``Merge the rewritten

pointers to C12--C14\\
\ref{odg:frac:sec:fractions} Fractions & 08 base: multiplier theorem,
rational-function classification, real constants, curves, localization,
models, scale defect, $\Tor_1$, monomials, Gaussian dichotomy; 09 region
$\Vreg$, denominator calculus, least denominators, orbits, focusing, density,
nowhere continuity, examples; 14's Gaussian direction criterion credited;
merge remarks \ref{odg:frac:rem:halving}, \ref{odg:frac:rem:elementary} and
\cref{odg:frac:cor:homogeneous}\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
